\section{Instructions}
\begin{itemize}
    \item This exam consists of four sections, each with a total of 33 points. You are required to choose three out of the four sections and answer the questions in the selected sections. You will earn an additional point for writing your name and student ID number on your answer sheet. The maximum possible score you can achieve is 100.
    \item Please clearly indicate your section choices at the very beginning of your answer sheet. If you answer questions in more than three sections, only the first three sections will be graded.
\end{itemize}

\section{Statement of the problems}

\noindent{\bfseries A. Machine Learning Theory}\par

\examstatement{Problem 1. [7 pts.]}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a class of binary classifiers over a domain $\mathcal{X}$. Let $\mathcal{D}$ be an unknown distribution over $\mathcal{X}$, and let $f$ be the target hypothesis in $\mathcal{H}$. Fix some $h \in \mathcal{H}$. Show that the variance of the empirical loss $L_S(h)$, over all possible samples $S$ of size $m$ drawn from $\mathcal{D}$, is inversely proportional to $m$. That is, show the following relationship:
\[
\Var_{S | x \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \bigl[ L_S(h) \bigr] \propto \frac{1}{m}.
\]
More specifically, prove the exact expression:
\[
\Var_{S | x \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \bigl[ L_S(h) \bigr] = \frac{L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h) \cdot (1 - L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h))}{m}.
\]

\examstatement{Problem 2. [7 pts.]}
Let $\mathcal{X}$ be a domain, and let $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \dots, \mathcal{D}_m$ be a sequence of distributions over $\mathcal{X}$. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a finite class of binary classifiers over $\mathcal{X}$, and let $f \in \mathcal{H}$. Suppose we obtain a sample $S = \{(x_1, y_1), \dots, (x_m, y_m)\}$, where the $i$-th instance $x_i$ is sampled from $\mathcal{D}_i$, and $y_i = f(x_i)$. Let $\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m$ denote the average as:
\[
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m = \frac{\mathcal{D}_1 + \dots + \mathcal{D}_m}{m}.
\]
Fix an accuracy parameter $\epsilon = \frac{1}{4}$. Show that
\[
\Pbb \left[ \exists h \in \mathcal{H} \text{ s.t. } L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h) > \epsilon \text{ and } L_{(S, f)}(h) = 0 \right] \le |\mathcal{H}| e^{-m/4}.
\]
\textit{Hint:} $\ln\left(\frac{3}{4}\right) < -\frac{1}{4}$.

\examstatement{Problem 3. [9 pts.]}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{H}'$ be two families of functions mapping from $\mathcal{X}$ to $\{0, 1\}$ with finite VC dimensions.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} Show that
    \[
    \VCdim(\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}') \le \VCdim(\mathcal{H}) + \VCdim(\mathcal{H}') + 1.
    \]
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Use this to determine the VC dimension of the hypothesis set formed by the union of axis-aligned rectangles and triangles in dimension 2. You may use the fact that the VC dimension of the hypothesis set formed by the union of triangles in dimension 2 is 7, without any proof.
\end{enumerate}

\examstatement{Problem 4. [10 pts.]}
Prove the following two statements:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} Let $\mathcal{D}$ be a distribution. Let $S = (z_1, \dots, z_m)$ be an i.i.d. sequence of examples. Let $A$ be a learning algorithm that is on-average replace-one stable with rate $\epsilon(m)$. Then:
    \[
    \E_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \left[ L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - L_S(A(S)) \right] \le \epsilon(m).
    \]
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} Let $\ell$ be a convex $\rho$-Lipschitz loss function. The regularised Empirical Risk Minimisation (ERM) satisfies:
    \[
    \E_S \bigl[ L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \bigr] \le L_{\mathcal{D}}(w^*) + \lambda \|w^*\|^2 + \frac{2\rho^2}{\lambda m},
    \]
    where
    \[
    w^* = \argmin_{w \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(w).
    \]
\end{enumerate}

\noindent{\bfseries B. Deep Learning and Reinforcement Learning}\par

\examstatement{Problem 1. [18 pts.]}
Consider a classification problem: the training dataset is given as $\{(\mathbf{x}_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^N$, where $\mathbf{x}_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ represents the input features, and $y_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, C\}$ represents the class labels. A supervised deep learning pipeline typically includes preparing training data, defining a hypothesis space, designing a training scheme, and optimizing the network. Answer the following questions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Define the hypothesis space consisting of all neural networks structured with:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item a feature extractor: a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) that maps the inputs to a learned feature representation,
        \item a classifier: a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) that maps the feature representation to class probabilities.
    \end{itemize}
    Please specify the input, output and the parameters for training.
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Specify an appropriate loss function for this classification problem and explain how stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is used to optimize the neural network.
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} To stabilize training, batch normalization is often applied. Describe how batch normalization works during the training and testing phase.
    \item \textbf{[3 pts.]} If the training error is unsatisfactory, describe what adjustments you can make to improve the expressivity of the neural network. Discuss at least two approaches.
    \item \textbf{[3 pts.]} If the training error is low but the testing error is high, propose strategies to reduce overfitting. Discuss at least two approaches.
\end{enumerate}

\examstatement{Problem 2. [15 pts.]}
Generative models aim to train a neural network generator to produce samples similar to the training data. The forward process of a diffusion model progressively adds noise to the data, transforming the data distribution into a normal distribution. The reverse process gradually denoises the data, reverting the normal distribution back to the data distribution. In score-based diffusion models, the forward process can be represented as a Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE). Consider the Variance Preserving (VP) SDE:
\[
d\mathbf{x} = -\frac{1}{2}\beta(t)\mathbf{x} dt + \sqrt{\beta(t)} d\mathbf{W}_t,
\]
where $\mathbf{W}_t$ is a standard Wiener process, and $\beta(t)$ is a time-dependent noise schedule.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Write down the reverse-time SDE corresponding to the forward process and explain why training the diffusion model requires score matching as follows:
    \[
    \min_{\theta} \E_t \left[ \lambda(t) \E_{\mathbf{x}_t} \left\| s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t, t) - \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t} \log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t) \right\|_2^2 \right],
    \]
    where $\lambda(t)$ is a weighting function, $s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t, t)$ is the neural network to train and $p_t$ is the marginal distribution of $\mathbf{x}_t$.
    \item \textbf{[6 pts.]} Prove that
    \[
    \E_{\mathbf{x}_t} \left\| s(\theta, t) - \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t} \log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t) \right\|_2^2 = \E_{(\mathbf{x}_0, \mathbf{x}_t)} \left\| s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t, t) - \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t} \log p_{t|0}(\mathbf{x}_t \mid \mathbf{x}_0) \right\|_2^2 + C,
    \]
    where $C$ is a constant, $p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)$ is the marginal distribution of $\mathbf{x}_t$, and $p_{t|0}(\mathbf{x}_t \mid \mathbf{x}_0)$ is the conditional distribution of $\mathbf{x}_t$ given the original data $\mathbf{x}_0$.
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} For the given VP SDE, what is the conditional distribution $p_{t|0}(\mathbf{x}_t \mid \mathbf{x}_0)$? Based on this, derive the final denoising score-matching loss function for training score-based diffusion models.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent{\bfseries C. Optimization Methods in Artificial Intelligence}\par

Let $f : \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}$ be an $L$-smooth and $\mu$-strongly convex function. The stochastic gradient is defined as:
\[
g(x, \xi) = \nabla f(x) + \xi,
\]
where $\xi$ is a zero-mean random variable with $\E \bigl[ \|\xi\|^2 \bigr] \le \sigma^2$. Consider the SGD method:
\[
x^{k+1} = \prox_{\gamma R} \bigl( x^k - \gamma g(x^k, \xi^k) \bigr),
\]
used to solve the optimization problem:
\[
\min_{x} f(x) + R(x).
\]

\examstatement{Problem 1. [5 pts.] L-Smoothness and $\mu$-Strong Convexity}
Provide the definitions of $L$-smoothness and $\mu$-strong convexity, respectively.

\examstatement{Problem 2. [6 pts.] Combining Smoothness and Strong Convexity}
Suppose that $f(x)$ is continuously differentiable, $L$-smooth, and $\mu$-convex.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Show that $g(x) = f(x) - \frac{\mu}{2}\|x\|^2$ is continuously differentiable, convex and $(L - \mu)$-smooth.
    \item Using the fact
    \[
    \frac{1}{L - \mu} \|\nabla g(x) - \nabla g(y)\|^2 \le \langle \nabla g(x) - \nabla g(y), x - y \rangle,
    \]
    show that
    \[
    \mu \|x - y\|^2 + \frac{1}{L} \|\nabla f(x) - \nabla f(y)\|^2 \le \left(1 + \frac{\mu}{L}\right) \langle \nabla f(x) - \nabla f(y), x - y \rangle.
    \]
\end{itemize}

\examstatement{Problem 3. [6 pts.] Recurrence Relation}
Using the non-expansiveness property of $\prox$ and the optimality condition of $\prox_{\gamma R}$, derive the recurrence relation for $\|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2$:
\[
\|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2 \le \|x^k - x^* - \gamma (\nabla f(x^k) - \nabla f(x^*) + \xi^k)\|^2.
\]

\examstatement{Problem 4. [6 pts.] Variance Decomposition}
What is variance decomposition? Explain how it is applied to $\|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2$, and derive:
\[
\E_k \bigl[ \|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2 \bigr] \le \|x^k - x^* - \gamma (\nabla f(x^k) - \nabla f(x^*))\|^2 + \gamma^2 \sigma^2,
\]
where $\E_k[\cdot]$ denotes the conditional expectation given $\xi^k, \dots, \xi^0$.

\examstatement{Problem 5. [5 pts.] Simplified Recurrence}
Using the tower property, prove that when $\gamma = \frac{2}{\mu + L}$, the recurrence simplifies to:
\[
\E \bigl[ \|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2 \bigr] \le (1 - \rho) \E \bigl[ \|x^k - x^*\|^2 \bigr] + \gamma^2 \sigma^2,
\]
where $\rho = \frac{4\mu L}{(\mu + L)^2}$.

\examstatement{Problem 6. [5 pts.] Complexity}
Prove that for any desired precision $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists a step size $\gamma$ such that:
\[
k \ge \frac{L/\mu + 3}{4} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}
\]
implies:
\[
\E \bigl[ \|x^k - x^*\|^2 \bigr] \le \varepsilon \|x^0 - x^*\|^2 + \frac{\gamma \sigma^2}{\mu}.
\]

\noindent{\bfseries D. Natural Language Processing}\par

\noindent\textbf{Short Answer Questions on Concepts}

\examstatement{Problem 1. [3 pts.]}
In a trigram language model, how is $p(w_3 \mid w_1, w_2)$ learned from a training corpus? In real-world applications, we may encounter situations where $(w_1, w_2)$, or even $w_1$ or $w_2$, do not appear in the training corpus. How can we estimate $p(w_3 \mid w_1, w_2)$ in such cases?

\examstatement{Problem 2. [3 pts.]}
What is the central idea behind representation learning? Which algorithms implement this concept?

\examstatement{Problem 3. [3 pts.]}
Why do current large language models (LLMs) use top-$P$ sampling during inference instead of greedy or beam search? Additionally, why is top-$P$ sampling considered superior to other methods, such as top-$K$ sampling?

\examstatement{Problem 4. [3 pts.]}
List several key points that contribute to the success of current LLM based approach of Artificial General Intelligence and explain their importance.

\examstatement{Problem 5. [3 pts.]}
Identify at least two advantages of the Transformer architecture compared to recurrent-based models (e.g., LSTM, GRU) for sequence-to-sequence tasks.

\noindent\textbf{Questions on Algorithm Analysis}

\examstatement{Problem 6. [5 pts.]}
Explain why the data scaling law, which describes the relationship between performance and sample size, holds true from the perspective of statistical learning. Please provide a detailed analysis process, avoiding overly simplistic assumptions (such as assuming the data follows a Gaussian distribution).

\examstatement{Problem 7. [5 pts.]}
We are implementing the Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF) procedure to train a Large Language Model (LLM). Assuming that a reward model has already been trained, explain how reinforcement learning is used to align the model with human preferences. The explanation should include details about data usage, the reinforcement learning loss function, and the gradient of the loss.

\noindent\textbf{Questions on Applied System Design}

\examstatement{Problem 8. [8 pts.]}
We have a general-purpose Large Language Model (LLM), but it performs poorly on question-answering tasks in a specific problem domain due to a lack of domain-specific knowledge. Although we have collected a large corpus of documents in this domain, we do not have labeled question-answering pairs. Design a system that can accurately answer questions within this domain. You may incorporate additional components, such as embedding models, into the system.

\noindent\rule{\linewidth}{0.4pt}

\section{Statement and solutions}

\subsection{What learn means in this exam}\label{sec:spr2025-learn}

The official problems use the verb \emph{learn} for several different objects: a hypothesis, a parameter, a feature map, a conditional probability, a policy, a class that is PAC-learnable, and (in the headings) a study route. Those are not five mysterious capacities. In the AI-theory framework of Section~A they are slices of one map. This note is the glossary for the whole transcript---Sections A--D---not a substitute for the PAC definitions later in Section~A.

\begin{definition}[Learning, in this exam]\label{def:spr2025-learn}
Fix an example space \(\mathcal{Z}\), a hypothesis class \(\mathcal{H}\), and a sample \(S=(z_1,\ldots,z_m)\). A \emph{learning algorithm} is a map
\[
A:\mathcal{Z}^m\to\mathcal{H},\qquad S\mapsto A(S).
\]
To \emph{learn} is to compute \(A(S)\). The data \(S\) are random, so the output \(A(S)\) is random. The quality of that output is a risk \(L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S))\) (or a return \(J\)) under an unknown \(\mathcal{D}\), not a claim that a particular token, trigram, or weight has been ``understood.''
\end{definition}

If \(\mathcal{H}=\{h_\theta:\theta\in\Theta\}\) is parameterised, then \(A(S)=h_{\widehat\theta_S}\) and ``learn a parameter'' is the same event written in coordinates. The class \(\mathcal{H}\) itself is \emph{specified}, not learned: vocabulary \(\mathcal{V}\), trigram order, network depth, the reserved symbol \(\mathrm{UNK}\), and the choice of loss are part of the specification of \(A\) and \(\mathcal{H}\).

\paragraph{How the verb is used in this transcript.}
The left column is the wording that actually appears. The middle column is the object that \(A\) (or a neighbouring map) returns. The right column is where the wording occurs.

\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\noindent

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.35cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.15cm}|
}
\hline
\textbf{Wording} & \textbf{Object} & \textbf{Where} \\
\hline

\hline

\hline

\hline

\hline

\end{tabularx}
}

\paragraph{What is not learning.}
A type outside \(\mathcal{V}\), or a cell with count zero, is not an element of \(\mathcal{H}\) that \(A\) can select from local evidence. Mapping that type to \(\mathrm{UNK}\) is a rewrite of the query so that it lies in \(\mathcal{V}^3\). Smoothing or backoff writes a number for an empty cell by sharing strength with other coordinates of the same \(A(S)\). In both cases the number is an output of \(A\), not a proof that ``the model learned that word / trigram.'' Inference rules (greedy, beam, top-\(P\)), retrieval indices, and a frozen \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) are likewise not learning: \(\theta\) is held fixed. Section~C studies the proximal-SGD recurrence that often \emph{implements} \(A\); the complexity bound there is about \(\|x^k-x^\star\|^2\), not about PAC learnability.

\paragraph{How to read the four sections with this glossary.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{A.} The official definition is restated in the \jump{sec:spr2025-a-framework}{Section~A framework}. PAC learnability is a property of \(\mathcal{H}\). Stability of \(A\) (Problem~4) is a property of the map, not of a single weight.
    \item \textbf{B.} ``Learned feature representation'' means the extractor coordinates of \(A(S)\). ``Deep learning'' names the class. Reinforcement learning changes the data-generating process (trajectories, a return \(J\)) but not the meaning of \(A\).
    \item \textbf{C.} ``Learning rate'' and stochastic gradients are the inner loop. Do not write that SGD ``learns the smoothness constant.''
    \item \textbf{D.} Problem~1 learns a conditional table; Problem~2 learns \(e_\theta\); Problem~6 discusses how that table or network improves with \(N\); Problem~7 learns \(\pi_\theta\) after \(r_\phi\) is already frozen; Problem~8 mostly does \emph{not} learn domain facts into \(\theta\).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Exam-ready one-liner.}
Write \(A:S\mapsto A(S)\in\mathcal{H}\). Then name the object: a predictor, a coordinate \(\theta\), a table \(\widehat p\), a map \(e_\theta\), or a policy \(\pi_\theta\). If the object is not already a member or coordinate of \(\mathcal{H}\), it cannot be learned from its own missing count.

\section{A. Machine Learning Theory}

\subsection{Problem 1. [7 pts.]}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a class of binary classifiers over a domain $\mathcal{X}$. Let $\mathcal{D}$ be an unknown distribution over $\mathcal{X}$, and let $f$ be the target hypothesis in $\mathcal{H}$. Fix some $h \in \mathcal{H}$. Show that the variance of the empirical loss $L_S(h)$, over all possible samples $S$ of size $m$ drawn from $\mathcal{D}$, is inversely proportional to $m$. That is, show the following relationship:
\[
\Var_{S | x \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \bigl[ L_S(h) \bigr] \propto \frac{1}{m}.
\]
More specifically, prove the exact expression:
\[
\Var_{S | x \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \bigl[ L_S(h) \bigr] = \frac{L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h) \cdot (1 - L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h))}{m}.
\]

\subsubsection{Preliminaries}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Bernoulli random variables:} Each individual 0-1 loss is an indicator of classification error.
    \item \textbf{Mean and variance of a Bernoulli variable:} If $Z \sim \text{Bernoulli}(p)$, then $\Var(Z) = p(1 - p)$.
    \item \textbf{Empirical loss as a sample mean:}
    \[
    L_S(h) = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} \mathbf{1}\{h(x_i) \neq f(x_i)\}.
    \]
    \item \textbf{Variance of the mean of independent random variables:}
    \[
    \Var\left(\frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} Z_i\right) = \frac{1}{m^2} \sum_{i=1}^{m} \Var(Z_i).
    \]
    \item \textbf{i.i.d.~sampling:} Independence makes the covariance terms vanish.
    \item \textbf{Interpretation of $1/m$ variance scaling:} Averaging independent observations reduces sampling fluctuation.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Notation:}\label{para:notation}

\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathcal{X}$: input/domain space.
    \item $x \in \mathcal{X}$: one input.
    \item $f : \mathcal{X} \to \{0, 1\}$: the unknown true labeling rule (target hypothesis).
    \item $h : \mathcal{X} \to \{0, 1\}$: a fixed candidate classifier.
    \item $\mathcal{D}$: unknown probability distribution over inputs $x$.
    \item $S = (x_1, \dots, x_m) \sim \mathcal{D}^m$: an i.i.d. sample of $m$ inputs.
    \item $y_i = f(x_i)$: the associated true label.
\end{itemize}


\begin{note}
    In this problem, the classifier $h$ is held fixed; only the random sample $S$ varies.
\end{note}
\begin{table}[ht]
    \centering
    \caption{\(L\)-related notation and meanings for Question 1.}
    \label{tab:L-notation}
    {\footnotesize
    \setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
    \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
      >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.35cm}|
      >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
      >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.1cm}|
      >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
      >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
    }
    \hline
    \textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Full name} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Random?} & \textbf{Meaning in Q1} \\
    \hline
    $\ell(h(x),f(x))$
      & Pointwise / example-wise loss
      & $\mathbf{1}\{h(x)\neq f(x)\}$
      & $x$ if sampled from $\mathcal{D}$
      & Error on one input: $1$ if $h$ errs, else $0$ \\
    \hline
    $\ell_f(h,x)$
      & Loss of $h$ vs.\ target $f$ at $x$
      & $\mathbf{1}\{h(x)\neq f(x)\}$
      & Same as above
      & Equivalent notation; labels given by $f$ \\
    \hline
    $L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h)$
      & Population loss / true risk
      & $\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}[\mathbf{1}\{h(x)\neq f(x)\}]$
      & Not random (fixed $\mathcal{D},f,h$)
      & True probability that $h$ errs on a random input \\
    \hline
    $L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)$
      & Population loss / true risk
      & Usually $\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\ell(h(x),y)]$
      & Depends on notation
      & True risk when $\mathcal{D}$ is over pairs $(x,y)$ \\
    \hline
    $L_S(h)$
      & Empirical loss / training error
      & $\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m \mathbf{1}\{h(x_i)\neq f(x_i)\}$
      & $S\sim\mathcal{D}^m$
      & Fraction of mistakes of $h$ on $S$ \\
    \hline
    $L_{S,f}(h)$, $L_{(S,f)}(h)$
      & Empirical loss with $f$ explicit
      & $\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m \mathbf{1}\{h(x_i)\neq f(x_i)\}$
      & Random through $S$
      & Same as $L_S(h)$; labels from $f$ \\
    \hline
    $p(1-p)$ with $p=L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h)$
      & Bernoulli variance factor
      & $L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h)\,(1-L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h))$
      & Not random for fixed setup
      & Variance of one loss $\ell(h(x),f(x))$ \\
    \hline
    $\operatorname{Var}_{S\sim\mathcal{D}^m}[L_S(h)]$
      & Variance of empirical loss
      & $\displaystyle\frac{L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h)\,(1-L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h))}{m}$
      & Over draws of $S$
      & Quantity asked in Q1 \\
    \hline
    \end{tabularx}
    }
\end{table}

\paragraph{A concrete example:}
In Question 1, the relevant loss is the \textbf{0--1 loss}:

\[
\ell_{0\text{--}1}(h(\boldsymbol{x}), f(\boldsymbol{x})) = \begin{cases} 0, & h(\boldsymbol{x}) = f(\boldsymbol{x}), \\ 1, & h(\boldsymbol{x}) \neq f(\boldsymbol{x}). \end{cases}
\]

Suppose $m = 5$, and for a fixed $h$ you obtain

\[
(\ell(h(\boldsymbol{x}_1), f(\boldsymbol{x}_1)), \dots, \ell(h(\boldsymbol{x}_5), f(\boldsymbol{x}_5))) = (0, 1, 0, 0, 1).
\]

Then

\[
L_S(h) = \frac{0 + 1 + 0 + 0 + 1}{5} = \frac{2}{5}.
\]

This says that $h$ makes errors on 40\% of this particular sample. It does \textbf{not} imply that the true risk is exactly $0.4$; the population loss $L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h)$ is the underlying error probability, whereas $L_S(h)$ is its random sample estimate. The purpose of Question 1 is to quantify the sampling variability of that estimate.

\paragraph{Difference among sample loss, population loss, and empirical loss:}
\noindent

{\small
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|l|X|X|X|X|}
    \hline
    \textbf{Level} 
    & \textbf{Name} 
    & \textbf{Formula in Question 1} 
    & \textbf{Input / averaging measure} 
    & \textbf{Interpretation} 
    \\ \hline

    One observation 
    & \textbf{Pointwise loss} (or per-example loss) 
    & $\ell_f(h, \boldsymbol{x}) = \mathbf{1}\{h(\boldsymbol{x}) \neq f(\boldsymbol{x})\}$ 
    & One specified $\boldsymbol{x}$ 
    & Did $h$ make an error on this particular input? 
    \\ \hline

    Entire population 
    & \textbf{Population loss}, \textbf{population risk}, \textbf{true risk}, or \textbf{expected risk} 
    & $L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h) = \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x} \sim \mathcal{D}}[\ell_f(h, \boldsymbol{x})]$ 
    & The unknown distribution $\mathcal{D}$ 
    & What is the long-run probability of error on a fresh input? 
    \\ \hline

    Finite sample 
    & \textbf{Empirical loss}, \textbf{empirical risk}, \textbf{training loss}, or \textbf{training error} 
    & $L_S(h) = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \ell_f(h, \boldsymbol{x}_i)$ 
    & The observed sample $S = (\boldsymbol{x}_1, \dots, \boldsymbol{x}_m)$ 
    & What fraction of errors does $h$ make on these $m$ training inputs? 
    \\ \hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}
The \textbf{empirical distribution} of a sample $S = (x_1, \dots, x_m)$ is
\[
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_S = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \delta_{x_i},
\]
where $\delta_{x_i}$ is the point mass at $x_i$. Therefore,
\[
L_S(h) = \mathbb{E}_{X \sim \widehat{\mathcal{D}}_S}[\ell_f(h, X)] = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \ell_f(h, x_i).
\]
So, for a statistician, empirical risk is simply an integral with respect to the \textbf{empirical measure}, while population risk is an integral with respect to the \textbf{true sampling measure} $\mathcal{D}$.


{\small
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|l|X|X|X|}
    \hline
    \textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Known after observing $S$?} & \textbf{Random before sampling?} & \textbf{Computable by the learner?} \\ \hline
    $\ell_f(h, x_i)$ for a labeled training point & Yes & Yes, because $x_i$ is random & Yes \\ \hline
    $L_S(h)$ & Yes & Yes, because the full sample $S$ is random & Yes \\ \hline
    $L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h)$ & Usually no & No, if $h, \mathcal{D}, f$ are fixed & Usually no, because $\mathcal{D}$ is unknown \\ \hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

For Question 1, $h$, $f$, and $\mathcal{D}$ are fixed. Thus the population risk
\[
p := L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h)
\]
is a fixed unknown number, but the empirical risk $L_S(h)$ is a random variable induced by drawing $S \sim \mathcal{D}^m$.

\subsubsection{Solution:}

Define

\[
Z_i := \ell_f(h, X_i) = \mathbf{1}\{h(X_i) \neq f(X_i)\}.
\]

Then, conditional on fixed $h, f, \mathcal{D}$,

\[
Z_1, \dots, Z_m \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \text{Bernoulli}(p), \quad p = L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h).
\]

And

\[
L_S(h) = \overline{Z}_m = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m Z_i.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\mathbb{E}_{S\mid x\sim  \mathcal{D}}[L_S(h)] = L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h),
\]

so empirical risk is an \textbf{unbiased estimator} of population risk. Moreover, by independence of the $Z_i$,

\begin{align*}
\operatorname{Var}_{S\mid x\sim  \mathcal{D}}[L_S(h)]
&= \operatorname{Var}\!\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m} Z_i\right)
= \frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m} \operatorname{Var}(Z_i)
= \frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m} p(1-p)
= \frac{p(1-p)}{m}
= \frac{L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h)(1 - L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(h))}{m}.
\end{align*}

This exact variance identity is what Question 1 asks you to prove: it quantifies how accurately the training error estimates the true error when $h$ is fixed.




\subsection{Problem 2. [7 pts.]}
Let $\mathcal{X}$ be a domain, and let $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \dots, \mathcal{D}_m$ be a sequence of distributions over $\mathcal{X}$. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a finite class of binary classifiers over $\mathcal{X}$, and let $f \in \mathcal{H}$. Suppose we obtain a sample $S = \{(x_1, y_1), \dots, (x_m, y_m)\}$, where the $i$-th instance $x_i$ is sampled from $\mathcal{D}_i$, and $y_i = f(x_i)$. Let $\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m$ denote the average as:
\[
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m = \frac{\mathcal{D}_1 + \dots + \mathcal{D}_m}{m}.
\]
Fix an accuracy parameter $\epsilon = \frac{1}{4}$. Show that
\[
\Pbb \left[ \exists h \in \mathcal{H} \text{ s.t. } L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h) > \epsilon \text{ and } L_{(S, f)}(h) = 0 \right] \le |\mathcal{H}| e^{-m/4}.
\]
\textit{Hint:} $\ln\left(\frac{3}{4}\right) < -\frac{1}{4}$.

\subsubsection{Preliminaries}

\paragraph*{Notations:}

We follow the notations in \cref{para:notation}. What's more,

{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.2cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Object} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\hline
Domain & $\mathcal{X}$ & The space of inputs/features \\
\hline
Label space & $\{0, 1\}$ & Binary classification labels \\
\hline
Hypothesis class & $\mathcal{H} \subseteq \{h : \mathcal{X} \to \{0, 1\}\}$ & All classifiers the learner may consider \\
\hline
Target hypothesis & $f \in \mathcal{H}$ & The true labeling rule \\
\hline
Example & $(x_i, y_i)$ & One labeled observation \\
\hline
Realizable label & $y_i = f(x_i)$ & Labels are noiseless and generated exactly by $f$ \\
\hline
Training sample & $S = \{(x_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^m$ & The observed labeled dataset \\
\hline
Finite class & $|\mathcal{H}| < \infty$ & The hypothesis class has a finite number of classifiers \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\par}

\begin{note}
    The assumption $f \in \mathcal{H}$ is the \textbf{realizability assumption}: the hypothesis class contains a perfect classifier, so a classifier that fits the data is not necessarily good, but at least $f$ itself has zero population and empirical loss. This is the standard setting for the basic finite-class PAC argument.
\end{note}


\subsubsection{What you may safely reuse}

Since you have already completed Question 1, you can reuse:

\begin{itemize}
    \item 0-1 pointwise loss;
    \item population loss as expected 0-1 loss;
    \item empirical loss as the sample average;
    \item the interpretation of a population loss as a misclassification probability;
    \item i.i.d. versus independent sampling.
\end{itemize}

But do \textbf{not} import the Bernoulli sample-mean variance calculation from Question 1. Question 2 uses a \textbf{probability-of-zero-errors} argument, not a variance argument.

\subsubsection{Common mistakes}

\begin{itemize}
    \item Treating $\boldsymbol{x}_i \sim \mathcal{D}_i$ as i.i.d. They are independent but generally have different distributions.
    \item Writing $\Pr(L_S(h) = 0) = (1 - L_{\bar{\mathcal{D}}_m, f}(h))^m$. This equality is generally false unless all $\mathcal{D}_i$ are identical.
    \item Forgetting that the product formula is valid only after $h$ is fixed.
    \item Replacing $L_{(S,f)}(h) = 0$ by ``small empirical error.'' The event here is exactly zero errors.
    \item Assuming events across different $h \in \mathcal{H}$ are independent. They are typically not; use only the union bound.
    \item Using the union bound before obtaining a bound for one fixed $h$.
    \item Missing the identity
\end{itemize}

\[
\sum_{i=1}^m L_{(\mathcal{D}_i, f)}(h) = m L_{(\bar{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h).
\]

\subsubsection{Readiness check}

The following five lines are the key to prove the question 2.

\[
\{L_{(S,f)}(h) = 0 \} \iff \bigcap_{i=1}^{m} \{h(x_i) = f(x_i)\},
\]

\[
\Pr(L_{(S,f)}(h) = 0) = \prod_{i=1}^{m} (1 - L_{(\mathcal{D}_i,f)}(h)),
\]

\[
\prod_{i=1}^{m} (1 - u_i) \leq \exp\left( -\sum_{i=1}^{m} u_i \right),
\]

\[
\frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} L_{(\mathcal{D}_i,f)}(h) = L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m,f)}(h),
\]

\[
\Pr\left( \bigcup_{h \in \mathcal{H}} B_h \right) \leq \sum_{h \in \mathcal{H}} \Pr(B_h).
\]

\subsubsection{Solution:}

Fix an arbitrary $h \in \mathcal{H}$, and define
\[
B_h := \left\{ L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h) > \frac{1}{4} \text{ and } L_{(S, f)}(h) = 0 \right\}.
\]
Then
\begin{align*}
\Pbb \left[ \exists h \in \mathcal{H} \text{ s.t. } L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h) > \epsilon \text{ and } L_{(S, f)}(h) = 0 \right]
&= \Pbb\Bigl(\bigcup_{h \in \mathcal{H}} B_h\Bigr)
\leq \sum_{h \in \mathcal{H}} \Pbb(B_h).
\end{align*}
For each fixed $h$: if $L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h) \le 1/4$, then $\Pbb(B_h)=0$. Otherwise $L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h) > 1/4$, and
\begin{align*}
\Pbb(B_h)
&= \Pbb\bigl(L_{(S,f)}(h)=0\bigr)
= \prod_{i=1}^{m}\bigl(1-L_{(\mathcal{D}_i,f)}(h)\bigr)
\leq \exp\Bigl(-\sum_{i=1}^{m} L_{(\mathcal{D}_i,f)}(h)\Bigr)
= \exp\bigl(-m L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h)\bigr)
    < e^{-m/4}.
\end{align*}
\begin{remark}[Why the first equality]
Once $h$ is fixed, $L_{(\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m, f)}(h)$ is a deterministic number: it is an expectation under $\overline{\mathcal{D}}_m$ and does not depend on the random sample $S$. The first conjunct in the definition of $B_h$ is therefore either always false or always true. In the present branch it is always true, so
\[
B_h = \bigl\{ L_{(S,f)}(h)=0 \bigr\}
\]
as events, and the first equality is just this identification. It is not a probabilistic estimate.
\end{remark}
Hence
\[
\sum_{h \in \mathcal{H}} \Pbb(B_h) < |\mathcal{H}|\, e^{-m/4}.
\]



\subsection{Problem 3. [9 pts.]}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{H}'$ be two families of functions mapping from $\mathcal{X}$ to $\{0, 1\}$ with finite VC dimensions.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} Show that
    \[
    \VCdim(\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}') \le \VCdim(\mathcal{H}) + \VCdim(\mathcal{H}') + 1.
    \]
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Use this to determine the VC dimension of the hypothesis set formed by the union of axis-aligned rectangles and triangles in dimension 2. You may use the fact that the VC dimension of the hypothesis set formed by the union of triangles in dimension 2 is 7, without any proof.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Preliminaries}

Given two binary hypothesis classes
\[
\mathcal{H}, \mathcal{H}' \subseteq \{0, 1\}^{\mathcal{X}},
\]
Their set-theoretic union is defined as 
\[
\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}' = \{g : g \in \mathcal{H} \text{ or } g \in \mathcal{H}'\}.
\]

\paragraph{Shattering: the key idea}

A class $\mathcal{H}$ shatters $C = \{x_1, \dots, x_n\}$ if

$$\forall (y_1, \dots, y_n) \in \{0, 1\}^n, \quad \exists h \in \mathcal{H} \quad \text{such that} \quad h(x_i) = y_i \text{ for all } i.$$

There are exactly $2^n$ possible binary labelings of $n$ points. Hence:

$$\mathcal{H} \text{ shatters } C \iff |\mathcal{H}|_C| = 2^n.$$

You need to be comfortable moving between these equivalent formulations:


\[
\text{``all labelings are realizable''} \iff \text{``the set is shattered''} \iff \text{``the restriction has cardinality } 2^n\text{''}.
\]

\begin{example}[Minimal example]
    

If $C = \{x_1, x_2\}$, then shattering means that all four label vectors must be realized:

$$(0, 0), \quad (0, 1), \quad (1, 0), \quad (1, 1).$$

If even one is missing—for example, no $h \in \mathcal{H}$ labels $x_1$ as $1$ and $x_2$ as $0$—then $\mathcal{H}$ does not shatter $C$.
\end{example}

\paragraph{VC dimension}


The \textbf{VC dimension} of $\mathcal{H}$ is

\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) := \sup \{|C| : C \subseteq \mathcal{X}, \mathcal{H} \text{ shatters } C\} .
\]

In words:

\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) \text{ is the largest number of points that can be shattered by } \mathcal{H}.
\]

If $\mathcal{H}$ can shatter arbitrarily large finite sets, then

\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) = \infty.
\]

If the largest shattered set has $d$ points, then

\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) = d.
\]

\paragraph{Exact logical meaning}

To prove
\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) = d,
\]
you need \textbf{two} statements:
\[
\exists C \subseteq \mathcal{X}, \quad |C| = d, \quad \text{such that } \mathcal{H} \text{ shatters } C,
\]
and
\[
\forall C' \subseteq \mathcal{X}, \quad |C'| = d + 1, \quad \mathcal{H} \text{ does not shatter } C'.
\]
The first is a \textbf{lower bound}; the second is an \textbf{upper bound}.

\begin{example}[Small example: thresholds on $\mathbb{R}$]
Consider
\[
\mathcal{H}_{\text{thr}} = \{h_a(x) = \mathbf{1}\{x \geq a\} : a \in \mathbb{R}\}.
\]

For one point $x_1$, the two possible labels can be realized:
\[
h_a(x_1) = 0 \quad \text{by choosing } a > x_1,
\]
and
\[
h_a(x_1) = 1 \quad \text{by choosing } a \leq x_1.
\]

Therefore, one point is shattered.

For two ordered points $x_1 < x_2$, the labeling
\[
(h(x_1), h(x_2)) = (1, 0)
\]
is impossible for any threshold $h_a(x) = \mathbf{1}\{x \geq a\}$: if $x_1$ is labeled $1$, then the larger point $x_2$ must also be labeled $1$. Hence no pair is shattered. Thus,
\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{thr}}) = 1.
\]

\end{example}

\begin{property}[Linear classifiers in $\mathbb{R}^N$]
\label{prop:vc-linear}
The halfspace class
\[
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lin}}
=
\bigl\{
x\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{\langle w,x\rangle+b\ge 0\}
:
w\in\mathbb{R}^N,\, b\in\mathbb{R}
\bigr\}
\]
has
\[
\VCdim(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lin}})=N+1.
\]
Any $N+1$ affinely independent points can be shattered; any $N+2$ points are affinely dependent, so some labeling is unrealizable. If the classifier is forced through the origin ($b=0$), the VC dimension drops to $N$.
\end{property}

\paragraph{Why it matters}

A large VC dimension means that a class can fit many different label patterns, including potentially random noise. This creates a larger risk of overfitting and requires more data for uniform generalization guarantees. A finite VC dimension is the key complexity condition behind the fundamental PAC-learning theory.



\paragraph{Growth function}

The \textbf{growth function} of $\mathcal{H}$ is

\[
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) := \max_{x_1, \dots, x_n \in \mathcal{X}} |\{(h(x_1), \dots, h(x_n)) : h \in \mathcal{H}\}|.
\]

It asks: among all choices of $n$ inputs, what is the maximum number of distinct labelings that $\mathcal{H}$ can produce?

Two facts:

\[
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) \leq 2^n,
\]

and

\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) \geq n \iff \Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) = 2^n.
\]

\begin{property}[Finite hypothesis class]
\label{prop:vc-finite}
If $\lvert\mathcal{H}\rvert=N<\infty$, then $\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n)\le N$ for every $n$. Shattering $n$ points requires $2^n\le N$, hence
\[
\VCdim(\mathcal{H})\le\lfloor\log_2 N\rfloor.
\]
The bound is the largest possible for a class of size $N$; it is attained when the $N$ classifiers realize $N$ distinct dichotomies.
\end{property}

\begin{definition}[Set-theoretic union of classifiers]
Let \(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}'\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{X}}\) be two binary hypothesis classes. Their set-theoretic union is the class
\[
\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}'
:=
\bigl\{g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}:g\in\mathcal{H}\ \text{or}\ g\in\mathcal{H}'\bigr\}.
\]
This is a union of functions, not a pointwise logical OR of labels: each single classifier \(g\) must belong entirely to \(\mathcal{H}\) or entirely to \(\mathcal{H}'\).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Growth function of a union]
\label{lem:growth-union}
For every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),
\[
\Pi_{\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}'}(n) \leq \Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) + \Pi_{\mathcal{H}'}(n).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix any \(x_1,\dots,x_n\in\mathcal{X}\). Every dichotomy realized by \(\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}'\) on these points is realized by some \(h\in\mathcal{H}\) or some \(h'\in\mathcal{H}'\), so
\[
\bigl\{(g(x_1),\dots,g(x_n)):g\in\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}'\bigr\}
=
\bigl\{(h(x_1),\dots,h(x_n)):h\in\mathcal{H}\bigr\}
\cup
\bigl\{(h'(x_1),\dots,h'(x_n)):h'\in\mathcal{H}'\bigr\}.
\]
Cardinality of a union therefore gives
\[
\bigl|(\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}')_{|(x_1,\dots,x_n)}\bigr|
\le
\bigl|\mathcal{H}_{|(x_1,\dots,x_n)}\bigr|
+
\bigl|\mathcal{H}'_{|(x_1,\dots,x_n)}\bigr|
\le
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n)+\Pi_{\mathcal{H}'}(n).
\]
The right-hand side is independent of the point tuple, so taking the maximum over all \(x_1,\dots,x_n\) yields the claim. The inequality may be strict: a labeling realized by both classes is counted twice on the right.
\end{proof}

Lemma~\ref{lem:growth-union} is the growth-function form of the restriction inequality \(|(\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}')_{|C}|\le|\mathcal{H}_{|C}|+|\mathcal{H}'_{|C}|\).

\begin{property}[Union of $N$ classes]
\label{prop:vc-union-n}
Once the two-class inequality
\[
\VCdim(\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}')
\le
\VCdim(\mathcal{H})+\VCdim(\mathcal{H}')+1
\]
is known (part~(a) below), iterating it $N-1$ times yields
\[
\VCdim\Bigl(\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{H}_i\Bigr)
\le
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\VCdim(\mathcal{H}_i)+N-1.
\]
The case $N=2$ is exactly part~(a). For axis-aligned rectangles and triangles this is $4+7+1=12$.
\end{property}

\paragraph{Sauer--Shelah lemma}
\label{lem:sauer-shelah}

Let
\[
d := \text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}) < \infty.
\]
Then for every $n > d$,
\[
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) \leq \sum_{k=0}^{d} \binom{n}{k}.
\]
Equivalently, for every $C \subseteq \mathcal{X}$ of size $n$,
\[
|\mathcal{H}_{|C}| \leq \sum_{k=0}^{d} \binom{n}{k}.
\]
\begin{proof}[Proof of the Sauer--Shelah lemma]
\label{prf:sauer-shelah}
Write \(\Phi_d(n):=\sum_{k=0}^{d}\binom{n}{k}\), with the conventions \(\Phi_{-1}(n):=0\) and \(\Phi_d(n)=2^n\) whenever \(n\le d\). It is enough to show the stronger claim: if \(\mathcal{H}\) shatters no subset of a finite set \(C\) of size \(d+1\), then \(|\mathcal{H}_{|C}|\le\Phi_d(|C|)\). Proceed by induction on \(n+d\), where \(n:=|C|\).

If \(d=0\), then \(\mathcal{H}\) cannot shatter a singleton, so all hypotheses agree on every point of \(C\) and \(|\mathcal{H}_{|C}|\le 1=\Phi_0(n)\). If \(n=0\) or \(n\le d\), the claim is immediate.

Now fix \(C=\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\) with \(n>d\ge 1\), and set \(C':=C\setminus\{x_n\}\). Let \(\mathcal{Y}:=\mathcal{H}_{|C}\subseteq\{0,1\}^n\). Write \(\mathcal{Y}_0\) for the projections of these vectors onto \(C'\), and write \(\mathcal{Y}_1\) for those projections that occur with \emph{both} labels on \(x_n\). Then
\[
|\mathcal{Y}|=|\mathcal{Y}_0|+|\mathcal{Y}_1|.
\]
The family \(\mathcal{Y}_0\) is a restriction of \(\mathcal{H}\) to \(C'\), so it shatters no \((d+1)\)-set and the inductive hypothesis gives \(|\mathcal{Y}_0|\le\Phi_d(n-1)\). If \(\mathcal{Y}_1\) shattered a \(d\)-set \(A\subseteq C'\), then \(\mathcal{Y}\) would shatter \(A\cup\{x_n\}\), a contradiction. Thus \(\mathcal{Y}_1\) shatters no \(d\)-set, and \(|\mathcal{Y}_1|\le\Phi_{d-1}(n-1)\). Pascal's identity \(\binom{n}{k}=\binom{n-1}{k}+\binom{n-1}{k-1}\) yields
\[
\Phi_d(n)=\Phi_d(n-1)+\Phi_{d-1}(n-1),
\]
so \(|\mathcal{H}_{|C}|\le\Phi_d(n)\). Taking the maximum over all \(n\)-point sets gives the growth-function bound. For \(n\ge d\ge 1\) one also has the coarser estimate \(\Phi_d(n)\le(en/d)^d\).
\end{proof}
\begin{note}
    Once $n > d$, the class cannot realize all $2^n$ labelings; its possible label patterns grow only polynomially in $n$ for fixed $d$, rather than exponentially. Sauer's lemma and the growth function are strong proof tools for the VC dimension. There was no earlier proof of this lemma in the present notes; the argument above is the standard last-point splitting induction (cf.\ Mohri--Rostamizadeh--Talwalkar, Thm.~3.17; Shalev-Shwartz--Ben-David, Lemma~6.10).
\end{note}

\paragraph{Notation}

Let \(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{rect}}\) be the hypothesis class of all axis-aligned rectangles in \(\mathbb{R}^2\), and let \(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{tri}}\) be the class of all triangles in \(\mathbb{R}^2\). Each \(h\) labels points inside the shaded region by \(1\) and outside by \(0\).


\begin{center}

    \draw[->] (0,-0.3) -- (0,3.4) node[above] {$x_2$};

\end{center}

\paragraph{Axis-aligned rectangles}

An \textbf{axis-aligned rectangle} is a rectangle whose sides are parallel to the coordinate axes. Formally, it has the form
\[
R_{a_1, a_2, b_1, b_2} = [a_1, a_2] \times [b_1, b_2],
\]
where
\[
a_1 \leq a_2, \quad b_1 \leq b_2.
\]

The associated classifier is
\[
h_{a_1, a_2, b_1, b_2}(\boldsymbol{x}) = \mathbf{1} \left\{ a_1 \leq x^{(1)} \leq a_2 \text{ and } b_1 \leq x^{(2)} \leq b_2 \right\}.
\]

Thus the complete rectangle class is
\[
\mathcal{H}_{\text{rect}} = \{ h_{a_1, a_2, b_1, b_2} : a_1 \leq a_2, \, b_1 \leq b_2 \}.
\]

\begin{proposition}[VC dimension of axis-aligned rectangles]
\label{prop:vc-rect}
\[
\VCdim(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{rect}})=4.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Both directions are required: a shattered $4$-point set, and the claim that no $5$-point set is shattered.

\paragraph{Lower bound: four points are shattered.}
Take the diamond
\[
C_4
=
\bigl\{
p_W=(-1,0),\;
p_E=(1,0),\;
p_S=(0,-1),\;
p_N=(0,1)
\bigr\}.
\]
Each point is the unique extremum in one axis direction: $p_W$ uniquely minimizes $x^{(1)}$, $p_E$ uniquely maximizes $x^{(1)}$, $p_S$ uniquely minimizes $x^{(2)}$, and $p_N$ uniquely maximizes $x^{(2)}$. For an arbitrary subset $S\subseteq C_4$, realize the labeling $\mathbf{1}_S$ as follows. If $S=\emptyset$, take any rectangle disjoint from $C_4$, e.g.\ $[2,3]\times[2,3]$. If $S\neq\emptyset$, take the tight axis-aligned bounding box of $S$,
\[
R_S
=
\bigl[\min_{p\in S}p^{(1)},\max_{p\in S}p^{(1)}\bigr]
\times
\bigl[\min_{p\in S}p^{(2)},\max_{p\in S}p^{(2)}\bigr].
\]
Every $p\in S$ lies in $R_S$ by construction. Conversely, if $p_W\notin S$ then every remaining point has first coordinate at least $0$, so $\min_{q\in S}q^{(1)}\ge 0>-1$ and $p_W\notin R_S$. The same one-sided comparison excludes $p_E$, $p_S$, and $p_N$ whenever they are omitted from $S$. Thus $R_S\cap C_4=S$, and $\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{rect}}$ shatters $C_4$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85, >=stealth]
  \draw[->] (-1.7,0) -- (1.9,0) node[right] {$x^{(1)}$};
  \draw[->] (0,-1.7) -- (0,1.9) node[above] {$x^{(2)}$};
  \filldraw[blue!70!black] (-1,0) circle (1.6pt) node[left] {$p_W$};
  \filldraw[blue!70!black] (1,0) circle (1.6pt) node[right] {$p_E$};
  \filldraw[blue!70!black] (0,-1) circle (1.6pt) node[below] {$p_S$};
  \filldraw[blue!70!black] (0,1) circle (1.6pt) node[above] {$p_N$};
  \draw[blue!55!black, dashed] (-1,-1) rectangle (1,1);
  \node[font=\small] at (0,-2.15) {A shattered $4$-point set for $\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{rect}}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Upper bound: no five points are shattered.}
Let $C\subset\mathbb{R}^2$ be any set of five points. Choose
\[
x_L\in\argmin_{x\in C}x^{(1)},
\quad
x_R\in\argmax_{x\in C}x^{(1)},
\quad
x_B\in\argmin_{x\in C}x^{(2)},
\quad
x_T\in\argmax_{x\in C}x^{(2)},
\]
and write $E:=\{x_L,x_R,x_B,x_T\}$. Then $|E|\le 4$, so there exists $x_*\in C\setminus E$. Any axis-aligned rectangle containing $E$ must satisfy
\[
a_1\le x_L^{(1)}=\min_{x\in C}x^{(1)},
\quad
a_2\ge x_R^{(1)}=\max_{x\in C}x^{(1)},
\quad
b_1\le x_B^{(2)}=\min_{x\in C}x^{(2)},
\quad
b_2\ge x_T^{(2)}=\max_{x\in C}x^{(2)},
\]
hence contains the bounding box of $C$ and therefore contains $x_*$ as well (ties are allowed: $x_*$ still lies between the chosen extrema). The dichotomy that labels $E$ by $1$ and $x_*$ by $0$ is therefore unrealizable. Thus no $5$-point set is shattered, and $\VCdim(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{rect}})=4$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.92, >=Stealth]
  % ---- left: five points and the bounding box of E ----
  \begin{scope}[shift={(0,0)}]
    \draw[->] (-0.25,0) -- (4.15,0) node[right] {$x^{(1)}$};
    \draw[->] (0,-0.25) -- (0,3.35) node[above] {$x^{(2)}$};
    \fill[orange!12] (0.55,0.45) rectangle (3.55,2.85);
    \draw[orange!70!black, thick, dashed] (0.55,0.45) rectangle (3.55,2.85);
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (0.55,1.55) circle (1.8pt) node[left] {$x_L$};
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (3.55,1.15) circle (1.8pt) node[right] {$x_R$};
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (1.85,0.45) circle (1.8pt) node[below] {$x_B$};
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (1.35,2.85) circle (1.8pt) node[above] {$x_T$};
    \filldraw[red!75!black] (2.15,1.55) circle (2.0pt) node[above right] {$x_*$};
    \node[font=\small, align=center] at (2.05,-0.95) {Four extrema $E$ determine the box;\\$x_*\notin E$ is trapped inside};
  \end{scope}

  % ---- right: any rectangle covering E also covers x_* ----
  \begin{scope}[shift={(6.35,0)}]
    \draw[->] (-0.25,0) -- (4.15,0) node[right] {$x^{(1)}$};
    \draw[->] (0,-0.25) -- (0,3.35) node[above] {$x^{(2)}$};
    \fill[blue!10] (0.25,0.2) rectangle (3.85,3.1);
    \draw[blue!70!black, thick] (0.25,0.2) rectangle (3.85,3.1);
    \fill[orange!12] (0.55,0.45) rectangle (3.55,2.85);
    \draw[orange!70!black, thick, dashed] (0.55,0.45) rectangle (3.55,2.85);
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (0.55,1.55) circle (1.8pt) node[left] {$1$};
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (3.55,1.15) circle (1.8pt) node[right] {$1$};
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (1.85,0.45) circle (1.8pt) node[below] {$1$};
    \filldraw[blue!70!black] (1.35,2.85) circle (1.8pt) node[above] {$1$};
    \filldraw[red!75!black] (2.15,1.55) circle (2.0pt) node[above right] {$0$?};
    \node[font=\small, align=center] at (2.05,-0.95) {Any rectangle containing $E$\\also contains $x_*$: $1$ on $E$ and $0$ on $x_*$ is impossible};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Axis-aligned boxes in $\mathbb{R}^d$]
The same extremal argument gives $\VCdim=2d$ for axis-aligned boxes in $\mathbb{R}^d$: shatter $2d$ points by placing one unique minimizer and one unique maximizer on each coordinate axis, and on any $2d+1$ points at least one point fails to be a chosen coordinate extremum, so it is trapped in the bounding box of the others. The planar case is $2\cdot 2=4$.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Triangles}

A triangle in $\mathbb{R}^2$ is the convex hull of three points $v_1, v_2, v_3 \in \mathbb{R}^2$:

\[
T = \text{conv}\{v_1, v_2, v_3\}.
\]

Equivalently, a nondegenerate triangle is the bounded intersection of three half-planes.

Its classifier is

\[
h_T(x) = \mathbf{1}\{x \in T\}.
\]

The triangle hypothesis class is

\[
\mathcal{H}_{\text{tri}} = \{h_T : T \text{ is a triangle in } \mathbb{R}^2\}.
\]

The question explicitly permits you to use

\[
\text{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{tri}}) = 7.
\]

No construction of seven shattered points, nor proof that eight cannot be shattered, is required.

\begin{proofsketch}[Why triangles have VC dimension $7$]
A triangle is the bounded intersection of three half-planes, so it has three edges and can cut the plane in at most three linear chops. This is the $k=3$ case of the general fact that convex $k$-gons in $\mathbb{R}^2$ have VC dimension $2k+1$ (Kearns--Vazirani, Ch.~3). The exam does not need the details below.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{figures/vc-tri/vc-tri-3-chops.jpg}\\[0.25em]
{\small Three edges give at most three chops along the circle.}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Lower bound: seven points on a circle are shattered.}
Place $C_7$ at the vertices of a regular heptagon. For an arbitrary labeling, write $P$ for the positive points and $N$ for the negative points. Then either $|P|\le 3$ or $|N|\le 3$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $|P|\le 3$, take $T=\mathrm{conv}(P)$ (a point, segment, or triangle). Convex position implies $T$ contains no point of $N$.
    \item If $|N|\le 3$, the negatives form at most three contiguous blocks along the circle (three positives create at most three gaps). Use one edge of $T$ to cut off each block.
\end{itemize}
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/vc-tri/vc-tri-7-few-positives.jpg}\\[0.2em]
{\small $|P|\le 3$: take $\mathrm{conv}(P)$.}
\end{minipage}\hfill
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/vc-tri/vc-tri-7-three-blocks.jpg}\\[0.2em]
{\small $|N|\le 3$: one edge per negative block.}
\end{minipage}
\end{center}
Every $2^7$ labeling is therefore realized, so $\VCdim(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{tri}})\ge 7$.

\paragraph{Upper bound: no eight points are shattered.}
Let $C$ have eight points.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If some $x_*\in C$ lies in $\mathrm{conv}(C\setminus\{x_*\})$, the labeling that marks the hull by $1$ and $x_*$ by $0$ is impossible: triangles are convex.
    \item If $C$ is in convex position, the alternating labeling $+-+-\,+-+-$ makes four isolated negative singletons. A triangle can cut off at most three contiguous hull-arcs, so this dichotomy is unrealizable. Equivalently: a triangle meets a circle in at most six points, but the alternating pattern would require eight crossings.
\end{itemize}
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/vc-tri/vc-tri-interior-point.jpg}\\[0.2em]
{\small Interior point: convexity forbids hull $1$, interior $0$.}
\end{minipage}\hfill
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/vc-tri/vc-tri-8-alternating.jpg}\\[0.2em]
{\small Alternating $8$-set: four isolated minuses, only three edges.}
\end{minipage}
\end{center}
Hence $\VCdim(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{tri}})=7$.
\end{proofsketch}

\subsubsection{Solution to part (a)}

Write \(d := \VCdim(\mathcal{H})\) and \(d' := \VCdim(\mathcal{H}')\). It is enough to show that no set of size \(n := d + d' + 2\) is shattered by \(\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}'\).

Fix any \(C \subseteq \mathcal{X}\) with \(|C| = n\). The restriction of the union satisfies
\[
\bigl|(\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}')_{|C}\bigr|
\leq \bigl|\mathcal{H}_{|C}\bigr| + \bigl|\mathcal{H}'_{|C}\bigr|
\leq \Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) + \Pi_{\mathcal{H}'}(n).
\]
By the Sauer--Shelah lemma,
\[
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n) \leq \Phi_d(n) := \sum_{k=0}^{d}\binom{n}{k},
\qquad
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}'}(n) \leq \Phi_{d'}(n) := \sum_{k=0}^{d'}\binom{n}{k}.
\]
Since \(n = d + d' + 2\), binomial symmetry gives \(\sum_{k=0}^{d'}\binom{n}{k} = \sum_{j=d+2}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\), and therefore
\[
\Phi_d(n) + \Phi_{d'}(n)
= \sum_{k=0}^{d}\binom{n}{k} + \sum_{j=d+2}^{n}\binom{n}{j}
= 2^{n} - \binom{n}{d+1}
< 2^{n}.
\]
Hence \(\bigl|(\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}')_{|C}\bigr| < 2^{n}\), so \(C\) is not shattered. Consequently
\[
\VCdim(\mathcal{H} \cup \mathcal{H}') \le d + d' + 1.
\]

\subsubsection{Solution to part (b)}

\textbf{No conclusion of equality follows from the given information.} The theorem from part (a) only says
\[
\text{VCdim} (\mathcal{H}_{\text{rect}} \cup \mathcal{H}_{\text{tri}}) \leq 4 + 7 + 1 = 12.
\]
There is also a trivial lower bound:
\[
\text{VCdim} (\mathcal{H}_{\text{rect}} \cup \mathcal{H}_{\text{tri}}) \geq \max\{4, 7\} = 7,
\]
because \(\mathcal{H}_{\text{tri}}\) is contained in the union. Hence the facts supplied imply only
\[
7 \leq \text{VCdim} (\mathcal{H}_{\text{rect}} \cup \mathcal{H}_{\text{tri}}) \leq 12.
\]
(The problem's wording ``determine'' is therefore answered by this sandwich bound, not by claiming equality with \(12\).)




\subsection{Problem 4. [10 pts.]}
Prove the following two statements:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} Let $\mathcal{D}$ be a distribution. Let $S = (z_1, \dots, z_m)$ be an i.i.d. sequence of examples. Let $A$ be a learning algorithm that is on-average replace-one stable with rate $\epsilon(m)$. Then:
    \[
    \E_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \left[ L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - L_S(A(S)) \right] \le \epsilon(m).
    \]
    \item \textbf{[5 pts.]} Let $\ell$ be a convex $\rho$-Lipschitz loss function. The regularised Empirical Risk Minimisation (ERM) satisfies:
    \[
    \E_S \bigl[ L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \bigr] \le L_{\mathcal{D}}(w^*) + \lambda \|w^*\|^2 + \frac{2\rho^2}{\lambda m},
    \]
    where
    \[
    w^* = \argmin_{w \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(w).
    \]
\end{enumerate}

\begin{note}
Below we use the regularizer \(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|^2\) (so \(F_S\) is \(\lambda\)-strongly convex). Under this convention the part~(b) bound reads
\(L_{\mathcal{D}}(w^*)+\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w^*\|^2+\tfrac{2\rho^2}{\lambda m}\).
\end{note}
