\paragraph{Basic learninig setup}

Let $\mathcal{Z} := \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{Y}$ be the example space, with $z = (x, y) \in \mathcal{Z}$ an example ($x$: input, $y$: label/response). A sample is $S = (z_1, \dots, z_m) \sim \mathcal{D}^m$ with $z_i \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathcal{D}$. A \textbf{learning algorithm} $A : \mathcal{Z}^m \to \mathcal{H}$ outputs a hypothesis $A(S) \in \mathcal{H}$. Now $A(S)$ is \textbf{data-dependent}, so basic i.i.d.\ arguments from Question 1 do not directly apply to training loss on $A(S)$.

For the learned model $A(S)$, the \textbf{generalization gap} is

\[
L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - L_{S}(A(S)).
\]

Part (a) controls its \textbf{expectation}:
\[
\mathbb{E}_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m} [L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - L_{S}(A(S))].
\]
This is not a high-probability result such as ``with probability at least $1 - \delta$.'' It is an expectation over all possible i.i.d. training datasets.


\paragraph{Replace-one sample}

The central question is: If one training observation changes, how much can the algorithm’s output—and hence its loss—change?

For each position $i$, let
\[
z'_i \sim \mathcal{D}
\]
be an independent fresh example, independent of $S$. Define the \textbf{replace-one sample}
\[
\boxed{S^{(i)} := (z_1, \dots, z_{i-1}, z'_i, z_{i+1}, \dots, z_m).}
\]
This is the sample obtained by deleting $z_i$ and replacing it by an independent draw $z'_i$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|l}
  \hline
  \textbf{Object} & \textbf{Contains at coordinate $i$} \\ \hline
  $S$ & $z_i$ \\ \hline
  $S^{(i)}$ & $z'_i$ \\ \hline
  $A(S)$ & Model trained with $z_i$ present \\ \hline
  $A(S^{(i)})$ & Model trained with $z_i$ replaced \\ \hline
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{On-average replace-one stability}

There are several nearby definitions in the literature, so check your lecture notes for its exact convention. A form directly suited to the displayed statement is:

\[
\frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} \mathbb{E}_{S, z_i'} \left[ \ell(A(S), z_i') - \ell(A(S^{(i)}), z_i') \right] \leq \epsilon(m). \tag{AROS}\label{eq:aros}
\]

An algorithm satisfying this condition is called on-average replace-one stable with rate $\epsilon(m)$.


\paragraph{Parameterized models}

A model is now denoted by $w$, and its risks are
\[
L_{\mathcal{D}}(w) = \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{D}}[\ell(w, z)],
\]
\[
L_{S}(w) = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} \ell(w, z_i).
\]
The population-risk minimizer is
\[
\boxed{w^* \in \arg \min_{w \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(w).}
\]
It is an \textbf{oracle comparator}: it minimizes the unknown population risk over $\mathcal{H}$. It generally cannot be computed, because $\mathcal{D}$ is unknown.

Do not confuse $w^*$ with $A(S)$:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Depends on observed sample $S$?} & \textbf{Computable in practice?} \\ \hline
$A(S)$ & Yes & Yes, it is the trained model \\ \hline
$w^*$ & No; it depends on $\mathcal{D}$ & Usually no \\ \hline
$L_S(w)$ & Yes & Yes \\ \hline
$L_{\mathcal{D}}(w)$ & No once $\mathcal{D}, w$ are fixed & Usually no \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}


\paragraph{Regularized ERM}

Ordinary empirical risk minimization (ERM) chooses a model with small training loss:

\[
\widehat{w}_{\text{ERM}} \in \arg \min_{w \in \mathcal{H}} L_S(w).
\]

A \textbf{regularized ERM} instead minimizes

\[
\boxed{F_S(w) = L_S(w) + \tfrac{\lambda}{2} \|w\|^2, \quad \lambda > 0,}
\]

and returns

\[
\boxed{A(S) \in \arg \min_{w \in \mathcal{H}} \bigl[L_S(w) + \tfrac{\lambda}{2} \|w\|^2\bigr].} \tag{RERM}
\]

Here $\|\cdot\|$ is normally the Euclidean norm:

\[
\|w\| = \|w\|_2 = \left( \sum_{j=1}^d w_j^2 \right)^{1/2}.
\]

The term $\tfrac{\lambda}{2} \|w\|^2$
is the $L_2$ \textbf{regularization penalty}, also called a \textbf{quadratic penalty}, \textbf{ridge regularization}, or \textbf{Tikhonov regularization}.
(We use the $\tfrac{\lambda}{2}$ convention so that $F_S$ is $\lambda$-strongly convex and the stability rate $2\rho^{2}/(\lambda m)$ matches the proof below.)

Its role is not merely to ``make $w$ small.'' Mathematically, it makes the objective strongly convex and therefore makes the optimizer less sensitive to replacement of a single data point.

\paragraph{Convexity}

The loss is convex in $w$ if, for every $w, v \in \mathcal{H}$ and every $t \in [0, 1]$,

\[ \ell(tw + (1 - t)v, z) \leq t\ell(w, z) + (1 - t)\ell(v, z). \]

This condition says that averaging two parameter vectors cannot produce a loss larger than the corresponding average of their losses.

Because an average of convex functions is convex,

\[ L_S(w) = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} \ell(w, z_i) \]

is convex whenever each $\ell(\cdot, z_i)$ is convex.

Examples:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Loss} & \textbf{Formula} & \textbf{\begin{tabular}[c]{@{}l@{}}Convex in \\ prediction/parameters?\end{tabular}} \\ \hline
Squared loss & $\ell(\hat{y}, y) = (\hat{y} - y)^2$ & Yes \\ \hline
Logistic loss & $\ell(w, (x, y)) = \log(1 + \exp(-y\langle w, x \rangle)), y \in \{-1, 1\}$ & Yes \\ \hline
Hinge loss & $\ell(w, (x, y)) = \max\{0, 1 - y\langle w, x \rangle\}$ & Yes \\ \hline
0-1 loss & $\mathbf{1}\{\text{sign}(\langle w, x \rangle) \neq y\}$ & No \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The 0-1 loss used in Questions 1-2 is not convex, which is why part (b) uses a different loss framework.

\paragraph{$\rho$-Lipschitz loss}

The loss is $\rho$-Lipschitz with respect to $w$ if

\begin{equation*}
\boxed{|\ell(w, z) - \ell(v, z)| \leq \rho \|w - v\| \quad \forall w, v \in \mathcal{H}, \forall z \in \mathcal{Z}.}
\end{equation*}

The constant $\rho$ controls the sensitivity of loss to a change in parameters.

If the loss is differentiable, a sufficient condition is

\begin{equation*}
\|\nabla_w \ell(w, z)\| \leq \rho \quad \text{for all } w, z.
\end{equation*}

This is the bridge from \textbf{parameter stability}

\begin{equation*}
\|A(S) - A(S^{(i)})\| \text{ is small}
\end{equation*}

to \textbf{loss stability}

\begin{equation*}
|\ell(A(S), z) - \ell(A(S^{(i)}), z)| \text{ is small.}
\end{equation*}

\paragraph{Strong convexity from the penalty}

A function $F$ is $\mu$-strongly convex if
\[
F(v) \geq F(w) + \langle g, v - w \rangle + \frac{\mu}{2}\|v - w\|^2,
\]
for every $w, v$, where $g \in \partial F(w)$ is a subgradient.

The quadratic function
\[
w \mapsto \tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|^2
\]
is $\lambda$-strongly convex. Therefore,
\[
F_S(w) = L_S(w) + \tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|^2
\]
is $\lambda$-strongly convex even if $L_S$ is only convex.

This has two consequences:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The minimizer $A(S)$ is typically unique.
    \item Changing one example from $S$ to $S^{(i)}$ cannot move the minimizer too far.
\end{itemize}

The proof of part (b) relies on the chain
\[
\text{quadratic regularization} \implies \text{strong convexity} \implies \text{parameter stability} \implies \text{loss stability}
\]


\paragraph{Solution to part (a)}

First, since $z_i' \sim \mathcal{D}$ is independent of $S$, for every $i$, by the definition of $L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S))$, we have
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}_S[L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S))] = \mathbb{E}_{S, z_i'}[\ell(A(S), z_i')].
\label{eq:expected-pop}
\end{equation}

Go to \cref{tab:L-notation} for the notation.

Averaging the same identity over $i \in \{1, \dots, m\}$ yields
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}_S[L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S))] = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \mathbb{E}_{S, z_i'}[\ell(A(S), z_i')].
\label{eq:expected-pop-avg}
\end{equation}

Next,
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}_S[L_S(A(S))] = \mathbb{E}_S\left[\frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \ell(A(S), z_i)\right] = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \mathbb{E}_S[\ell(A(S), z_i)].
\label{eq:expected-empirical}
\end{equation}

We claim that, for every $i$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:switch}
\mathbb{E}_S[\ell(A(S), z_i)] = \mathbb{E}_{S, z_i'}[\ell(A(S^{(i)}), z_i')].
\end{equation}
Indeed, write
\[
S_{-i} := (z_1, \dots, z_{i-1}, z_{i+1}, \dots, z_m).
\]
Then $S = (S_{-i}, z_i)$ and $S^{(i)} = (S_{-i}, z_i')$. Since $z_i, z_i'$ are independent draws from $\mathcal{D}$, the pairs
\[
(S_{-i}, z_i) \quad \text{and} \quad (S_{-i}, z_i')
\]
have the same distribution. Therefore,
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_S[\ell(A(S), z_i)] &= \mathbb{E}_{S_{-i}, z_i} [\ell(A(S_{-i}, z_i), z_i)] \\
&= \mathbb{E}_{S_{-i}, z_i'} [\ell(A(S_{-i}, z_i'), z_i')]
  && \text{(replace $z_i$ by $z_i'$)} \\
&= \mathbb{E}_{S, z_i'}[\ell(A(S^{(i)}), z_i')],
\end{align*}
which proves \eqref{eq:switch}. Thus we have
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_{S} [L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - L_{S}(A(S))]
&= \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^{m} \mathbb{E}_{S, z_i'} \bigl[\ell(A(S), z_i') - \ell(A(S^{(i)}), z_i')\bigr]
\leq \epsilon(m),
\end{align*}
by the on-average replace-one stability definition \eqref{eq:aros}.


\paragraph{Solution to part (b)}

By the definition of $A(S)$, we have
\[
A(S) = \arg \min_{w \in \mathcal{H}} \left[L_S(w)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|^2\right]=\arg\min_{w \in \mathcal{H}} F_S(w).
\]
In particular, $F_S(A(S)) \le F_S(w^*)$, so
\[
L_{S}(A(S)) + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|A(S)\|^{2} \leq L_{S}(w^*) + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|w^*\|^{2}.
\]
Using the expected-gap decomposition and part~(a),
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_{S}[L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S))]
&= \mathbb{E}_{S}[L_{S}(A(S))] + \mathbb{E}_{S} [L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - L_{S}(A(S))] \\
&\leq \mathbb{E}_{S}\Bigl[L_{S}(w^*) + \tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w^*\|^{2} - \tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|A(S)\|^{2}\Bigr] + \epsilon(m) \\
&\leq \mathbb{E}_{S}[L_{S}(w^*)] + \tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w^*\|^{2} + \epsilon(m)
= L_{\mathcal{D}}(w^*) + \tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w^*\|^{2} + \epsilon(m).
\end{align*}
\begin{remark}[Why the last equality]
The comparator \(w^*=\argmin_{w\in\mathcal{H}}L_{\mathcal{D}}(w)\) is a deterministic function of \(\mathcal{D}\); it does not depend on the random sample \(S\). For any fixed \(w\), Question~1 gives the unbiasedness identity
\[
\mathbb{E}_S[L_S(w)]=L_{\mathcal{D}}(w).
\]
Applying it at \(w=w^*\) yields \(\mathbb{E}_S[L_S(w^*)]=L_{\mathcal{D}}(w^*)\). The remaining two summands are already non-random: \(\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|w^*\|^2\) is a constant, and \(\epsilon(m)\) is a deterministic stability rate. The last step is therefore this identification, not a new estimate.
\end{remark}
It suffices to show that
\[
  \epsilon(m) \leq \frac{2\rho^2}{\lambda m}.
\]
Fix $i$ and let $w = A(S)$, $w' = A(S^{(i)})$. Define
\[
F_S(u) = L_S(u) + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|u\|^2, \quad F_{S^{(i)}}(u) = L_{S^{(i)}}(u) + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|u\|^2.
\]
Both $F_S$ and $F_{S^{(i)}}$ are $\lambda$-strongly convex\footnote{A function $F$ is $\lambda$-strongly convex if, for every $u,v$ and every subgradient $g\in\partial F(u)$,
\[
F(v)\ge F(u)+\langle g,v-u\rangle+\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|v-u\|^2.
\]
If $F$ is differentiable this is the same inequality with $g=\nabla F(u)$; if $F$ is twice differentiable it is equivalent to $\nabla^2 F\succeq\lambda I$. In particular, at a minimizer one has $0\in\partial F(u)$, so the inner-product term drops and $F(v)\ge F(u)+\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|v-u\|^2$. The official statement assumes only that $\ell$ is convex and $\rho$-Lipschitz; it never names strong convexity. Convexity of $\ell$ makes $L_S$ and $L_{S^{(i)}}$ convex. The quadratic $u\mapsto\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\|u\|^2$ is $\lambda$-strongly convex, and adding a convex function does not decrease the parameter. Hence both $F_S$ and $F_{S^{(i)}}$ are $\lambda$-strongly convex. (If the penalty is written $\lambda\|u\|^2$ as on the official sheet, the parameter becomes $2\lambda$.)}, so
\[
F_S(w') \geq F_S(w) + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|w' - w\|^2, \qquad F_{S^{(i)}}(w) \geq F_{S^{(i)}}(w') + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|w - w'\|^2.
\]
Adding these two inequalities yields
\[
\lambda \|w - w'\|^2 \leq F_S(w') - F_{S^{(i)}}(w') + F_{S^{(i)}}(w) - F_S(w).
\]
Note that $S$ and $S^{(i)}$ differ only in the $i$-th sample. Therefore, for any $u$,
\[
F_S(u) - F_{S^{(i)}}(u) = \frac{1}{m}\bigl[\ell(u, z_i) - \ell(u, z_i')\bigr].
\]
Substituting above,
\[
\lambda \|w - w'\|^2 \leq \frac{1}{m} \bigl[\ell(w, z_i') - \ell(w', z_i') + \ell(w', z_i) - \ell(w, z_i)\bigr].
\]
By the $\rho$-Lipschitz property of $\ell$, we have
\[
|\ell(w, z) - \ell(w', z)| \leq \rho \|w - w'\|,
\]
thus,
\[
\ell(w, z_i') - \ell(w', z_i') \leq \rho \|w - w'\|,
\]
and
\[
\ell(w', z_i) - \ell(w, z_i) \leq \rho \|w - w'\|.
\]
Summing, we have
\[
\lambda \|w - w'\|^2 \leq \frac{2\rho}{m} \|w - w'\|.
\]
If $w = w'$, the inequality is trivial. Otherwise, dividing both sides by $\lambda \|w - w'\|$ gives
\[
\|A(S) - A(S^{(i)})\| \leq \frac{2\rho}{\lambda m}.
\]
For every test point $z \in \mathcal{Z}$,
\begin{align*}
|\ell(A(S), z) - \ell(A(S^{(i)}), z)|
  &\leq \rho \|A(S) - A(S^{(i)})\|
  && \text{($\rho$-Lipschitz)} \\
  &\leq \rho \cdot \frac{2\rho}{\lambda m}
  && \text{(parameter stability)} \\
  &= \frac{2\rho^2}{\lambda m}.
\end{align*}

Thus,
\begin{equation}
|\ell(A(S), z) - \ell(A(S^{(i)}), z)| \leq \frac{2\rho^2}{\lambda m}. \tag{13}\label{eq:loss-stability}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Further learnings}

\subsubsection{Framework}\label{sec:spr2025-a-framework}

Let $\mathcal{X}$ denote the input space, $\mathcal{Y}$ the output/label space, and $\mathcal{Z} := \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{Y}$ the example space. A sample is $Z = (X, Y) \sim \mathcal{D}$, where $\mathcal{D}$ is an unknown distribution over $\mathcal{Z}$. A hypothesis is a prediction function $h : \mathcal{X} \to \widehat{\mathcal{Y}}$, and the hypothesis class is $\mathcal{H} \subseteq \{h : \mathcal{X} \to \widehat{\mathcal{Y}}\}$. The training sample is $S = (Z_1, \dots, Z_m) \sim \mathcal{D}^m$. A learning algorithm is a mapping $A : \mathcal{Z}^m \to \mathcal{H}$, i.e., $S \mapsto A(S)$. This is the official specialisation of \jump{def:spr2025-learn}{the exam-wide definition of learn}.

\begin{center}
\fbox{The data $S$ are random, so the learned hypothesis $A(S)$ is also random.}
\end{center}

This distinction is central to Problem 4.

\paragraph{Loss and risk.}
A loss function is a mapping
\[
\ell : \bar{\mathcal{Y}} \times \mathcal{Y} \to [0, \infty).
\]
Given a hypothesis $h$ and example $z = (x, y)$, define the loss incurred by $h$ on $z$ as
\[
\ell(h, z) := \ell(h(x), y).
\]
The \textbf{population risk} is
\[
\boxed{L_{\mathcal{D}}(h) := \mathbb{E}_{(X, Y) \sim \mathcal{D}}[\ell(h(X), Y)].}
\]
The \textbf{empirical risk} is
\[
\boxed{L_S(h) := \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m \ell(h, Z_i).}
\]
We aim to construct $A(S)$ so that $L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S))$ is small, even though we do not know $\mathcal{D}$.

\paragraph{ERM.}
The principle of \textbf{Empirical Risk Minimization (ERM)} chooses
\[
\boxed{A_{\text{ERM}}(S) \in \arg \min_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_S(h).}
\]
ERM is an algorithmic principle, not a theorem. Its validity relies on empirical risk being a good uniform approximation of population risk over $\mathcal{H}$:
\[
\sup_{h \in \mathcal{H}} |L_S(h) - L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)|
\]
being small. This question is resolved by uniform convergence and VC theory.

\paragraph{Realizable vs.\ agnostic learning.}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Assumption} & \textbf{Benchmark} \\ \hline
Realizable learning & $\exists f \in \mathcal{H}$ such that $Y = f(X)$ a.s. & $L_{\mathcal{D}}(f) = 0$ \\ \hline
Agnostic learning & No perfect $h \in \mathcal{H}$ is assumed & $\inf_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)$ \\ \hline
Noisy-label setting & $Y$ can be random given $X$ & Bayes risk or best-in-class risk \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Problems 1--2 use the realizable setting:
\[
Y = f(X), \quad f \in \mathcal{H}.
\]

Problem 4, in contrast, reflects the agnostic (general-loss) setting, working directly with labeled examples $z = (x, y)$ and general losses $\ell(w, z)$.

\paragraph{Key distinction.}
In the realizable case, a consistent learner can attain zero empirical risk:
\[
L_S(A(S)) = 0.
\]
But this does \textbf{not} in itself imply zero population risk:
\[
L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) = 0.
\]
The entire theory of PAC learning asks: \emph{Under what conditions on $\mathcal{H}$, $m$, and the algorithm $A$ does small (or zero) training risk imply small population risk?} This is the unifying principle behind Problems 1--4.

\subsubsection{PAC learning}

We now formalize what it means for a hypothesis class to be learnable from finitely many random examples. This is the direct conceptual continuation of Problems 1–2 and the prerequisite for uniform convergence and VC-based sample complexity.

Our learning goal is to make small risk with high probability:
\[
\Pr_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m} [L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon] \geq 1 - \delta,
\]
where \(f \in \mathcal{H}\) is the target function. For realizable learning, we assume \(f \in \mathcal{H}\), so that the target itself has zero risk: \(L_{\mathcal{D},f}(f) = 0\). (This does \emph{not} imply \(L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) = 0\); that is what PAC asks for approximately, with high probability.)

\begin{definition}[Sample complexity]
Fix a hypothesis class \(\mathcal{H}\) and a learning algorithm \(A\).
A function \(m_{\mathcal{H}} : (0,1)^2 \to \mathbb{N}\) is a \textbf{sample complexity} for \(A\) on \(\mathcal{H}\) if,
for every \(\epsilon, \delta \in (0,1)\), every distribution \(\mathcal{D}\) over \(\mathcal{X}\), and every target \(f \in \mathcal{H}\),
\[
m \geq m_{\mathcal{H}}(\epsilon, \delta)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\Pr_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m}\bigl[L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon\bigr] \geq 1 - \delta.
\]
Intuitively: \(m_{\mathcal{H}}(\epsilon,\delta)\) is a sufficient training-set size guaranteeing error at most \(\epsilon\) with probability at least \(1-\delta\).
It is not uniquely determined---any larger function also works---so theorems typically give an \emph{upper bound} on some valid \(m_{\mathcal{H}}\).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[PAC learnability]
A hypothesis class $\mathcal{H}$ is \textbf{PAC learnable} if there exist
\begin{itemize}
    \item a learning algorithm $A$, and
    \item a sample complexity function $m_{\mathcal{H}} : (0, 1)^2 \to \mathbb{N}$ for $A$ on $\mathcal{H}$
\end{itemize}
such that for every $\epsilon, \delta \in (0,1)$, every distribution $\mathcal{D}$ over $\mathcal{X}$, and every target function $f \in \mathcal{H}$, if $m \geq m_{\mathcal{H}}(\epsilon, \delta)$, then
\begin{equation}\tag{PAC}
\Pr_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \left[ L_{\mathcal{D}, f}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon \right] \geq 1 - \delta
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Meaning of ``PAC''.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Probably}: The guarantee need not hold on every sample, but with high probability over random samples:
    \[
    \Pr_{S}[\text{success}] \geq 1 - \delta.
    \]
    \item \textbf{Approximately}: We aim not for perfect learning but for population error at most $\epsilon$:
    \[
    L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon,
    \]
    rather than requiring exactly zero population error.
    \item \textbf{Correct}: The learned hypothesis matches the target well under $\mathcal{D}$---in expectation, it achieves risk close to the best possible in $\mathcal{H}$.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{Finite-class PAC theorem}

\begin{theorem}[PAC sample complexity: finite class]
Assume the realizable setting: \(f \in \mathcal{H}\) and labels are given by \(y = f(x)\).
If \(\mathcal{H}\) is finite, then \(\mathcal{H}\) is PAC learnable, and every consistent learner
\(A\) (in particular, every ERM rule that returns some \(h \in \mathcal{H}\) with \(L_S(h) = 0\))
is a PAC learner for \(\mathcal{H}\), with sample complexity
\[
m_{\mathcal{H}}(\epsilon, \delta) \leq \left\lceil \frac{\ln |\mathcal{H}| + \ln(1/\delta)}{\epsilon} \right\rceil.
\]
Equivalently: whenever
\[
m \geq \frac{1}{\epsilon} \left( \ln |\mathcal{H}| + \ln \frac{1}{\delta} \right),
\]
one has
\[
\Pr_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m}\bigl[L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon\bigr] \geq 1 - \delta
\]
for every distribution \(\mathcal{D}\) over \(\mathcal{X}\) and every target \(f \in \mathcal{H}\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix a hypothesis $h \in \mathcal{H}_{\text{bad}}(\epsilon)$, where $\mathcal{H}_{\text{bad}}(\epsilon) \triangleq \{ h \in \mathcal{H} : L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h) > \epsilon \}$. For such $h$,
\[
L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h) > \epsilon.
\]
For $h$ to have zero empirical risk, it must classify every sample point correctly. Since the examples are i.i.d.,
\begin{align*}
\Pr_{S}[L_S(h) = 0] &= \Pr_{X_1, \dots, X_m}[h(X_i) = f(X_i) \text{ for every } i] \\
&= \prod_{i=1}^m \Pr_{X_i \sim \mathcal{D}}[h(X_i) = f(X_i)] \\
&= (1 - L_{\mathcal{D},f}(h))^m \\
&< (1 - \epsilon)^m \\
&\leq e^{-\epsilon m}.
\end{align*}

Now apply the union bound to all bad hypotheses:
\begin{align*}
\Pr_{S}[\exists h \in \mathcal{H}_{\text{bad}}(\epsilon) : L_S(h) = 0] 
&\leq \sum_{h \in \mathcal{H}_{\text{bad}}(\epsilon)} \Pr_{S}[L_S(h) = 0] \\
&\leq |\mathcal{H}|e^{-\epsilon m}.
\end{align*}

If \(A\) is consistent, then \(L_S(A(S)) = 0\). Hence the event \(L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) > \epsilon\)
implies \(A(S) \in \mathcal{H}_{\text{bad}}(\epsilon)\) and \(L_S(A(S)) = 0\), and therefore
\[
\Pr_{S}[L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) > \epsilon] \leq |\mathcal{H}|e^{-\epsilon m}.
\]
To ensure this failure probability is at most $\delta$, require
\[
|\mathcal{H}|e^{-\epsilon m} \leq \delta.
\]
Taking natural logarithms,
\[
\ln |\mathcal{H}| - \epsilon m \leq \ln \delta.
\]
Since $\ln \delta = -\ln(1/\delta)$, we find
\[
\epsilon m \geq \ln |\mathcal{H}| + \ln \frac{1}{\delta}.
\]
Thus,
\[
m \geq \frac{\ln |\mathcal{H}| + \ln(1/\delta)}{\epsilon}.
\]
Therefore, any consistent learner achieves \(L_{\mathcal{D},f}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon\) with probability at least \(1 - \delta\), provided \(m\) satisfies the above bound.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Realizable versus agnostic PAC}

\noindent

{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.8cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Realizable PAC} & \textbf{Agnostic PAC} \\
\hline
Data model & $Y = f(X)$ for some $f \in \mathcal{H}$ & $(X, Y) \sim \mathcal{D}$ arbitrary \\
\hline
Perfect classifier in $\mathcal{H}$? & Yes & Not assumed \\
\hline
Comparator & $f$, with risk $0$ & $h_{\mathcal{H}}^* \in \arg \min_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)$ \\
\hline
Required guarantee & $L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon$ & $L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \leq L_{\mathcal{D}}(h_{\mathcal{H}}^*) + \epsilon$ \\
\hline
Finite-class rate & $O(1/\epsilon)$ & $O(1/\epsilon^2)$ \\
\hline
Main mechanism & Probability a bad $h$ is accidentally consistent & Uniform convergence of empirical risks \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
}

The agnostic setting is closer to realistic statistical modeling: labels may be noisy, the features may not determine labels completely, or $\mathcal{H}$ may be misspecified. The primary text introduces it by replacing the deterministic target-labeling rule with a joint distribution over $(X, Y)$.


\paragraph{Agnostic learning setup}
Let $\mathcal{D}$ be any distribution over labeled pairs $Z = (X, Y) \in \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{Y}$. In the agnostic case, there may be no $f \in \mathcal{H}$ with $Y = f(X)$ a.s. For binary classification with 0-1 loss, $\ell(h, (x, y)) = \mathbf{1}\{h(x) \neq y\}$, and the population risk is $L_{\mathcal{D}}(h) = \Pr_{(X,Y) \sim \mathcal{D}}[h(X) \neq Y]$. The best-in-class hypothesis is
\[
h_{\mathcal{H}}^* \in \arg\min_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)
\]
with $L_{\mathcal{D}}(h_{\mathcal{H}}^*) > 0$ in general. The learner cannot generally compute $h_{\mathcal{H}}^*$ since $\mathcal{D}$ is unknown.

\paragraph{Agnostic PAC definition}
\begin{definition}[Agnostic PAC learnability]
A class $\mathcal{H}$ is \textbf{agnostically PAC learnable} if there exist a learning algorithm $A$ and sample-complexity function $m_{\mathcal{H}}$ such that, for every $\epsilon, \delta \in (0, 1)$, every distribution $\mathcal{D}$ over $\mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{Y}$, and every $m \geq m_{\mathcal{H}}(\epsilon, \delta)$,
we have
\[
\Pr_{S \sim \mathcal{D}^m} \left[ L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \leq \inf_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(h) + \epsilon \right] \geq 1 - \delta.
\]
\end{definition}

The learner is not asked to achieve zero population error. It is asked to perform nearly as well as the unknown best hypothesis in the chosen class.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Target guarantee} \\ \hline
Realizable PAC & $L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \leq \epsilon$ \\ \hline
Agnostic PAC & $L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) \leq \inf_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(h) + \epsilon$ \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The quantity
\[
L_{\mathcal{D}}(A(S)) - \inf_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)
\]
is called the \textbf{excess risk} or \textbf{estimation error}.

\paragraph{Hoeffding's inequality}

Let
\[
Z_1, \dots, Z_m
\]
be independent random variables with
\[
Z_i \in [a, b]
\]
almost surely. Then, for every $t > 0$,
\[
\boxed{\Pr \left[ \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m Z_i - \mathbb{E}[Z_i] \geq t \right] \leq \exp \left( -\frac{2mt^2}{(b-a)^2} \right).}
\]

When $Z_i \in [0, 1]$,
\[
\Pr \left[ \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m Z_i - \mathbb{E}[Z_i] \geq t \right] \leq e^{-2mt^2}.
\]

The two-sided version is
\[
\boxed{\Pr \left[ \left| \frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m Z_i - \mathbb{E}[Z_i] \right| \geq t \right] \leq 2e^{-2mt^2}.} \quad (\text{Hoeffding})
\]

For a fixed hypothesis $h$, define
\[
Z_i := \ell(h, Z_i^{\text{data}}),
\]
where $Z_i^{\text{data}} \sim \mathcal{D}$. Under 0-1 loss,
\[
Z_i \in \{0, 1\}, \quad \mathbb{E}[Z_i] = L_{\mathcal{D}}(h),
\]
and
\[
\frac{1}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m Z_i = L_S(h).
\]
Thus Hoeffding gives

\begin{equation*}
\boxed{\Pr_{S} [|L_{S}(h) - L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)| \geq t] \leq 2e^{-2mt^{2}}.}
\end{equation*}

This is a fixed-$h$ concentration result.

\paragraph{Why fixed-$h$ is insufficient}

ERM chooses
\[
\widehat{h}_S \in \arg \min_{h \in \mathcal{H}} L_S(h).
\]
But $\widehat{h}_S$ depends on $S$. Thus you cannot simply substitute $\widehat{h}_S$ into a theorem that was proved only for one hypothesis fixed independently of the sample.

The correct event is
\[
\{\exists h \in \mathcal{H} : |L_S(h) - L_{\mathcal{D}}(h)| > t\}.
\]
This motivates the central definition.

\input{ml-universal-review}

\section{B. Deep Learning and Reinforcement Learning}

\subsection{Problem 1. [18 pts.]}
Consider a classification problem: the training dataset is given as $\{(\mathbf{x}_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^N$, where $\mathbf{x}_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ represents the input features, and $y_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, C\}$ represents the class labels. A supervised deep learning pipeline typically includes preparing training data, defining a hypothesis space, designing a training scheme, and optimizing the network. Answer the following questions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Define the hypothesis space consisting of all neural networks structured with:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item a feature extractor: a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) that maps the inputs to a learned feature representation,
        \item a classifier: a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) that maps the feature representation to class probabilities.
    \end{itemize}
    Please specify the input, output and the parameters for training.
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} Specify an appropriate loss function for this classification problem and explain how stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is used to optimize the neural network.
    \item \textbf{[4 pts.]} To stabilize training, batch normalization is often applied. Describe how batch normalization works during the training and testing phase.
    \item \textbf{[3 pts.]} If the training error is unsatisfactory, describe what adjustments you can make to improve the expressivity of the neural network. Discuss at least two approaches.
    \item \textbf{[3 pts.]} If the training error is low but the testing error is high, propose strategies to reduce overfitting. Discuss at least two approaches.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 1}

This problem tests the complete supervised-DL pipeline: model class $\to$ loss $\to$ optimization $\to$ normalization $\to$ underfitting versus overfitting remedies.

\paragraph{(a) Hypothesis space — 4 points}
Let the learned feature dimension be $m$. Define the probability simplex
\[
\Delta^{C-1} = \left\{ p \in \mathbb{R}^C : p_c \geq 0, \sum_{c=1}^C p_c = 1 \right\}.
\]

The desired learned function is
\[
f_\theta : \mathbb{R}^d \to \Delta^{C-1}.
\]

For an input $x$, the output $f_\theta(x)$ is a vector of $C$ class probabilities. Its $c$-th coordinate is interpreted as
\[
[f_\theta(x)]_c = p_\theta(Y = c \mid X = x).
\]

}
\end{center}


Let the feature extractor be an $L_F$-layer MLP:

\[
h^{(0)} = x \in \mathbb{R}^d,
\]

\[
h^{(\ell)} = \phi_\ell \left( W_\ell^F h^{(\ell-1)} + b_\ell^F \right), \quad \ell = 1, \dots, L_F,
\]

where

\[
h^{(L_F)} =: z \in \mathbb{R}^m
\]

is the learned feature representation. Here $\phi_\ell$ is a componentwise nonlinear activation function, such as ReLU.

Let the classifier be an $L_G$-layer MLP. Set
\[
q^{(0)} = z,
\]
\[
q^{(r)} = \psi_r \left( W_r^G q^{(r-1)} + b_r^G \right), \quad r = 1, \dots, L_G - 1,
\]
and let the final classifier layer produce logits
\[
u = W_{L_G}^G q^{(L_G-1)} + b_{L_G}^G \in \mathbb{R}^C.
\]
The class-probability output is obtained by softmax:
\[
[f_\theta(x)]_c = \text{softmax}(u)_c = \frac{\exp(u_c)}{\sum_{k=1}^C \exp(u_k)}, \quad c = 1, \dots, C.
\]
Therefore, the hypothesis space is
\[
\mathcal{H} = \{ f_\theta = g_{\theta_G} \circ e_{\theta_F} : \mathbb{R}^d \to \Delta^{C-1}, \theta = (\theta_F, \theta_G) \in \Theta \}.
\]
The trainable parameter vector consists of every weight matrix and bias vector:
\[
\theta_F = \{ W_\ell^F, b_\ell^F \}_{\ell=1}^{L_F}, \quad \theta_G = \{ W_r^G, b_r^G \}_{r=1}^{L_G}.
\]
If batch normalization is used, its learnable scale and shift parameters $\{ \gamma, \beta \}$ are also included in $\theta$.


{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.8cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Aspect} & \textbf{Feature Extractor} & \textbf{Classifier / Prediction Head} \\
\hline
\textbf{Main role} & Learns a representation of the input. & Converts the learned representation into a prediction over classes. \\
\hline
\textbf{Mathematical map} & $h_{\phi} : \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^m$ & $g_{\psi} : \mathbb{R}^m \to \mathbb{R}^C$ \\
\hline
\textbf{Input} & Raw features $x \in \mathbb{R}^d$. & Learned feature vector $h_{\phi}(x) \in \mathbb{R}^m$. \\
\hline
\textbf{Output} & A latent feature representation / embedding $h$. & Usually $C$ logits $z \in \mathbb{R}^C$, followed by softmax probabilities. \\
\hline
\textbf{What it should learn} & Task-relevant patterns, invariances, and nonlinear structure in the data. & Which regions of the feature space correspond to each class. \\
\hline
\textbf{Interpretation of output} & Usually has no direct probabilistic or semantic interpretation coordinate-by-coordinate. & After softmax, each coordinate is interpreted as $p_{\theta}(y = c \mid x)$. \\
\hline
\textbf{Typical architecture} & Often the larger ``backbone'': MLP, CNN, Transformer, ResNet, etc. & Often a small MLP or a linear layer on top of the extractor. \\
\hline
\textbf{Transfer-learning use} & Can often be reused across related tasks or datasets. & Is commonly replaced or fine-tuned when the label set changes. \\
\hline
\textbf{Parameters} & $\phi$: parameters that determine how inputs are represented. & $\psi$: parameters that determine how representations are mapped to labels. \\
\hline
\textbf{Training signal} & Receives gradients indirectly through the classifier and loss. & Receives gradients directly from the classification loss. \\
\hline
\textbf{Main intuition} & ``Turn raw data into features that make the task easy.'' & ``Make the final decision using those features.'' \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
}

A correct answer must clearly distinguish the following:
\[
\text{input }x
\longrightarrow
\text{representation }z
\longrightarrow
\text{logits }u
\longrightarrow
\text{probabilities }f_\theta(x).
\]

\subparagraph{Scoring points}
\begin{itemize}
    \item 1 point: feature extractor MLP and its input/output.
    \item 1 point: classifier MLP and logits.
    \item 1 point: softmax mapping logits to $C$ probabilities.
    \item 1 point: complete parameter set and hypothesis-space definition.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{(b) Loss function and SGD --- 4 points}


For multi-class classification, use the softmax cross-entropy loss. For example $i$,
\[
\ell_i(\theta) = -\log p_\theta(y_i \mid x_i) = -\log [f_\theta(x_i)]_{y_i}.
\]

Equivalently, if $t_i \in \{0,1\}^C$ is the one-hot encoding of $y_i$, then
\[
\ell_i(\theta) = -\sum_{c=1}^{C} t_{ic} \log [f_\theta(x_i)]_c.
\]

The empirical-risk objective, optionally with $L_2$ regularization, is
\[
J(\theta) = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \ell_i(\theta) + \frac{\lambda}{2} \|\theta\|_2^2.
\]

At iteration $t$, choose a mini-batch $B_t \subseteq \{1, \dots, N\}$ with $|B_t| = b$. The mini-batch gradient is
\[
g_t = \frac{1}{b} \sum_{i \in B_t} \nabla_\theta \ell_i(\theta_t) + \lambda \theta_t.
\]

\begin{remark}[Why the $+\lambda\theta_t$ term]
The objective is
\[
J(\theta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_i(\theta)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta\|_2^2,
\]
so
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla_\theta\ell_i(\theta)
+\nabla_\theta\Bigl(\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta\|_2^2\Bigr)
=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla_\theta\ell_i(\theta)+\lambda\theta.
\]
The last summand is therefore the exact gradient of the $L_2$ penalty (the factor $\tfrac12$ is chosen so that this derivative is simply $\lambda\theta$, not $\lambda\theta$ with an extra $2$). In the SGD estimator we replace only the data-average by a mini-batch average; the regularizer does not depend on the sample, so its contribution remains the deterministic term $\lambda\theta_t$. Equivalently, this is the gradient form of weight decay:
\[
\theta_{t+1}
=\theta_t-\eta_t\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in B_t}\nabla_\theta\ell_i(\theta_t)+\lambda\theta_t\Bigr)
=(1-\eta_t\lambda)\,\theta_t-\eta_t\cdot\tfrac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in B_t}\nabla_\theta\ell_i(\theta_t).
\]
\end{remark}

SGD updates the parameters as
\[
\boxed{\theta_{t+1} = \theta_t - \eta_t g_t,}
\]

where $\eta_t > 0$ is the learning rate. The gradients are computed by backpropagation, i.e. reverse-mode automatic differentiation through the computational graph.

\subparagraph{Why cross-entropy is appropriate}

The network output is a categorical distribution:
\[
p_\theta(Y = c \mid \boldsymbol{x}), \quad c = 1, \dots, C.
\]

The probability that the model assigns to the observed correct label is
\[
p_\theta(y_i \mid \boldsymbol{x}_i).
\]

Maximizing the likelihood of all labels is equivalent to minimizing negative log-likelihood:
\[
\max_\theta \prod_{i=1}^N p_\theta(y_i \mid \boldsymbol{x}_i) \iff \min_\theta -\sum_{i=1}^N \log p_\theta(y_i \mid \boldsymbol{x}_i).
\]

That is exactly the cross-entropy objective.


\subparagraph{Training loop}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Randomly initialize $\theta_0$.
    \item Sample a mini-batch $B_t$.
    \item Perform the forward pass to compute logits and softmax probabilities.
    \item Compute average mini-batch cross-entropy.
    \item Backpropagate to obtain $g_t$.
    \item Update $\theta_t$ using SGD, momentum, or Adam.
    \item Repeat over mini-batches and epochs; choose hyperparameters by validation performance.
\end{enumerate}

The distinction is essential:

\begin{center}
\fbox{backpropagation computes $g_t$; \quad SGD uses $g_t$ to update $\theta$.}
\end{center}

\subparagraph{Scoring points}
\begin{itemize}
    \item 1 point: correct softmax cross-entropy.
    \item 1 point: empirical-risk objective, ideally including regularization.
    \item 1 point: mini-batch gradient.
    \item 1 point: SGD update and explanation of backpropagation.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{(c) Batch normalization --- 4 points}

Batch normalization (BN) normalizes each hidden coordinate using statistics computed over a mini-batch during training, then applies a learned affine transformation. Its aim is to make optimization more stable and improve gradient propagation in deep networks.

\subparagraph{Training phase}

Suppose a layer produces pre-activation values $a_{ni}$, where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $n=1,\ldots,b$ indexes examples in the current mini-batch;
    \item $i$ indexes one hidden unit/channel;
    \item $b=|B_t|$ is the mini-batch size.
\end{itemize}

For each coordinate $i$, compute mini-batch mean and variance:
\[
\mu_{B,i}
=
\frac{1}{b}\sum_{n=1}^{b}a_{ni},
\]
\[
\sigma^2_{B,i}
=
\frac{1}{b}\sum_{n=1}^{b}(a_{ni}-\mu_{B,i})^2.
\]

Normalize:
\[
\widehat{a}_{ni}
=
\frac{a_{ni}-\mu_{B,i}}
{\sqrt{\sigma^2_{B,i}+\varepsilon}},
\]
where $\varepsilon>0$ is a small numerical-stability constant.

Then restore flexible scale and shift using trainable parameters:
\[
\boxed{
\widetilde{a}_{ni}
=
\gamma_i\widehat{a}_{ni}+\beta_i.
}
\]

The activation function is usually subsequently applied:
\[
h_{ni}=\phi(\widetilde{a}_{ni}).
\]

During training, $\gamma_i$ and $\beta_i$ are optimized with weights and biases by backpropagation. BN therefore does \textbf{not} force a layer to have permanently fixed mean zero and variance one: the learned affine transformation can produce an appropriate scale and offset.

\subparagraph{Testing / inference phase}

At test time, the test example should not be normalized using its own statistics or the statistics of an arbitrary test mini-batch. Doing so makes the prediction depend on other test examples and can be unstable, especially for a single input.

Instead, use running averages accumulated during training:
\[
\overline{\mu}_i
\leftarrow
\alpha \overline{\mu}_i+(1-\alpha)\mu_{B,i},
\]
\[
\overline{\sigma}_i^2
\leftarrow
\alpha \overline{\sigma}_i^2
+
(1-\alpha)\sigma_{B,i}^2.
\]

At inference, compute
\[
\boxed{
\widetilde{a}_i
=
\gamma_i
\frac{a_i-\overline{\mu}_i}
{\sqrt{\overline{\sigma}_i^2+\varepsilon}}
+
\beta_i.
}
\]

Thus:

{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.2cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Phase} & \textbf{Mean/variance used} & \textbf{Are $\gamma,\beta$ learned?} \\
\hline
Training & Current mini-batch $\mu_{B,i},\sigma^2_{B,i}$ & Yes \\
\hline
Testing/inference & Stored running statistics $\overline{\mu}_i,\overline{\sigma}_i^2$ & Fixed learned values \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
}

The key separation is: batch statistics are used during training; moving averages accumulated in training are used for inference.

\begin{remark}[Why not use test mini-batch statistics?]
A single test example (or a tiny test batch) yields unreliable mean/variance estimates. Worse, normalizing one test point against other unrelated test points couples predictions across the batch, which is undesirable for deployment. Running averages estimated once during training give a fixed, deterministic transformation at inference.
\end{remark}

\subparagraph{Scoring points}
\begin{itemize}
    \item 1 point: per-mini-batch mean and variance.
    \item 1 point: normalization with $\varepsilon$.
    \item 1 point: learnable $\gamma,\beta$.
    \item 1 point: running mean/variance for test-time inference.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{(d) High training error: increase expressivity --- 3 points}

Low training error is not required here; the premise is that the network cannot fit even the training data satisfactorily. This can indicate \textbf{underfitting}, insufficient model capacity, or an optimization problem.

\begin{center}

  \node[qbox] (Q) at (0,3.85)

  % leaf gap $\ge 2.9$cm > box width $2.15$cm

  \node[box=teal] (D) at (-4.6,-0.65)

    {Optimizer /\\grad.\ clip};

  % trunk
  \draw[arr] (P) -- (Q);

  % fork bus below Q, above Cap/Opt
  \coordinate (fork) at (0,2.85);
  \draw[draw=black!45, thick] (Q.south) -- (fork);
  \draw[arr] (fork) -| (Cap.north);
  \draw[arr] (fork) -| (Opt.north);

  % labels ABOVE the horizontal arms, clear of Cap/Opt
  \node[lab=teal] at (-2.55,3.05) {yes: enlarge $\mathcal{H}$};
  \node[lab=orange] at (2.55,3.05) {no: fix training};

  % Cap / Opt to leaves
  \coordinate (capbus) at (-4.6,0.45);
  \coordinate (optbus) at (4.6,0.45);
  \draw[draw=black!45, thick] (Cap.south) -- (capbus);
  \draw[arr] (capbus) -| (W.north);
  \draw[arr] (capbus) -- (D.north);
  \draw[arr] (capbus) -| (B.north);
  \draw[draw=black!45, thick] (Opt.south) -- (optbus);
  \draw[arr] (optbus) -| (O1.north);
  \draw[arr] (optbus) -| (O2.north);

  \node[font=\tiny, text=black!55, align=center] at (0,-1.85)
    {Exam cue: naming width/depth alone is incomplete---state \emph{why} it raises expressivity,\\and note that high train error $\not\Rightarrow$ insufficient capacity.};
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\end{center}

\subparagraph{Approach 1: increase width}

Increase the number of hidden units in one or more MLP layers:
\[
h^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_\ell}
\quad\longrightarrow\quad
h^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_\ell'},
\qquad
m_\ell'>m_\ell.
\]
This increases the number of weights and the number of nonlinear features available to represent complex decision boundaries.

\subparagraph{Approach 2: increase depth}

Increase the number of hidden layers:
\[
L_F\to L_F',
\qquad L_F'>L_F,
\]
and/or increase the depth of the classifier MLP. Additional compositions of affine maps and nonlinearities can represent more hierarchical and complicated functions, often more parameter-efficiently than a shallow network.

\subparagraph{Other valid approaches}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Use a less restrictive activation function or avoid excessive saturation; e.g.\ replace sigmoid/tanh units that saturate strongly with ReLU-family activations.
    \item Increase the feature-representation dimension $m$ if the extractor contains a severe bottleneck.
    \item Use an architecture with a more appropriate inductive bias: CNNs for images, Transformers/RNNs for sequences, graph neural networks for graph data.
    \item If capacity is already adequate, improve \textbf{optimization} rather than expressivity: adjust learning rate, initialization, normalization, number of epochs, optimizer, or gradient clipping.
\end{itemize}

The last point is important: poor training error does not logically prove that the architecture lacks expressivity. The optimizer may simply have failed to find good parameters.

\begin{checkpoint}[Diagnostic check before claiming ``need more expressivity'']
Run the following checks in order. Only after~(1)--(3) fail should you primarily blame capacity.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em]
  \item \textbf{Optimization sanity.}
  Try a learning-rate sweep, better initialization, BN/normalization, more epochs, and a different optimizer (e.g.\ Adam).
  If training error drops substantially, the original architecture was already expressive enough.
  \item \textbf{Capacity probe.}
  Temporarily widen or deepen the network (or remove an obvious bottleneck $m$).
  If training error still stays high under a careful optimizer, capacity is a plausible culprit.
  \item \textbf{Inductive-bias check.}
  Ask whether the architecture matches the data type (MLP on images vs.\ CNN; bag-of-features vs.\ Transformer on sequences).
  A wrong bias can look like ``insufficient expressivity'' even at large width/depth.
  \item \textbf{Logical guardrail.}
  Remember the counterexample: a large MLP with a pathological learning rate can have high training error despite huge $\mathcal{H}$.
  High training error $\not\Rightarrow$ small hypothesis class.
\end{enumerate}
\vspace{0.35em}
\noindent\textbf{Exam self-test.}
Which one-sentence reply earns the point for ``increase depth''?
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em]
  \item \textit{Good:} ``More layers compose affine maps and nonlinearities, enabling hierarchical features that a shallow net may need exponentially more units to approximate.''
  \item \textit{Weak:} ``Just add more layers.'' (states the action, not the expressivity effect)
\end{itemize}
\end{checkpoint}

\subparagraph{Scoring points}
\begin{itemize}
    \item 1.5 points: increase width, with an expressivity explanation.
    \item 1.5 points: increase depth, with an expressivity explanation.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{(e) Low training error, high test error: reduce overfitting --- 3 points}

This is the classical overfitting pattern:
\[
\text{training error low}
\qquad\text{but}\qquad
\text{test error high}.
\]
The model fits idiosyncrasies or noise in the training set but does not generalize well.

\subparagraph{Strategy 1: weight decay / $L_2$ regularization}

Instead of minimizing the empirical risk alone, optimize the regularized objective
\[
J(\theta)
=
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N\ell_i(\theta)
+
\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta\|_2^2,
\qquad
\lambda\ge 0.
\]
The second term is an explicit penalty on the Euclidean size of the parameters. Large $\|\theta\|_2$ typically allows the network to form sharp, highly curved decision boundaries that can interpolate idiosyncrasies of the finite training sample; the $L_2$ penalty discourages such large weights and therefore reduces the \emph{effective} complexity of the fitted function.

In gradient form, the penalty contributes exactly $\lambda\theta$ (cf.\ the mini-batch update in part~(b)):
\[
\theta_{t+1}
=
(1-\eta_t\lambda)\,\theta_t
-
\eta_t\cdot\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in B_t}\nabla_\theta\ell_i(\theta_t).
\]
Thus each SGD step multiplies the current parameters by a factor $(1-\eta_t\lambda)<1$ before applying the data gradient: this is why $L_2$ regularization is also called \textbf{weight decay}. Intuitively, unless the training loss keeps pushing a coordinate upward, that coordinate is continually shrunk toward zero.

Practical remarks for the exam answer:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Choose $\lambda$ on a \textbf{validation} set (never on the test set). Too small $\lambda$ barely reduces overfitting; too large $\lambda$ underfits and raises training error.
  \item The same $\lambda$ need not apply to every parameter group: biases and BatchNorm $(\gamma,\beta)$ are often left unpenalized.
  \item Weight decay attacks overfitting by shrinking the hypothesis that SGD can reach, not by changing the architecture itself.
\end{itemize}

\subparagraph{Strategy 2: dropout}

Dropout is a stochastic regularizer applied to hidden activations (or, less commonly, inputs). Fix a keep-probability $p\in(0,1]$. During training, for a hidden vector $h\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$, draw an independent Bernoulli mask $m\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)^{m}$ and replace
\[
h \;\longmapsto\; m\odot h
\]
(coordinatewise). Units with $m_j=0$ are temporarily removed from the forward and backward pass of that mini-batch. Because the surviving units must still predict well, the network cannot rely on brittle co-adaptations such as ``unit~$A$ is useful only when unit~$B$ is also on.'' This encourages redundant, more robust features and reduces overfitting.

At test time, randomness is removed so that predictions are deterministic. Ordinary (inverted) dropout keeps the training-time scaling $1/p$ during training and, at inference, uses the full network with all units active and no further rescaling; equivalently, one may train without the $1/p$ factor and multiply outgoing weights by $p$ at test time. In either convention:
\[
\text{train: random subnetwork per mini-batch};
\qquad
\text{test: fixed full network}.
\]

Why this helps when training error is already low but test error is high:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Training still fits the data (often almost as well), but each update sees a different thinned network, which acts like an inexpensive ensemble over $2^{m}$ submodels sharing weights.
  \item The resulting predictor is less sensitive to particular hidden units and therefore less likely to memorize training-set noise.
  \item Typical choices: $p\approx 0.5$ on fully connected hidden layers, higher keep rates (e.g.\ $0.8$--$0.9$) on input or convolutional layers; tune $p$ on validation data.
\end{itemize}

\begin{remark}[Weight decay vs.\ dropout]
Weight decay is a \emph{deterministic} penalty in parameter space; dropout is a \emph{stochastic} perturbation in activation space. Both reduce overfitting, but they are complementary: decay shrinks $\|\theta\|_2$, while dropout changes which units interact on each forward pass. An exam answer should state the train/test difference for dropout explicitly.
\end{remark}

\subparagraph{Other valid strategies}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Early stopping:} stop at the epoch with the best validation loss rather than the smallest training loss.
    \item \textbf{Data augmentation:} create label-preserving transformations, such as image crops, flips, rotations, or domain-valid perturbations.
    \item \textbf{Reduce capacity:} use fewer layers, narrower layers, or a smaller representation dimension $m$.
    \item \textbf{Collect more training data:} effective when available data are insufficient relative to model complexity.
    \item \textbf{Label smoothing:} replace one-hot targets with slightly softened target probabilities.
    \item \textbf{Ensembling:} average multiple models; this often improves test accuracy but increases compute.
\end{itemize}

Regularization, data augmentation, and early stopping are standard tools in deep learning.

\subparagraph{Scoring points}
A complete 3-point answer can state any two of the following with an explanation:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Weight decay / $L_2$ regularization.
    \item Dropout.
    \item Data augmentation.
    \item Early stopping based on validation data.
    \item Reduced model capacity.
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{Concise answer template}

For a time-limited exam, your answer can be organized as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Architecture:} define $z=e_{\theta_F}(x)$, logits $u=g_{\theta_G}(z)$, and probabilities $p_\theta(y=c\mid x)=\softmax(u)_c$; list $\theta$.
    \item \textbf{Objective and training:} give cross-entropy, empirical risk, mini-batch gradient, and SGD update.
    \item \textbf{BN:} mini-batch mean/variance $\to$ normalize $\to$ learned $\gamma,\beta$; use moving averages at test time.
    \item \textbf{High training error:} widen/deepen network or remove a bottleneck; distinguish capacity from optimization failure.
    \item \textbf{High test error:} weight decay, dropout, augmentation, early stopping, or smaller architecture.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{What to study next}

Study the following in this exact order:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Derive softmax cross-entropy from the categorical likelihood.
    \item Derive $\nabla_u \ell = \softmax(u)-t$ for one example.
    \item Derive backpropagation for a two-layer MLP.
    \item Compute one complete mini-batch SGD update by hand.
    \item Compare batch normalization, layer normalization, dropout, and weight decay.
\end{enumerate}

\subparagraph{Self-check}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Why does the hypothesis-space output lie in $\Delta^{C-1}$, rather than merely $\mathbb{R}^C$?
    \item What exact quantity is averaged over examples in the cross-entropy objective?
    \item Why are current mini-batch statistics inappropriate for a single test example?
    \item Give a counterexample showing that high training error need not mean insufficient expressivity.
    \item Explain why reducing a network's width may lower test error while raising training error.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Framework}

This is the \textbf{standard supervised-learning framework for multi-class classification}. Its goal is: from labeled examples, learn a function that maps a new input $x$ to one of $C$ classes; deep learning uses a \textbf{neural network} as that function. ``Learn'' here is the map \(A(S)=f_{\widehat\theta}\) of \jump{def:spr2025-learn}{the exam-wide note}; ``deep'' only names the class. This framework is the foundation you need before studying \textbf{CNNs}, \textbf{Transformers}, \textbf{generative models}, or \textbf{deep reinforcement learning}. 

{\footnotesize

\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.2cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Term} & \textbf{It answers} & \textbf{Example} \\
\hline
\textbf{Supervised learning} & \textit{What feedback is available?} & You observe image--label pairs $(x, \text{cat/dog})$ \\
\hline
\textbf{Deep learning} & \textit{What model class is used?} & A CNN, Transformer, or MLP \\
\hline
\textbf{Supervised deep learning} & Both & Train a CNN to classify images from labeled examples \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
}

A learning system has five logically separate pieces:

\[\boxed{data \rightarrow model class \rightarrow loss/objective \rightarrow optimization \rightarrow evaluation}\]
