\href{https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Cute_kitten.jpg}{Cute kitten}, and
\href{https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Domestic_White_and_Gray_Cat.JPG}{Domestic White and Gray Cat}.
The forward and reverse panels are locally generated by adding Gaussian pixel noise to
the first image and arranging the resulting states in opposite directions; they are not
downloaded figures.

\paragraph{The SDE language.}

\begin{definition}[Standard Wiener process]
A $d$-dimensional standard Wiener process, also called a standard Brownian motion, is an
$\mathbb{R}^d$-valued stochastic process
$W=(W_t)_{t\geq 0}$ satisfying the following properties:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item $W_0=0$ almost surely, and $t\mapsto W_t$ has continuous sample paths almost
    surely.
    \item For every $0\leq s<t$, the increment $W_t-W_s$ is Gaussian with
    \[
    W_t-W_s\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,(t-s)I_d\right).
    \]
    \item Increments over disjoint time intervals are independent. In particular,
    $W_t-W_s$ is independent of the history
    $\mathcal{F}_s:=\sigma(W_u:0\leq u\leq s)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Quadratic covariation]
Let $U$ and $V$ be continuous semimartingales. Their quadratic covariation up to time
$t$ is denoted by $[U,V]_t$ and is defined, whenever the limit exists, by
\[
[U,V]_t
:=
\lim_{\lVert\pi\rVert\to0}
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
\bigl(U_{t_{k+1}}-U_{t_k}\bigr)
\bigl(V_{t_{k+1}}-V_{t_k}\bigr),
\qquad
\pi=\{0=t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_n=t\},
\]
where $\lVert\pi\rVert:=\max_k(t_{k+1}-t_k)$ is the mesh of the partition. The
subscript $t$ means that the accumulated quadratic covariation is taken over the time
interval $[0,t]$; it is not a power or an ordinary product. For a $d$-dimensional
process $W=(W^1,\ldots,W^d)$, the notation $[W]_t$ denotes the matrix
\[
[W]_t:=\bigl([W^i,W^j]_t\bigr)_{1\leq i,j\leq d}.
\]
For a standard Wiener process, the coordinates have
\[
[W^i,W^j]_t=\delta_{ij}t,
\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad
[W]_t=tI_d.
\]
Informally, Brownian increments have size $\sqrt{\mathrm{d}t}$, so their squared
increments accumulate at order $\mathrm{d}t$; this is why Brownian motion has nonzero
quadratic variation even though its ordinary finite-variation derivative does not exist.
\end{definition}

\begin{property}[Standard Wiener process]
The following standard properties will be used without proof:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $W_t$ is a martingale and a Markov process\footnote{A stochastic process
    $(Y_t)_{t\geq0}$ is Markov if, for every $0\leq s<t$ and every bounded measurable
    function $\varphi$, its conditional expectation satisfies
    $\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Y_t)\mid\mathcal{F}_s]
    =\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Y_t)\mid Y_s]$ almost surely. Thus, conditional on the present
    state $Y_s$, the future does not depend on the earlier history. For the Wiener process,
    $W_t\mid W_s\sim\mathcal{N}(W_s,(t-s)I_d)$.} with respect to its natural
    filtration;
    \item for $s,t\geq0$,
    \[
    \operatorname{Cov}(W_s,W_t)=\min(s,t)I_d;
    \]
    \item its quadratic covariation satisfies
    \[
    [W]_t=tI_d,
    \qquad\text{formally written as}\qquad
    \mathrm{d}W_t\,\mathrm{d}W_t^{\top}=I_d\,\mathrm{d}t;
    \]
    \item it has the Brownian scaling property
    \[
    (W_{ct})_{t\geq0}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}
    (\sqrt{c}\,W_t)_{t\geq0},
    \qquad c>0;
    \]
    \item for a deterministic square-integrable vector or scalar integrand $h$, the It\^o
    integral $\int h(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_s$ is centered Gaussian,\footnote{For a deterministic
    square-integrable integrand, the It\^o integral is defined as the $L^2$ limit of partition
    sums $\sum_k h(s_k)(W_{s_{k+1}}-W_{s_k})$. Each finite sum is Gaussian because it is a
    linear combination of independent Gaussian Brownian increments, and it has mean zero
    because every increment has mean zero. Gaussian laws are closed under these $L^2$ limits,
    so the limiting It\^o integral remains centered Gaussian.} and its covariance is
    obtained by integrating the squared integrand against $\mathrm{d}s$. This is the
    It\^o-isometry principle used below.
\end{itemize}
\end{property}

The continuous-time formulation describes the forward noising path by the It\^o SDE

\[
\mathrm{d}X_t=f(X_t,t)\,\mathrm{d}t+g(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_t,
\qquad
t\in[0,T],
\]

where $X_t\in\mathbb{R}^d$ is the noisy data variable, $f$ is the drift, $g$ is the
diffusion coefficient, and $W_t$ is a $d$-dimensional standard Wiener process. The time
variable is a noise level or continuation parameter; it need not be physical time. In
particular,

\[
W_{t+\Delta t}-W_t\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Delta t\,I).
\]

Consequently, over a short interval,

\[
X_{t+\Delta t}-X_t
\approx
f(X_t,t)\Delta t+g(t)\sqrt{\Delta t}\,\varepsilon,
\qquad
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I).
\]

The drift increment is of order $\Delta t$, whereas the stochastic increment is of order
$\sqrt{\Delta t}$. Thus $W_t$ cannot be manipulated as an ordinary differentiable
function, which is why the It\^o product rule and It\^o isometry are needed below.

\begin{theorem}[It\^o product rule]
Let $U_t$ and $V_t$ be continuous semimartingales. Their product satisfies
\[
\mathrm{d}(U_tV_t)
=U_t\,\mathrm{d}V_t+V_t\,\mathrm{d}U_t+\mathrm{d}[U,V]_t,
\]
where $[U,V]_t$ denotes their quadratic covariation. In particular, if $a$ is a
deterministic $C^1$ function and $X_t$ is a vector-valued semimartingale, then
\[
\mathrm{d}\bigl(a(t)X_t\bigr)
=a(t)\,\mathrm{d}X_t+a'(t)X_t\,\mathrm{d}t,
\]
because a deterministic finite-variation function has zero quadratic covariation with
$X_t$. This is the integrating-factor identity used to solve the linear VP-SDE.
\end{theorem}

\begin{note}[Why the cross term vanishes for $a(t)X_t$]
The function $a(t)$ in the special formula is a scalar deterministic function, while
$X_t\in\mathbb{R}^d$ is a vector. Therefore $a(t)X_t\in\mathbb{R}^d$ means scalar
multiplication, coordinate by coordinate:
\[
a(t)X_t
=\bigl(a(t)X_t^1,\ldots,a(t)X_t^d\bigr).
\]
Applying the scalar product rule to each coordinate gives
\[
\mathrm{d}\bigl(a(t)X_t^i\bigr)
=a(t)\,\mathrm{d}X_t^i+X_t^i\,\mathrm{d}a(t)
+\mathrm{d}[a,X^i]_t.
\]
Because $a$ is deterministic and $C^1$, it has finite variation on every bounded time
interval and
\[
\mathrm{d}a(t)=a'(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.
\]
Its increments are of order $\Delta t$, whereas the Brownian part of the increments of
$X_t$ is of order $\sqrt{\Delta t}$. Consequently, the sums defining the quadratic
covariation satisfy
\[
\sum_k \Delta a_k\,\Delta X^i_k
\longrightarrow 0,
\qquad
\Delta a_k=O(\Delta t_k),
\]
so $[a,X^i]_t=0$. Thus the bracket term is absent, and collecting the coordinate
identities yields
\[
\mathrm{d}\bigl(a(t)X_t\bigr)
=a(t)\,\mathrm{d}X_t+a'(t)X_t\,\mathrm{d}t.
\]
The essential assumption is finite variation, not merely the fact that $a$ is a
semimartingale: a stochastic semimartingale with a nonzero martingale component could
have nonzero quadratic covariation with $X_t$. More generally, if $A(t)$ is a
deterministic $C^1$ matrix and $X_t$ is a vector, then the same reasoning gives
\[
\mathrm{d}\bigl(A(t)X_t\bigr)
=A(t)\,\mathrm{d}X_t+A'(t)X_t\,\mathrm{d}t.
\]
\end{note}

\begin{theorem}[It\^o isometry]
Let $W_t$ be a $d$-dimensional standard Wiener process and let
$H_t\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}$ be a predictable process satisfying
\[
\mathbb{E}\int_0^T\lVert H_s\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty.
\]
Then the It\^o integral is centered and satisfies
\[
\mathbb{E}\left\|
\int_0^t H_s\,\mathrm{d}W_s
\right\|^2
=
\mathbb{E}\int_0^t\lVert H_s\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2\,\mathrm{d}s.
\]
When $H_s$ is deterministic,\footnote{Here deterministic means that $H_s$ is fixed in
advance: for every time $s$, it does not depend on the sampled Brownian path or on any other
source of randomness. Equivalently, there is a nonrandom function $h:[0,T]\to
\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}$ such that $H_s(\omega)=h(s)$ for every outcome $\omega$.} the
integral is a centered Gaussian vector and its covariance\footnote{For an
$m$-dimensional random vector $Y$, its covariance matrix is
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Y)
:=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)^\top\bigr].
\]
If $Y$ is centered, this reduces to $\operatorname{Cov}(Y)=\mathbb{E}[YY^\top]$.
Thus the right-hand side below is not the definition of covariance; it is the covariance
obtained by applying the It\^o-isometry calculation to this stochastic integral.} is
\[
\operatorname{Cov}\left(\int_0^t H_s\,\mathrm{d}W_s\right)
=\int_0^t H_sH_s^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}s.
\]
The appearance of $\mathrm{d}s$ reflects the quadratic variation
$\mathrm{d}W_s\,\mathrm{d}W_s^{\top}=I_d\,\mathrm{d}s$; it is the second-moment form of
the stochastic integration rule, not a replacement for the Brownian increment
$\mathrm{d}W_s$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proofsketch}[Idea of the It\^o-isometry proof]
First take a step-process integrand
\[
H_s=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}H_k\,\mathbf{1}_{(t_k,t_{k+1}]}(s),
\qquad
0=t_0<\cdots<t_n=t,
\]
where each $H_k$ is known at time $t_k$. The defining integral is then
\[
\int_0^tH_s\,\mathrm{d}W_s
=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}H_k\Delta W_k,
\qquad
\Delta W_k:=W_{t_{k+1}}-W_{t_k}.
\]
Each Brownian increment has conditional mean zero and covariance
$\operatorname{Cov}(\Delta W_k\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_k})=(t_{k+1}-t_k)I_d$. Hence the
cross terms have expectation zero, and
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}\left\|\sum_kH_k\Delta W_k\right\|^2
&=\sum_k\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\operatorname{tr}\!\left(H_k\Delta W_k\Delta W_k^\top H_k^\top\right)\right] \\
&=\sum_k\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert H_k\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2\bigr]
(t_{k+1}-t_k)
=\mathbb{E}\int_0^t\lVert H_s\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^2\,\mathrm{d}s.
\end{aligned}
\]
This is the essential calculation: independent mean-zero Brownian increments remove the
cross terms, while their second moment turns into a time increment. General predictable
square-integrable integrands are obtained by $L^2$-approximating them with such step
processes; the displayed equality makes the corresponding integrals Cauchy in $L^2$ and
therefore extends the identity. If $H$ is deterministic, the finite sum is a linear
combination of independent Gaussian increments, so it is centered Gaussian with covariance
$\sum_kH_kH_k^\top(t_{k+1}-t_k)$. Passing to the same $L^2$ limit gives
$\int_0^tH_sH_s^\top\,\mathrm{d}s$.
\end{proofsketch}

\paragraph{The VP choice.}
For the SDE in the question,

\[
f(x,t)=-\frac12\beta(t)x,
\qquad
g(t)=\sqrt{\beta(t)},
\qquad
\beta(t)>0.
\]

The drift continuously contracts the signal while the diffusion term injects Gaussian
noise. If $\operatorname{Cov}(X_0)=I$, then the explicit solution below satisfies
$\operatorname{Cov}(X_t)=I$ at every time. More generally,

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(X_t)
=\alpha^2(t)\operatorname{Cov}(X_0)
+\bigl(1-\alpha^2(t)\bigr)I,
\]

so the covariance is controlled and is gradually moved toward the unit covariance rather
than growing without bound as it would under a pure Brownian-noise process. This is the
precise sense in which the process is called variance preserving.

\subsubsection{Solving the VP-SDE step by step}

Define the integrated noise schedule and the signal coefficient by

\[
B(t):=\int_0^t\beta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,
\qquad
\alpha(t):=e^{-B(t)/2}
=\exp\left(-\frac12\int_0^t\beta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right).
\]

The notation $\alpha(t)$ here is a deterministic signal-retention coefficient, not a
network parameter. Since

\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\alpha(t)^{-1}
=\frac12\beta(t)\alpha(t)^{-1},
\]

the deterministic process $\alpha(t)^{-1}$ is the integrating factor. Applying the It\^o
product rule, with no quadratic-variation term because $\alpha(t)^{-1}$ is deterministic,
gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{d}\bigl(\alpha(t)^{-1}X_t\bigr)
&=\alpha(t)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}X_t
+X_t\,\mathrm{d}\bigl(\alpha(t)^{-1}\bigr) \\
&=\alpha(t)^{-1}
\left[-\frac12\beta(t)X_t\,\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}W_t\right]
+\frac12\beta(t)\alpha(t)^{-1}X_t\,\mathrm{d}t \\
&=\alpha(t)^{-1}\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}W_t.
\end{aligned}
\]

Integrating from $0$ to $t$ and using $\alpha(0)=1$ yields

\[
\alpha(t)^{-1}X_t
=X_0+\int_0^t\alpha(s)^{-1}\sqrt{\beta(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W_s,
\]

and hence

\[
X_t
=\alpha(t)X_0
+\alpha(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W_s.
\]

Conditionally on $X_0$, the first term $\alpha(t)X_0$ is deterministic, so
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(X_t\mid X_0)
=
\alpha^2(t)\,
\operatorname{Cov}(I_t),
\qquad
I_t
:=
\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W_s.
\]
The integrand
\[
h_0(s)
:=
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}
\]
is a nonrandom scalar. This is the setting in which It\^o isometry gives a covariance
matrix, not merely an $L^2$ identity. Approximate $I_t$ on a partition
$0=s_0<\cdots<s_n=t$ by
\[
I_t
\approx
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}h_0(s_k)\Delta W_k,
\qquad
\Delta W_k:=W_{s_{k+1}}-W_{s_k}.
\]
A $d$-dimensional standard Wiener process has independent Gaussian increments with
\[
\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_k]=0,
\qquad
\operatorname{Cov}(\Delta W_k)=\Delta s_k\,I,
\qquad
\Delta s_k:=s_{k+1}-s_k.
\]
Cross terms vanish: $\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_j\Delta W_k^\top]=0$ for $j\neq k$. Hence
\[
\operatorname{Cov}\Bigl(\sum_k h_0(s_k)\Delta W_k\Bigr)
=
\sum_k h_0(s_k)^2\Delta s_k\,I.
\]
Sending the mesh to zero is It\^o isometry for a deterministic scalar integrand:
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(I_t)
=
\int_0^t h_0(s)^2\,\mathrm{d}s\,I
=
\int_0^t\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I.
\]
The same finite sum is a linear image of independent Gaussians, so $I_t$ is centered
Gaussian; the $L^2$ limit keeps both the law and the covariance. Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(X_t\mid X_0)
=
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I.
\]
The remaining equalities are ordinary calculus. Use $\alpha(s)=e^{-B(s)/2}$, so
$\alpha^{-2}(s)=e^{B(s)}$. Then
\[
\int_0^t\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s
=
\int_0^t\beta(s)e^{B(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s.
\]
Since $B'(s)=\beta(s)$ and $B(0)=0$,
\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}e^{B(s)}=\beta(s)e^{B(s)},
\qquad
\int_0^t\beta(s)e^{B(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s
=
e^{B(t)}-1.
\]
Multiply by $\alpha^2(t)=e^{-B(t)}$:
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(X_t\mid X_0)
=
\alpha^2(t)\bigl(e^{B(t)}-1\bigr)I
=
\bigl(1-\alpha^2(t)\bigr)I.
\]

Writing

\[
\sigma^2(t):=1-\alpha^2(t),
\qquad
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I),
\]

we obtain the two equivalent forms

\[
X_t\mid X_0=x_0
\sim
\mathcal{N}\left(\alpha(t)x_0,\sigma^2(t)I\right),
\]

and

\[
X_t=\alpha(t)X_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon.
\]

\begin{note}
The last display is a reparameterized form of the explicit stochastic-integral solution,
not a new approximation. Define

\[
Z_t
:=
\alpha(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W_s.
\]

Then

\[
X_t=\alpha(t)X_0+Z_t.
\]

Conditionally on $X_0$, the integrand in $Z_t$ is deterministic, so $Z_t$ is a centered
Gaussian vector. Before computing its covariance, it is useful to distinguish the roles
of $\mathrm{d}W_s$ and $\mathrm{d}s$. The former is the random Brownian increment used to
construct $Z_t$; the latter appears only after taking a second moment. Thus, the
appearance of $\mathrm{d}s$ below does not mean that $\mathrm{d}W_s$ has been replaced.
To make this precise, write

\[
h(s):=\alpha(t)\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}.
\]

Approximate the It\^o integral by a partition sum:

\[
\int_0^t h(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_s
\approx
\sum_k h(s_k)\Delta W_k,
\qquad
\Delta W_k:=W_{s_{k+1}}-W_{s_k}.
\]

For a $d$-dimensional standard Wiener process, the increments are independent and satisfy

\[
\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_k]=0,
\qquad
\operatorname{Cov}(\Delta W_k)=\Delta s_k\,I,
\qquad
\Delta s_k:=s_{k+1}-s_k.
\]

Here $I=I_d$ is the $d\times d$ identity matrix. Since
$\Delta W_k\in\mathbb{R}^d$ is a $d$-dimensional Brownian increment, each coordinate has
variance $\Delta s_k$, while different coordinates have zero covariance. Consequently,
its covariance matrix is $\Delta s_k I_d$, not the scalar $\Delta s_k$ alone.

Therefore, independence eliminates the cross-covariance terms and gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Cov}\left(\sum_k h(s_k)\Delta W_k\right)
&=
\sum_k h^2(s_k)\operatorname{Cov}(\Delta W_k) \\
&=
\sum_k h^2(s_k)\Delta s_k\,I.
\end{aligned}
\]

Taking the limit of the Riemann sums produces

\[
\operatorname{Cov}\left(\int_0^t h(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_s\right)
=
\int_0^t h^2(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,I.
\]

This is the content of It\^o isometry in the present deterministic-integrand setting.
It is also sometimes expressed formally as

\[
\mathrm{d}W_s\,\mathrm{d}W_s^{\top}=I\,\mathrm{d}s,
\]

which is shorthand for the quadratic covariation rule of Brownian motion, not ordinary
calculus multiplication. Substituting the definition of $h(s)$ gives the covariance
display below:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Cov}(Z_t)
&=
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I \\
&=
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t
\beta(s)e^{B(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I \\
&=
\alpha^2(t)\bigl(e^{B(t)}-1\bigr)I \\
&=
\bigl(1-\alpha^2(t)\bigr)I
=\sigma^2(t)I.
\end{aligned}
\]

The third equality uses

\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}e^{B(s)}
=\beta(s)e^{B(s)}.
\]

Therefore,

\[
Z_t\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^2(t)I\right).
\]

If $\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$, then
$\sigma(t)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2(t)I)$, so we may write

\[
Z_t=\sigma(t)\varepsilon,
\qquad
X_t=\alpha(t)X_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon.
\]

This representation is exact in distribution and is computationally convenient: one
samples $X_0$, samples $\varepsilon$, and constructs $X_t$ directly, without simulating
the forward SDE with many Euler--Maruyama steps.
\end{note}

This closed-form transition is important computationally: training does not need to
simulate the forward SDE with many Euler--Maruyama steps. It can sample $x_0$, sample a
time $t$, sample $\varepsilon$, and construct $x_t$ in one operation.

\subsubsection{Score, denoising, and reverse dynamics}

For a differentiable density $p_t$, the score is the vector field

\[
\operatorname{score}(x,t):=\nabla_x\log p_t(x).
\]

It points in the local direction of greatest increase of the log-density. The marginal
density of the noisy variable is

\[
p_t(x_t)
=\int p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)p_0(x_0)\,\mathrm{d}x_0.
\]

The transition density is known from the solved VP-SDE, but the data density $p_0$ is
unknown, so the marginal density and its score are generally unavailable in closed form.

\paragraph*{Why $p_{t\mid 0}$ is explicit.}
The solved VP-SDE is the affine It\^o integral
\[
X_t
=
\alpha(t)X_0
+
\alpha(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W_s
=
\alpha(t)X_0+Z_t.
\]
The integrand of $Z_t$ is a deterministic scalar times $I_d$, hence independent of $X_0$.
The covariance of $Z_t$ is the It\^o-isometry calculation specialized to this integrand,
as follows. Write
\[
Z_t
=
\int_0^t h(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_s,
\qquad
h(s)
:=
\alpha(t)\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}.
\]
The map $s\mapsto h(s)$ is nonrandom. Approximate the integral by a left-endpoint sum
on a partition $0=s_0<\cdots<s_n=t$:
\[
Z_t
\approx
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}h(s_k)\Delta W_k,
\qquad
\Delta W_k:=W_{s_{k+1}}-W_{s_k}.
\]
Each increment of a $d$-dimensional standard Wiener process is independent of the past,
centered, and Gaussian, with
\[
\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_k]=0,
\qquad
\operatorname{Cov}(\Delta W_k)=\Delta s_k\,I,
\qquad
\Delta s_k:=s_{k+1}-s_k.
\]
Independent mean-zero increments kill the cross terms
$\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_j\Delta W_k^\top]=0$ for $j\neq k$, so
\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Cov}\Bigl(\sum_k h(s_k)\Delta W_k\Bigr)
&=
\sum_k h(s_k)^2\operatorname{Cov}(\Delta W_k)
=
\sum_k h(s_k)^2\Delta s_k\,I.
\end{aligned}
\]
The mesh-to-zero limit is the Riemann integral (this is It\^o isometry for a
deterministic scalar integrand):
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Z_t)
=
\int_0^t h(s)^2\,\mathrm{d}s\,I
=
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I.
\]
The same finite-sum argument shows that $Z_t$ is a linear image of independent Gaussians,
hence centered Gaussian; the $L^2$ limit preserves both the Gaussian law and the
covariance. Now insert $\alpha(s)=e^{-B(s)/2}$, so $\alpha^{-2}(s)=e^{B(s)}$. Then
\[
\int_0^t\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s
=
\int_0^t\beta(s)e^{B(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s.
\]
The integrand is an exact derivative: $B'(s)=\beta(s)$ and $B(0)=0$, therefore
\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}e^{B(s)}=\beta(s)e^{B(s)},
\qquad
\int_0^t\beta(s)e^{B(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s
=
e^{B(t)}-1.
\]
Multiplying by $\alpha^2(t)=e^{-B(t)}$ gives
\[
\operatorname{Cov}(Z_t)
=
\alpha^2(t)\bigl(e^{B(t)}-1\bigr)I
=
\bigl(1-\alpha^2(t)\bigr)I
=
\sigma^2(t)I,
\]
where $\sigma^2(t):=1-\alpha^2(t)$. Thus $Z_t\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2(t)I)$.
Conditioning on $X_0=x_0$ therefore only shifts the mean:
\[
X_t\mid X_0=x_0
\sim
\mathcal{N}\bigl(\alpha(t)x_0,\sigma^2(t)I\bigr).
\]
For $t>0$ one has $\sigma(t)>0$, so this law is absolutely continuous on $\mathbb{R}^d$.
A nondegenerate Gaussian $\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)$ with
$\mu=\alpha(t)x_0$ and $\Sigma=\sigma^2(t)I$ has density
\[
(2\pi)^{-d/2}(\det\Sigma)^{-1/2}
\exp\Bigl(-\tfrac12(x-\mu)^\top\Sigma^{-1}(x-\mu)\Bigr).
\]
Here $\det\Sigma=\sigma^{2d}(t)$ and $\Sigma^{-1}=\sigma^{-2}(t)I$, so the transition
density is
\[
p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
=
\bigl(2\pi\sigma^2(t)\bigr)^{-d/2}
\exp\Bigl(
-\frac{\lVert x_t-\alpha(t)x_0\rVert_2^2}{2\sigma^2(t)}
\Bigr).
\]
No unknown object enters: $\alpha$ and $\sigma$ are determined by the schedule $\beta$.
The data law $p_0$ never appears. (At $t=0$, $\sigma(0)=0$ and the transition is
$\delta_{x_0}$, which has no Lebesgue density; the formulas below are for $t>0$.)
This is the sense in which ``the transition density is known.'' The marginal
$p_t(x_t)=\int p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)p_0(x_0)\,\mathrm{d}x_0$ still mixes against the
unknown $p_0$, so $p_t$ and $\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t$ remain unavailable.
The conditional score, however, is explicit. From the Gaussian transition,

\[
\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
=\text{constant}
-\frac{\|x_t-\alpha(t)x_0\|_2^2}{2\sigma^2(t)},
\]

and therefore

\[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
=-\frac{x_t-\alpha(t)x_0}{\sigma^2(t)}
=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma(t)}.
\]

The link between the known conditional score and the unknown marginal score follows by
differentiating the marginalization identity:

\[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)
=\mathbb{E}\left[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid X_0)
\mid X_t=x_t
\right].
\]

Thus the conditional score is an unbiased regression target for the marginal score. A
related identity makes the denoising interpretation explicit:

\[
\mathbb{E}[X_0\mid X_t=x_t]
=\frac{1}{\alpha(t)}
\left[x_t+\sigma^2(t)\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)\right].
\]

The network may therefore be parameterized to predict the score $s_\theta$, the injected
noise $\varepsilon_\theta$, the clean sample $\widehat{x}_{0,\theta}$, or a linear
$v$-parameterization. In the present problem, the network directly predicts the score;
the noise-prediction form is equivalent through

\[
s_\theta(x_t,t)=-\frac{\varepsilon_\theta(x_t,t)}{\sigma(t)}.
\]

For the general forward SDE, the reverse-time SDE, written with $t$ decreasing from $T$
to $0$, is

\[
\mathrm{d}X_t
=\left[f(X_t,t)-g^2(t)\nabla_x\log p_t(X_t)\right]\mathrm{d}t
+g(t)\,\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_t,
\qquad
t:T\downarrow0.
\]

For the VP-SDE this becomes

\[
\mathrm{d}X_t
=\left[
-\frac12\beta(t)X_t-\beta(t)\nabla_x\log p_t(X_t)
\right]\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_t.
\]

Because the reverse integration direction has $\mathrm{d}t<0$, the time convention must
be stated when writing signs. In computation, the unknown score is replaced by
$s_\theta(X_t,t)$.

If one prefers a time variable that increases during generation, set
$\tau=T-t$ and $Y_\tau=X_{T-\tau}$. The equivalent reverse SDE is then

\[
\mathrm{d}Y_\tau
=\left[
\frac12\beta(T-\tau)Y_\tau
+\beta(T-\tau)\nabla_y\log p_{T-\tau}(Y_\tau)
\right]\mathrm{d}\tau
+\sqrt{\beta(T-\tau)}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{W}_\tau.
\]

The two displays describe the same process under different time conventions. This
reparameterization is often convenient when applying standard forward-time numerical
schemes, but it does not remove the need to approximate the score.

\begin{remark}[Interpretation of score-based diffusion]
A score-based diffusion model is a generative model that learns the score field $\nabla_x \log p_t(x)$ of noisy data distributions $p_t$, rather than directly parameterizing the original data density $p_0$. The forward diffusion is deliberately chosen to progressively corrupt data into a tractable Gaussian-like distribution. Although the reverse-time dynamics depend on the generally unknown marginal score $\nabla_x \log p_t(x)$, this score can be learned by a neural network using denoising score matching, because the conditional perturbation kernel $p_{t|0}(x_t \mid x_0)$ is known analytically. Starting from noise and integrating the learned reverse SDE then yields new samples approximately distributed as $p_0$.
\end{remark}




\begin{note}
    The central learning route is
    \[
    \text{forward SDE} \quad \Longrightarrow \quad p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0) \quad \Longrightarrow \quad \nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0) \quad \Longrightarrow \quad s_\theta(x_t,t).
    \]
    Do not try to learn $p_t$ itself in this problem. The neural network learns its score, which is enough to construct the reverse-time dynamics.
\end{note}

\subsubsection{How to work through this problem}

This problem should be studied as one dependency chain, not as three unrelated formula-recall exercises. Work through it in the following order:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item First understand the forward process: clean data $x_0$ are gradually converted into noisy variables $x_t$.
    \item Then identify the obstruction to generation: the reverse process requires the marginal score $\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)$, but the data distribution is unknown.
    \item Next prove that the inaccessible marginal-score objective has the same minimizer as a computable conditional-score objective.
    \item Finally solve the VP-SDE explicitly, substitute its Gaussian score, and obtain the loss used by a training algorithm.
\end{enumerate}

For each marked part below, read the short exam-ready statement first, then study the derivation underneath it. The derivation is for understanding; the compressed statement is what should remain available under time pressure.

\subsubsection{Preliminaries}

\paragraph{Data distribution, noisy distribution, and transition kernel.}
Let $p_0$ denote the unknown data distribution. A clean training example is written as $x_0 \sim p_0$. After running the forward noising process from time $0$ to time $t$, the random variable $x_t$ has:

\begin{itemize}
    \item the \textbf{conditional density} $p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)$ when a particular clean example $x_0$ is fixed;
    \item the \textbf{marginal density} $p_t(x_t)$ after averaging over all possible clean examples.
\end{itemize}

They are related by

\[
p_t(x_t) = \int p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0) p_0(x_0)\, \mathrm{d}x_0.
\]

The first density will be explicitly computable for the VP-SDE. The second one usually is not.

\paragraph{Score function.}
For a differentiable density $p(x)$ on $\mathbb{R}^d$, its \textbf{score} is the vector field

\[
\nabla_x \log p(x).
\]

It points locally in the direction in which the log-density increases fastest. For a one-dimensional Gaussian $X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)$,

\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\log p(x) = -\frac{x-\mu}{\sigma^2}.
\]

Thus a point to the right of the mean receives a negative score, and a point to the left receives a positive score: the score points back toward the high-density region. In this problem, the desired but unknown score is $\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)$.

\paragraph{Stochastic differential equations.}
A time-inhomogeneous SDE has the form

\[
\mathrm{d}x_t = f(x_t,t)\,\mathrm{d}t + g(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_t,
\]

where $f$ is the drift, $g$ controls the magnitude of injected random noise, and $W_t$ is a standard Wiener process. The VP-SDE in the question has

\[
f(x,t) = -\frac{1}{2}\beta(t)x,
\qquad
g(t) = \sqrt{\beta(t)}.
\]

The positive schedule $\beta(t)$ determines how fast signal is removed and noise is added. ``Variance preserving'' means that, when the data have been normalized appropriately, the process keeps a controlled variance scale rather than allowing it to explode.

\paragraph{Reverse-time convention.}
The reverse SDE below is written with the time variable running from $T$ \emph{down to} $0$. Under this convention its differential still uses $\mathrm{d}t$, but during generation the numerical time steps are negative. Stating this convention explicitly prevents the most common sign error in this topic.

\subsubsection{Notation}

{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.65cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Object} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Role in this problem} \\
\hline
$x_0$ & Clean sample drawn from $p_0$ & A data point from the training distribution \\
\hline
$x_t$ & Noisy version of $x_0$ at time $t$ & Network input during training \\
\hline
$p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)$ & Conditional transition density & Known analytically for the VP-SDE \\
\hline
$p_t(x_t)$ & Marginal density of $x_t$ & Unknown density whose score is needed in reverse sampling \\
\hline
$\beta(t)$ & Positive noise schedule & Sets the instantaneous noising rate \\
\hline
$\alpha(t)$ & $\exp\!\left(-\frac12\int_0^t\beta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)$ & Signal coefficient in the VP transition kernel \\
\hline
$\sigma^2(t)$ & $1-\alpha^2(t)$ & Conditional noise variance \\
\hline
$s_\theta(x_t,t)$ & Neural score model & Approximation to $\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)$ \\
\hline
$\lambda(t)$ & Nonnegative weighting function & Reweights loss contributions across noise levels \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\par}

\subsubsection{A one-dimensional sanity check}

Suppose temporarily that $x_0=2$ is a fixed scalar and that, at a particular time $t$, $\alpha(t)=0.8$ and $\sigma(t)=0.6$. Then

\[
x_t = 0.8\cdot 2 + 0.6\varepsilon,
\qquad
\varepsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1).
\]

Hence $x_t\mid x_0 \sim \mathcal{N}(1.6,0.36)$, and its conditional score is

\[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
= -\frac{x_t-1.6}{0.36}
= -\frac{\varepsilon}{0.6}.
\]

This example already contains the final training target: sample a clean datum and a Gaussian noise vector, construct $x_t$, and ask the network to predict a vector proportional to the negative injected noise. The time $t$ is an input because the scale $\sigma(t)$ changes with the amount of corruption.

\subsubsection{What you may safely reuse from Problem 1}

Problem 1 established the supervised-learning pipeline: a hypothesis class, a loss function, and optimization by stochastic gradient descent. Here the hypothesis class is the family of time-conditioned neural vector fields $s_\theta(x,t)$, and the loss is again an expectation estimated with mini-batches. What changes is the target: it is not a class label but a vector-valued score.

\subsubsection{Readiness check}

Before attempting the formal solution, make sure every line below has a clear meaning.

\[
p_t(x_t) = \int p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)p_0(x_0)\,\mathrm{d}x_0.
\]

\[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)
= \mathbb{E}\left[\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)\mid x_t\right].
\]

\[
\mathrm{d}x_t
= \left[f(x_t,t)-g^2(t)\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)\right]\mathrm{d}t
+ g(t)\,\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_t,
\qquad t:T\to 0.
\]

\[
x_t = \alpha(t)x_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon,
\qquad
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I).
\]

\[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
= -\frac{x_t-\alpha(t)x_0}{\sigma^2(t)}
= -\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma(t)}.
\]

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 2}

\paragraph{(a) Reverse-time SDE and the role of score matching --- 4 points}

\paragraph{Detailed explanation in the notation of the question.}
The forward VP process in the question is
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_t
=-\frac12\beta(t)\mathbf{X}_t\,\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_t,
\qquad
0\leq t\leq T.
\]
Here $\mathbf{X}_t$ is the random noisy data vector, while $\mathbf{x}_t$ denotes a
possible realized value of it. The schedule $\beta(t)>0$ controls the instantaneous
strength of both signal contraction and injected noise. Let $p_t$ be the density of
$\mathbf{X}_t$. The score
\[
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)\in\mathbb{R}^d
\]
points locally toward regions where the noisy-data density increases most rapidly.

\begin{theorem}[Reverse-time SDE for a scalar diffusion coefficient]
Let $(\mathbf{X}_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}$ solve the forward SDE
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_t
=f(\mathbf{X}_t,t)\,\mathrm{d}t
+g(t)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_t,
\]
where $g(t)>0$ is scalar. Assume the standard time-reversal regularity conditions: the
diffusion is nonexplosive; $f$ and $g$ are sufficiently smooth; $g$ stays positive; the
transition densities exist and have a controlled short-time Gaussian expansion; and
$p_t>0$ is $C^{1,2}$ for interior times. These assumptions ensure that the local
expansions and their first two conditional moments used in the proof are valid. Then its
time reversal can be represented, using the same time coordinate run from $T$ down to $0$,
by
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_t
=\left[
f(\mathbf{X}_t,t)
-g^2(t)\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\log p_t(\mathbf{X}_t)
\right]\mathrm{d}t
+g(t)\,\mathrm{d}\overline{\mathbf{W}}_t,
\qquad
t:T\downarrow0,
\]
where this $t:T\downarrow0$ display is backward-time notation. A precise increasing-time
form, including its Brownian motion and filtration, is derived in the proof below. In
particular, the reverse process has the same marginal density $p_t$ at each time $t$ as
the forward process, but it is traversed in the opposite temporal direction.

For the VP-SDE in this problem,
\[
f(\mathbf{x},t)=-\frac12\beta(t)\mathbf{x},
\qquad
g(t)=\sqrt{\beta(t)},
\]
so the reverse-time dynamics are
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_t
=\left[
-\frac12\beta(t)\mathbf{X}_t
-\beta(t)\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{X}_t)
\right]\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{\mathbf{W}}_t,
\qquad
t:T\downarrow0.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We give a direct small-time proof, without generator or adjoint notation. The question is
simple: after observing the present state $\mathbf{X}_t=\mathbf{x}$, where was the process
most likely to have been a very short time $h$ earlier?

Use an increasing clock for the reversed process:
\[
\tau:=T-t,
\qquad
\mathbf{Y}_\tau:=\mathbf{X}_{T-\tau}.
\]
Fix a time $t$, a point $\mathbf{x}$ with $p_t(\mathbf{x})>0$, and a small $h>0$. Define
the unknown backward displacement
\[
\mathbf{Z}_h:=\mathbf{X}_{t-h}-\mathbf{x}.
\]
Then $\mathbf{Z}_h=\mathbf{Y}_{\tau+h}-\mathbf{Y}_\tau$ whenever
$\mathbf{Y}_\tau=\mathbf{x}$. Bayes' rule gives its exact conditional density:
\[
\mathbb{P}\!\left(
\mathbf{Z}_h\in\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}
\mid\mathbf{X}_t=\mathbf{x}
\right)
=\frac{
p_{t-h}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z})\,
p_{t-h,t}(\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z})}
{p_t(\mathbf{x})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}.
\]
The numerator has two factors. The first says how common a possible past point is; the
second says how likely the forward SDE is to travel from that point to the observed present
point.

Set $a(t):=g^2(t)$ and write
$\mathbf{s}_t(\mathbf{x}):=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\log p_t(\mathbf{x})$. On the natural
Brownian scale $\mathbf{z}=O(\sqrt{h})$, the forward SDE has the local Gaussian form
\[
\mathbf{X}_t
\approx
\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z}
+h f(\mathbf{x},t)
+g(t)\sqrt{h}\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},
\qquad
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},I).
\]
Thus, viewed as a function of the possible past displacement $\mathbf{z}$, its likelihood
has the first-order expansion. Under the regularity assumptions in the theorem, its
remainder is controlled by an integrable Gaussian bound, so it contributes only $o(h)$ to
the conditional first two moments below:
\[
p_{t-h,t}(\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z})
=\phi_{a(t)h}(\mathbf{z})
\left[
1-\frac{\mathbf{z}\cdot f(\mathbf{x},t)}{a(t)}
+O(h)
\right],
\]
where $\phi_{a h}$ is the centered Gaussian density with covariance $ahI$. Taylor
expansion of the marginal density similarly gives
\[
\frac{p_{t-h}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{z})}{p_t(\mathbf{x)}
}
=1+\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{s}_t(\mathbf{x})+O(h).
\]
The $O(h)$ notation above includes the time change in $p_{t-h}$ and the quadratic
Taylor terms; it is valid on the $\mathbf{z}=O(\sqrt{h})$ scale.

Multiplying the two Bayes factors gives
\[
\mathbb{P}\!\left(
\mathbf{Z}_h\in\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}
\mid\mathbf{X}_t=\mathbf{x}
\right)
=\phi_{a(t)h}(\mathbf{z})
\left[
1+\mathbf{z}\cdot
\left(
\mathbf{s}_t(\mathbf{x})
-\frac{f(\mathbf{x},t)}{a(t)}
\right)
+O(h)
\right]\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}.
\]
This formula is the whole source of the score term. The ordinary forward drift shifts the
most likely past location by $-h f(\mathbf{x},t)$. Bayes' rule additionally favors past
points in locally denser regions, and the density slope
$\mathbf{s}_t(\mathbf{x})=\nabla\log p_t(\mathbf{x})$ shifts the mean by
$a(t)h\mathbf{s}_t(\mathbf{x})$.

Using the first two moments of the centered Gaussian in the preceding display gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\mathbf{Z}_h\mid\mathbf{X}_t=\mathbf{x}
\right]
&=
h\left[
-f(\mathbf{x},t)
+g^2(t)\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\log p_t(\mathbf{x})
\right]+o(h),\\
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\mathbf{Z}_h\mathbf{Z}_h^\top
\mid\mathbf{X}_t=\mathbf{x}
\right]
&=
g^2(t)hI+o(h).
\end{aligned}
\]
Under the stated smoothness and transition-density assumptions, the controlled remainders
justify taking these conditional moments. The standard local-characteristics
characterization then identifies the continuous Markov diffusion with these local mean and
covariance rates. Therefore the increasing-time reversed process satisfies
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_\tau
=\left[
-f(\mathbf{Y}_\tau,T-\tau)
+g^2(T-\tau)
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\log p_{T-\tau}(\mathbf{Y}_\tau)
\right]\mathrm{d}\tau
+g(T-\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_\tau,
\]
where $\mathbf{B}_\tau$ is Brownian motion for the increasing-time filtration of
$\mathbf{Y}$. Returning to the original coordinate, $\mathrm{d}\tau=-\mathrm{d}t$, gives
the stated backward-time SDE. Finally, substitute
$f(\mathbf{x},t)=-\beta(t)\mathbf{x}/2$ and $g^2(t)=\beta(t)$ to obtain the VP formula.
\end{proof}

\comment{Superseded generator-based proof retained only in source history.}
\[
\mathbb{P}\!\left(
\mathbf{X}_{t-h}\in\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}
\mid\mathbf{X}_t=\mathbf{x}
\right)
=\frac{
p_{t-h}(\mathbf{y})p_{t-h,t}(\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{y})}
{p_t(\mathbf{x})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.
\]
To see explicitly what this identity gives, write
\[
(\mathcal{R}_h\varphi)(\mathbf{x})
:=
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\varphi(\mathbf{Y}_{\tau+h})
\mid\mathbf{Y}_\tau=\mathbf{x}
\right].
\]
Let $\mathsf{P}_{t-h,t}^*$ denote the forward operator that carries a density at time
$t-h$ to a density at time $t$. Multiplying the Bayes formula by $p_t(\mathbf{x})$ gives
\[
p_t\,\mathcal{R}_h\varphi
=\mathsf{P}_{t-h,t}^*\bigl(p_{t-h}\varphi\bigr).
\]
For small $h$, the definitions of the generator and the density derivative give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathsf{P}_{t-h,t}^*
\bigl(p_{t-h}\varphi\bigr)
&=
\bigl(I+h\mathcal{L}_t^*+o(h)\bigr)
\bigl(p_t\varphi-h\varphi\,\partial_t p_t+o(h)\bigr)\\
&=
p_t\varphi
+h\left[
\mathcal{L}_t^*(p_t\varphi)
-\varphi\,\mathcal{L}_t^*p_t
\right]+o(h).
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_\tau\varphi)(\mathbf{x})
=\frac{
\mathcal{L}_t^*(p_t\varphi)(\mathbf{x})
-\varphi(\mathbf{x})\mathcal{L}_t^*p_t(\mathbf{x})}
{p_t(\mathbf{x})},
\qquad
t=T-\tau.
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{L}_t^*(p_t\varphi)-\varphi\mathcal{L}_t^*p_t
&=
-p_t f\cdot\nabla\varphi
+\frac{g^2(t)}{2}
\left[\Delta(p_t\varphi)-\varphi\Delta p_t\right]\\
&=
p_t\left[
\bigl(-f+g^2(t)\nabla\log p_t\bigr)\cdot\nabla\varphi

\right].
\end{aligned}

$\Delta(p_t\varphi)=p_t\Delta\varphi
+2\nabla p_t\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi\Delta p_t$. Thus there exists a Brownian

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_\tau
=\left[
-f(\mathbf{Y}_\tau,T-\tau)
+g^2(T-\tau)\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}

The symbol $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_t$ denotes a Brownian motion appropriate to this
reverse-time process. The apparent sign issue is entirely a time-direction convention:
generation advances from noisy time $T$ to clean time $0$, so its numerical increments
$\Delta t$ are negative.

If one prefers a clock that increases during generation, set
\[
\tau:=T-t,
\qquad
\mathbf{Y}_\tau:=\mathbf{X}_{T-\tau}.
\]
Then $\tau$ increases from $0$ to $T$, and the equivalent SDE is
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}_\tau
=\left[
\frac12\beta(T-\tau)\mathbf{Y}_\tau
+\beta(T-\tau)
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\log p_{T-\tau}(\mathbf{Y}_\tau)
\right]\mathrm{d}\tau
+\sqrt{\beta(T-\tau)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_\tau.
\]
This is the same reverse dynamics in the ordinary forward numerical direction. It says:
the learned score term pushes a noise sample toward regions that look like noised data,
while the Brownian term supplies the stochasticity required by the reverse diffusion.

\paragraph{Why a neural score model is needed.}
To generate, one would initialize $\mathbf{X}_T$ from an approximately standard Gaussian
distribution and simulate the reverse SDE. The obstacle is that the drift needs
$\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)$, but the marginal density $p_t$ is unknown:
it averages the unknown data distribution over all clean samples. We therefore use a
time-conditioned neural vector field
\[
s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
\approx
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t).
\]
The ideal population loss is
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SM}}(\theta)
:=
\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[
\lambda(t)\,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_t\sim p_t}
\left\|
s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\|_2^2
\right].
\]
The outer expectation chooses a noise time; $\lambda(t)\geq0$ says how much errors at
that time matter; the inner expectation averages noisy samples at that time. Part (b)
explains why this unavailable target can be replaced by an explicit conditional target,
and part (c) gives the concrete loss that is actually optimized.

\begin{note}[What to state for four points]
Write the reverse SDE, substitute the VP coefficients
$f(\mathbf{x},t)=-\beta(t)\mathbf{x}/2$ and $g^2(t)=\beta(t)$, and then state the
causal chain:
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\text{reverse sampler needs the marginal score}\\
&\qquad\longrightarrow\ \text{the marginal score is unknown}\\
&\qquad\longrightarrow\ \text{learn }s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)\text{ by score matching}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The reverse-time formula is a standard theorem; the examinable point here is recognizing
which term is unknown and why it must be learned.
\end{note}

\paragraph{Compact exam-ready version.}
State the reverse-time SDE with the convention $t:T\to 0$, substitute $f(x,t)=-\beta(t)x/2$ and $g^2(t)=\beta(t)$, and explain that the unknown marginal score is replaced by a time-conditioned neural approximation $s_\theta(x_t,t)$ trained by weighted score matching.

For a forward SDE

\[
\mathrm{d}x_t = f(x_t,t)\,\mathrm{d}t+g(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_t,
\]

the associated reverse-time SDE, with time run from $T$ down to $0$, is

\[
\mathrm{d}x_t
= \left[f(x_t,t)-g^2(t)\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)\right]\mathrm{d}t
+g(t)\,\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_t.
\]

Here $\overline{W}_t$ is a Wiener process for the reverse-time dynamics. Substituting the VP coefficients gives

\[
\mathrm{d}x_t
= \left[-\frac12\beta(t)x_t-\beta(t)\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)\right]\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{W}_t,
\qquad t:T\to 0.
\]

If the score $\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)$ were known, starting from a simple terminal Gaussian $x_T$ and numerically simulating this SDE backward would produce samples close to the data distribution $p_0$. Strictly speaking, $p_T=\mathcal{N}(0,I)$ only in the idealized limit $\alpha(T)=0$; a practical noise schedule chooses $T$ so that $\alpha(T)\approx 0$. The drift, however, contains the unknown marginal score. We therefore introduce a neural approximation $s_\theta(x_t,t)$ and fit it using the weighted score-matching objective

\[
\min_\theta\;
\mathbb{E}_t\left[
\lambda(t)\,
\mathbb{E}_{x_t\sim p_t}
\left\|s_\theta(x_t,t)-\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)\right\|_2^2
\right].
\]

At an optimum with sufficient model capacity, the regression function is the true score. Replacing the score in the reverse SDE by $s_\theta$ yields the learned generative dynamics.

\paragraph{(b) Why the denoising target is equivalent --- 6 points}

\paragraph{Detailed derivation in the notation of the question.}
Fix a time $t$. The random pair in this part is generated in two stages:
\[
\mathbf{x}_0\sim p_0,
\qquad
\mathbf{x}_t\sim p_{t\mid 0}(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_0).
\]
Thus $\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t\in\mathbb{R}^d$ are jointly random. The network input is
the noisy vector $\mathbf{x}_t$, whereas the clean vector $\mathbf{x}_0$ is available
during training only because we began from a training sample.

There are two $\mathbb{R}^d$-valued score vectors:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\mathbf{x}_t)
&:=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t),
&&\text{the unknown marginal score,}\\
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
&:=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log
p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0),
&&\text{the conditional score available from the forward kernel.}
\end{aligned}
\]
The statement to prove compares the two losses
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\theta)
&:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_t\sim p_t}
\left\|
s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\|_2^2,\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\theta)
&:=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)}
\left\|
s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\|_2^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
The first loss cannot be evaluated directly because $p_t$ is unknown; the second can be
estimated from a clean sample and a known forward-noising kernel.
Assume throughout this derivation that the displayed score vectors have finite second
moments, so that all squared losses and conditional expectations below are well-defined.

\paragraph{Step 1: the conditional score averages to the marginal score.}
Start from the marginalization identity
\[
p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)
=\int
p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
p_0(\mathbf{x}_0)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_0.
\]
Assume that differentiation may pass through the integral and that
$p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)>0$. Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)
&=
\frac{\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)}
{p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)}\\
&=
\frac{1}{p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)}
\int
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}
p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
p_0(\mathbf{x}_0)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_0\\
&=
\int
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}
\log p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
\frac{
p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
p_0(\mathbf{x}_0)}
{p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)}
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_0\\
&=
\int
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
p_{0\mid t}(\mathbf{x}_0\mid\mathbf{x}_t)
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}_0\\
&=
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\mid\mathbf{x}_t
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]
The third line uses
$\nabla p=p\,\nabla\log p$, and the fourth uses Bayes' rule. This is the key
identity: after observing $\mathbf{x}_t$, the inaccessible marginal score is the
conditional mean of the accessible conditional score.

\paragraph{Step 2: expand the conditional loss around that conditional mean.}
Define
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf{u}_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t)
&:=
s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\mathbf{x}_t),\\
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
&:=
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\mathbf{x}_t)
-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t).
\end{aligned}
\]
Then
\[
s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
=\mathbf{u}_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t)
+\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t).
\]
Applying $\|\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}\|_2^2
=\|\mathbf{u}\|_2^2
+2\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle
+\|\mathbf{v}\|_2^2$ gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\theta)
&=
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\theta)
+2\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\left\langle
\mathbf{u}_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t),
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\rangle
\right]\\
&\quad+
\mathbb{E}\left\|
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\|_2^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
The cross term is zero, with no independence assumption, because the tower property gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\left\langle
\mathbf{u}_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t),
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\rangle
\right]
&=
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_t}\!\left[
\left\langle
\mathbf{u}_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t),
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\mid\mathbf{x}_t
\right]
\right\rangle
\right]\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Indeed, the final conditional expectation is zero by Step 1. Hence
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\theta)
=
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\theta)
+D_t,
\qquad
D_t:=
\mathbb{E}\left\|
\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\mathbf{x}_t)
-\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)
\right\|_2^2.
\]
Why is $D_t$ independent of $\theta$? Fix $t$. The random pair
$(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)$ is sampled from the fixed data distribution $p_0$ and the
fixed forward kernel $p_{t\mid0}$. The two score functions
$\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}$ and $\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}$ are determined entirely by
those distributions. In contrast, $\theta$ occurs only in the neural network
$s_\theta$. Since no $s_\theta$ occurs anywhere in the displayed definition of $D_t$, the
same nonnegative number $D_t$ is obtained for every choice of $\theta$. Equivalently,
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\theta)
=
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\theta)+C_t,
\qquad
C_t:=-D_t.
\]
Therefore the two losses have the same minimizers.

\begin{note}[Why the identity is not magic]
Conditional expectation is the least-squares projection onto functions of the observed
variable $\mathbf{x}_t$. Here
$\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{marg}}(\mathbf{x}_t)$ is precisely the best
$\mathbf{x}_t$-only prediction of the random target
$\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{cond}}(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)$. The residual
$\mathbf{v}$ is orthogonal in $L^2$ to every function of $\mathbf{x}_t$, including the
network error $\mathbf{u}_\theta$. The vanished cross term is exactly this orthogonality.
\end{note}

\paragraph{Compact proof in auxiliary notation.}
Differentiate the marginalization identity to show that the marginal score is the conditional expectation of the conditional score. Expanding the conditional objective around this conditional mean makes the cross term vanish, leaving a constant gap independent of $\theta$; therefore both objectives have the same minimizers.

Fix a time $t$ and abbreviate

\[
a(x_t):=\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t),
\qquad
q(x_0,x_t):=\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0).
\]

First, differentiate the marginalization identity. Assuming the usual regularity that permits differentiation under the integral sign,

\begin{align*}
\nabla_{x_t}p_t(x_t)
&= \int \nabla_{x_t}p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)p_0(x_0)\,\mathrm{d}x_0 \\
&= \int p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
   \nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)p_0(x_0)\,\mathrm{d}x_0.
\end{align*}

Dividing by $p_t(x_t)$ gives the conditional-score identity

\[
a(x_t)
= \int q(x_0,x_t)p(x_0\mid x_t)\,\mathrm{d}x_0
= \mathbb{E}\left[q(x_0,x_t)\mid x_t\right].
\]

Now expand the conditional-score objective around its conditional mean:

\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_{(x_0,x_t)}\left\|s_\theta(x_t,t)-q(x_0,x_t)\right\|_2^2
&= \mathbb{E}_{(x_0,x_t)}
   \left\|s_\theta(x_t,t)-a(x_t)+a(x_t)-q(x_0,x_t)\right\|_2^2 \\
&= \mathbb{E}_{x_t}\left\|s_\theta(x_t,t)-a(x_t)\right\|_2^2 \\
&\quad + \mathbb{E}_{(x_0,x_t)}\left\|a(x_t)-q(x_0,x_t)\right\|_2^2.
\end{align*}

The cross term vanishes because $s_\theta(x_t,t)-a(x_t)$ is a function of $x_t$ alone, whereas

\[
\mathbb{E}\left[a(x_t)-q(x_0,x_t)\mid x_t\right]=0.
\]

Rearranging proves the requested statement:

\[
\mathbb{E}_{x_t}\left\|s_\theta(x_t,t)-\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)\right\|_2^2
= \mathbb{E}_{(x_0,x_t)}\left\|s_\theta(x_t,t)-\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)\right\|_2^2+C,
\]

where

\[
C=-\mathbb{E}_{(x_0,x_t)}
\left\|\nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t)-\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)\right\|_2^2.
\]

The sign of $C$ is not important; what matters is that it does not depend on $\theta$. Therefore both objectives have exactly the same minimizers.

\paragraph{(c) VP transition density and final training loss --- 5 points}

\paragraph{Detailed derivation in the notation of the question.}
We now keep a clean data vector $\mathbf{x}_0$ fixed and ask for the conditional law of
the random vector $\mathbf{X}_t$. Start with the VP-SDE
\[
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_t
=-\frac12\beta(t)\mathbf{X}_t\,\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_t.
\]
Define the accumulated noise rate and signal coefficient by
\[
B(t):=\int_0^t\beta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,
\qquad
\alpha(t):=\exp\!\left(-\frac12B(t)\right).
\]
Since
\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\alpha(t)^{-1}
=\frac12\beta(t)\alpha(t)^{-1},
\]
the deterministic scalar $\alpha(t)^{-1}$ is an integrating factor. Applying the
It\^o product rule, with no quadratic-covariation term because the integrating factor is
deterministic, gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{d}\bigl(\alpha(t)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_t\bigr)
&=
\alpha(t)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{X}_t
+\mathbf{X}_t\,\mathrm{d}\bigl(\alpha(t)^{-1}\bigr)\\
&=
\alpha(t)^{-1}
\left[
-\frac12\beta(t)\mathbf{X}_t\,\mathrm{d}t
+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_t
\right]
+\frac12\beta(t)\alpha(t)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_t\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=
\alpha(t)^{-1}\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_t.
\end{aligned}
\]
The drift terms cancel. Integrating from $0$ to $t$ and using
$\mathbf{X}_0=\mathbf{x}_0$ yields
\[
\mathbf{X}_t
=\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0
+\alpha(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_s.
\]

\paragraph{Why this produces a Gaussian transition.}
The stochastic integral above has mean zero and is Gaussian because its integrand is
deterministic. Its covariance follows from It\^o isometry:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Cov}\left(
\alpha(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_s
\right)
&=
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I\\
&=
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t
\beta(s)e^{B(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I\\
&=
\alpha^2(t)\bigl(e^{B(t)}-1\bigr)I\\
&=
\bigl(1-\alpha^2(t)\bigr)I.
\end{aligned}
\]
In the third line, use
$(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}s)e^{B(s)}=\beta(s)e^{B(s)}$. Define
\[
\sigma^2(t):=1-\alpha^2(t).
\]
We always take
\[
\sigma(t):=\sqrt{1-\alpha^2(t)}\geq0.
\]
Conditioned on $\mathbf{X}_0=\mathbf{x}_0$, the mean is
$\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0$ and the covariance is $\sigma^2(t)I$. Mean and covariance
alone do not name a law: many distributions share those two moments. The Gaussian
conclusion uses a stronger fact about the Wiener integral. Write
\[
\mathbf{Z}_t
=
\alpha(t)\int_0^t
\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_s
\approx
\sum_k h(s_k)\Delta\mathbf{W}_k,
\qquad
h(s)
:=
\alpha(t)\frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}.
\]
Each Brownian increment $\Delta\mathbf{W}_k$ is Gaussian. The coefficients $h(s_k)$
are nonrandom, so every finite-sum approximant is a linear image of Gaussians, hence
Gaussian. An $L^2$ limit of Gaussian random vectors is Gaussian (characteristic
functions converge to those of $\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2(t)I)$). The integrand does
not depend on $\mathbf{x}_0$, so $\mathbf{Z}_t$ is independent of
$\mathbf{X}_0$. Therefore
\[
\mathbf{X}_t
=
\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0+\mathbf{Z}_t
\]
is exactly Gaussian, not merely matched in the first two moments, and
\[
p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
=\mathcal{N}\left(
\mathbf{x}_t;
\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0,
\sigma^2(t)I
\right).
\]
Equivalently, to sample from this conditional distribution directly, draw
$\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},I)$ and set
\[
\mathbf{x}_t
=\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0+\sigma(t)\boldsymbol{\epsilon}.
\]
This direct formula is exact in distribution; no small-step forward SDE simulation is
needed during training.

\paragraph{From the conditional Gaussian to the trainable target.}
The log density of the conditional Gaussian, up to terms independent of
$\mathbf{x}_t$, is
\[
\log p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
=-\frac{
\left\|
\mathbf{x}_t-\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0
\right\|_2^2}
{2\sigma^2(t)}
+\text{constant}.
\]
Differentiating with respect to the noisy variable gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}
\log p_{t\mid 0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
&=
-\frac{
\mathbf{x}_t-\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0}
{\sigma^2(t)}\\
&=
-\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{\sigma(t)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The second line uses
$\mathbf{x}_t-\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0
=\sigma(t)\boldsymbol{\epsilon}$. Thus the regression target is not
$\boldsymbol{\epsilon}$ itself: for a score network it is the vector
$-\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/\sigma(t)$.

Substituting this target into the conditional loss from part (b) gives the loss that can
be estimated from data:
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DSM}}(\theta)
:=
\mathbb{E}_{\substack{
t\sim\rho,\,\mathbf{x}_0\sim p_0,\\
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},I)}}
\left[
\lambda(t)
\left\|
s_\theta\bigl(
\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0+\sigma(t)\boldsymbol{\epsilon},
t
\bigr)
+\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{\sigma(t)}
\right\|_2^2
\right],
\]
where $\rho$ is the chosen distribution of training times. The plus sign inside the norm
is correct:
\[
s_\theta-\left(-\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{\sigma(t)}\right)
=s_\theta+\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{\sigma(t)}.
\]

\begin{note}[Where does the DSM loss come from?]
DSM means \emph{denoising score matching}. It is a standard name for this training
construction, but it is important to separate a modeling choice from what is derived in
this problem.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Definition.} The score of a density $q(\mathbf{x})$ is, by definition,
    $\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\log q(\mathbf{x})$. Thus the reverse-time sampler is motivated by
    its need for the marginal score $\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)$.

    \item \textbf{Modeling choice.} We choose to fit the neural vector field
    $s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)$ to that score by least-squares regression:
    \[
    \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SM},t}(\theta)
    :=
    \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_t\sim p_t}
    \left\|
    s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
    -\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}_t)
    \right\|_2^2.
    \]
    This squared loss is not forced by the VP-SDE; it is the usual regression objective.
    It is nonnegative and, for a sufficiently expressive model, its population minimum is
    attained when the network equals the target score almost surely. The time weight
    $\lambda(t)$ and sampling law $\rho$ are also user-chosen design choices.

    \item \textbf{Derivation in this problem.} The target in the preceding loss is unknown
    because $p_t$ is unknown. Part (b) proves that replacing it with the conditional score
    changes the objective only by a constant independent of $\theta$:
    \[
    \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SM},t}(\theta)
    =
    \mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x}_0,\mathbf{x}_t)}
    \left\|
    s_\theta(\mathbf{x}_t,t)
    -\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}
    \log p_{t\mid0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
    \right\|_2^2
    +C_t.
    \]
    Part (c) derives from the VP-SDE that
    \[
    \mathbf{x}_t
    =\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0+\sigma(t)\boldsymbol{\epsilon},
    \qquad
    \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log
    p_{t\mid0}(\mathbf{x}_t\mid\mathbf{x}_0)
    =-\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{\sigma(t)}.
    \]
    Substituting this explicit conditional score gives exactly the DSM loss above.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore the reverse SDE explains \emph{why} a score is useful for generation, but it is
not needed to derive the loss algebra. Once the least-squares score-matching objective is
chosen, the required chain is
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\text{marginal score matching}\\
&\qquad\longrightarrow\ \text{conditional-score surrogate from (b)}\\
&\qquad\longrightarrow\ \text{explicit Gaussian target from (c)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
For this derivation, learn the definition of a score, least-squares regression,
differentiating a Gaussian log density, and the conditional-expectation argument in part
(b). You do not need to memorize DSM as an unexplained formula.
\end{note}

In a mini-batch implementation, sample $\mathbf{x}_0$, $t$, and
$\boldsymbol{\epsilon}$; form $\mathbf{x}_t$ using the direct formula; evaluate the
network; and take an SGD step. The endpoint $t=0$ is not sampled because
$\sigma(0)=0$ makes the score target singular.

\begin{note}[What to state for five points]
Write the three-line chain
\[
\text{VP-SDE}
\;\longrightarrow\;
\mathbf{x}_t=\alpha(t)\mathbf{x}_0+\sigma(t)\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
\;\longrightarrow\;
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}
=-\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/\sigma(t),
\]
then substitute this score into the conditional denoising loss. Define the distributions
of $\mathbf{x}_0$ and $\boldsymbol{\epsilon}$, and explain the plus sign in the final
norm. This is enough for a concise answer once the derivation is understood.
\end{note}

\paragraph{Compact exam-ready version.}
Solve the linear VP-SDE to obtain $x_t=\alpha(t)x_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$ and $\sigma^2(t)=1-\alpha^2(t)$. The conditional Gaussian score is $-\varepsilon/\sigma(t)$; substituting it gives the computable denoising score-matching loss.

Define

\[
\alpha(t):=\exp\left(-\frac12\int_0^t\beta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right).
\]

Using the integrating factor $\alpha(t)^{-1}$ to solve the linear VP-SDE gives

\[
x_t
= \alpha(t)x_0
+ \alpha(t)\int_0^t \frac{\sqrt{\beta(s)}}{\alpha(s)}\,\mathrm{d}W_s.
\]

The stochastic integral is Gaussian with mean zero. Its covariance is

\begin{align*}
\alpha^2(t)\int_0^t\frac{\beta(s)}{\alpha^2(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,I
&= \left(1-\alpha^2(t)\right)I.
\end{align*}

Set

\[
\sigma^2(t):=1-\alpha^2(t).
\]

Consequently,

\[
p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
= \mathcal{N}\left(x_t;\alpha(t)x_0,\sigma^2(t)I\right),
\]

or, equivalently,

\[
x_t=\alpha(t)x_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon,
\qquad
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I).
\]

Differentiating the log density of this Gaussian with respect to $x_t$ yields

\[
\nabla_{x_t}\log p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)
=-\frac{x_t-\alpha(t)x_0}{\sigma^2(t)}
=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma(t)}.
\]

Substitution into the denoising score-matching objective gives the final practical loss:

\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DSM}}(\theta)
= \mathbb{E}_{\substack{t,\,x_0\sim p_0,\\ \varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}}
\left[
\lambda(t)
\left\|
s_\theta\bigl(\alpha(t)x_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon,t\bigr)
+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma(t)}
\right\|_2^2
\right].
\]

This is computable from clean training data without evaluating either $p_0$ or $p_t$. In practice, sample a mini-batch of $x_0$, sample times $t\in(0,T]$ and Gaussian noises $\varepsilon$, form the perturbed inputs $x_t$, evaluate the network, and take one SGD step on this loss. The endpoint $t=0$ is excluded because $\sigma(0)=0$ makes the displayed score target singular; implementations commonly sample from $[\delta,T]$ for a small $\delta>0$.

\paragraph{Equivalent noise-prediction parameterization.}
Many diffusion implementations predict the noise itself. If

\[
s_\theta(x_t,t)=-\frac{\varepsilon_\theta(x_t,t)}{\sigma(t)},
\]

then the loss becomes

\[
\mathbb{E}_{t,x_0,\varepsilon}
\left[
\underbrace{\frac{\lambda(t)}{\sigma^2(t)}}_{\widetilde{\lambda}(t)}
\left\|\varepsilon_\theta(x_t,t)-\varepsilon\right\|_2^2
\right].
\]

Thus the familiar noise-prediction loss and score matching are not competing ideas; they are two parameterizations of the same conditional Gaussian target.

\subsubsection{Training and sampling: the engineering bridge}

The mathematical construction maps directly to the usual diffusion-model workflow:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Draw a mini-batch of clean examples $x_0$ from the dataset.
    \item Draw $t$ from the prescribed time distribution and $\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$.
    \item Form $x_t=\alpha(t)x_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon$.
    \item Feed both $x_t$ and $t$ to the network, then optimize $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DSM}}(\theta)$ by SGD.
    \item To generate, initialize $x_T$ from the terminal Gaussian and numerically solve the learned reverse-time SDE from $T$ to $0$.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{What is computed in practice.}
The forward SDE is designed by the modeler; it is not learned from the training data.
The learned object is the time-conditioned vector field

\[
(x,t)\longmapsto s_\theta(x,t)\approx\nabla_x\log p_t(x).
\]

At training time, the complete procedure is:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Draw a clean sample $x_0\sim p_{\mathrm{data}}$.
    \item Draw a time $t$ from a prescribed distribution on $(0,T]$.
    \item Draw $\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$.
    \item Form $x_t=\alpha(t)x_0+\sigma(t)\varepsilon$ using the exact transition formula.
    \item Use the known conditional score $-\varepsilon/\sigma(t)$ as the regression target.
    \item Update the parameters of $s_\theta$ by stochastic gradient descent.
\end{enumerate}

At sampling time, choose $T$ sufficiently large that $\alpha(T)\approx0$, initialize
$x_T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$, replace the true score in the reverse SDE by
$s_\theta(x_t,t)$, and numerically integrate from $T$ down to $0$. For example, after
reparameterizing time by $\tau=T-t$ and writing $Y_\tau=X_{T-\tau}$, Euler--Maruyama
gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
Y_{\tau+\Delta\tau}
\approx{}&Y_\tau+
\left[
\frac12\beta(t)Y_\tau+\beta(t)s_\theta(Y_\tau,t)
\right]\Delta\tau \\
&+\sqrt{\beta(t)\Delta\tau}\,z,
\qquad
z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I),
\qquad
t=T-\tau.
\end{aligned}
\]

This also explains why diffusion sampling is often slower than a single feed-forward
generation step: each time step generally requires another network evaluation.

\paragraph{Probability-flow ODE and related SDE families.}
The same marginal densities $p_t$ are also generated by the deterministic probability-flow
ODE

\[
\mathrm{d}X_t
=\left[f(X_t,t)-\frac12g^2(t)\nabla_x\log p_t(X_t)\right]\mathrm{d}t.
\]

It is deterministic conditional on its initial value, but random samples are still obtained
because the terminal initial value is random. This gives a continuous-time perspective on
deterministic samplers such as DDIM-type methods. The VP-SDE is not the only forward
choice: variance-exploding (VE) SDEs increase the noise scale through diffusion, while
sub-VP SDEs modify the variance schedule. These families share the same conceptual
pipeline:

\[
\begin{gathered}
\text{design a forward perturbation}
\;\longrightarrow\;
\text{learn the score at each noise level} \\
\longrightarrow\;
\text{solve reverse dynamics}.
\end{gathered}
\]

\paragraph{Applications.}
Once a score model has been learned, the same framework can be used for unconditional
generation of images, audio, video, molecules, or physical configurations; conditional
generation from text, class labels, sketches, or measurements; and restoration tasks such
as super-resolution, deblurring, and inpainting. In inverse problems with an observation
model such as

\[
y=Ax+\eta,
\]

the diffusion model supplies a learned prior while the reconstruction procedure also
enforces consistency with the observation. Conditional generation replaces the marginal
score by

\[
\nabla_x\log p_t(x\mid c),
\]

where $c$ may be a text condition, a class, or an observed signal.

\subsubsection{Common mistakes}

\begin{itemize}
    \item Confusing $p_t(x_t)$ with $p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)$. Only the latter is analytically known from a fixed clean sample.
    \item Writing the reverse drift with the wrong sign without stating whether time is running upward or downward.
    \item Claiming that $C$ in part (b) is positive. Its sign depends on which side of the identity it is placed on; only its independence from $\theta$ matters.
    \item Omitting $t$ from the network input, even though the target scale depends on $\sigma(t)$.
    \item Treating score prediction and noise prediction as unrelated objectives.
    \item Forgetting that the score is vector-valued in $\mathbb{R}^d$, so the loss uses a squared Euclidean norm.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Exam-ready compression}

For a timed answer, retain the following proof skeleton: state the reverse-SDE formula and specialize it to $f(x,t)=-\beta(t)x/2$, $g^2(t)=\beta(t)$; prove $\nabla\log p_t=\mathbb{E}[\nabla\log p_{t\mid 0}\mid x_t]$ by differentiating the marginal density; use the conditional-expectation orthogonality to obtain the constant-gap loss identity; then state

\[
p_{t\mid 0}(x_t\mid x_0)=\mathcal{N}\left(\alpha(t)x_0,\left(1-\alpha^2(t)\right)I\right),
\]

and substitute its Gaussian score into the denoising score-matching loss.

\subsubsection{Scope of the present problem}

The material above gives the continuous-time score-based diffusion background needed to
understand the three exam parts. The following topics remain outside the derivation
requested by this problem:

\begin{itemize}
    \item discrete-time DDPM derivations, including the finite-step variational lower bound;
    \item detailed numerical solver and sampler comparisons;
    \item the architecture of U-Nets, time embeddings, classifier-free guidance, or latent diffusion;
    \item comparisons among diffusion models, VAEs, GANs, and other generative-model families;
    \item reinforcement-learning foundations such as Markov decision processes, Bellman equations, policy gradients, or Q-learning;
    \item machine-learning security, which should be studied separately through adversarial examples, poisoning and backdoors, privacy attacks, model extraction, and robustness defenses.
\end{itemize}

These are coverage gaps, not prerequisites for the 15-point answer above. They should be collected in a later extension section so that the exam-specific argument remains readable and the boundary between directly tested and supplementary material stays explicit.

\input{dl-universal-review}

\subsection{Reinforcement Learning: Zero-Background Exam Preparation Supplement}
\label{sec:rl-supplement}

\paragraph{Syllabus target and how to use this supplement.}
The official syllabus for this part explicitly names three reinforcement-learning topics:
\jump{unit:1}{Bellman equations}, \jump{unit:2}{Q-learning}, and
\jump{unit:3}{policy gradients}. The Spring 2026 paper confirms that
these objects may be tested together with \jump{unit:1}{policy iteration},
\jump{def:actor-critic}{actor--critic} methods, and
\jump{unit:4}{preference alignment}. This supplement is therefore a prerequisite-ordered route from a
one-step decision problem to an exam-ready answer. It is supplementary material: it does
not alter either of the two Spring 2025 questions above.

\subsubsection{Unit 0: the change from supervised learning to sequential decisions}
\label{unit:0}

In supervised learning, a training example supplies an input $x$ and a target $y$,\iffalse The
essential distinction is what information the data provide. \emph{Supervised learning} receives
labeled pairs $(x_i,y_i)$ and learns to predict the externally supplied target $y$ from $x$,
for example a class label, a real-valued response, or the next word in a labeled training
objective. \emph{Unsupervised learning} receives only inputs $(x_i)$, with no task label
attached to each example; it seeks structure in their distribution, such as clusters,
low-dimensional representations, or a density/generative model $p(x)$. It does \emph{not}
mean ``learning nothing'': the learning signal comes from properties of the inputs
themselves. \emph{Semi-supervised learning} combines a small labeled set
$(x_i,y_i)$ with a larger unlabeled set $(x_j)$, using the latter to improve the prediction
task under assumptions linking data geometry and labels.

\par\smallskip
\emph{Generative} and \emph{discriminative} describe what a model represents, rather than
whether labels are supplied. A \emph{discriminative model} directly predicts a target from
an input, for example by modeling the conditional probability $p(y\mid x)$ or by learning
a decision function $x\mapsto\widehat y$. Logistic regression and a neural image
classifier are standard examples. It is designed to answer ``which label should this
input receive?'', but it does not normally describe how an input $x$ itself is generated.
A \emph{generative model} models a joint distribution $p(x,y)$, equivalently
$p(x\mid y)p(y)$, or in an unlabeled setting a data distribution $p(x)$. It can assign
probabilities to inputs and, in principle, sample new inputs: first sample a label
$y\sim p(y)$ and then sample $x\sim p(x\mid y)$, or simply sample $x\sim p(x)$ when there
is no label. A generative classifier can still predict labels through Bayes' rule
\[
p(y\mid x)=\frac{p(x\mid y)p(y)}{\sum_{y'}p(x\mid y')p(y')}.
\]
Thus a supervised classifier may be discriminative or generative; the two classifications
are a different axis from supervised, unsupervised, and semi-supervised learning. An
unconditional diffusion model learns $p(x)$ and is therefore an unsupervised generative
model. A conditional diffusion model can additionally use a supplied label or text
condition, so it can be trained with supervised information while remaining generative.}\fi
and the
loss can be evaluated immediately after a prediction. In reinforcement learning (RL), an
agent repeatedly acts in an environment. An action can change the next state, and its
consequences can remain relevant for many later steps. The data distribution is therefore
generated by the current policy rather than supplied as a fixed labeled data set.

\paragraph{Learning paradigms and modeling targets.}
The essential distinction between supervised, unsupervised, and semi-supervised learning is
what information the data provide. \emph{Supervised learning} receives labeled pairs
$(x_i,y_i)$ and learns to predict the externally supplied target $y$ from $x$, for example
a class label, a real-valued response, or the next word in a labeled training objective.
\emph{Unsupervised learning} receives only inputs $(x_i)$, with no task label attached to
each example; it seeks structure in their distribution, such as clusters,
low-dimensional representations, or a density/generative model $p(x)$. It does \emph{not}
mean ``learning nothing'': the learning signal comes from properties of the inputs
themselves. \emph{Semi-supervised learning} combines a small labeled set $(x_i,y_i)$ with
a larger unlabeled set $(x_j)$, using the latter to improve the prediction task under
assumptions linking data geometry and labels.

Generative and discriminative modeling describe a different axis: what a model represents,
rather than whether labels are supplied. A \emph{discriminative model} directly predicts a
target from an input, for example by modeling $p(y\mid x)$ or learning a decision function
$x\mapsto\widehat y$. Logistic regression and a neural image classifier are standard
examples. It is designed to answer ``which label should this input receive?'', but it does
not normally describe how an input $x$ itself is generated. A \emph{generative model}
models a joint distribution $p(x,y)$, equivalently $p(x\mid y)p(y)$, or in an unlabeled
setting a data distribution $p(x)$. It can assign probabilities to inputs and, in
principle, sample new inputs: sample $y\sim p(y)$ and then $x\sim p(x\mid y)$, or sample
$x\sim p(x)$ when there is no label. A generative classifier can still predict labels
through Bayes' rule
\[
p(y\mid x)=\frac{p(x\mid y)p(y)}{\sum_{y'}p(x\mid y')p(y')}.
\]
Thus a supervised classifier may be discriminative or generative. An unconditional
diffusion model learns $p(x)$ and is therefore an unsupervised generative model. A
conditional diffusion model can use a supplied label or text condition, so it can be
trained with supervised information while remaining generative.

\begin{example}[One image collection, viewed in four ways]
Suppose a laboratory has $100{,}000$ images of cats and dogs. Each image is denoted by
$x$, and its species label, when available, is $y\in\{\text{cat},\text{dog}\}$.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Supervised discriminative classification.} If every image has a species
    label, train a classifier $q_\theta(y\mid x)$ by cross-entropy. Given a new photograph,
    it outputs a probability such as $q_\theta(\text{cat}\mid x)=0.97$. The task is
    prediction of $y$ from $x$; this model is not designed to produce a new cat image.

    \item \textbf{Supervised generative classification.} With the same labeled images, one
    may instead learn\footnote{Here \emph{learn} means choosing the parameter $\theta$ of a
    model family $q_\theta$ from the training data, rather than assuming that the true
    distributions are already known. For labeled examples $(x_i,y_i)$, a standard choice is
    maximum likelihood: choose $\theta$ to make the observed pairs probable, equivalently to
    minimize the average negative log-likelihood
    \[
    -\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n
    \bigl[\log q_\theta(x_i\mid y_i)+\log q_\theta(y_i)\bigr].
    \]
    After fitting, $q_\theta(x\mid\text{cat})$ is an approximation to the distribution of
    cat images in the population, and similarly for dogs. Thus it can assign a likelihood to
    an image or be sampled to generate a new one.} $q_\theta(x\mid\text{cat})$,
    $q_\theta(x\mid\text{dog})$, and the
    class probabilities $q_\theta(y)$.\footnote{These quantities can be fitted
    \emph{simultaneously} because they are parts of one joint model
    \[
    q_\theta(x,y)=q_\theta(x\mid y)q_\theta(y).
    \]
    Each labeled training pair $(x_i,y_i)$ contributes one loss
    \[
    -\log q_\theta(x_i\mid y_i)-\log q_\theta(y_i),
    \]
    and minimizing the sum of these losses updates all parameters that occur in it. In a
    simple implementation, one can fit a separate conditional density model to cat images
    and to dog images, while estimating $q_\theta(y)$ by the observed class frequencies.
    In a modern conditional generative model, one normally uses a single parameter-sharing
    network $q_\theta(x\mid y)$: the label $y$ is an additional input, and training examples
    with both labels update the same shared $\theta$. Thus ``learn together'' does not require
    the three distributions to be identical; it means that their parameters are chosen by one
    joint likelihood objective.} The classifier is then obtained through
    \[
    q_\theta(y\mid x)
    =\frac{q_\theta(x\mid y)q_\theta(y)}
    {\sum_{y'}q_\theta(x\mid y')q_\theta(y')}.
    \]
    This model can both classify an image and, in principle,
    \hyperref[ex:supervised-cat-generation]{generate an image conditional on the request
    ``cat''}. It uses labels, so it is supervised, but its modeling target is
    generative rather than discriminative.

    \item \textbf{Unsupervised generative learning.} If the laboratory removes all species
    labels, a diffusion model can still learn a distribution $q_\theta(x)$ over images.
    It may generate plausible animal images, but it has no externally supplied notion of
    the words ``cat'' and ``dog''. This is unsupervised and generative.

    \item \textbf{Semi-supervised learning.} If only $100$ images are labeled but the other
    $99{,}900$ are not, a semi-supervised classifier uses both sets. For example, it may
    encourage images that lie in the same high-density visual cluster to receive similar
    labels, while the $100$ labels determine which cluster should be called ``cat'' or
    ``dog''. The unlabeled images help the classification task, but do not create labels by
    themselves.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{How a request for a cat image is generated.}
\label{ex:supervised-cat-generation}
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    node distance=5mm,
    box/.style={
        draw,
        rounded corners=2pt,
        minimum height=15mm,
        align=center,
        font=\small,
        inner sep=4pt
    },
    request/.style={box, fill=blue!10, text width=1.8cm},
