    condition/.style={box, fill=orange!15, text width=1.9cm},
    noise/.style={box, fill=gray!15, text width=2.0cm},
    sampler/.style={box, fill=green!12, text width=3.15cm},
    output/.style={box, fill=purple!12, text width=2.05cm},
    flow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, thick}
]
\node[request] (prompt) {User request\\``cat''};
\node[condition, right=of prompt] (label) {Fix condition\\$y=\text{cat}$};
\node[noise, right=of label] (noise) {Draw random seed\\$x_T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$};
\node[sampler, right=of noise] (sampler) {Conditional sampler\\$q_\theta(x\mid y=\text{cat})$\\[-1pt]
\footnotesize autoregressive sampling\\or reverse diffusion};
\node[output, right=of sampler] (image) {Output\\$x_{\mathrm{new}}$\\a new cat image};

\draw[flow] (prompt) -- (label);
\draw[flow] (label) -- (noise);
\draw[flow] (noise) -- (sampler);
\draw[flow] (sampler) -- (image);
\node[below=7mm of sampler, font=\footnotesize\itshape, text=gray!70!black,
text width=9.0cm, align=center] {The request fixes the class condition; a new
random seed produces a different draw from the same learned cat-image distribution.};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

At generation time, the request ``cat'' fixes the conditioning label:
\[
y:=\text{cat}.
\]
The generator then runs a sampling algorithm for the learned conditional distribution%
\footnote{The symbol $\theta$ is the collection of trainable model parameters (for example,
the weights of a neural network); it is \emph{not} itself a probability distribution.
The notation $q_\theta$ denotes a family of candidate probability models indexed by those
parameters. After training, the parameters are fixed at some fitted value $\widehat{\theta}$
(often still written simply as $\theta$). For this fixed value and for $y=\text{cat}$,
$q_{\widehat{\theta}}(\,\cdot\mid\text{cat})$ is a conditional probability distribution
over possible images. In a continuous image representation it is more precisely a
\emph{probability density}: an exact single image has probability zero, while a small
neighborhood of similar images has positive probability. In some modern models, especially
diffusion models, the neural network does not evaluate a normalized numerical density
directly; instead, its label-conditioned reverse sampler implicitly defines the distribution
from which images are drawn.} and returns a random image $X_{\mathrm{new}}$:
\[
X_{\mathrm{new}}\sim q_\theta(\,\cdot\mid y=\text{cat}).
\]
Here $x_{\mathrm{new}}$ denotes one particular realized output of the random image
$X_{\mathrm{new}}$. Thus the label tells the model \emph{which} conditional distribution to
sample; the randomness in the sampling algorithm determines \emph{which particular cat
image} is returned. The exact implementation depends on the chosen generative model. For
example:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item In an autoregressive image model, write an image as a sequence of pixel values or
    discrete image tokens $x=(x_1,\ldots,x_m)$ and factorize
    \[
    q_\theta(x\mid\text{cat})
    =\prod_{j=1}^{m}q_\theta(x_j\mid x_1,\ldots,x_{j-1},\text{cat}).
    \]
    Start with no pixels, then sample $x_1$, sample $x_2$ conditional on $x_1$ and the label
    ``cat'', and continue until a complete image has been sampled.

    \item In a conditional diffusion model, start from random noise
    $x_T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)$. Run the learned reverse denoising steps from time $T$ down to
    $0$, supplying the label ``cat'' to the score/denoising network at every step. The final
    output $x_0$ is the generated image. Different initial noise draws give different cat
    images while the fixed condition biases all of them toward the cat distribution.
\end{enumerate}
The class probability $q_\theta(y)$ is not needed once the user has specified ``cat''.
It is used in the Bayes classifier above and in \emph{unconditional} generation, where one
would first draw a class $Y\sim q_\theta(y)$ and then draw
$X\sim q_\theta(x\mid Y)$. Finally, modeling a likelihood does not automatically guarantee
an efficient sampler; a generative model intended to produce images must provide a tractable
sampling procedure such as one of the two procedures above.

This example also shows why the terms should not be collapsed: ``supervised versus
unsupervised'' specifies the available data, whereas ``generative versus discriminative''
specifies the probability object or prediction rule being learned.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Discounted Markov decision process]\label{def:mdp}
A discounted Markov decision process (MDP) is a tuple
\[
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P,r,\gamma),
\]
where $\mathcal{S}$ is a state space, $\mathcal{A}$ is an action space,
$P(s'\mid s,a)$ is the transition probability of the next state $s'$ after taking action
$a$ in state $s$, $r(s,a):=\mathbb{E}[R_{t+1}\mid S_t=s,A_t=a]$ is the expected one-step
reward, and
$\gamma\in[0,1)$ is the discount factor. A stochastic policy $\pi$ specifies
$\pi(a\mid s)$, the probability of selecting action $a$ in state $s$.
\end{definition}

\begin{note}[Where the reward variable $R_{t+1}$ comes from]
At time $t$, the environment first presents the agent with state $S_t$. The agent chooses
$A_t$, and the environment then responds with two observed outcomes: a scalar reward
$R_{t+1}$ and the next state $S_{t+1}$. The time order is
\[
S_t
\quad\xrightarrow{\;\text{agent chooses }A_t\;}\quad
(R_{t+1},S_{t+1}).
\]
Here $R_{t+1}$ is the feedback for the transition initiated by the current action $A_t$:
the environment returns it together with the successor state $S_{t+1}$. Accordingly,
$R_t$ denotes feedback already observed from the preceding decision $A_{t-1}$; it is not
the reward caused by the current action $A_t$. In general, the current transition reward
need not be determined by the action alone; it may depend on the starting state, the action,
and the realized successor state:
\[
R_{t+1}\sim\mathcal{R}(\,\cdot\mid S_t,A_t,S_{t+1}).
\]
At time $t$, the agent has already observed $R_t$ and state $S_t$, then chooses $A_t$; this
new action gives rise to the next reward $R_{t+1}$.

The index is $t+1$ because this reward is observed \emph{after} the decision made at time
$t$. Thus the next action $A_{t+1}$ is chosen only after the agent has seen $R_{t+1}$ and
$S_{t+1}$.

The reward signal is part of the task specification or observed environment feedback; it is
not computed from the return. For example, in a game it may be the change in score, for a
robot it may be minus travel time or energy cost, and in reinforcement learning from
human feedback (RLHF) it may be a learned
human-preference score. In a model-based problem, one may know its expected value
$r(s,a)$. In a model-free problem, the agent instead observes sample rewards
$R_{t+1}$ from experience.

The reward need not be manually assigned by a human at every step, and it need not be
subjective. It is useful to distinguish the \emph{source of the measured signal} from the
\emph{choice of what objective to optimize}:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Automatic and objective measurement.} In chess, $R_{t+1}$ can be the
    automatically computed change in game score or the terminal win/loss signal. For a
    delivery robot, it can be the objectively measured negative travel time or energy use.
    In a trading simulator, it can be the realized profit and loss. No human needs to type
    in these rewards after each action.

    \item \textbf{A designed objective with objectively measured components.} A system
    designer may specify
    \[
    R_{t+1}
    =-\text{travel time}-1000\,\mathbf{1}\{\text{collision occurs}\}.
    \]
    The decision to penalize collisions by $1000$ is a modeling or value judgment, but once
    the formula is fixed, the reward is computed automatically from observed outcomes. Thus
    ``humans chose the objective'' does not imply that each realized reward is subjective.

    \item \textbf{Human-preference feedback.} In RLHF, people provide comparisons such as
    ``completion $y^+$ is preferable to $y^-$''. A fitted reward model then maps a new
    completion to a scalar score. This reward source reflects human preferences, so its
    target is partly subjective, even though the trained model evaluates it automatically at
    deployment time.
\end{itemize}
In all three cases, $R_{t+1}$ is simply the scalar feedback used by the RL algorithm to
evaluate consequences. The reward can measure an external physical/economic effect, encode a
designed trade-off, or approximate human preference.

More explicitly, a reward can be deterministic or random. In a detailed transition model,
after realizing $S_{t+1}=s'$ one may have
\[
R_{t+1}\sim\mathcal{R}(\,\cdot\mid S_t=s,A_t=a,S_{t+1}=s').
\]
If the reward is a deterministic function $\rho(s,a,s')$, then
$R_{t+1}=\rho(s,a,s')$ and
\[
r(s,a)
=\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}P(s'\mid s,a)\rho(s,a,s').
\]
For instance, delivery may give the realized reward $R_1=-1$ for travel, then returning
from the customer may give $R_2=5$. Seen from time $0$, these rewards appear in chronological
order as
\[
G_0=R_1+\gamma R_2+\gamma^2R_3+\cdots=-1+\gamma(5)
\]
when the second transition terminates the episode. This is why the return sums
$R_{t+1},R_{t+2},\ldots$: it evaluates every consequence that occurs after the current
decision, in the order in which it occurs.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Agent choice versus environment response]
Your interpretation is exactly the starting point: $\pi(a\mid s)$ describes the probability
of the \emph{agent's action} after seeing $s$, whereas $P(s'\mid s,a)$ describes the
probability of the \emph{environment's next state} after that action has already been fixed.
One time step has two stages:
\[
S_t=s
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
A_t\sim\pi(\,\cdot\mid s)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
S_{t+1}\sim P(\,\cdot\mid s,A_t).
\]
Equivalently, for particular values $a$ and $s'$,
\[
\mathbb{P}(A_t=a,S_{t+1}=s'\mid S_t=s)
=\pi(a\mid s)P(s'\mid s,a).
\]
The action $a$ influences which next states are likely, but it normally does not let the
agent choose the exact realized state $s'$: that part is determined by the environment and
can contain noise or uncertainty.

For example, at a depot a delivery robot may use
\[
\pi(\texttt{deliver}\mid\text{depot})=0.8,
\qquad
\pi(\texttt{wait}\mid\text{depot})=0.2.
\]
Suppose it takes \texttt{deliver}. The action itself is now definite, but a traffic event may
still make the environmental response random:
\[
P(\text{customer}\mid\text{depot},\texttt{deliver})=0.9,
\qquad
P(\text{traffic delay}\mid\text{depot},\texttt{deliver})=0.1.
\]
Thus \texttt{deliver} does \emph{not} mean that the next state must be
\texttt{customer}; it changes the distribution of possible next states. In contrast, if
\texttt{wait} deterministically leaves the robot at the depot, then
\[
P(\text{depot}\mid\text{depot},\texttt{wait})=1.
\]
This deterministic case is allowed. A policy may be deterministic while the environment is
random (always attempt delivery, but traffic is uncertain), or the environment may be
deterministic while the policy is random (the agent flips a coin between two actions). The
two probability laws are therefore conceptually independent: $\pi$ is controlled or learned
by the agent, while $P$ is a property of the environment.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Minimal probability model]
Starting from $S_0=s$, a trajectory is generated by
\[
A_t\sim\pi(\,\cdot\mid S_t),
\qquad
S_{t+1}\sim P(\,\cdot\mid S_t,A_t).
\]
The Markov assumption is precisely that, conditional on $(S_t,A_t)$, the distribution of
$S_{t+1}$ does not depend on earlier states and actions. The reward may equivalently be
random; then $r(s,a)$ denotes its conditional expectation. For the core finite-MDP
derivations below, this distinction changes only notation.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Recent past-paper signal]
The Fall 2025 paper contains a 10-point policy-gradient question: six points for deriving
the trajectory-gradient theorem and four points for proving the state-only baseline identity.
The Spring 2026 paper instead asks, in eight points, for a Bellman equation, policy
iteration, actor--critic with unknown $P$, and an RLHF or DPO objective. The units below
therefore separate \emph{derivation skills} from \emph{model-and-objective specification
skills}; both forms should be practised closed-book.
\end{note}

\begin{example}[A trajectory is not an i.i.d.\ labeled example]
Consider a delivery robot with states $\{\text{depot},\text{customer},\text{terminal}\}$.
At the depot it chooses either \texttt{deliver} or \texttt{wait}. Choosing
\texttt{deliver} yields reward $-1$ for travel and reaches the customer; from the customer,
returning to the terminal yields reward $+5$. A trajectory can therefore be
\[
(\text{depot},\texttt{deliver},-1,\text{customer},
\texttt{return},5,\text{terminal}).
\]
With discount factor $\gamma$, its return is $-1+5\gamma$. The choice at the depot is
evaluated partly through a reward observed only after a later transition. This delayed
credit assignment is the feature absent from ordinary one-shot supervised learning.
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[Unit 0: trajectory bookkeeping]
For a trajectory
\[
(S_0,A_0,R_1,S_1,A_1,R_2,S_2)
\]
with $R_1=2$, $R_2=-1$, and $\gamma=0.9$, compute $G_0$ if $S_2$ is terminal. State
which conditional distribution produces $A_1$, and which conditional distribution produces
$S_2$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Unit 0: trajectory bookkeeping]
The values $R_1=2$ and $R_2=-1$ are the two realized reward signals supplied by the
exercise; they are not computed from the return. Because $S_2$ is terminal, every reward
after $R_2$ is zero. Hence the return from time $0$ has only two nonzero terms:
\[
G_0=R_1+\gamma R_2
=2+0.9(-1)=1.1.
\]
At time $1$, the agent chooses the next action using its policy, so
\[
A_1\sim\pi(\,\cdot\mid S_1).
\]
After that action has been chosen, the environment produces the next state, so
\[
S_2\sim P(\,\cdot\mid S_1,A_1).
\]
Thus $\pi$ describes the agent's action-selection randomness, whereas $P$ describes the
environment's state-transition randomness.
\end{solution}

\begin{definition}[Return, value, action value, and advantage]\label{def:return-value}
The discounted return from time $t$ is
\[
G_t:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^kR_{t+k+1}.
\]
For an episodic problem, this notation uses the standard absorbing-terminal convention:
after termination all later rewards are zero and $V^\pi(s_{\mathrm{terminal}})=0$.
For a policy $\pi$\footnote{A \emph{policy} is the agent's decision rule: it specifies how
the agent chooses an action after observing the current state. In the stochastic-policy
notation used here, $\pi(\,\cdot\mid s)$ is a probability distribution over all actions
available at $s$. For example, at a depot one might have
$\pi(\texttt{deliver}\mid\text{depot})=0.8$ and
$\pi(\texttt{wait}\mid\text{depot})=0.2$; the agent then randomly selects one of these two
actions with the stated probabilities. A deterministic policy is the special case that gives
probability $1$ to one action at each state. The policy is chosen or learned by the agent,
whereas the transition kernel $P(s'\mid s,a)$ describes how the \emph{environment} responds
once an action has been selected. In $V^\pi$ and $Q^\pi$, the superscript $\pi$ means that
all future actions are generated by this fixed decision rule.}, the state-value function,
action-value function, and advantage are
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s)&:=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s],\\
Q^\pi(s,a)&:=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s,A_t=a],\\
A^\pi(s,a)&:=Q^\pi(s,a)-V^\pi(s).
\end{aligned}
\]
The superscript $\pi$ means that future actions are sampled from $\pi$. The advantage
measures whether action $a$ is better or worse than the policy's average choice at $s$.
\end{definition}

\begin{note}[Motivation: one objective, then three ways to evaluate it]
A trajectory produces many rewards, not just one:
\[
R_{t+1},R_{t+2},R_{t+3},\ldots.
\]
To decide whether one sequence of decisions is better than another, RL needs to combine these
future consequences into a single numerical objective. The \emph{return}\footnote{A policy
$\pi$ affects the return by determining how future actions are sampled:
\[
A_k\sim\pi(\,\cdot\mid S_k).
\]
Those actions affect the next-state distribution through
$S_{k+1}\sim P(\,\cdot\mid S_k,A_k)$ and therefore affect the rewards that later appear in
$G_t$. Thus a policy does \emph{not} change the arithmetic formula for the return of an
already realized trajectory; once the realized rewards are, say, $2,-1,5$, the return is
always $2+\gamma(-1)+\gamma^2(5)$. Instead, changing $\pi$ changes which trajectories and
reward sequences are likely to occur, hence changes the probability distribution of the
random variable $G_t$. This is why values carry a superscript:
\[
V^\pi(s)=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s].
\]
For example, a delivery policy that often selects \texttt{deliver} may more often incur a
travel cost but also reach the later delivery reward, while a policy that always selects
\texttt{wait} may remain at the depot and receive neither outcome. The subscript $\pi$ on
the expectation records that the probability law of the future trajectory was generated by
that policy.}
\[
G_t=R_{t+1}+\gamma R_{t+2}+\gamma^2R_{t+3}+\cdots
\]
does exactly this: it is the total future reward seen from time $t$, with rewards farther in
the future receiving progressively smaller weight. For the delivery example, an immediate
travel cost $-1$ followed by reward $+5$ one step later has return
\[
-1+5\gamma.
\]
With $\gamma=0.9$, this is $3.5$, so the future delivery reward is still valuable; with
$\gamma=0.1$, it is $-0.5$, so the delayed reward is weighted much less.

The discount factor has both an interpretation and a mathematical role. It expresses a
preference for sooner rewards and limits the importance of distant, uncertain outcomes.
It also makes an infinite-horizon sum well behaved: if $|R_t|\leq R_{\max}$ and
$0\leq\gamma<1$, then
\[
|G_t|
\leq R_{\max}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\gamma^k
=\frac{R_{\max}}{1-\gamma}<\infty.
\]
For an episodic task, the sum is effectively finite because rewards after the terminal state
are zero. Thus the infinite-series notation is a convenient uniform convention, not a claim
that an episode literally lasts forever.

Once the return is the quantity of interest, the three functions answer three distinct
decision questions:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $V^\pi(s)$ is the expected return \emph{before} choosing the present action. It asks:
    ``If I am at state $s$ and continue to follow policy $\pi$, how good is my situation on
    average?'' This is the basic quantity for evaluating a policy.

    \item $Q^\pi(s,a)$ is the expected return when the first action is fixed to be $a$. It
    asks: ``How good is it to take this particular action now, then follow $\pi$ afterwards?''
    To improve a policy, one must compare competing actions at the same state, so state value
    alone is insufficient.

    \item $A^\pi(s,a)=Q^\pi(s,a)-V^\pi(s)$ asks the comparison in relative form: ``Is action
    $a$ better or worse than the policy's usual performance at this state?'' A positive
    advantage means that forcing $a$ is better than the policy's average first choice; a
    negative advantage means worse. Averaged over the policy's own action choice,
    \[
    \sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a\mid s)A^\pi(s,a)=0,
    \]
    so advantage removes the state-dependent baseline and isolates which actions deserve to
    be encouraged or discouraged.
\end{itemize}
\end{note}

\begin{example}[A two-state calculation]
Consider an MDP with a decision state $s$ and a terminal state
$s_{\mathrm{terminal}}$. At $s$, the available actions are
$a_{\mathrm{stop}}$ and $a_{\mathrm{wait}}$. The environment's transitions and rewards are
deterministic \emph{once the action has been chosen}:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
\text{current state} & \text{action} & \text{reward} & \text{next state}\\
\hline
s & a_{\mathrm{stop}} & 1 & s_{\mathrm{terminal}}\\
s & a_{\mathrm{wait}} & 0 & s
\end{array}
\]
Thus the relevant transition probabilities are
\[
P(s_{\mathrm{terminal}}\mid s,a_{\mathrm{stop}})=1,
\qquad
P(s\mid s,a_{\mathrm{wait}})=1.
\]
The environment here is deterministic; the only randomness comes from the policy. Suppose
the policy uses
\[
\pi(a_{\mathrm{stop}}\mid s)=p,
\qquad
\pi(a_{\mathrm{wait}}\mid s)=1-p.
\]
At each visit to $s$, it flips this same biased coin again. If it chooses
$a_{\mathrm{stop}}$, it receives $R_{t+1}=1$ and terminates. If it chooses
$a_{\mathrm{wait}}$, it receives $R_{t+1}=0$ and returns to $s$, where the same decision is
made again.

Consequently, the return $G_0$ is random. For example, the three possible beginning
trajectories are
\[
\begin{aligned}
&(s,a_{\mathrm{stop}},1,s_{\mathrm{terminal}}),
&&G_0=1,
&&\text{with probability }p,\\
&(s,a_{\mathrm{wait}},0,s,a_{\mathrm{stop}},1,s_{\mathrm{terminal}}),
&&G_0=\gamma,
&&\text{with probability }(1-p)p,\\
&(s,a_{\mathrm{wait}},0,s,a_{\mathrm{wait}},0,s,a_{\mathrm{stop}},1,
s_{\mathrm{terminal}}),
&&G_0=\gamma^2,
&&\text{with probability }(1-p)^2p.
\end{aligned}
\]
In general, after $n$ consecutive waits followed by stopping, the return is $\gamma^n$ and
the probability is $(1-p)^np$. Hence the state value---the average return under this
policy---is
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s)
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-p)^np\,\gamma^n\\
&=p\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigl(\gamma(1-p)\bigr)^n
=\frac{p}{1-\gamma(1-p)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The same result follows from one-step conditioning. With probability $p$ the policy stops
now and contributes reward $1$; with probability $1-p$ it waits, receives $0$, and returns
to exactly the same decision state, whose future value is again $V^\pi(s)$:
\[
V^\pi(s)
=p\cdot1+(1-p)\bigl[0+\gamma V^\pi(s)\bigr]
=\frac{p}{1-\gamma(1-p)}.
\]
For instance, when $p=\tfrac12$ and $\gamma=0.9$,
\[
V^\pi(s)=\frac{1/2}{1-0.9(1/2)}=\frac{10}{11}\approx0.909.
\]
This example displays the distinct roles of all RL objects: $\pi$ decides whether to stop or
wait, $P$ determines the state resulting from that action, $R_{t+1}$ is the corresponding
feedback, $G_0$ is the random total discounted reward on one trajectory, and $V^\pi(s)$ is
its expectation. It also shows the central RL phenomenon: waiting has immediate reward zero,
but it changes the distribution of future returns by postponing the chance to stop.
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Unit 1: Bellman equations and dynamic programming}
\label{unit:1}

\begin{remark}[Motivation: replacing a whole future trajectory by one next-state value]
The definition
\[
V^\pi(s)=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s]
\]
appears to require averaging over every possible infinite future trajectory beginning at
$s$. Even in a small MDP\footnote{MDP abbreviates \emph{Markov decision process}: the
mathematical model $\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P,r,\gamma)$ in \cref{def:mdp}.
It specifies possible states $\mathcal{S}$, actions $\mathcal{A}$, an environment transition
law $P$, a reward rule $r$, and a discount factor $\gamma$. ``Decision process'' means that
an agent repeatedly chooses actions; ``Markov'' means that, once the current state and
current action are known, the distribution of the next state does not depend on the earlier
history.}, the number of possible future action--state paths grows rapidly
with the horizon. The Bellman expectation equations are useful because they avoid enumerating
this whole branching tree.

The key observation is to look only one step ahead:
\[
G_t=R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}.
\]
If the agent is currently at $s$, the fixed policy first chooses an action $a$ with
probability $\pi(a\mid s)$, receives expected reward $r(s,a)$, and reaches $s'$ with
probability $P(s'\mid s,a)$. From that point onwards, the agent again follows the same policy
$\pi$. Therefore the entire remaining random future can be summarized by the single number
$V^\pi(s')$, rather than by listing all later trajectories. In words, the first Bellman
equation says:
\[
\text{value now}
=\text{expected immediate reward}
+\text{discounted expected value after one transition}.
\]

The two displayed equations answer slightly different questions. $V^\pi(s)$ asks, ``before
choosing an action at $s$, how good is the policy on average?'' and therefore averages over
the first action using $\pi(a\mid s)$. In contrast, $Q^\pi(s,a)$ asks, ``if I force the first
action to be $a$, how good is that choice?'' It does not average over this first action, but
after reaching $s'$ it again averages over the policy's next action $a'$. Thus the theorem
turns policy evaluation into a system of recursive equations (and, for finite state spaces,
typically a system of linear equations), which is the starting point for dynamic programming.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Bellman expectation equations]\label{thm:bellman-expectation}
For a discounted MDP and a fixed policy $\pi$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s)
&=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a\mid s)
\left[r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)V^\pi(s')\right],\\
Q^\pi(s,a)
&=r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)\sum_{a'\in\mathcal{A}}
\pi(a'\mid s')Q^\pi(s',a').
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix a state $s$. By the definition of return,
\[
G_t=R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}.
\]
Insert this identity into the definition of the state value and condition first on the
action selected by the fixed policy:
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s)
&=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_\pi[R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}\mid S_t=s]\\
&=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a\mid s)\,
\mathbb{E}_\pi[R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}\mid S_t=s,A_t=a].
\end{aligned}
\]
For a fixed pair $(s,a)$, the expected immediate reward is $r(s,a)$. Next condition on the
successor state. Since
$P(s'\mid s,a)=\mathbb{P}(S_{t+1}=s'\mid S_t=s,A_t=a)$ and, after reaching $s'$, all
later actions again follow $\pi$, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}_\pi[R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}\mid S_t=s,A_t=a]
&=r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)\,
\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_{t+1}\mid S_{t+1}=s']\\
&=r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)V^\pi(s').
\end{aligned}
\]
Substitution into the preceding display proves the state-value Bellman expectation equation.

For the action value, the first action is no longer random: it is fixed to $a$. The same
first-step decomposition therefore gives
\[
Q^\pi(s,a)
=r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)V^\pi(s').
\]
It remains only to write the state value at $s'$ in terms of action values. Before choosing
the next action, the policy averages over its possible choices:
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s')
&=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_{t+1}\mid S_{t+1}=s']\\
&=\sum_{a'\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a'\mid s')\,
\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_{t+1}\mid S_{t+1}=s',A_{t+1}=a']\\
&=\sum_{a'\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a'\mid s')Q^\pi(s',a').
\end{aligned}
\]
Inserting this identity into the previous equation for $Q^\pi(s,a)$ proves the second
display. The reusable proof pattern is therefore: decompose return into immediate reward
plus discounted future return, condition on the next random variable, and recognize the
remaining conditional expectation as a value function.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Optimal values and Bellman optimality]\label{def:bellman-optimality}
The optimal state value and action value are
\[
V^*(s):=\sup_\pi V^\pi(s),
\qquad
Q^*(s,a):=\sup_\pi Q^\pi(s,a).
\]
For finite discounted MDPs, they satisfy
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^*(s)
&=\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
\left[r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)V^*(s')\right],\\
Q^*(s,a)
&=r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)
\max_{a'}Q^*(s',a').
\end{aligned}
\]
A greedy policy $\pi^*$ selects an action attaining the corresponding maximum.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Bellman contraction principle]
Define the Bellman evaluation operator
\[
(T^\pi V)(s)
:=\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)
\left[r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)V(s')\right].
\]
For finite discounted MDPs,
\[
\lVert T^\pi V-T^\pi U\rVert_\infty
\leq\gamma\lVert V-U\rVert_\infty.
\]
Thus $T^\pi$ has the unique fixed point $V^\pi$, and repeated application
$V_{k+1}=T^\pi V_k$ converges to it. The optimality operator obtained by replacing the
policy average by $\max_a$ has the same contraction property and fixed point $V^*$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $V$ and $U$ be any two candidate value functions. For each state $s$, the reward terms
cancel when the two operator values are subtracted, so
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|(T^\pi V)(s)-(T^\pi U)(s)\right|
&=\left|
\gamma\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)
\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)\bigl(V(s')-U(s')\bigr)
\right|\\
&\leq
\gamma\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)
\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)\left|V(s')-U(s')\right|\\
&\leq
\gamma\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)
\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)\lVert V-U\rVert_\infty\\
&=\gamma\lVert V-U\rVert_\infty.
\end{aligned}
\]
The final equality uses that $\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)=1$ and
$\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)=1$. Taking the maximum over $s$ proves
\[
\lVert T^\pi V-T^\pi U\rVert_\infty
\leq\gamma\lVert V-U\rVert_\infty.
\]
Because $\gamma<1$ and the finite-state value-function space is complete in this norm,
the Banach fixed-point theorem gives a unique fixed point and convergence of the iterates.
The Bellman expectation equation shows that this fixed point is $V^\pi$.

For the optimality operator $T^*$, use
\[
\left|\max_a f(a)-\max_a g(a)\right|
\leq\max_a|f(a)-g(a)|
\]
in the same calculation. This yields the identical $\gamma$-contraction bound, so $T^*$ has
the unique fixed point $V^*$.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}[Bellman equation versus Bellman evaluation operator]
The two displays have almost the same algebraic expression, but they play different logical
roles.
\begin{itemize}
    \item The \emph{Bellman expectation equation} is an equation for one special, initially
    unknown function: the true value function $V^\pi$. It says
    \[
    V^\pi(s)
    =\sum_a\pi(a\mid s)
    \left[r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)V^\pi(s')\right].
    \]
    In words: if one already knew the correct expected future value $V^\pi(s')$ at every
    possible next state, then one step of averaging returns the same function $V^\pi$.

    \item The \emph{Bellman evaluation operator} $T^\pi$ is a rule that can be applied to
    \emph{any} candidate function $V:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}$, whether or not it is correct.
    It produces a new candidate function $T^\pi V$. The input is written $V$, rather than
    $V^\pi$, precisely because it need not yet equal the true value function. The policy has
    not disappeared: it is contained both in the operator's name $T^\pi$ and in the factors
    $\pi(a\mid s)$ on the right-hand side.
\end{itemize}

The link between them is the fixed-point identity
\[
T^\pi V^\pi=V^\pi.
\]
Thus the Bellman equation identifies the desired answer, while the operator supplies a
procedure for finding it. Starting with any guess $V_0$ (for example, $V_0(s)=0$ for every
state), policy evaluation repeatedly applies the operator:
\[
V_0
\quad\longmapsto\quad
V_1=T^\pi V_0
\quad\longmapsto\quad
V_2=T^\pi V_1
\quad\longmapsto\quad\cdots.
\]

The contraction principle explains why this procedure is reliable rather than merely a
formal rewriting. For every two candidate functions $U,V$, applying $T^\pi$ shrinks their
maximum difference by at least the factor $\gamma<1$:
\[
\lVert T^\pi V-T^\pi U\rVert_\infty
\leq\gamma\lVert V-U\rVert_\infty.
\]
Consequently, there can be only one fixed point, and the successive guesses converge to it.

For the preceding stop/wait example, a candidate value is just one number $v$ at the
nonterminal state, and the operator is
\[
T^\pi(v)=p+(1-p)\gamma v.
\]
For example, $T^\pi(0)=p$ is the first estimate obtained by pretending that all future value
is zero. The true value is the special number satisfying
\[
v=T^\pi(v),
\]
namely $v=p/[1-\gamma(1-p)]$. This is exactly the distinction above: $v$ is an arbitrary
input to the operator, whereas $V^\pi(s)$ is the unique input left unchanged by it.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Planning versus learning]
Policy evaluation uses the known model $P$ to compute $V^\pi$. Policy improvement replaces
$\pi$ by a policy greedy with respect to $Q^\pi$, and policy iteration alternates these two
steps. This is \emph{planning}: it assumes that $P$ and $r$ are available. If the transition
model is unknown and only sampled transitions are observed, the agent must instead estimate
values or gradients from data.
\end{note}

\begin{example}[One policy-improvement step]
Let $\gamma=1/2$. At state $s$, action $a_0$ terminates with reward $1$, while $a_1$
gives reward $0$ and moves deterministically to $s'$. At $s'$, the only action terminates
with reward $4$. Suppose the current policy chooses $a_0$ at $s$. Its evaluation gives
\[
V^\pi(s)=1,
\qquad
V^\pi(s')=4.
\]
The policy-improvement comparison is
\[
Q^\pi(s,a_0)=1,
\qquad
Q^\pi(s,a_1)=0+\frac12V^\pi(s')=2.
\]
Thus the improved policy chooses $a_1$. This is a complete policy-iteration step: evaluate
the current policy, compare its one-step look-ahead values, then replace it by a greedy
policy.
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[Unit 1: Bellman and improvement]
In the preceding example, replace the reward at $s'$ by an unknown number $c>0$. For
which values of $c$ does policy improvement choose $a_1$? Then explain in one sentence why
this calculation is model-based planning rather than Q-learning.
\end{exercise}

\begin{definition}[Model-based planning and Q-learning]\label{def:planning-qlearning}
\emph{Model-based planning} means choosing or improving actions by using an explicit model of
the environment: its transition probabilities $P(s'\mid s,a)$ and expected rewards
$r(s,a)$. For example, policy evaluation and policy iteration compute quantities such as
\[
r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)V(s')
\]
by summing over the model's possible successor states before actually taking the action.
The model may be supplied in advance, as in a textbook MDP, or learned from data first; the
essential feature is that planning subsequently uses this explicit $P,r$ model.

\emph{Q-learning} is a model-free temporal-difference control method. It does not require
the agent to know or explicitly estimate $P(s'\mid s,a)$. More precisely, at some time $t$,
suppose that the agent is in state $S_t=s$, chooses $A_t=a$, and then observes the transition
outcomes
\[
R_{t+1}=r,
\qquad
S_{t+1}=s'.
\]
Here $s'$ is simply the particular \emph{successor state} observed after acting in $s$; the
prime is a label, not a derivative. Thus $(s,a,r,s')$ records one complete observed transition:
current state, chosen action, observed reward, and next state. After this one sample, Q-learning
updates its action-value estimate directly by
\[
Q_{k+1}(s,a)
=Q_k(s,a)+\alpha_k
\left[r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')-Q_k(s,a)\right].
\]
The maximization now asks which action $a'$ would look best \emph{from the newly reached state}
$s'$; $a'$ is only a dummy action variable and need not equal the action $a$ already taken.
If $s'$ is terminal, the standard convention is
\[
\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')=0,
\]
since no later reward remains after a terminal state.
Thus model-based planning averages over all next states predicted by a model, whereas
Q-learning uses one observed next state at a time and gradually learns from many such
samples.
\end{definition}

\begin{solution}[Unit 1: Bellman and improvement]
The action $a_0$ terminates immediately with reward $1$, so
\[
Q^\pi(s,a_0)=1.
\]
If the agent instead forces action $a_1$, it receives reward $0$, reaches $s'$, and then
the only available action yields reward $c$. Hence $V^\pi(s')=c$ and
\[
Q^\pi(s,a_1)=0+\gamma V^\pi(s')=\gamma c.
\]
Policy improvement chooses $a_1$ precisely when its action value exceeds that of $a_0$:
\[
\gamma c>1.
\]
For the stated $\gamma=\tfrac12$, this is equivalent to $c>2$. At $c=2$ the two actions tie,
so either action can be chosen by a greedy improved policy. This is model-based planning
because the calculation explicitly used the known transition $s\xrightarrow{a_1}s'$ and
the known value/reward at $s'$, rather than estimating a target from observed sample
transitions.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Unit 2: model-free control and Q-learning}
\label{unit:2}

The term \emph{temporal difference} (TD) refers to comparing predictions at two consecutive
decision times: the current time $t$ and the next time $t+1$. It is not a derivative with
respect to time. The TD error compares a current value estimate with a one-step bootstrap
target.\footnote{The name comes from the return identity
\[
G_t=R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}.
\]
At time $t$, the agent observes the new one-step information $R_{t+1}$, while the remaining
return $G_{t+1}$ belongs to the next decision time. TD learning replaces this still-unknown
future return by an estimate made at that next time, so it compares a prediction at time $t$
with immediate evidence plus a prediction at time $t+1$. Suppose the agent has just taken
action $a$ in state $s$. Before
seeing its outcome, $Q(s,a)$ is its current guess of the total discounted reward that this
choice will eventually yield. The agent then observes an immediate reward $r$ and a next state
$s'$. It still does not know the entire future return from $s'$, so it replaces that unknown
quantity by its present estimate of the best future value,
$\max_{a'}Q(s',a')$. Consequently,
\[
\underbrace{r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q(s',a')}_{\text{one-step bootstrap target}}
\]
is a new one-step-informed guess for the value of the \emph{already taken} pair $(s,a)$.
It is called a \emph{bootstrap} target because it uses one observed step, then ``pulls itself
up'' using the current value estimate for the unobserved remainder, rather than waiting to
observe the complete return. The TD error is ``new target minus old guess.'' For example, if
$Q(s,a)=5$, $r=1$, $\gamma=0.9$, and
$\max_{a'}Q(s',a')=8$, then the target is $1+0.9(8)=8.2$ and the TD error is
$8.2-5=3.2$. A positive error says that the old guess was too small, so the update increases
$Q(s,a)$.} For an action-value estimate $Q$ and a transition
$(s,a,r,s')$, the Q-learning TD error is
\[
\delta
:=r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q(s',a')-Q(s,a).
\]
The tabular update is
\[
Q_{k+1}(s,a)
=Q_k(s,a)+\alpha_k
\left[r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')-Q_k(s,a)\right].
\]

\begin{note}[How to read one tabular Q-learning update]
\noindent\textbf{Purpose and motivation: what is being learned.}
The goal of reinforcement-learning \emph{control} is not merely to record rewards; it is to
choose actions that yield a large \emph{long-run} discounted return. If the environment's
transition law $P$ and rewards $r$ were known, the agent could use model-based planning to
calculate which action is best. In Q-learning they are not assumed known. Instead, the agent
interacts with the environment and learns an estimate of the optimal action value
\[
Q^*(s,a)
=\mathbb{E}\!\left[
R_{t+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\gamma^jR_{t+1+j}
\;\middle|\;
S_t=s,\ A_t=a,\ \text{then act optimally}
\right].
\]
In words, $Q^*(s,a)$ answers: ``if I take $a$ now in $s$ and make the best choices thereafter,
how much discounted reward should I expect?'' Once the estimates are accurate enough, the
agent can choose a greedy action
\[
\arg\max_a Q(s,a)
\]
in each state. Thus the purpose of the update at this point is to turn individual observed
experiences into increasingly accurate estimates that can guide future choices.

There is no separate mysterious event called ``starting learning'' in the formula. One
initializes a table $Q_0$ (often arbitrarily), explores by taking an action, observes one
transition $(s,a,r,s')$, and then applies the displayed update. This repeats after every new
experience. The update is needed precisely because the initial entries are guesses: the
observed reward and successor state provide new evidence about whether the old guess
$Q_k(s,a)$ was too high or too low.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Why create \(Q_{k+1}\) from \(Q_k\), and what is the final target?}
It is important not to interpret a single update as having already ``optimized'' $Q_k$.
$Q_k$ is the agent's current, incomplete estimate after its first $k$ learning updates, and
$Q_{k+1}$ is the same table after incorporating one further observation. The point of producing
$Q_{k+1}$ is therefore to avoid discarding the new information in that observation. The desired
end point of the whole sequence
\[
Q_0,\ Q_1,\ Q_2,\ \ldots
\]
is the optimal action-value function $Q^*$, characterized by the optimal Bellman fixed-point
equation
\[
Q^*(s,a)
=\mathbb{E}\!\left[
R_{t+1}+\gamma\max_{a'}Q^*(S_{t+1},a')
\;\middle|\;
S_t=s,\ A_t=a
\right].
\]
This equation says that the correct value of taking $a$ now equals its expected immediate
reward plus the discounted value of making the best choice afterward. The Q-learning update is
a sample-based way to solve this equation when the expectation and the transition law are
unknown: it repeatedly nudges $Q_k$ toward the right-hand side using observed transitions.

If $Q_k$ has reached $Q^*$, then the TD error has conditional mean zero: there is no remaining
\emph{systematic} correction to make. Individual sampled updates can still move up or down
because the environment can be random, so the sequence need not increase monotonically. Under
the standard tabular assumptions, the repeated updates converge to $Q^*$. Only after learning
has made $Q_k$ a good approximation to $Q^*$ do we extract the useful decision rule
\[
\pi_{\mathrm{greedy}}(s)\in\arg\max_a Q_k(s,a).
\]
Thus the table itself is an intermediate representation: its practical purpose is to support a
policy that selects high-return actions. This is the purpose of approximating $Q^*$: it turns
unknown future consequences into an action rule. The agent does not need the table merely as a
record of rewards; it needs $Q_k(s,a)$ to compare the long-run consequences of the currently
available actions and choose the largest one. We approximate rather than calculate $Q^*$ exactly
because the transition probabilities and expected rewards are usually unknown in Q-learning, and
even a known model can be too large to solve exactly.

The word \emph{tabular} refers to the finite-state, finite-action representation in which the
agent stores one ordinary number for each state--action pair. One can picture $Q_k$ as a table:
the rows are states $s$, the columns are actions $a$, and the entry in row $s$, column $a$ is
$Q_k(s,a)$. The subscript $k$ counts learning updates, not physical time. After the $k$th
observed transition, Q-learning changes only the one table entry corresponding to the pair
$(s,a)$ that was actually tried.

The square brackets in the displayed update do not denote a new operation and need not be
``opened'' in any special sense. They merely group the TD error:
\[
\underbrace{r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')}_{\text{new one-step target}}
-
\underbrace{Q_k(s,a)}_{\text{old table entry}}.
\]
Thus Q-learning does \emph{not} assume that this target is the exact answer after one random
transition. Rather, it moves the old entry by only the fraction $\alpha_k$ of the discrepancy.
Equivalently,
\[
Q_{k+1}(s,a)
=(1-\alpha_k)Q_k(s,a)
+\alpha_k\left[r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')\right].
\]
\textbf{The learning rate \(\alpha_k\).} The number $\alpha_k$ is the \emph{step size}: it
specifies what fraction of the difference between the new target and old guess is accepted on
the $k$th update. For $0<\alpha_k\leq 1$, the preceding display is a weighted average of the
old guess and the new target. In particular,
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{choice of learning rate} & \text{effect on the table entry}\\
\hline
\alpha_k=0 & \text{ignore this experience; no learning occurs}\\
\alpha_k=1 & \text{replace the old entry completely by this one sampled target}\\
\alpha_k=0.1 & \text{move only \(10\%\) of the way from the old entry toward the target}
\end{array}
\]
The target is based on only one random transition, so it can be unusually favorable or
unfavorable. A smaller learning rate averages the evidence from many transitions and is more
stable, but changes the table more slowly; a larger rate reacts more quickly, but can fluctuate
more. The subscript $k$ permits the rate to vary during learning. In the classical stationary
tabular setting, one typically lets it decrease suitably as a state--action pair is revisited,
so that early experience can correct a poor initialization while later noise is averaged out.

We perform the update because the true optimal value $Q^*(s,a)$ is initially unknown. Each
experience supplies some evidence about it; repeated corrections from many transitions gradually
make the table useful for choosing actions. If the entry were never updated, the agent would
retain its initial arbitrary guess forever.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{A complete small environment and two actual updates.}
Consider a delivery robot with three nonterminal states:
\[
\mathsf{D}=\text{depot},\qquad
\mathsf{C}=\text{customer reached},\qquad
\mathsf{L}=\text{traffic delay}.
\]
At the depot it can choose \texttt{deliver}; at $\mathsf{C}$ its only action is
\texttt{finish}, and at $\mathsf{L}$ its only action is \texttt{resume}. The action
\texttt{deliver} is genuinely random: it sometimes reaches the customer and sometimes reaches
a delay. Let the discount be $\gamma=0.9$. Suppose that, before the next experience, the
agent's current table contains
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{state} & \text{action} & \text{current table entry}\\
\hline
\mathsf{D} & \texttt{deliver} & Q_k(\mathsf{D},\texttt{deliver})=5\\
\mathsf{C} & \texttt{finish} & Q_k(\mathsf{C},\texttt{finish})=8\\
\mathsf{L} & \texttt{resume} & Q_k(\mathsf{L},\texttt{resume})=2
\end{array}
\]
and uses learning rate $\alpha_k=0.1$. These are only current guesses, not values supplied by
the environment.

First suppose the robot experiences
\[
\mathsf{D}
\xrightarrow[\;r=1\;]{\texttt{deliver}}
\mathsf{C}.
\]
This single observed transition means
\[
s=\mathsf{D},\qquad
a=\texttt{deliver},\qquad
r=1,\qquad
s'=\mathsf{C}.
\]
Because \texttt{finish} is the only available action at $\mathsf{C}$,
\[
\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')
=\max_{a'}Q_k(\mathsf{C},a')
=Q_k(\mathsf{C},\texttt{finish})
=8.
\]
Hence the target, TD error, and updated entry are respectively
\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{target}&=1+0.9(8)=8.2,\\
\text{TD error}&=8.2-5=3.2,\\
Q_{k+1}(\mathsf{D},\texttt{deliver})
&=5+0.1(3.2)=5.32.
\end{aligned}
\]
Only the entry for $(\mathsf{D},\texttt{deliver})$ changes. In particular, the entries $8$ and
$2$ in the other rows are not changed by this experience.

On a later attempt, suppose traffic produces the different observed transition
\[
\mathsf{D}
\xrightarrow[\;r=-2\;]{\texttt{deliver}}
\mathsf{L}.
\]
Now $s'=\mathsf{L}$, so the target is $-2+0.9(2)=-0.2$. Starting from the newly updated value
$5.32$, the next correction is
\[
Q_{k+2}(\mathsf{D},\texttt{deliver})
=5.32+0.1\bigl[-0.2-5.32\bigr]
=4.768.
\]
The second experience lowers the same table entry because it was worse than the old estimate.
Repeated samples of both possible outcomes make the entry settle toward the expected
discounted return of choosing \texttt{deliver}; the agent does not need to know the
probabilities of customer arrival or traffic delay in advance.
\end{note}

The update is omitted after terminal states by setting the future-value contribution to
zero; equivalently, an absorbing terminal state has value zero.

\begin{property}[What Q-learning estimates]
Q-learning is model-free and off-policy: samples may be collected by an exploratory
behavior policy, but the target uses $\max_{a'}Q(s',a')$ and therefore aims at the greedy
optimal policy. In the finite tabular setting, convergence to $Q^*$ requires sufficient
exploration, suitable diminishing step sizes, and stationary dynamics. With a neural
network $Q_\phi$, bootstrapping, function approximation, and off-policy sampling can
interact unstably; target networks and replay buffers are practical stabilizers, not a
replacement for the tabular theorem.
\end{property}

\begin{note}[How deep Q-learning's ingredients interact]
The finite-table convergence result and neural-network Q-learning are related, but they are not
the same algorithmic setting. In a finite table, changing $Q(s,a)$ changes only that one entry.
With a neural network, the agent represents all values by one parameterized function
\[
Q_\phi(s,a),
\]
where $\phi$ is one shared collection of weights. Updating $\phi$ from one transition can
therefore change the estimated values of many other state--action pairs. This is
\emph{function approximation}: it permits learning in enormous state spaces such as images,
but it removes the independent-entry structure used in the tabular convergence theorem.

The usual deep-Q-learning data flow is as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item A \emph{behavior policy} $\mu$ interacts with the environment. It must explore--for
    example, choose a random action occasionally--so that the data include more than the actions
    the current network happens to prefer. The environment dynamics are assumed
    \emph{stationary}: the same $(s,a)$ has the same reward and successor-state distribution
    throughout training. Otherwise, old and new observations describe different tasks.

    \item Each observed transition $(s,a,r,s')$ is stored in a \emph{replay buffer}. Training
    later draws a roughly random mini-batch from this buffer rather than using only the most
    recent, highly correlated trajectory.

    \item A frozen copy $Q_{\bar\phi}$ of the network forms the bootstrap target
    \[
    y=r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_{\bar\phi}(s',a').
    \]
    The current network $Q_\phi$ is then trained to reduce the squared TD error
    \[
    \bigl[y-Q_\phi(s,a)\bigr]^2.
    \]
    This gradient step changes $\phi$, while $\bar\phi$ is held fixed for many such steps.

    \item Periodically, or by a slow moving average, the target parameters are refreshed:
    \[
    \bar\phi\leftarrow\phi.
    \]
    The resulting improved $Q_\phi$ can define a more greedy behavior policy, while a small
    amount of exploration remains necessary to keep collecting informative transitions.
\end{enumerate}

The source of difficulty is the interaction of three facts, often called the
\emph{deadly triad}. \emph{Bootstrapping} makes the target depend on a value estimate;
\emph{function approximation} makes one update alter many estimates; and
\emph{off-policy sampling} uses data from $\mu$ while the target assumes future greedy actions.
If the same rapidly changing network appears on both sides of the TD target, an error in one
estimate can alter the target, be propagated to related states by shared parameters, and then be
reinforced using data that may not adequately cover those states. Values can then oscillate or
diverge rather than settle.

Target networks reduce this moving-target feedback by keeping $Q_{\bar\phi}$ temporarily fixed.
Replay buffers reduce temporal correlation, reuse rare but useful transitions, and make the
training distribution change more slowly. Exploration provides coverage of relevant actions;
diminishing or otherwise suitably small step sizes prevent single noisy samples from dominating.
These tools often make deep Q-learning workable in practice, but they do not by themselves give
the same general convergence guarantee as finite tabular Q-learning.
\end{note}

\begin{note}[Do not conflate the three update families]
\begin{itemize}
    \item Monte Carlo methods use the complete sampled return $G_t$; they do not bootstrap.
    \item TD methods bootstrap from an estimated future value, for example
    $r+\gamma V(S_{t+1})$.
    \item Dynamic programming uses an expectation over the known transition model $P$.
\end{itemize}
Q-learning is a TD control method: it replaces a model expectation by a sample transition
and replaces the next-policy average by a maximum.
\end{note}

\begin{example}[One Q-learning update]
Suppose a sampled nonterminal transition has
\[
Q_k(s,a)=2,
\qquad
r=1,
\qquad
\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')=4,
\qquad
\gamma=0.9,
\qquad
\alpha_k=\frac12.
\]
The target is $1+0.9(4)=4.6$, the TD error is $4.6-2=2.6$, and the update is
\[
Q_{k+1}(s,a)
=2+\frac12(2.6)=3.3.
\]
Only the entry corresponding to the visited pair $(s,a)$ changes in tabular Q-learning.
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[Unit 2: terminal transition and exploration]
For a terminal transition with current estimate $Q_k(s,a)=5$, reward $r=-2$, and
step size $\alpha_k=0.1$, compute the Q-learning update. Then explain why a purely greedy
behavior policy can prevent the usual tabular convergence guarantee.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Unit 2: terminal transition and exploration]
Start from the standard Q-learning update for an arbitrary nonterminal sampled transition
$(s,a,r,s')$:
\[
Q_{k+1}(s,a)=Q_k(s,a)+\alpha_k
\left[r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')-Q_k(s,a)\right].
\]
The bracket is the TD error: immediate reward plus the estimated best future value, minus the
old estimate. For a terminal transition there is no future decision and no later reward, so
\[
\underbrace{\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')}_{\text{future-value contribution}}=0.
\]
Writing the terminal target as $y_{\mathrm{terminal}}:=r$, the standard formula therefore
becomes
\[
Q_{k+1}(s,a)
=Q_k(s,a)+\alpha_k\bigl(y_{\mathrm{terminal}}-Q_k(s,a)\bigr)
=5+0.1(-2-5)=4.3.
\]
The term $\gamma\max_{a'}Q(s',a')$ is absent because termination has no future decision
step. A purely greedy behavior policy can prevent the usual convergence guarantee: if an
action happens to have a low initial estimate, a greedy policy may never select it again.
The agent then receives no data that could reveal that this neglected action is actually
better. Sufficient exploration requires every relevant state--action pair to continue being
visited often enough.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Unit 3: policy gradient and actor--critic}
\label{unit:3}

\begin{definition}[Expected-return objective \(J(\theta)\)]\label{def:j-theta}
Let $\pi_\theta(a\mid s)$ be a differentiable policy with trainable parameter vector $\theta$.
Together with an initial-state distribution and the environment transition law, this policy
induces a probability distribution over trajectories
\[
\tau=(S_0,A_0,S_1,A_1,\ldots).
\]
The \emph{expected-return objective} is the scalar-valued function
\[
J:\Theta\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},
\qquad
J(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}
\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^tR_{t+1}\right],
\]
or equivalently
\[
J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}[G_0].
\]
The notation $\tau\sim\pi_\theta$ is shorthand for drawing an initial state, drawing each
action from $\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid S_t)$, and drawing each successor state and reward from the
environment. Thus $J(\theta)$ averages the random return of one run over all runs that the
current policy can produce. Policy-gradient methods seek a parameter value
\[
\theta^*\in\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}J(\theta).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{note}[Why introduce \(J(\theta)\) when the return \(G_t\) already exists?]
The two objects describe the same rewards at different levels. The \emph{return}
\[
G_t:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\gamma^jR_{t+1+j}
\]
is a random variable associated with one realized trajectory, viewed from one particular
decision time $t$. It answers: ``after the actions and environmental randomness in this run
have occurred, how much discounted reward remained from time $t$?'' In particular,
$G_0=R_1+\gamma R_2+\cdots$ is the total discounted reward of one complete run.

By contrast,
\[
J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}[G_0]
\]
is a single deterministic number once the policy parameters $\theta$ are fixed. It averages
over all trajectories that could occur: randomness in the initial state, in the policy's
actions when it is stochastic, and in the environment. It answers a different question:
``how good is the entire policy $\pi_\theta$ on average?'' The basic comparison is therefore
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
& G_t & J(\theta)\\
\hline
\text{object} & \text{realized future reward in one run} &
\text{expected performance across all runs}\\
\text{depends on} & \text{a sampled trajectory and time }t &
\text{the policy parameters }\theta\\
\text{role} & \text{feedback/sample evidence} &
\text{objective to maximize when choosing a policy}
\end{array}
\]

The motivation for $J$ is optimization. Reinforcement learning ultimately wants to choose
parameters $\theta$ for a policy, so it needs one criterion that compares, for example,
$\pi_{\theta_1}$ with $\pi_{\theta_2}$. A single $G_0$ is not enough for this comparison:
one policy may obtain a lucky high reward in one run and have poor average performance. The
expectation in $J(\theta)$ removes this run-to-run noise conceptually, and the notation
$J(\theta)$ makes explicit that changing $\theta$ changes the distribution of future
trajectories and hence the expected return.

In practice, the expectation defining $J(\theta)$ is usually not available in closed form.
The agent samples trajectories, computes realized returns such as $G_t$, and uses them to
estimate the gradient of $J$. This is why the policy-gradient formula has both objects:
$\nabla_\theta J(\theta)$ states the quantity to improve, while $G_t$ is the trajectory sample
that tells the update how favorable the sampled decision was. Using $G_t$ rather than the full
$G_0$ at every time $t$ is also a credit-assignment device: rewards before time $t$ cannot be
affected by the action $A_t$, whereas the return-to-go $G_t$ contains only later rewards that
can be affected by it.
\end{note}

The policy-gradient theorem is
commonly written in the score-function form
\phantomsection\label{eq:policy-gradient}
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}
\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}
\gamma^t\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)\,G_t\right].
\]
Under the standard differentiability and integrability conditions that justify exchanging
the derivative and trajectory expectation, it follows from differentiating the trajectory
likelihood and using
$\nabla_\theta p_\theta(\tau)=p_\theta(\tau)\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(\tau)$.
The terms in $\log p_\theta(\tau)$ that arise from environment transitions disappear
because $P$ does not depend on $\theta$.

\begin{note}[Why the policy gradient has this particular \(\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta\) form]
\noindent\textbf{What the gradient means.}
The symbol $\nabla_\theta J(\theta)$ is the vector of derivatives of the average policy
performance with respect to every policy parameter in $\theta$. It is \emph{not} a derivative
with respect to time. A policy-gradient method uses an update of the form
\[
\theta\leftarrow\theta+\eta\,\widehat{\nabla_\theta J(\theta)},
\]
so the formula is meant to estimate a parameter direction that increases expected return. We
cannot normally differentiate a realized return $G_t$ directly: rewards and state transitions
may be random, discrete, or supplied by an unknown environment. Instead, we differentiate the
\emph{probability of sampling the trajectory} that produced the return.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Step 1: the log-derivative trick.}
For simplicity, write the expectation over discrete trajectories as a sum (the continuous case
uses an integral). Since
\[
J(\theta)=\sum_\tau p_\theta(\tau)G_0(\tau),
\]
and the reward attached to a fixed realized trajectory does not itself depend on $\theta$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
&=\sum_\tau \nabla_\theta p_\theta(\tau)\,G_0(\tau)\\
&=\sum_\tau p_\theta(\tau)\,
\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(\tau)\,G_0(\tau)\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}
\left[G_0\,\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(\tau)\right].
\end{aligned}
\]
The middle equality uses
\[
\nabla_\theta p_\theta(\tau)
=p_\theta(\tau)\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(\tau),
\]
which is useful because it moves the derivative onto sampled policy probabilities.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Step 2: only the policy probabilities depend on \(\theta\).}
Write the trajectory out as the alternating sequence
\[
\tau
=
(S_0,A_0,S_1,A_1,S_2,A_2,\dots).
\]
It is generated in order: $S_0\sim p(\,\cdot\,)$, then $A_t\sim\pi_\theta(\,\cdot\mid S_t)$, then $S_{t+1}\sim P(\,\cdot\mid S_t,A_t)$. The chain rule therefore begins as
\[
p_\theta(\tau)
=
p(S_0)\,
p(A_0\mid S_0)\,
p(S_1\mid S_0,A_0)\,
p(A_1\mid S_0,A_0,S_1)\,
p(S_2\mid S_0,A_0,S_1,A_1)
\cdots.
\]
The MDP Markov property cuts the histories: given $S_t$, the action does not depend on earlier states or actions, so $p(A_t\mid S_t,A_{<t},S_{<t})=\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)$; given $(S_t,A_t)$, the next state does not depend on the earlier past, so $p(S_{t+1}\mid S_t,A_t,\text{past})=P(S_{t+1}\mid S_t,A_t)$. The joint therefore collapses to
\[
p_\theta(\tau)
=
p(S_0)\prod_{t\geq0}
\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)P(S_{t+1}\mid S_t,A_t).
\]
If the environment is not parameterized by $\theta$, then
\[
\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(\tau)
=\sum_{t\geq0}\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t).
\]
\noindent Here $\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)$ is called a \emph{score term}.
It points in the parameter direction that would make the sampled action $A_t$ more probable
in state $S_t$. Thus a favorable return multiplying this term reinforces the sampled action,
while an unfavorable return reverses that pressure.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Step 3: why the formula uses \(\gamma^tG_t\).}
Substitution into Step 1 first gives a full-return expression:
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\left[
\sum_{t\geq0}
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\sum_{u\geq0}\gamma^uR_{u+1}
\right].
\]
For a fixed decision time $t$, rewards with $u<t$ occurred before the action $A_t$ was
chosen and cannot be caused by it. Their contributions vanish in expectation after conditioning
on the past,\footnote{Here ``conditioning on the past'' means temporarily treating everything
already observed before choosing $A_t$ as fixed: the earlier states, actions, and rewards,
including $R_{u+1}$ for $u<t$. The only remaining random choice is then
$A_t\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid S_t)$. To see the cancellation without any abbreviation, let
$\mathcal{H}_t$ denote this past information and fix one particular index $u<t$. The exact
claim is that the corresponding term has expectation zero. Its four-step derivation is
\[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\gamma^uR_{u+1}\,
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\right]
=\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\gamma^uR_{u+1}\,
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\;\middle|\;\mathcal{H}_t
\right]\right].
\tag{1}
\]
Equation (1) is the tower property: average first while the past is held fixed, then average
over the possible pasts. Since $u<t$, the scalar $\gamma^uR_{u+1}$ is already known once
$\mathcal{H}_t$ is fixed, so it can be pulled outside the inner expectation:
\[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\gamma^uR_{u+1}\,
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\;\middle|\;\mathcal{H}_t
\right]\right]
=\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\gamma^uR_{u+1}\,
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\;\middle|\;\mathcal{H}_t
\right]\right].
\tag{2}
\]
Conditionally, the only averaging is over $A_t$, whose probabilities are
$\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)$:
\[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\;\middle|\;\mathcal{H}_t
\right]
=\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t).
\tag{3}
\]
Finally, use $\pi_\theta\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta=\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta$ and
the fact that action probabilities sum to one:
\[
\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
=\nabla_\theta\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
=\nabla_\theta 1
=0.
\tag{4}
\]
Combining (1)--(4) proves that \emph{each} summand with $u<t$ has expectation zero; summing
over all such $u$ therefore still gives zero. If $C$ denotes any such already fixed quantity (for
example an earlier reward), then
\[
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
C\,\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\;\middle|\;\text{past up to time }t
\right]
=C\,
\mathbb{E}_{A_t\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid S_t)}
\left[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)\right]
=0.
\]
The cancellation is therefore an \emph{average} statement, not a claim that an earlier reward
is literally zero on each trajectory. Intuitively, changing $\theta$ can redistribute
probability among actions, but the probabilities must still sum to one. In a two-action policy
with $\pi_\theta(a_1\mid s)=p_\theta$ and
$\pi_\theta(a_0\mid s)=1-p_\theta$, the average score is
\[
p_\theta\frac{\nabla_\theta p_\theta}{p_\theta}
+(1-p_\theta)\frac{-\nabla_\theta p_\theta}{1-p_\theta}
=0.
\]
Thus an outcome that occurred before $A_t$ was selected has no systematic gradient signal for
how the probability of $A_t$ should change.} because
\[
\mathbb{E}_{A_t\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid S_t)}
\left[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)\right]
=\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
=\nabla_\theta\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
=0.
\]
The algebraic step between the first sum and the gradient of the probability sum is
\[
\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)\,
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)
=\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t).
\]
Thus the displayed expectation is the gradient of
\(\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid S_t)=1\), which must be zero.
The remaining future-reward suffix is exactly
\[
\sum_{u=t}^{\infty}\gamma^uR_{u+1}
=\gamma^t\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\gamma^jR_{t+1+j}
=\gamma^tG_t.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\left[
\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)G_t
\right],
\]
which is the policy-gradient formula above. It is not an arbitrary gradient shape: the
logarithm comes from differentiating a probability distribution, and $G_t$ appears because it
is precisely the part of the return that the current action can influence.

Differentiating $J(\theta)$ by the log-derivative trick first gives a full-return
estimator. Terms involving rewards before time $t$ vanish after conditioning on the past,
which permits replacement by the return-to-go and then by
$Q^{\pi_\theta}(S_t,A_t)$.\footnote{This replacement is an application of conditional
expectation, not an assertion that a realized random return $G_t$ literally equals its action
value. By definition,
\[
Q^{\pi_\theta}(s,a)
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\!\left[
G_t\mid S_t=s,\ A_t=a
\right].
\]
Thus $Q^{\pi_\theta}(S_t,A_t)$ is the mean of all possible future returns after the currently
realized state--action pair has been fixed. For a stationary MDP and stationary policy, this
conditional mean does not otherwise depend on $t$.

Fix one time $t$ and abbreviate the score multiplier by
\[
Z_t
:=\gamma^t\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t).
\]
Once $(S_t,A_t)$ is known, $Z_t$ is known as well: it is a deterministic function of that
state--action pair. The tower property and the rule for taking a known factor out of a
conditional expectation give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[Z_tG_t]
&=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\!\left[
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[Z_tG_t\mid S_t,A_t]\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\!\left[
Z_t\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[G_t\mid S_t,A_t]\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\!\left[
\gamma^t\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)\,
Q^{\pi_\theta}(S_t,A_t)\right].
\end{aligned}
\]
Summing this identity over $t$ yields the displayed $Q^{\pi_\theta}$ form. The purpose is
often variance reduction: $G_t$ is one noisy sampled return, whereas
$Q^{\pi_\theta}(S_t,A_t)$ is its conditional average. In practice the exact action value is
usually unknown and is estimated by a critic.}:
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\left[
\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
Q^{\pi_\theta}(S_t,A_t)
\right].
\]
This is the six-point derivation pattern used in the Fall 2025 paper. If
$d^\pi(s):=\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t\mathbb{P}_\pi(S_t=s)$ is the unnormalized discounted
state-visitation measure, the same expression is the corresponding sum over states and
actions. A factor $1/(1-\gamma)$ appears only after replacing $d^\pi$ by its normalized
probability version.
\end{note}

\begin{theorem}[Baseline identity]\label{thm:baseline}
For any function $b(s)$ independent of the selected action,
\[
\mathbb{E}_{A\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid s)}
\left[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A\mid s)b(s)\right]=0.
\]
Consequently, each $G_t$ in the policy-gradient estimator may be replaced by
$G_t-b(S_t)$ without changing its expectation. Taking $b=V^\pi$ gives the advantage
signal $A^\pi(S_t,A_t)$ and typically reduces variance.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first prove the displayed identity for a fixed state $s$. Assume for clarity that the action
space is finite; for a continuous action space, replace the sums below by integrals under the
usual regularity conditions allowing differentiation under the integral. Since $b(s)$ is
independent of the action selected at $s$, it is constant when averaging over
$A\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid s)$. The left-hand side is
\[
b(s)\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
=b(s)\sum_a\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
=b(s)\nabla_\theta 1
=0.
\]
The first equality uses
\[
\pi_\theta(a\mid s)\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
=\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta(a\mid s),
\]
while the second uses $\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)=1$. This proves the first claim.

It remains to prove the ``consequently'' statement for the whole trajectory estimator. Define
\[
H
:=\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)G_t
\]
and its baseline-subtracted counterpart
\[
\widetilde H
:=\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\bigl(G_t-b(S_t)\bigr).
\]
Subtracting the two estimators and applying the tower property conditional on $S_t$ gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[H-\widetilde H]
&=\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\!\left[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)b(S_t)\right]\\
&=\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\!\left[
\mathbb{E}_{A_t\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid S_t)}
\left[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)b(S_t)
\;\middle|\;S_t
\right]\right]\\
&=\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[0]\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Therefore $\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[\widetilde H]
=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[H]=\nabla_\theta J(\theta)$, which proves that the baseline does
not change the expected policy gradient.

Finally, choose $b=V^\pi$. Since
\[
Q^\pi(S_t,A_t)
=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t,A_t],
\]
we have
\[
\mathbb{E}_\pi\!\left[
G_t-V^\pi(S_t)\mid S_t,A_t
\right]
=Q^\pi(S_t,A_t)-V^\pi(S_t)
=A^\pi(S_t,A_t).
\]
Thus $G_t-V^\pi(S_t)$ is a sample signal whose conditional mean is the advantage: it tells
whether the selected action was better or worse than the policy's ordinary continuation from
the same state. The centering often reduces sampling variability, but the unbiasedness proved
above does not rely on any particular amount of variance reduction.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Actor--critic]\label{def:actor-critic}
An actor--critic method maintains an actor $\pi_\theta(a\mid s)$ and a critic
$V_\phi(s)$ or $Q_\phi(s,a)$. The critic is fitted from sampled TD targets, for example
\[
\delta_t:=R_{t+1}+\gamma V_\phi(S_{t+1})-V_\phi(S_t),
\]
and the actor update uses $\delta_t$ or another estimated advantage instead of the exact
return $G_t$. With an exact critic, the conditional expectation of this TD signal is an
advantage; with a learned bootstrapped critic, it is a lower-variance but generally biased
approximation.
When $P$ is unknown, the critic supplies the learned estimate that policy evaluation would
otherwise obtain by planning with the model.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[A one-state policy-gradient calculation]
Consider a one-step bandit with actions $a_1,a_0$, rewards $1,0$, and policy
\[
\pi_\theta(a_1\mid s)=\sigma(\theta),
\qquad
\pi_\theta(a_0\mid s)=1-\sigma(\theta).
\]
Here
\[
\sigma(\theta):=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\theta}}
\]
is the sigmoid function. There is only one decision, no later state, and no discounting issue,
so the return $G_0$ is exactly the observed reward $R$. Thus the definition of $J$ gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
J(\theta)
&=\mathbb{E}_{A\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid s)}[R]\\
&=\pi_\theta(a_1\mid s)\cdot1+\pi_\theta(a_0\mid s)\cdot0\\
&=\sigma(\theta).
\end{aligned}
\]
This is the \emph{direct} route: it uses the definition of $J$, not the score-function formula.
Because $\theta$ is a scalar in this example, $\nabla_\theta$ is an ordinary derivative.
Moreover,
\[
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\sigma(\theta)
=\frac{e^{-\theta}}{(1+e^{-\theta})^2}
=\sigma(\theta)\bigl(1-\sigma(\theta)\bigr).
\]
Therefore direct differentiation gives
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)=\sigma(\theta)\bigl(1-\sigma(\theta)\bigr).
\]
The score-function identity is a \emph{second route} that must give the same answer. Its
terms for the two possible sampled actions are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
\text{action} & R &
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A\mid s) &
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A\mid s)\,R\\
\hline
a_1 & 1 &
\nabla_\theta\log\sigma(\theta)=1-\sigma(\theta) &
1-\sigma(\theta)\\
a_0 & 0 &
\nabla_\theta\log(1-\sigma(\theta))=-\sigma(\theta) &
0
\end{array}
\]
The action $a_1$ contributes its score because its reward is $1$; the action $a_0$ contributes
zero because its reward is $0$. Taking the probability-weighted average gives
\[
\mathbb{E}\left[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A\mid s)R
\right]
=\pi_\theta(a_1\mid s)\bigl(1-\sigma(\theta)\bigr)
=\sigma(\theta)\bigl(1-\sigma(\theta)\bigr).
\]
This agrees with the direct derivative above, so the policy-gradient formula is verified in a
case where $J(\theta)$ can be computed exactly. The example has no delayed credit assignment,
but it isolates the mechanism by which a sampled reward changes the probability of the sampled
action.
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[Unit 3: baseline and critic signal]
First prove, for a finite action space, that a baseline $b(s)$ independent of the sampled
action contributes zero expected score gradient. Next suppose an actor--critic transition
has $R_{t+1}=1$, $\gamma=0.9$, $V_\phi(S_t)=3$, and $V_\phi(S_{t+1})=5$. Compute
$\delta_t$ and state whether the sampled action is reinforced or discouraged.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Unit 3: baseline and critic signal]
Condition on a fixed state $s$. Since $b(s)$ does not depend on which action is sampled, it
can be factored outside the action sum:
\[
\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid s)b(s)
=b(s)\sum_a\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
=b(s)\nabla_\theta\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
=b(s)\nabla_\theta 1=0.
\]
Thus subtracting $b(s)$ changes the random estimator's variance but not its expectation, so
it does not bias the policy gradient.

For the stated transition, the one-step TD error is
\[
\delta_t
=R_{t+1}+\gamma V_\phi(S_{t+1})-V_\phi(S_t)
=1+0.9(5)-3
=2.5.
\]
It is positive, so the observed outcome was better than the critic predicted. In the usual
actor update, this positive signal reinforces the sampled action by increasing its
probability. This conclusion is relative to the current critic estimate; the critic becomes
reliable only through repeated TD fitting.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Unit 4: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) and preference alignment}
\label{unit:4}

For language-model alignment, a prompt $x$ plays the role of a state or context, a full
completion $y$ plays the role of an action, and the language model is a contextual policy
$\pi_\theta(y\mid x)$. Alongside the trainable policy one keeps a frozen
\emph{reference policy} $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)$, typically the supervised
fine-tuned copy of the same model, from which $\pi_\theta$ is initialized and against
which later updates will be measured; see \cref{def:j-rlhf}. Preference data contain
triples $(x,y^+,y^-)$ indicating that $y^+$ is preferred to $y^-$. A reward model
$r_\phi(x,y)$ can be trained with the Bradley--Terry loss
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)
=-\mathbb{E}_{(x,y^+,y^-)}
\log\sigma\!\left(r_\phi(x,y^+)-r_\phi(x,y^-)\right),
\]
where $\sigma(u):=(1+e^{-u})^{-1}$.

\begin{remark}[Why introduce a Bradley--Terry reward-model loss?]\label{rem:rm-bt}
The earlier RL framework assumes that, after an action, the environment supplies a numerical
reward $R_{t+1}$. In language-model alignment, however, one often does \emph{not} have such a
ready-made scalar target. A human reviewer can much more naturally compare two completions to
the same prompt and say ``$y^+$ is better than $y^-$'' than assign an objectively calibrated
number such as $7.3$ to one completion. A preference pair therefore tells us an \emph{ordering},
not an absolute reward.

The role of the reward model $r_\phi(x,y)$ is to turn this comparison-only feedback into a
scalar signal that an RL algorithm can subsequently optimize. The Bradley--Terry modeling
assumption\footnote{A preference pair is a noisy binary observation, not a numerical
reward, so some likelihood relating latent scores to $\mathbb{P}(y^+\succ y^-\mid x)$ is
required before $r_\phi$ can be estimated from $\{(x,y^+,y^-)\}$. Bradley--Terry is the
random-utility model in which each completion has latent utility
$U_y=r_\phi(x,y)+\epsilon_y$ with i.i.d.\ Gumbel noise $\epsilon_y$. Then
\[
\mathbb{P}(U_{y^+}>U_{y^-}\mid x)
=\sigma\!\bigl(r_\phi(x,y^+)-r_\phi(x,y^-)\bigr),
\]
which is also Luce's choice axiom restricted to two alternatives. The logistic of a score
difference is therefore not an arbitrary link: it is the closed-form model induced by
i.i.d.\ extreme-value noise; it depends on $r_\phi$ only through differences, matching
the fact that a comparison never identifies an additive constant; and it is strictly
monotone, so later maximizing $r_\phi$ ranks completions in the same order as the
implied win probabilities. The displayed loss is ordinary Bernoulli negative
log-likelihood (logistic regression on those differences). A Gaussian-noise
(Thurstone--Mosteller) alternative yields a probit link and is statistically similar, but
lacks a closed-form CDF and is not the form used in standard RLHF.} is
\[
\mathbb{P}_\phi\!\left(y^+\succ y^-\mid x\right)
=\sigma\!\left(r_\phi(x,y^+)-r_\phi(x,y^-)\right).
\]
Thus the model predicts a high probability of the observed preference precisely when its score
for the preferred completion exceeds its score for the rejected completion. The loss in the
preceding display is the negative log-likelihood of all observed comparisons under this
probability model. Minimizing it chooses parameters $\widehat\phi$ that make the observed
human comparisons likely:
\[
\widehat\phi\in\arg\min_\phi\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi).
\]

Only the difference
$r_\phi(x,y^+)-r_\phi(x,y^-)$ appears. This is appropriate because a comparison identifies
which completion is preferred but not a universal zero point: adding the same constant to both
scores leaves every predicted preference probability unchanged. Hence the fitted reward is not
an objectively measured ``true utility'' on an absolute scale. It is a learned surrogate score
whose useful property is that it ranks completions in agreement with the preference data.

After fitting, reward-model RLHF treats $r_{\widehat\phi}(x,y)$ as the scalar reward in the
KL-regularized objective of \cref{def:j-rlhf} and optimizes the policy toward high-scoring
completions. This extra modeling stage is needed because ordinary RL cannot directly
optimize a data set that contains only pairwise statements $y^+\succ y^-$. It is not the
only possible route: \jump{rem:dpo}{DPO}, introduced below, avoids fitting a separate
scalar reward model and uses the same preference pairs directly to train the policy.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[KL-regularized RLHF objective]\label{def:j-rlhf}
Let $\pi_\theta(y\mid x)$ be the trainable language-model policy, with parameter $\theta$
in the same role as in \cref{def:j-theta}. Let $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)$ be a
\emph{frozen} reference policy, typically the supervised fine-tuned model at which
$\pi_\theta$ is initialized, and which is not updated during the RL stage. Let $r_\phi$
be an already fitted reward model as in \cref{rem:rm-bt}, and let $\mathcal{D}$ be a
