distribution over prompts. Reward-model RLHF updates $\theta$ by maximizing
\[
J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(\theta)
:=
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\bigl[r_\phi(x,y)\bigr]
-\beta\,
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}
\left[
\mathrm{KL}\bigl(
\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)
\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)
\bigr)
\right],
\]
where $\beta>0$ controls how strongly the learned policy is kept near the reference.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[What $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ is]\label{rem:j-rlhf}
The letter $J$ is the standard RL name for a \emph{performance objective}: a scalar that
one \emph{maximizes}, not a supervised loss that one minimizes. In \cref{def:j-theta},
\[
J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[G_0]
\]
was expected return along a whole trajectory. The same object is being formed here, with
a language-model identification of the MDP slots. A prompt $x$ is the context; a
completion $y$ is treated as a single action rather than a long chain of token-level
steps; and $\pi_\theta(y\mid x)$ is the current trainable policy, the same $\theta$ as in
\unitref{3}. The already-fitted scalar $r_\phi(x,y)$ occupies the slot of the return
$G_0$ of that action---or equivalently of the one-step reward $R_{t+1}$ in \unitref{0}, since
there is only one decision. It is \emph{not} an environment-supplied score such as a game
point or a travel-time cost. As in \cref{rem:rm-bt}, it is a learned surrogate that
converts pairwise human comparisons into a number an RL algorithm can maximize. After the
Bradley--Terry stage, $\phi$ is frozen; only $\theta$ moves.
That is the meaning of a \emph{fixed} reward in the Gibbs argument of
\cref{rem:dpo-gibbs}: $r$ still depends on $\phi$, but by Stage~2 the map
$(x,y)\mapsto r_{\widehat\phi}(x,y)$ is a frozen function, not a second trainable
argument of $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$.

The first term is therefore expected reward under the current policy,
\[
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}\bigl[r_\phi(x,y)\bigr],
\]
and it is optimized by the \jump{eq:policy-gradient}{policy-gradient} mechanism of
\unitref{3}. That term alone is not a safe alignment objective. Because $r_\phi$ is a
learned surrogate, a policy allowed to wander arbitrarily far from the pretrained model
can find high-scoring completions that exploit idiosyncrasies of $r_\phi$ rather than
genuine human preference. This is \emph{reward hacking}.\footnote{Reward hacking is the
failure mode in which a policy maximizes a \emph{proxy} for the intended objective rather
than the intended objective itself: Goodhart's law applied to a fitted reward. The
intended target is human preference, but the algorithm only ever sees $r_\phi$, a network
trained on a finite comparison set. That network necessarily has idiosyncratic
high-score regions---stock phrases that happened to correlate with preference in the
data, completions far off the training support of $r_\phi$, or even near-nonsensical
token strings to which the model assigns a spuriously large value. An unconstrained
maximizer of $\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[r_\phi]$ will seek those regions, producing
verbose, repetitive, sycophantic, or degenerate completions that a human rater would
reject, while $r_\phi$ reports a high score. A standard illustration outside language
models is an agent in a racing game that learns to drive in circles collecting bonus
items instead of finishing the race, because the bonus items dominate the shaped reward.
The KL term is a trust-region against this: it keeps $\pi_\theta$ near
$\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$, which is the region where $r_\phi$ was actually trained and is
therefore a more reliable ranking of genuine preference.}

The two arguments of the KL are not two competing learners. The current policy
$\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)$ is the object being optimized: it is the language model's
distribution over completions given the prompt, and $\theta$ is the trainable parameter.
The reference $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)$ is a \emph{fixed} baseline for the same
conditional, typically the supervised fine-tuned model. It has no trainable parameter in
this stage; its role is to mark the region of token space where ordinary language remains
intact and where $r_\phi$ is a more trustworthy ranking. Expanding the definition,
\[
\mathrm{KL}\bigl(
\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)
\bigr)
=\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}.
\]
The KL is nonnegative and vanishes if and only if
$\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)=\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)$. It is \emph{subtracted},
rather than added, because $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ is maximized: subtracting a nonnegative
quantity charges the policy for changing the completion distribution away from the
reference. Adding it would reward drift. Equivalently,
\[
J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\Bigl[
r_\phi(x,y)
-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
\Bigr],
\]
so the KL is simply a modification of the per-completion return: the policy still
maximizes an expected return, as in \cref{def:j-theta}, but each sampled $y$ is scored by
$r_\phi$ minus a cost for being more probable under $\pi_\theta$ than under
$\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$. Larger $\beta$ means a tighter leash.

Thus $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ is not a likelihood of preference data. The comparisons
$(x,y^+,y^-)$ were already consumed when $r_\phi$ was fitted. At this stage one samples
fresh completions $y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)$, scores them with the frozen reward
model, and updates $\theta$ by a policy-gradient method (commonly PPO). This is the
object \jump{prob:spr2026-rlhf}{Spring 2026 Problem~7} asks for, once it assumes that a
reward model has already been trained.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ is written this way]\label{rem:j-rlhf-shape}
The display in \cref{def:j-rlhf} is Unit~3's $J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[G_0]$, rewritten for a
language model and then given a leash. It is not a unique law of nature, but each
slot is forced by a different job: what is being scored, who generates the
completion, and how far the policy is allowed to drift.

The prompt $x$ is exogenous. The policy generates $y\mid x$, not $x$. Prompts come
from the distribution one actually serves---the user stream, or a curated prompt
set---so the outer law is $x\sim\mathcal{D}$. There is no reason to average
uniformly over all strings, and no reason to let $\pi_\theta$ sample $x$. The KL
term has the same outer expectation because
$\mathrm{KL}(\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x))$ is already
a distance between \emph{conditional} completion laws at a fixed $x$. Averaging over
$\mathcal{D}$ asks: on the prompts we care about, how far has the answer distribution
moved.

The completion must be $y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)$. The object being optimized is
the current policy, so expected reward is the mean score of completions \emph{that
policy would emit}. Sampling $y$ from the data set or from $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$ would
evaluate a different behaviour. Because $r_\phi$ is frozen, $\theta$ enters the first
term only through this sampling law: change $\pi_\theta$, change which $y$ appear,
change $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$. That is why the RL stage is on-policy, and why a
policy-gradient update is the native estimator.

The KL is written $\pi_\theta\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$ and subtracted. Expanding,
\[
\mathrm{KL}\bigl(
\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)
\bigr)
=\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)},
\]
so the penalty is averaged under the same on-policy law as the reward. One is
charging the completions the current model actually produces, not the completions
the reference would have produced. The reverse order
$\mathrm{KL}(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\,\|\,\pi_\theta)$ would average under
$\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$ and impose a different geometry. The minus sign is required
because $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ is maximized: the KL is nonnegative and vanishes if and
only if the two conditionals agree, so subtracting it taxes drift. Adding it would
pay the policy to leave $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$. The constraint is on $y\mid x$ only;
regularizing a joint law over $(x,y)$ would pretend that $\theta$ generates prompts.

The two-expectation layout is cosmetic. The inner average in the KL is already over
$y\sim\pi_\theta$, so the whole objective is one on-policy expectation
\[
J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\Bigl[
r_\phi(x,y)
-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
\Bigr].
\]
Read in one sentence: on the real prompt distribution, score the current policy's
own samples with the frozen surrogate $r_\phi$, and charge those same samples for
log-ratio drift from the frozen SFT reference. That is the entire shape.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[A two-completion RLHF toy]
\label{ex:rlhf-toy}
One prompt and two possible completions:
\[
x=\text{``2+2=?''},
\qquad
y_a=\text{``4''},
\qquad
y_b=\text{``5''}.
\]
The policy is a single Bernoulli parameter. Write
\[
\pi_\theta(y_a\mid x)=p,
\qquad
\pi_\theta(y_b\mid x)=1-p,
\qquad
p=\sigma(\theta)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\theta}}.
\]
The frozen reference is the SFT model, say $p_{\mathrm{ref}}=\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_a\mid x)=0.7$. The reward model, in this toy, is just two numbers $\phi=(r_a,r_b)$ with $r_a=r_\phi(x,y_a)$ and $r_b=r_\phi(x,y_b)$. In a real implementation $\phi$ is the weight vector of a language-model backbone plus a scalar head; the role is the same.

\begin{center}
{\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|p{3.6cm}|X|}
\hline
\textbf{Status} & \textbf{In this toy} \\ \hline
Known (samples) & the prompt \(x\); the two completions; the human comparison \(y_a\succ y_b\) \\ \hline
Known (chosen) & the architecture \(p=\sigma(\theta)\); the BT loss; \(\beta>0\); the frozen \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) \\ \hline
Unknown & a closed-form human utility, or a density \(p(y\mid x)\) \\ \hline
Stage 1 learns & \(\phi=(r_a,r_b)\) \\ \hline
Then frozen & \(r_{\widehat\phi}\), e.g.\ \(r_a=2\), \(r_b=0\) \\ \hline
Stage 2 learns & \(\theta\), equivalently \(p\) \\ \hline
Not learned & \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\), \(\beta\), the already labelled pair \\ \hline
\end{tabularx}
}
\end{center}

The human does \emph{not} prescribe ``correct answers score \(2\)''. The human only clicks that \(y_a\) is better than \(y_b\).

\paragraph{Stage 1.}
The single preference triple is \((x,y^+,y^-)=(x,y_a,y_b)\). Bradley--Terry gives
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)
=
-\log\sigma(r_a-r_b).
\]
Minimizing it forces \(r_a>r_b\). Suppose the fit is \(r_a=2\), \(r_b=0\). Then \(\phi\) is frozen.

\paragraph{Stage 2.}
The KL-regularized objective of \cref{def:j-rlhf} collapses to a function of \(p\):
\[
J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(p)
=
p\cdot 2+(1-p)\cdot 0
-\beta\Bigl(
p\log\frac{p}{0.7}
+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{0.3}
\Bigr).
\]
The first term is expected reward under the current policy (the slot occupied by \(\mathbb{E}[G_0]\) in \cref{def:j-theta}). The second term penalizes drift from \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\). Maximizing \(J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}\) raises \(p\) above \(0.7\) because \(r_a>r_b\), but the KL keeps \(p\) from collapsing to \(1\). The update is still a policy-gradient step: sample \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\,\cdot\mid x)\), multiply by the frozen \(r_{\widehat\phi}(x,y)\), and subtract the KL contribution.

\paragraph{Dictionary.}
A prompt is the state; a full completion is the action; \(r_{\widehat\phi}(x,y)\) occupies the slot of \(G_0\) (or of a one-step \(R\)); \(J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}\) is the same letter \(J\) as in Unit~3, with a learned surrogate reward and a KL leash. Comparisons \((x,y^+,y^-)\) are consumed in Stage~1 and do not reappear as a likelihood in Stage~2.
\end{example}

Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is an alternative that uses the same preference
pairs to train the policy directly, without a separate reward model and without
on-policy RL. Its common objective is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}(\theta)
:={}&-\mathbb{E}_{(x,y^+,y^-)}
\log\sigma\Bigg(
\beta\Big[
\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y^+\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y^+\mid x)}
-\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y^-\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y^-\mid x)}
\Big]\Bigg).
\end{aligned}
\]

\begin{remark}[What the DPO loss is]\label{rem:dpo}
DPO does not introduce a new alignment \emph{goal}. It is a change of variables that
implements the same KL-regularized preference model without a separate reward network
and without on-policy RL.

For a fixed reward $r$---fixed in the sense of \cref{rem:j-rlhf}, not independent
of $\phi$---the unconstrained maximizer of $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$
(\cref{def:j-rlhf}) has the Gibbs
form
\[
\pi_r(y\mid x)
=\frac{1}{Z(x)}\,
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)\,
\exp\bigl(r(x,y)/\beta\bigr),
\]
where $Z(x)$ is the partition function. Inverting gives the implicit reward
\[
r(x,y)
=\beta\log\frac{\pi_r(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}+\beta\log Z(x).
\]
The partition function cancels in a pairwise difference, so the Bradley--Terry
probability becomes a function of policy log-ratios only. Substituting that difference
into $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}$ (\cref{rem:rm-bt}) produces $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}$. Minimizing the
DPO loss therefore fits a policy whose implied reward ranks $(y^+,y^-)$ in agreement
with the observed preferences, while the ratios against $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$ retain the
same KL geometry as $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$.

Operationally, DPO is offline supervised learning on a fixed preference data set: each
update looks only at pairs already in the data, not at new rollouts
$y\sim\pi_\theta$. That is its niche: same preference likelihood, no reward-model
training stage, no PPO loop. The cost is that the policy cannot query a scalar scorer
on completions it has just invented, so there is no on-policy exploration against
$r_\phi$.
The Gibbs display itself is a theorem, not a modelling assumption; the precise
claim, the two KLs, and the extra hypotheses needed to pass from RLHF to DPO are
in \cref{rem:dpo-gibbs}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why $\pi_r$ is Gibbs, and what ``fixed $r$'' means]\label{rem:dpo-gibbs}
The distribution $\pi_r$ is not an assumption placed on the policy. It is the
unique unconstrained maximizer of $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ once a reward function has
been frozen. One does not write the trainable $\pi$ in Gibbs form and then
optimize. One \emph{constructs} an auxiliary target $\pi_r$ from the frozen $r$
and $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$, and then recognizes that $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ is a KL
to that target.

Fix a prompt $x$ and drop it from the notation. For an arbitrary conditional
$\pi$ and a fixed scalar function $r$,
\[
\sum_y\pi(y)\,r(y)
-\beta\,\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\bigr)
=
-\beta\,\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\pi\,\|\,\pi_r\bigr)
+\beta\log Z,
\]
where $\pi_r\propto\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\,e^{r/\beta}$ is the display in
\cref{rem:dpo} and $Z$ collects the normalizing constant. The left-hand side is
$J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ at this $x$. The right-hand side is at most $\beta\log Z$,
with equality if and only if $\pi=\pi_r$. Hence
\[
\max_\pi J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\min_\pi\mathrm{KL}(\pi\,\|\,\pi_r),
\]
and the unique solution is the Gibbs law. The same stationarity condition comes
from a Lagrange multiplier on $\sum_y\pi(y)=1$. The exponential tilt
$\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\,e^{r/\beta}$ is what that calculation produces; it is the
same shape as a Boltzmann distribution, with $r$ playing the role of a negative
energy and $\beta$ a temperature.

This identity is a \emph{characterization of the optimum}, not a training recipe.
One does not minimize $\mathrm{KL}(\pi\,\|\,\pi_r)$ in code. The target $\pi_r$
is defined in terms of $r$; if it were already known, the Stage~2 problem would
be finished. Classical RLHF still maximizes the original $J$ by a
policy-gradient method. DPO inverts the same optimality relation and eliminates
$r$, as in \cref{rem:dpo}.

Two Kullback--Leibler divergences appear, and they are not the same object. The
leash in \cref{def:j-rlhf} is $\mathrm{KL}(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})$: a
penalty for leaving the SFT reference. After completing the square, both the
reward term and that leash are absorbed into a single
$\mathrm{KL}(\pi\,\|\,\pi_r)$ against the Gibbs target. The second argument
changed. Rewriting $J$ as one KL does not mean the original regularizer was
discarded; it was folded into the definition of $\pi_r$.

``Fixed $r$'' does not mean $r$ is independent of $\phi$. In Stage~1, $r_\phi$
is trained and $\phi$ moves. After $\widehat\phi$ is obtained, the map
$(x,y)\mapsto r_{\widehat\phi}(x,y)$ is locked. The Gibbs theorem is a statement
about Stage~2 only: given that frozen function, the best unconstrained
conditional is $\pi_r$. If $\phi$ were still being updated jointly with $\pi$,
the maximizer would move with $r_\phi$ and there would be no single closed-form
$\pi_r$. In the two-completion toy of \cref{ex:rlhf-toy}, ``fixed $r$'' is the
step that freezes $(r_a,r_b)=(2,0)$ before $J(p)$ is maximized in $p$.

RLHF does not automatically become DPO. The Gibbs form is a theorem in the
unconstrained space of all conditionals, for a frozen $r$, with $\beta>0$ and
$Z(x)<\infty$, and with the KL ordered as
$\mathrm{KL}(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})$. Passing to DPO adds modelling
hypotheses. The parametric family $\pi_\theta$ must be rich enough to
approximate $\pi_r$; otherwise PPO on $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(r_{\widehat\phi})$ and
minimization of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}$ can converge to different points.
The explicit class $\{r_\phi\}$ and the implicit class
$\{\beta\log(\pi_\theta/\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})+c(x)\}$ need not coincide, so the
fitted rankings need not coincide. Stage~2 of Route~A scores fresh
$y\sim\pi_\theta$; DPO only reweights already labelled pairs. Pairwise data
identify $r$ only up to a function of $x$, which is why $Z(x)$ cancels and why
DPO recovers an ordering rather than one particular numerical $r_{\widehat\phi}$.
Online or iterative DPO puts the missing on-policy samples back; it does not
remove these hypotheses.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What DPO changes relative to RLHF]\label{rem:dpo-upgrade}
DPO is not a new alignment problem. The target is still the same as
$J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ (\cref{def:j-rlhf}): raise the chance of preferred completions
while remaining near $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$. What changes is the
\emph{implementation}. Classical RLHF is a two-stage pipeline. Stage~1 fits an
explicit scalar $r_\phi$ from pairwise comparisons. Stage~2 freezes that scorer and
runs on-policy RL (typically PPO) to maximize expected $r_\phi$ under a KL leash.
That second stage is the expensive part: fresh rollouts $y\sim\pi_\theta$, a
variance-reduced policy-gradient loop, and an extra failure mode in which the policy
hacks the proxy $r_\phi$.

DPO collapses the two stages into one supervised update on $\pi_\theta$. It keeps the
preference triples $(x,y^+,y^-)$, the Bradley--Terry likelihood, the frozen reference
$\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$, and the same KL geometry. It discards the standalone reward
network and the PPO loop. The upgrade is a change of variables
(\cref{rem:dpo}): the optimal policy of $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ already encodes $r$ as a
log-ratio against $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$, so one may fit that implicit reward
directly. Operationally the update no longer samples new completions from the current
policy; it only reweights labeled pairs already in the data. That is why DPO is
cheaper and more stable to run, and also why it cannot score a completion the model
has just invented. Later variants (online/iterative DPO, IPO, ORPO, KTO) mostly put
back exploration or change the loss shape; they do not replace the alignment goal.
The shared goal is the unconstrained KL-regularized preference model of
\cref{rem:dpo-gibbs}, not an automatic identity of finite-sample PPO and DPO
minima.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Overview: three objects in the RLHF pipeline]\label{rem:rlhf-overview}
These three displays are not three competing losses for the same job. They occupy
different stages, train different parameters, and consume different data. Two nearby
objects are easy to confuse with them and should be kept off the list:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{SFT} (supervised fine-tuning) is next-token cross-entropy on
    demonstration data; see \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-sft}. It clones human-written
    answers and typically produces the reference policy $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$. It is
    not a preference loss.
    \item \textbf{PPO} is a variance-reduced policy-gradient \emph{algorithm} commonly
    used to maximize $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ (\cref{def:j-rlhf}). It is not a fourth objective.
\end{itemize}

Route~A is classical reward-model RLHF: fit $r_\phi$ from comparisons, then treat the
language model as an RL policy that maximizes expected $r_\phi$ under a KL leash.
Route~B is DPO: skip the explicit $r_\phi$ and fold the same KL-regularized Bradley--Terry
model into a supervised loss on $\pi_\theta$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  font=\footnotesize,
  node distance=6mm and 11mm,
  box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, inner sep=3.5pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth}, thick, draw=black!65}
]
\node[box] (pref) {preference pairs $(x,y^+,y^-)$};
\node[box, below left=12mm and 16mm of pref] (rm) {$\min_\phi\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}$};
\node[box, below=of rm] (rew) {reward model $r_\phi$};
\node[box, below=of rew] (jobj) {$\max_\theta J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$};
\node[box, below right=12mm and 16mm of pref] (dpo) {$\min_\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}$};
\node[box, below=34mm of pref] (pi) {aligned policy $\pi_\theta$};
\draw[arr] (pref) -- node[left, font=\scriptsize, pos=0.35] {Route A} (rm);
\draw[arr] (rm) -- (rew);
\draw[arr] (rew) -- (jobj);
\draw[arr] (jobj) -- (pi);
\draw[arr] (pref) -- node[right, font=\scriptsize, pos=0.35] {Route B} (dpo);
\draw[arr] (dpo) -- (pi);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.2pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.22}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.15cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
& $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)$
& $J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(\theta)$
& $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}(\theta)$ \\
\hline
Type &
supervised loss; \emph{minimize} &
RL objective; \emph{maximize} &
supervised loss; \emph{minimize} \\
\hline
Trains &
reward model $r_\phi$ only &
policy $\pi_\theta$ &
policy $\pi_\theta$ \\
\hline
Data &
frozen pairs $(x,y^+,y^-)$ &
fresh $y\sim\pi_\theta$, scored by frozen $r_\phi$ &
frozen pairs $(x,y^+,y^-)$ \\
\hline
Needs $r_\phi$? &
this is how $r_\phi$ is born &
yes, already fitted &
no; $r$ is implicit in $\log(\pi_\theta/\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})$ \\
\hline
On-policy? &
no &
yes &
no \\
\hline
Niche &

same KL-preference model as Route~A, written directly in policy coordinates \\
\hline
Use when &
you will run reward-model RL, or need to score new completions not in the data &
$r_\phi$ exists; you want on-policy exploration; \jump{prob:spr2026-rlhf}{Spring 2026 Problem~7} &
offline preference data; you want to avoid RM training and the PPO loop \\
\hline
Distinct risk &
a misspecified $r_\phi$ that later gets exploited &
reward hacking: $\pi_\theta$ over-optimizes $r_\phi$ &
overfit the labeled pairs; no exploration on new $y$ \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
}

The three objects are listed together because an exam answer must name the \emph{right
stage}. If the problem says a reward model has already been trained, write
$J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}$ from \cref{def:j-rlhf} (or its
\jump{eq:policy-gradient}{policy-gradient} estimator), not $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}$
and not DPO. If the problem gives only preference pairs and asks for a direct policy
objective, write $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}$ (\cref{rem:dpo}). If it asks how human
comparisons become a scalar reward, write $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}$
(\cref{rem:rm-bt}).
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[One preference-pair loss]
For one preference triple $(x,y^+,y^-)$, suppose a reward model assigns
\[
r_\phi(x,y^+)=2,
\qquad
r_\phi(x,y^-)=0.
\]
Its Bradley--Terry loss for this pair is
\[
-\log\sigma(2)\approx 0.127.
\]
If instead the reward ordering were reversed, the loss would be
$-\log\sigma(-2)\approx 2.127$, much larger. Thus the loss does not demand an absolute
reward scale; it rewards a positive \emph{difference} between the preferred and rejected
completion.
\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[Unit 4: preference objective diagnosis]
For a fixed prompt $x$, explain the meaning of each term in
\[
\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}[r_\phi(x,y)]
-\beta\,\mathrm{KL}\bigl(
\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)
\bigr).
\]
Then, in the DPO objective, state whether the loss decreases or increases when the
log-probability ratio of $y^+$ relative to $\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$ rises while the analogous
ratio for $y^-$ is fixed.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Unit 4: preference objective diagnosis]
For a fixed prompt $x$, the reward term
\[
\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}[r_\phi(x,y)]
\]
is the average score assigned by the learned reward model to completions sampled from the
current policy. Maximizing it encourages the policy to assign more probability to completions
that people are predicted to prefer. The second term
\[
-\beta\,\mathrm{KL}\bigl(
\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)
\bigr)
\]
penalizes departure from the reference policy. Its coefficient $\beta$ sets the trade-off:
larger $\beta$ keeps the learned policy closer to the pretrained/reference behavior, whereas
smaller $\beta$ permits more change in pursuit of reward.

For DPO, write the relevant logit for one preference pair as
\[
z_\theta
=\beta\left[
\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y^+\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y^+\mid x)}
-\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y^-\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y^-\mid x)}
\right].
\]
The pair loss is $-\log\sigma(z_\theta)$. If the log-probability ratio of $y^+$ rises while
the ratio of $y^-$ is fixed, then $z_\theta$ rises. Since $\sigma$ is increasing,
$\sigma(z_\theta)$ rises and $-\log\sigma(z_\theta)$ decreases. Therefore this change is
rewarded by the DPO objective: it makes the preferred completion relatively more likely than
the rejected completion, compared with the reference policy.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Unit 5: four-week zero-background learning route}
\label{unit:5}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Week 1 --- MDP language.} Learn the \unitref{0} objects; derive $V^\pi$ and
    $Q^\pi$ from the first-step decomposition; solve the two-state example without notes.
    \item \textbf{Week 2 --- Bellman and control.} Derive both Bellman expectation and
    optimality equations (\unitref{1}); explain policy evaluation, improvement, and iteration in words;
    implement or hand-calculate two Q-learning updates (\unitref{2}).
    \item \textbf{Week 3 --- gradients from trajectories.} Derive the
    \jump{eq:policy-gradient}{score-function gradient} (\unitref{3}); prove the
    \jump{thm:baseline}{baseline identity}; explain why an
    \jump{def:actor-critic}{actor--critic} can operate when $P$ is unknown.
    \item \textbf{Week 4 --- alignment and timed rehearsal.} Write the reward-model,
    KL-regularized RLHF, and DPO objectives (\unitref{4}); complete the drills in
    \unitref{6} closed-book; compare the result against the answer skeleton.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{note}[Weekly mastery gates]
Do not advance because a formula looks familiar. At the end of Week 1, derive a Bellman
equation from $G_t=R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}$. At the end of Week 2, compute a Q-learning
update and explain why it differs from planning. At the end of Week 3, reproduce the
policy-gradient formula with the factor $\gamma^t$ and prove the baseline identity. At the
end of Week 4, write either the KL-regularized RLHF objective (\cref{def:j-rlhf}) or the
DPO loss (\cref{rem:dpo}) from memory
and explain every random variable and hyperparameter in it.
\end{note}

\subsubsection{Unit 6: closed-book drills and answer skeleton}
\label{unit:6}

\begin{exercise}[Bellman derivation]
Starting from $G_t=R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}$, derive the Bellman expectation equation for
$V^\pi(s)$ and state the corresponding policy-improvement rule.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Bellman derivation]
Start from the one-step decomposition of the return and condition on the current state:
\[
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s)
&=\mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t\mid S_t=s]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_\pi[R_{t+1}+\gamma G_{t+1}\mid S_t=s].
\end{aligned}
\]
The policy chooses $a$ with probability $\pi(a\mid s)$, and after this action the
environment reaches $s'$ with probability $P(s'\mid s,a)$. Conditional on
$S_{t+1}=s'$, the expected remaining return is $V^\pi(s')$. Therefore
\[
V^\pi(s)
=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a\mid s)
\left[
r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)V^\pi(s')
\right].
\]
Having evaluated $V^\pi$, policy improvement chooses an action with the largest one-step
look-ahead value:
\[
\pi_{\mathrm{new}}(s)\in
\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
\left[
r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'\in\mathcal{S}}
P(s'\mid s,a)V^\pi(s')
\right]
=\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^\pi(s,a).
\]
Thus policy evaluation computes how good the present policy is, and policy improvement
replaces its current action choice by a greedy one.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}[Q-learning diagnosis]
For a transition $(s,a,r,s')$, write the Q-learning target and update. State which
assumption is violated if the environment changes over time, and explain why replacing
$\max_{a'}Q(s',a')$ by a known transition-model expectation would no longer be model-free.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Q-learning diagnosis]
For a sampled nonterminal transition, Q-learning uses the target
\[
y_k=r+\gamma\max_{a'\in\mathcal{A}}Q_k(s',a')
\]
and updates only the visited entry:
\[
\begin{aligned}
Q_{k+1}(s,a)
&=Q_k(s,a)+\alpha_k\bigl[y_k-Q_k(s,a)\bigr]\\
&=Q_k(s,a)+\alpha_k
\left[r+\gamma\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a')-Q_k(s,a)\right].
\end{aligned}
\]
For a terminal transition, use $y_k=r$, because there is no future action-value term. If the
environment changes over time, the stationarity (time-homogeneity) assumption is violated:
the same pair $(s,a)$ no longer has one fixed reward law and transition kernel. Old samples
then describe a different problem from new samples.

If the transition model were known, one could instead form the planning target
\[
r(s,a)+\gamma\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s,a)\max_{a'}Q_k(s',a').
\]
This expression explicitly requires $P$, so the resulting method is model-based dynamic
programming rather than model-free Q-learning. Q-learning is model-free precisely because it
uses the observed successor $s'$ in a sample transition instead of summing over a known model.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}[Policy gradient and preference alignment]
Write a score-function estimator for $\nabla_\theta J(\theta)$, show why subtracting a
state-only baseline does not bias it, and state a KL-regularized objective for aligning
$\pi_\theta(y\mid x)$ to preference-derived rewards.
\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}[Policy gradient and preference alignment]
For one trajectory sampled from $\pi_\theta$, a Monte-Carlo score-function estimator is
\[
\widehat{\nabla_\theta J}=
\sum_{t\geq0}\gamma^t
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(A_t\mid S_t)
\bigl(G_t-b(S_t)\bigr),
\]
where $b(S_t)$ is any baseline depending on the state but not on the sampled action. Its
expected baseline contribution is zero, because at a fixed state $s$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a\mid s)b(s)
&=b(s)\sum_a\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta(a\mid s)\\
&=b(s)\nabla_\theta\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)
=b(s)\nabla_\theta1=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Consequently, the baseline can reduce variance without changing the expected gradient.

Given preference-derived reward $r_\phi(x,y)$ and reference policy
$\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}$, a KL-regularized alignment objective is
\[
\begin{aligned}
J_{\mathrm{RLHF}}(\theta)
={}&\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\bigl[r_\phi(x,y)\bigr]\\
&-\beta\,
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}
\left[
\mathrm{KL}\bigl(
\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)
\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)
\bigr)
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]
The first term promotes preferred completions according to the reward model; the second
prevents the aligned policy from drifting too far from the reference model.
\end{solution}

\begin{note}[Eight-point RL answer skeleton]
For a compact MDP/RLHF question, organize the answer in three moves:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Define $(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P,r,\gamma)$ and write the Bellman equation.
    \item Contrast model-based policy iteration with the unknown-$P$ setting. There, the
    critic learns a value or advantage from trajectories, while the actor follows a
    policy-gradient update.
    \item For preference alignment, identify $(x,y^+,y^-)$; state either the
    reward-model-plus-KL objective or the DPO loss; and explain the role of the reference
    policy.
\end{enumerate}
Do not claim that actor--critic performs exact policy evaluation when $P$ is unknown.
\end{note}

\subsubsection{Coverage checklist}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Bellman equation:} \hyperref[unit:1]{Units~1}--\hyperref[unit:2]{2}; derive by first-step conditioning and
    connect the fixed point to policy evaluation.
    \item \textbf{Q-learning:} \unitref{2}; write the TD target, update, exploration role, and
    model-free/off-policy distinction (\cref{def:planning-qlearning}).
    \item \textbf{Policy gradient:} \unitref{3}; write the
    \jump{eq:policy-gradient}{score-function estimator}, \jump{thm:baseline}{baseline
    identity}, and \jump{def:actor-critic}{actor--critic} interpretation.
    \item \textbf{2026 extension:} \unitref{4} and the answer skeleton; explain preference data,
    reward-model RLHF or DPO, and KL regularization (\cref{rem:rlhf-overview}).
\end{itemize}

\section{C. Optimization Methods in Artificial Intelligence}

Let $f : \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}$ be an $L$-smooth and $\mu$-strongly convex function. The stochastic gradient is defined as:
\[
g(x, \xi) = \nabla f(x) + \xi,
\]
where $\xi$ is a zero-mean random variable with $\E \bigl[ \|\xi\|^2 \bigr] \le \sigma^2$. Consider the SGD method:
\[
x^{k+1} = \prox_{\gamma R} \bigl( x^k - \gamma g(x^k, \xi^k) \bigr),
\]
used to solve the optimization problem:
\[
\min_{x} f(x) + R(x).
\]

\subsection{Background: the composite problem, the proximal map, and the noise model}

The setup is a constant-step \emph{proximal SGD} method for a composite objective, not unconstrained gradient descent. Write

\[
F(x):=f(x)+R(x).
\]

The six problems that follow prove one mean-square guarantee for this iteration: the memory of the initialization contracts linearly, while persistent gradient noise leaves a nonzero error floor.

\paragraph{What $f$ and $R$ are.}
The term $f$ is the smooth data-fitting piece. It is assumed differentiable, $L$-smooth, and $\mu$-strongly convex,\footnote{A differentiable $f:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ is $L$-smooth if $\nabla f$ is $L$-Lipschitz:
\[
\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|\leq L\|x-y\|
\qquad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d,
\]
\Cref{lem:descent} converts this Lipschitz-gradient hypothesis into the equivalent quadratic upper bound used as a working form of smoothness.
It is $\mu$-strongly convex if
\[
f(y)\geq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|y-x\|^2
\qquad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d.
\]
The first inequality is an upper quadratic model: the graph cannot bend faster than $L$. The second is a lower quadratic model: the graph cannot be flatter than $\mu$. \Cref{lem:strong-monotone} records the equivalent first-order form $\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle\geq\mu\|x-y\|^2$. Together they pinch the curvature between $\mu$ and $L$, which forces $0<\mu\leq L$. This is the pair of definitions requested in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-1}{Problem~1}.} so $\nabla f$ exists and the curvature of $f$ is pinched between $\mu$ and $L$. The term $R$ is the regularizer or constraint. Typical standing hypotheses, used from \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3} onward, are that $R:\mathbb{R}^d\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ is proper, convex, and lower-semicontinuous. It may fail to be differentiable, and it may take the value $+\infty$ in order to encode a hard constraint.

This split is the reason the algorithm treats the two terms differently. Machine-learning objectives are often of the form

\[
\min_x
\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \ell(x;z_i)}_{f}
+
\underbrace{\lambda\Omega(x)}_{R}.
\]

The loss $f$ is differentiable and expensive, hence a candidate for stochastic gradients. The regularizer $R$ supplies structure---sparsity, a constraint, nonnegativity---and is handled by an exact proximal step rather than by a gradient of $R$.

Common choices make the distinction concrete. If $R\equiv 0$, there is no regularizer, $\prox_{\gamma R}$ is the identity, and the method reduces to ordinary SGD. If $R(x)=\lambda\|x\|_1$, the proximal map is coordinatewise soft-thresholding (Lasso). If $R(x)=\lambda\|x\|_2^2$, the proximal map is a linear shrinkage. If $R=I_C$ is the convex indicator of a closed convex set $C$, taking the value $0$ on $C$ and $+\infty$ off $C$, then

\[
\prox_{\gamma I_C}(v)=\mathrm{proj}_C(v),
\]

and the method is projected SGD. These four identities are derived in \cref{ex:prox-basic}. In all of these cases $x^*$ means a minimizer of $F=f+R$, not of $f$ alone. The descent lemma and the strong-convexity equivalences used as working forms of the two curvature assumptions are recorded in \jump{sol:spr2025-opt-1}{the solution of Problem~1}.

\paragraph{The proximal map.}
For $\gamma>0$,

\[
\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
:=\argmin_{z\in\mathbb{R}^d}
\left\{
R(z)+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2
\right\}.
\]

From a point $v$, one seeks a $z$ that is close to $v$ while also making $R(z)$ small.\footnote{The operator is called \emph{proximal}, from Latin \emph{proximus} ``nearest''. It is not a ``proxy'' in the sense of a stand-in or surrogate (as in a proxy reward or a proxy model). Moreau's proximal mapping generalizes nearest-point projection: $\mathrm{proj}_C(v)$ is the point of $C$ nearest to $v$, and
\[
\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
=\argmin_z\Bigl(R(z)+\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2\Bigr)
\]
is the point that stays as close as possible to $v$ while also decreasing $R$. When $R=I_C$, the two coincide, which is why the indicator example above reduces to $\mathrm{proj}_C$. The quadratic $\|z-v\|^2$ is a proximity penalty, so the output is a nearby point rather than an unconstrained minimizer of $R$. The abbreviation $\mathrm{prox}$ therefore names a proximity operator: an implicit step that remains proximal to its input.} Larger $\gamma$ weights the regularizer more heavily. This is an implicit, or backward, gradient step on $R$. If $R$ is differentiable, the first-order condition reads

\[
z=\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
z=v-\gamma\nabla R(z);
\]

the gradient of $R$ is evaluated at the \emph{output} $z$, not at the input $v$.
The quadratic is the squared Euclidean $2$-norm: $\|z-v\|^2=\|z-v\|_2^2$. That is
the Moreau proximal mapping on a Hilbert space; Problems~3--6 use only this
geometry.

\begin{remark}[Why the SGD update uses a proximal step]\label{rem:prox-motivation}
The proximal map is not an ornament on ordinary SGD. It is there because the
objective has already been split as $F=f+R$, and the two pieces are not the same
kind of function.

The data term $f$ is differentiable and typically a finite sum, so $\nabla f$
may be replaced by a stochastic estimator $g(x,\xi)$. The regularizer $R$
supplies structure---an $\ell_1$ penalty, a closed convex constraint via $I_C$,
nonnegativity---and is often nondifferentiable, or even $+\infty$ off a feasible
set. The joint explicit step
\[
x\leftarrow x-\gamma\bigl(\nabla f(x)+\nabla R(x)\bigr)
\]
is then undefined. A subgradient of $R$ can be written down, but one explicit
subgradient step does not enforce a hard constraint and does not produce the
exact zeros of Lasso.

Proximal SGD therefore treats the two terms differently. An explicit,
possibly noisy, gradient step is taken on $f$ only,
\[
v=x^k-\gamma g(x^k,\xi^k),
\]
and $R$ is handled by one exact implicit step
\[
x^{k+1}=\prox_{\gamma R}(v).
\]
The quadratic \(\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|_2^2\) keeps the output near the point
just produced by the data gradient; $R(z)$ pulls that output toward the
desired structure. Noise lives in $g$, not in the prox: each regularizer step
is deterministic, so sparsity or feasibility is not washed out by the minibatch.

Three reasons this split is forced. First, $f$ is expensive and stochastic, while
a typical $R$ has a cheap closed-form prox (identity, soft-threshold, shrinkage,
or $\mathrm{proj}_C$). Second, a minimizer of $F$ is a fixed point of the same
map with a noiseless gradient,
\[
x^*=\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)\bigr);
\]
the algorithm is that identity with $\nabla f$ replaced by $g$.
Nonexpansiveness of $\prox_{\gamma R}$ is what lets $R$ drop out of the
one-step comparison in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3}. Third, smoothing
$R$ and feeding $\nabla(f+R_{\varepsilon})$ to ordinary SGD would discard exact
zeros and soften hard constraints. The prox keeps $R$ as written.

If $R\equiv 0$, then $\prox_{\gamma R}$ is the identity and the method is
ordinary SGD. The proximal step is needed only when there is a genuine
nonsmooth or constrained piece to enforce after the stochastic gradient on $f$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Proximal SGD versus Adam and Transformer training]\label{rem:prox-vs-adam}
The three objects share a skeleton and then diverge. Each step starts from a
stochastic gradient of a smooth loss, then applies an operator to produce the
next iterate. They are not three solvers for the same exam problem.

In this section the operator is \(\prox_{\gamma R}\). The problem is composite
and convex: \(F=f+R\), with \(f\) \(L\)-smooth and \(\mu\)-strongly convex.
Problems~1--6 prove a constant-step mean-square bound for
\[
x^{k+1}=\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^k-\gamma g(x^k,\xi^k)\bigr).
\]
There is no momentum buffer and no per-coordinate second moment. The prox is
an exact implicit step on \(R\); the noise \(\xi\) lives only in the estimator
of \(\nabla f\).

In Part~B the same backpropagation output \(g_t\) is consumed by a different
operator. Momentum replaces \(g_t\) by a velocity. Adam adds an RMSProp-style
second moment and a bias correction:
momentum on the direction, plus a diagonal rescaling of the step
(\jump{prop:dlu-mom}{Deep Learning exam formulas}, and the optimizer family in
Part~B Problem~1). That is a practical response to elongated, poorly scaled,
nonconvex landscapes. It does \emph{not} restore the convex GD theorems of
this section, and it does not implement \(\prox_{\gamma R}\) for an
\(\ell_1\) penalty or a hard constraint.

A Transformer question is about the \emph{model}, not about this recurrence.
Self-attention and the MLP blocks determine how \(g_t\) is computed. The
training loop almost always uses Adam or AdamW, i.e.\ the Part~B rule, not the
proximal iteration above. The sentence in the Deep Learning notes that
``convex \(L\)-smooth theorems motivate step sizes, but deep nets are
nonconvex and mini-batch noisy'' is exactly the gap between Part~C and
Part~B.

The only tight special case is \(R\equiv 0\): then \(\prox_{\gamma R}\) is the
identity and the method is plain SGD, the no-momentum, non-adaptive endpoint
of the Part~B family. A looser link is \(\ell_2\) weight decay, whose prox is
linear shrinkage; AdamW's decoupled decay is related in spirit and is
\emph{not} the algorithm analyzed in Problems~3--6. Do not start a Nesterov or
Adam Lyapunov argument here.

{\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.2pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
\noindent
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.35cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
& Proximal SGD (this part)
& Adam / momentum (Part~B)
& Transformer training loop \\
\hline

convex composite $F=f+R$ &

same as Part~B; the architecture is the model \\
\hline
Update &
$x^{k+1}=\prox_{\gamma R}(x^k-\gamma g)$ &
first moment $m_t$, second moment $v_t$, bias correction &
Adam or AdamW on Transformer weights \\
\hline
What the operator does &
exact implicit step on $R$ (proj / soft-threshold / shrinkage) &
smooth the direction and rescale each coordinate from gradient history &
does not change: architecture produces $g_t$, Adam consumes it \\
\hline

\hline

the $m_t,v_t$ lines and the Adam sentence &

\hline
\end{tabularx}
}

\paragraph{Where the randomness is.}
One may use the exact gradient \(\nabla f\) in the update. That is deterministic
proximal gradient descent, not SGD: it is legal, and the error floor vanishes
because \(\sigma=0\), but each step must scan the whole sum that defines \(f\).
Replacing \(\nabla f\) by \(g=\nabla f+\xi\) is the whole point of SGD: a cheap
unbiased estimator of the same direction. The two writings of the noise are the
same object. In Part~B the randomness is the mini-batch \(B\), via
\[
g_B(x)=\frac1{|B|}\sum_{i\in B}\nabla\ell(x;z_i),
\]
with no extra symbol \(\xi\). Here one sets
\(\xi:=g_B(x)-\nabla f(x)\), so \(g(x,\xi)=\nabla f(x)+\xi\),
\(\mathbb{E}[\xi\mid x]=0\), and \(\mathbb{E}[\|\xi\|^2]\le\sigma^2\). The
randomness was always ``which samples this step sees''; the additive \(\xi\) is
that sampling error, written so Problems~4--6 need only unbiasedness and a
second-moment bound.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Convex subdifferential]\label{def:subdifferential}
Let $R:\mathbb{R}^d\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ be convex. The \emph{subdifferential} of $R$ at a point $z$ with $R(z)<+\infty$ is the set of supporting slopes

\[
\partial R(z)
:=
\bigl\{
g\in\mathbb{R}^d
:
R(y)\geq R(z)+\langle g,y-z\rangle
\text{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R}^d
\bigr\}.
\]

If $R(z)=+\infty$, one sets $\partial R(z)=\emptyset$. When $R$ is differentiable at $z$, the set collapses to a singleton: $\partial R(z)=\{\nabla R(z)\}$. A point $z$ minimizes $R$ if and only if $0\in\partial R(z)$.
\end{definition}

All three closing claims follow from the supporting-slope definition.

If $R(z)=+\infty$, the inequality $R(y)\geq R(z)+\langle g,y-z\rangle$ would require $R(y)=+\infty$ for every $y$. For a proper $R$ this is impossible, so no such $g$ exists and one sets $\partial R(z)=\emptyset$. Equivalently: supporting hyperplanes are only formed at points of the effective domain.

If $R$ is convex and differentiable at $z$, the first-order characterization of convexity is exactly

\[
R(y)\geq R(z)+\langle\nabla R(z),y-z\rangle
\qquad\text{for all }y,
\]

so $\nabla R(z)\in\partial R(z)$. Conversely, if $g\in\partial R(z)$, then for every direction $h$ and every $t>0$,

\[
\frac{R(z+th)-R(z)}{t}\geq\langle g,h\rangle.
\]

Sending $t\downarrow 0$ yields $\langle\nabla R(z),h\rangle\geq\langle g,h\rangle$ for every $h$, hence $g=\nabla R(z)$. Thus $\partial R(z)=\{\nabla R(z)\}$.

The third claim is Fermat's rule, and it is the definition rewritten rather than a separate theorem. If $z$ minimizes $R$, then $R(y)\geq R(z)$ for every $y$, which is the supporting-slope inequality with $g=0$:

\[
R(y)\geq R(z)+\langle 0,y-z\rangle
\qquad\text{for all }y,
\]

so $0\in\partial R(z)$. Conversely, if $0\in\partial R(z)$, the same inequality with $g=0$ reads $R(y)\geq R(z)$ for every $y$, so $z$ is a global minimizer.

\begin{note}[What the three claims do and do not use]
The empty-subdifferential convention and Fermat's rule never use convexity: they are bookkeeping and a rewrite of the definition. The singleton claim \emph{does} use convexity, because only then is $\nabla R(z)$ automatically a supporting slope; without convexity a differentiable function need not satisfy $R(y)\geq R(z)+\langle\nabla R(z),y-z\rangle$. Convexity is also what makes $\partial R$ a usable calculus object: at a point in the relative interior of the effective domain, $\partial R(z)$ is nonempty, closed, and convex. Combining the second and third claims recovers the familiar $\nabla R(z)=0$ at a differentiable minimizer. The composite first-order condition $0\in\nabla f(x^*)+\partial R(x^*)$ used later is Fermat applied to $F=f+R$.
\end{note}

In the general convex case the same identity uses \cref{def:subdifferential}:

\[
z=\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
v\in z+\gamma\partial R(z).
\]

This is the optimality condition of $\prox_{\gamma R}$ invoked in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3}.

\begin{note}[Why the identity holds when $R$ is not differentiable]\label{note:prox-foc}
Write

\[
\Phi(w):=R(w)+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|w-v\|^2.
\]

By definition, $z=\prox_{\gamma R}(v)$ if and only if $z$ minimizes $\Phi$. Fermat's rule then says this happens if and only if $0\in\partial\Phi(z)$. Differentiability of $R$ is not required: the rule uses only the supporting-slope definition of $\partial\Phi$.

The quadratic $Q(w):=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|w-v\|^2$ is finite and $C^1$ on all of $\mathbb{R}^d$, with

\[
\nabla Q(w)=\frac{1}{\gamma}(w-v),
\qquad
\partial Q(w)=\Bigl\{\frac{1}{\gamma}(w-v)\Bigr\}.
\]

Because $Q$ is real-valued and continuous, the convex subdifferential sum rule applies at any $z$ with $R(z)<+\infty$:

\[
\partial\Phi(z)
=\partial R(z)+\partial Q(z)
=\partial R(z)+\frac{1}{\gamma}(z-v).
\]

Hence $0\in\partial\Phi(z)$ if and only if

\[
-\frac{1}{\gamma}(z-v)\in\partial R(z),
\]

which rearranges to $v\in z+\gamma\partial R(z)$. The quadratic is strongly convex, so the minimizer of $\Phi$ is unique whenever it exists, and the two sides of the displayed identity name the same unique $z$.

If $R$ is differentiable at $z$, then $\partial R(z)=\{\nabla R(z)\}$ and the identity collapses to the earlier implicit step $z=v-\gamma\nabla R(z)$. Nondifferentiability only enlarges $\partial R(z)$ from a singleton to a set; the same algebra continues to hold. This is the condition used to identify $x^*$ in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3}.
\end{note}

Two further properties are used constantly.

The map is nonexpansive: it is $1$-Lipschitz,

\[
\|\prox_{\gamma R}(u)-\prox_{\gamma R}(v)\|\le\|u-v\|.
\]

The proof, from monotonicity of $\partial R$ (or equivalently from strong convexity of the prox objective), is recorded as \cref{lem:prox-nonexpansive} in \jump{sol:spr2025-opt-3}{the solution of Problem~3}. Consequently a proximal step cannot increase distances. That is the source of the inequality in the one-step recurrence of \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3}.

The identity $x^*=\prox_{\gamma R}(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*))$ is not a second definition of the proximal map. It is the original definition, evaluated at a carefully chosen input. The completing-the-square and the resulting fixed-point characterization are recorded in \jump{sol:spr2025-opt-3}{the solution of Problem~3} (\cref{lem:prox-fixed}).

The algorithm is the same map with $\nabla f(x^k)$ replaced by the noisy estimator $g(x^k,\xi^k)$. Subtracting the two identities, and using nonexpansiveness to drop the proximal map, is the whole of \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3}. After that comparison, $R$ itself almost disappears from the algebra: the regularizer is absorbed into the proximal calculus.

\begin{example}[Four proximal maps from regression]\label{ex:prox-basic}
Each of the four regularizers named above is obtained by substituting into the definition, and, when useful, into the first-order condition $v\in z+\gamma\partial R(z)$. Throughout, $\gamma>0$ and $\lambda\geq0$.

If there is no regularizer, $R\equiv0$, the objective reduces to the proximity term alone:

\[
\prox_{\gamma0}(v)
=\argmin_{z\in\mathbb{R}^d}\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2
=v.
\]

The proximal step is the identity, and proximal SGD collapses to ordinary SGD.

The ridge penalty $R(z)=\lambda\|z\|_2^2$ is differentiable, with $\nabla R(z)=2\lambda z$. The implicit-step identity $z=v-\gamma\nabla R(z)$ becomes

\[
z=v-2\gamma\lambda z,
\]

hence

\[
\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
=\frac{1}{1+2\gamma\lambda}\,v.
\]

Every coordinate is scaled by the same factor in $(0,1]$. This is linear shrinkage toward the origin: the same map that appears as the ridge estimator after a gradient step on a least-squares loss. (If one adopts the convention $R(z)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|z\|_2^2$ instead, the factor is $1/(1+\gamma\lambda)$.)

The Lasso penalty $R(z)=\lambda\|z\|_1$ separates across coordinates, so it is enough to compute the scalar map and apply it entrywise. For $d=1$ the first-order condition reads

\[
v\in z+\gamma\lambda\,\partial|\cdot|(z),
\qquad
\partial|z|
=
\begin{cases}
\{+1\}, & z>0,\\
[-1,1], & z=0,\\
\{-1\}, & z<0.
\end{cases}
\]

If $z>0$, then $v=z+\gamma\lambda$, so $z=v-\gamma\lambda$, which is consistent only when $v>\gamma\lambda$. If $z<0$, then $z=v+\gamma\lambda$, consistent only when $v<-\gamma\lambda$. If $z=0$, then $v\in[-\gamma\lambda,\gamma\lambda]$. Therefore

\[
\prox_{\gamma\lambda|\cdot|}(v)
=\operatorname{sign}(v)\,\max\bigl\{|v|-\gamma\lambda,\,0\bigr\}.
\]

In $\mathbb{R}^d$ the same rule acts coordinatewise:

\[
\bigl(\prox_{\gamma\lambda\|\cdot\|_1}(v)\bigr)_i
=\operatorname{sign}(v_i)\,\max\bigl\{|v_i|-\gamma\lambda,\,0\bigr\}.
\]

This is soft-thresholding: coordinates smaller than $\gamma\lambda$ in absolute value are set to zero, and the others are shrunk by $\gamma\lambda$ toward the origin. It is the proximal step of ISTA for Lasso.

Finally, let $R=I_C$ be the indicator of a nonempty closed convex set $C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^d$, with $I_C=0$ on $C$ and $I_C=+\infty$ off $C$. Off $C$ the proximal objective is infinite, so the argmin is constrained to $C$:

\[
\prox_{\gamma I_C}(v)
=\argmin_{z\in C}\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2
=\mathrm{proj}_C(v).
\]

Here $\mathrm{proj}_C(v)$ is the Euclidean projection of $v$ onto $C$: the (unique) nearest point of $C$ to $v$,
\[
\mathrm{proj}_C(v)
:=\argmin_{z\in C}\|z-v\|^2
=\argmin_{z\in C}\|z-v\|.
\]

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  >=Stealth,
  font=\small,
  scale=1.08
]
  \def\R{1.62}
  \coordinate (O) at (0,0);
  \coordinate (V) at (2.95,1.42);
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\vlen}{sqrt(2.95*2.95+1.42*1.42)}
  \coordinate (P) at ({\R*2.95/\vlen},{\R*1.42/\vlen});
  \coordinate (Z) at (0.22,-0.78);

  % Convex set C
  \fill[teal!11] (O) circle (\R);
  \draw[teal!50!black, thick] (O) circle (\R);
  \node[teal!35!black] at (-0.28,-0.12) {$C$};

  % Equidistant circle through proj_C(v): first contact with C
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\gap}{\vlen-\R}
  \draw[orange!45, dashed] (V) circle (\gap);

  % Supporting line at P (tangent, orthogonal to v - proj)
  \coordinate (T1) at ($(P)!1.55cm!90:(V)$);
  \coordinate (T2) at ($(P)!1.55cm!-90:(V)$);
  \draw[gray!55, thick] (T1) -- (T2);
  \node[gray!60, font=\scriptsize, anchor=south west] at (T1)
    {supporting line};

  % Residual / projection path
  \draw[orange!70!black, very thick, ->] (V) -- (P);
  \node[orange!60!black, font=\scriptsize, above left=-2pt]
    at ($(V)!0.42!(P)$) {$\|v-\mathrm{proj}_C(v)\|$};

  % Right angle between residual and supporting line
  \coordinate (Ra) at ($(P)!3.4mm!(V)$);
  \coordinate (Rb) at ($(P)!3.4mm!-90:(V)$);
  \draw[gray!65] (Ra) -- ($(Ra)+(Rb)-(P)$) -- (Rb);

  % Comparison point: any other z in C is farther
  \draw[gray!50, dashed] (V) -- (Z);
  \fill[black!50] (Z) circle (1.25pt);
  \node[black!50, below, font=\scriptsize] at (Z) {$z\in C$};
  \node[gray!55, font=\scriptsize, right=1pt] at ($(V)!0.58!(Z)$)
    {farther};

  % Points v and proj_C(v)
  \fill[orange!75!black] (V) circle (1.7pt);
  \node[orange!70!black, above right=-1pt] at (V) {$v$};
  \fill[purple!70!black] (P) circle (1.7pt);
  \node[purple!65!black, below left=-1pt] at (P) {$\mathrm{proj}_C(v)$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

The orange point $v$ lies outside $C$. The purple point $\mathrm{proj}_C(v)$ is the first contact of $C$ with the dashed circle centered at $v$; equivalently, the residual $v-\mathrm{proj}_C(v)$ is orthogonal to a supporting line of $C$ at that contact. Any other $z\in C$ is strictly farther from $v$.

The two argmins coincide because $t\mapsto t^2$ is strictly increasing on $[0,+\infty)$. The factor $1/(2\gamma)$ is a positive constant, so minimizing $\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2$ over $C$ is the same optimization problem as minimizing $\|z-v\|^2$ over $C$. That is why the proximal minimizer equals $\mathrm{proj}_C(v)$, independently of $\gamma>0$. Closed convexity of $C$ supplies uniqueness of the nearest point. The proximal map is therefore ordinary Euclidean projection. In particular, if $C=\mathbb{R}_+^d$ (nonnegative coefficients), then

\[
\bigl(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathbb{R}_+^d}(v)\bigr)_i
=\max\{v_i,0\}.
\]

\end{example}

\paragraph{Why the stochastic gradient is written $g(x,\xi)=\nabla f(x)+\xi$.}
Exact gradient descent would use $\nabla f(x^k)$. In learning problems $\nabla f$ is typically an expectation or a full-data average,

\[
\nabla f(x)=\E_{z\sim\mathcal{D}}\bigl[\nabla\ell(x;z)\bigr],
\]

which is too expensive to form at every iteration. Stochastic gradient descent replaces it by an unbiased estimator computed from one sample or a mini-batch.\footnote{A \emph{mini-batch} is a small random subset $B\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}$ of the training indices, of size $b=|B|$ with $1\le b\ll n$ (typical values are $32$--$256$). If $f(x)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n f_i(x)$, the mini-batch gradient is
\[
g_B(x)=\frac1b\sum_{i\in B}\nabla f_i(x).
\]
Uniform sampling (with or without replacement) gives $\E[g_B(x)]=\nabla f(x)$, so the estimator remains unbiased. The exam's additive noise is then $\xi=g_B(x)-\nabla f(x)$. The endpoint $b=1$ is classical one-sample SGD; $b=n$ is full-batch gradient descent and forces $\xi=0$. Averaging $b$ terms typically reduces the second moment like $1/b$, which is why the next paragraph says that mini-batching merely reduces $\sigma^2$. The word ``mini'' distinguishes this subset from a full pass over the dataset (one \emph{batch}, or one epoch).} The exam encodes that estimator as an additive noise model

\[
g(x,\xi)=\nabla f(x)+\xi,
\qquad
\E[\xi]=0,
\qquad
\E\bigl[\|\xi\|^2\bigr]\le\sigma^2.
\]

This is an analysis abstraction, not a new algorithm. Unbiasedness keeps the expected direction a descent direction for $f$. The second-moment bound $\sigma^2$ is the only quantitative handle on the noise; it becomes the floor $\gamma\sigma^2/\mu$ in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-6}{Problem~6}. Writing $g=\nabla f+\xi$ also splits the proof into two pieces: smoothness and strong convexity act only on $\nabla f$, while all randomness sits in $\xi$.

The same model covers a finite-sum objective $f(x)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_i f_i(x)$ with $g=\nabla f_i(x)$, in which case $\xi=\nabla f_i(x)-\nabla f(x)$, and it covers mini-batching, which merely reduces $\sigma^2$. It does not cover biased gradient estimators.

\paragraph{The update as a forward--backward step.}
Unrolling the definition of $g$ gives

\[
x^{k+1}
=\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^k-\gamma\nabla f(x^k)-\gamma\xi^k\bigr).
\]

The iteration is therefore two maps in sequence: a stochastic forward step on the smooth term $f$, followed by an exact backward step on $R$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  >=Stealth,
  node distance=0.48cm and 0.72cm,
  box/.style={draw=#1!60!black, fill=#1!7, rounded corners=3pt,
              align=center, inner sep=5pt, text width=3.35cm, font=\scriptsize},
  arrow/.style={->, thick, draw=black!55}
]
  \node[box=teal] (xk) {$x^k$};
  \node[box=orange, right=of xk] (vk)
    {$v^k=x^k-\gamma g(x^k,\xi^k)$\\[-1pt]
     {\tiny stochastic forward step on $f$}};
  \node[box=purple, right=of vk] (xnext)
    {$x^{k+1}=\prox_{\gamma R}(v^k)$\\[-1pt]
     {\tiny exact backward step on $R$}};
  \draw[arrow] (xk) -- (vk);
  \draw[arrow] (vk) -- (xnext);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

If $\xi^k=0$, this is deterministic proximal gradient, and strong convexity together with smoothness yield linear convergence to $x^*$. The noise term $-\gamma\xi^k$ injects a fresh error at every step, so constant-step proximal SGD cannot converge exactly; it converges to a ball whose radius scales like $\sqrt{\gamma}\,\sigma/\sqrt{\mu}$. \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-5}{Problems~5} and \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-6}{6} quantify that floor.

\begin{note}[Three distinctions that the later algebra depends on]
The proximal map is not a synonym for projection: projection is the special case $R=I_C$. Soft-thresholding is a proximal map that is not a projection onto a constraint set.

The noise is added to $\nabla f$, not to $R$. Randomness comes from the data term; each proximal step on $R$ is exact.

Finally, $x^*\in\argmin(f+R)$ need not satisfy $\nabla f(x^*)=0$. The fixed-point identity above keeps the term $\nabla f(x^*)$. That term vanishes only in the unconstrained case $R\equiv 0$.
\end{note}

\subsection{Problem 1. [5 pts.] L-Smoothness and \texorpdfstring{$\mu$}{mu}-Strong Convexity}\label{prob:spr2025-opt-1}
Provide the definitions of $L$-smoothness and $\mu$-strong convexity, respectively.

\subsection{Problem 2. [6 pts.] Combining Smoothness and Strong Convexity}\label{prob:spr2025-opt-2}
Suppose that $f(x)$ is continuously differentiable, $L$-smooth, and $\mu$-convex.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Show that $g(x) = f(x) - \frac{\mu}{2}\|x\|^2$ is continuously differentiable, convex and $(L - \mu)$-smooth.
    \item Using the fact
    \[
    \frac{1}{L - \mu} \|\nabla g(x) - \nabla g(y)\|^2 \le \langle \nabla g(x) - \nabla g(y), x - y \rangle,
    \]
    show that
    \[
    \mu \|x - y\|^2 + \frac{1}{L} \|\nabla f(x) - \nabla f(y)\|^2 \le \left(1 + \frac{\mu}{L}\right) \langle \nabla f(x) - \nabla f(y), x - y \rangle.
    \]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Problem 3. [6 pts.] Recurrence Relation}\label{prob:spr2025-opt-3}
Using the non-expansiveness property of $\prox$ and the optimality condition of $\prox_{\gamma R}$, derive the recurrence relation for $\|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2$:
\[
\|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2 \le \|x^k - x^* - \gamma (\nabla f(x^k) - \nabla f(x^*) + \xi^k)\|^2.
\]

\subsection{Problem 4. [6 pts.] Variance Decomposition}\label{prob:spr2025-opt-4}
What is variance decomposition? Explain how it is applied to $\|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2$, and derive:
\[
\E_k \bigl[ \|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2 \bigr] \le \|x^k - x^* - \gamma (\nabla f(x^k) - \nabla f(x^*))\|^2 + \gamma^2 \sigma^2,
\]
where $\E_k[\cdot]$ denotes the conditional expectation given $\xi^k, \dots, \xi^0$.

\subsection{Problem 5. [5 pts.] Simplified Recurrence}\label{prob:spr2025-opt-5}
Using the tower property, prove that when $\gamma = \frac{2}{\mu + L}$, the recurrence simplifies to:
\[
\E \bigl[ \|x^{k+1} - x^*\|^2 \bigr] \le (1 - \rho) \E \bigl[ \|x^k - x^*\|^2 \bigr] + \gamma^2 \sigma^2,
\]
where $\rho = \frac{4\mu L}{(\mu + L)^2}$.

\subsection{Problem 6. [5 pts.] Complexity}\label{prob:spr2025-opt-6}
Prove that for any desired precision $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists a step size $\gamma$ such that:
\[
k \ge \frac{L/\mu + 3}{4} \log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}
\]
implies:
\[
\E \bigl[ \|x^k - x^*\|^2 \bigr] \le \varepsilon \|x^0 - x^*\|^2 + \frac{\gamma \sigma^2}{\mu}.
\]

\subsubsection{Learning map: what this section is really proving}

The six questions form one proof of the standard constant-step stochastic proximal-gradient guarantee. The target is not exact convergence to $x^*$: persistent gradient noise leaves a nonzero error floor. The proof separates the two effects:

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  >=Stealth,
  node distance=0.38cm and 0.62cm,
  box/.style={draw=#1!60!black, fill=#1!7, rounded corners=3pt,
              align=center, inner sep=5pt, text width=2.48cm, font=\scriptsize},
  arrow/.style={->, thick, draw=black!55}
]
  \node[box=teal] (defs) {P1\\Smoothness and\\strong convexity};
  \node[box=orange, right=of defs] (interpolation) {P2\\Interpolation\\inequality};
  \node[box=purple, right=of interpolation] (prox) {P3\\Proximal\\one-step bound};
  \node[box=blue, below=of interpolation] (noise) {P4\\Conditional-noise\\decomposition};
  \node[box=green!55!black, right=of noise] (contract) {P5\\Mean-square\\contraction};
  \node[box=red!65!black, right=of contract] (complexity) {P6\\Iteration complexity\\and noise floor};
  \draw[arrow] (defs) -- (interpolation);
  \draw[arrow] (interpolation) -- (prox);
  \draw[arrow] (prox) -- (noise);
  \draw[arrow] (noise) -- (contract);
  \draw[arrow] (contract) -- (complexity);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

The central recurrence obtained in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-5}{Problem~5} is

\[
\E\left[\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2\right]
\leq (1-\rho)\E\left[\|x^k-x^*\|^2\right]+\gamma^2\sigma^2.
\]

The factor $1-\rho$ contracts the memory of the initialization, whereas $\gamma^2\sigma^2$ is the new error injected at every stochastic step. \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-6}{Problem~6} only unrolls this recurrence carefully.

\subsubsection{How to study the six problems}

Do not memorize the displayed results in isolation. Work through the section in this order:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Read the opening background until $R$, $\prox_{\gamma R}$, and the additive noise model $g=\nabla f+\xi$ are no longer black boxes.
  \item Learn the two curvature assumptions in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-1}{Problem~1} as inequalities involving gradients and function values.
  \item Use \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-2}{Problem~2} to turn the two assumptions into one quantitative inequality relating displacement and gradient difference.
  \item Use the proximal fixed-point identity in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3} to compare one noisy update with the optimum.
  \item In \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-4}{Problem~4}, condition on the past so that the current noise has mean zero; this removes the cross term.
  \item Choose the special step size in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-5}{Problem~5} so that the remaining gradient-norm term cancels exactly.
  \item Sum the geometric recurrence in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-6}{Problem~6} and distinguish optimization error from the irreducible stochastic-noise floor.
\end{enumerate}

For each problem, first read the exam-ready result, then reconstruct the derivation. The short answer gives the timed-exam skeleton; the intervening steps explain why it is true.

\subsubsection{Preliminaries and conventions}

\paragraph{The composite objective and the proximal map.}
The composite objective $F=f+R$, the proximal map $\prox_{\gamma R}$, and the additive noise model $g(x,\xi)=\nabla f(x)+\xi$ are defined in the opening background. For Problems 3--6 we assume $R:\mathbb{R}^d\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ is proper, lower-semicontinuous, and convex, and that a minimizer $x^*\in\argmin F$ exists. If $R\equiv0$, then $\prox_{\gamma R}$ is the identity and the algorithm becomes ordinary SGD.

\paragraph{The two curvature assumptions.}
Throughout, $0<\mu\leq L$. The inequality $\mu\leq L$ is not cosmetic: a differentiable function cannot be both $L$-smooth and $\mu$-strongly convex with $\mu>L$.

\begin{itemize}
  \item $L$-smoothness limits how quickly the gradient can change.
  \item $\mu$-strong convexity supplies a uniform amount of positive curvature and hence a unique minimizer for $f$ itself.
\end{itemize}

Their combination is the source of the linear contraction below. Neither condition alone gives the full result: smoothness alone does not rule out flat directions, and strong convexity without a smoothness bound does not prescribe a stable fixed step size.

\paragraph{Filtration and stochastic-gradient assumptions.}
Let

\[
\mathcal{F}_k:=\sigma(\xi^0,\ldots,\xi^{k-1})
\]

be the information available immediately before drawing $\xi^k$. Then $x^k$ is $\mathcal{F}_k$-measurable. The calculation in Problems 4--6 requires the standard conditional assumptions

\[
\E\left[\xi^k\mid\mathcal{F}_k\right]=0,
\qquad
\E\left[\|\xi^k\|^2\mid\mathcal{F}_k\right]\leq\sigma^2.
\]

We write $\E_k[\cdot]:=\E[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_k]$. The wording in the question says that $\E_k$ is conditional on $\xi^k,\ldots,\xi^0$; taken literally, that would already reveal $\xi^k$, so the mean-zero cross term used in the requested inequality would not vanish. The pre-sampling convention above is the necessary and standard interpretation.

\subsubsection{Notation}

\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\noindent

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.55cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Object} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Role in the proof} \\
\hline
$F$ & $F=f+R$ & Composite objective minimized by proximal SGD \\
\hline
$x^*$ & A point in $\argmin F$ & Reference point in every error norm \\
\hline
$\Delta_k$ & $x^k-x^*$ & Current displacement from the optimum \\
\hline
$h_k$ & $\nabla f(x^k)-\nabla f(x^*)$ & Deterministic gradient difference \\
\hline
$\xi^k$ & Stochastic-gradient noise & Mean-zero perturbation at iteration $k$ \\
\hline
$\gamma$ & Positive step size & Balances contraction against noise sensitivity \\
\hline
$\rho$ & $4\mu L/(\mu+L)^2$ & Mean-square contraction coefficient at the special step size \\
\hline
