\par}

\subsubsection{Readiness check}

Before starting the formal derivations, make sure the following three facts are meaningful.

\[
0\in\nabla f(x^*)+\partial R(x^*)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
x^*=\prox_{\gamma R}\left(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)\right).
\]

\[
\|a+b\|^2=\|a\|^2+2\langle a,b\rangle+\|b\|^2.
\]

\[
\E\left[\|Z\|^2\mid\mathcal{F}\right]
=\left\|\E\left[Z\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\right\|^2
+\E\left[\left\|Z-\E\left[Z\mid\mathcal{F}\right]\right\|^2\mid\mathcal{F}\right].
\]

The last identity is the conditional bias--variance decomposition. In \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-4}{Problem~4} it says that the only contribution of a mean-zero random perturbation is its conditional second moment.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 1}\label{sol:spr2025-opt-1}

\paragraph{Definitions of smoothness and strong convexity --- 5 points}

\paragraph{Exam-ready result.}
For all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d$, $f$ is $L$-smooth if its gradient is $L$-Lipschitz, and $f$ is $\mu$-strongly convex if the graph of $f$ lies above every tangent plane plus the quadratic $\mu\|y-x\|^2/2$.

\begin{lemma}[Descent lemma]\label[lemma]{lem:descent}
Let $f:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ be continuously differentiable, and assume that $\nabla f$ is $L$-Lipschitz:
\[
\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|\leq L\|x-y\|
\qquad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d.
\]
Then, for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
\[
f(y)\leq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\|y-x\|^2.
\]
In particular, the gradient step of size $1/L$ yields
\[
f\Bigl(x-\frac{1}{L}\nabla f(x)\Bigr)
\leq f(x)-\frac{1}{2L}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By the fundamental theorem of calculus along the segment $t\mapsto x+t(y-x)$,
\[
f(y)-f(x)
=\int_0^1\langle\nabla f\bigl(x+t(y-x)\bigr),y-x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.
\]
Subtract the first-order (tangent) term and apply Cauchy--Schwarz together with $L$-Lipschitz continuity of $\nabla f$:
\begin{align*}
f(y)-f(x)-\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle
&=\int_0^1\bigl\langle\nabla f\bigl(x+t(y-x)\bigr)-\nabla f(x),\,y-x\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t \\
&\leq\int_0^1\bigl\|\nabla f\bigl(x+t(y-x)\bigr)-\nabla f(x)\bigr\|\,\|y-x\|\,\mathrm{d}t \\
&\leq\int_0^1 Lt\|y-x\|^2\,\mathrm{d}t
=\frac{L}{2}\|y-x\|^2.
\end{align*}
For the second claim, substitute $y=x-\frac1L\nabla f(x)$:
\[
f(y)
\leq f(x)-\frac1L\|\nabla f(x)\|^2+\frac{L}{2}\cdot\frac1{L^2}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2
=f(x)-\frac1{2L}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2.
\]
Thus a single gradient step of size $1/L$ strictly decreases $f$ unless $\nabla f(x)=0$, which is why the inequality is called a descent lemma. The argument uses only $C^1$ regularity and the Lipschitz bound on $\nabla f$; convexity is not required.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Strong convexity $\Leftrightarrow$ strong monotonicity]\label[lemma]{lem:strong-monotone}
Let $f:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ be continuously differentiable and let $\mu>0$. The following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
    \item \emph{Quadratic lower bound.} For all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
    \[
    f(y)\geq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|y-x\|^2.
    \]
    \item \emph{Strong monotonicity of $\nabla f$.} For all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
    \[
    \langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle\geq\mu\|x-y\|^2.
    \]
    \item \emph{Convex shift.} The function $g(x):=f(x)-\frac{\mu}{2}\|x\|^2$ is convex. Equivalently, $\nabla g(x)=\nabla f(x)-\mu x$ is monotone:
    \[
    \langle\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y),x-y\rangle\geq 0.
    \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\textup{(i)}$\Rightarrow$\textup{(ii)}.
Write the quadratic lower bound at the ordered pairs $(x,y)$ and $(y,x)$:
\begin{align*}
f(y)
&\geq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|y-x\|^2,\\
f(x)
&\geq f(y)+\langle\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|x-y\|^2.
\end{align*}
Adding these two inequalities cancels $f(x)$ and $f(y)$, and yields
\[
0\geq\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),y-x\rangle+\mu\|x-y\|^2,
\]
which rearranges to the strong-monotonicity bound.

\textup{(ii)}$\Rightarrow$\textup{(i)}.
Integrate $\nabla f$ along the segment $t\mapsto x_t:=x+t(y-x)$:
\[
f(y)-f(x)
=\int_0^1\langle\nabla f(x_t),y-x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t
=\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle
+\int_0^1\langle\nabla f(x_t)-\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.
\]
Strong monotonicity at the pair $(x_t,x)$ gives
\[
\langle\nabla f(x_t)-\nabla f(x),x_t-x\rangle
\geq\mu\|x_t-x\|^2.
\]
Since $x_t-x=t(y-x)$, this is $\langle\nabla f(x_t)-\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle\geq\mu t\|y-x\|^2$. Substitute and integrate:
\[
f(y)-f(x)
\geq\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle
+\int_0^1\mu t\|y-x\|^2\,\mathrm{d}t
=\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|y-x\|^2.
\]

\textup{(i)}$\Leftrightarrow$\textup{(iii)}.
Expanding the definition of $g$ and using
\[
\|y\|^2-\|x\|^2-2\langle x,y-x\rangle=\|y-x\|^2
\]
shows that the convex first-order inequality $g(y)\geq g(x)+\langle\nabla g(x),y-x\rangle$ is exactly the quadratic lower bound for $f$. For a $C^1$ function, convexity is equivalent to monotonicity of the gradient, and
\[
\langle\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y),x-y\rangle
=\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle-\mu\|x-y\|^2,
\]
so monotonicity of $\nabla g$ is exactly strong monotonicity of $\nabla f$.
\end{proof}

The definition of $L$-smoothness is

\[
\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|
\leq L\|x-y\|,
\qquad \text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d.
\]

Equivalently, by \cref{lem:descent}, $L$-smoothness supplies the quadratic upper bound

\[
f(y)\leq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\|y-x\|^2.
\]

The same lemma also records the namesake consequence: the step $y=x-\frac1L\nabla f(x)$ yields $f(y)\le f(x)-\frac1{2L}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2$, so the value descends unless $\nabla f(x)=0$.

The definition of $\mu$-strong convexity is

\[
f(y)\geq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\|y-x\|^2,
\qquad \text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^d.
\]

For differentiable $f$, \cref{lem:strong-monotone} says that this quadratic lower bound is equivalent to strong monotonicity of the gradient:

\[
\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle
\geq\mu\|x-y\|^2.
\]

One direction adds the two tangent inequalities with $(x,y)$ swapped; the function values cancel and the two linear terms become a gradient inner product. The converse integrates $\nabla f$ along the segment from $x$ to $y$ and feeds the monotonicity bound into the integrand. The same lemma also records the shift used in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-2}{Problem~2}: $f$ is $\mu$-strongly convex if and only if $x\mapsto f(x)-\frac{\mu}{2}\|x\|^2$ is convex, which is the same as $\nabla f-\mu I$ being monotone.

Geometrically, the quadratic lower bound says the graph of $f$ lies above every tangent plus a $\mu$-paraboloid. Strong monotonicity says the same thing in first-order language: as one moves from $y$ to $x$, the gradient change has a component at least $\mu\|x-y\|$ in that direction, so gradient differences point away from each other. It is useful intuition, but \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-2}{Problem~2} needs a sharper interpolation that also retains a $\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|^2$ term.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 2}\label{sol:spr2025-opt-2}

\paragraph{Combining smoothness and strong convexity --- 6 points}

\paragraph{Exam-ready result.}
Let $g(x)=f(x)-\mu\|x\|^2/2$. Strong convexity of $f$ makes $g$ convex, and the shifted gradient $\nabla g=\nabla f-\mu I$ is $(L-\mu)$-Lipschitz. Apply the given co-coercivity inequality to $\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y)=\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)-\mu(x-y)$, expand, and divide by $L$.

\paragraph{First claim: properties of the shifted function.}
The function $g$ is continuously differentiable because both $f$ and $x\mapsto\mu\|x\|^2/2$ are continuously differentiable, with

\[
\nabla g(x)=\nabla f(x)-\mu x.
\]

By \cref{lem:strong-monotone}, the definition of strong convexity rearranges as

\[
g(y)\geq g(x)+\langle\nabla g(x),y-x\rangle,
\]

so $g$ is convex.

To see its smoothness transparently, use the standard smoothness--strong-convexity shift lemma. Since $f$ has an $L$-Lipschitz gradient and is $\mu$-strongly convex, its Hessian exists almost everywhere and satisfies

\[
\mu I\preceq \nabla^2 f(z)\preceq LI
\qquad\text{for almost every }z.
\]

Therefore

\[
0\preceq\nabla^2g(z)=\nabla^2f(z)-\mu I\preceq(L-\mu)I
\qquad\text{almost everywhere}.
\]

Integrating the gradient along the line segment from $y$ to $x$ gives

\[
\|\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y)\|
\leq(L-\mu)\|x-y\|.
\]

Thus $g$ is $(L-\mu)$-smooth. This argument is valid under the stated $C^{1,1}$ regularity; if one assumes $f$ is twice continuously differentiable, it is simply the familiar Hessian proof. The question's phrase ``$\mu$-convex'' is interpreted here as $\mu$-strongly convex, consistently with the section setup.

\paragraph{Second claim: the interpolation inequality.}
Assume first that $L>\mu$ so that the given co-coercivity inequality can be used directly. Define

\[
d:=x-y,
\qquad
q:=\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y).
\]

Since $\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y)=q-\mu d$, the supplied fact reads

\[
\frac{1}{L-\mu}\|q-\mu d\|^2
\leq\langle q-\mu d,d\rangle.
\]

Multiplication by $L-\mu$ and expansion yield

\begin{align*}
\|q\|^2-2\mu\langle q,d\rangle+\mu^2\|d\|^2
&\leq(L-\mu)\langle q,d\rangle-\mu(L-\mu)\|d\|^2.
\end{align*}

Move the terms involving $d$ and $q$ to opposite sides:

\[
\|q\|^2+\mu L\|d\|^2
\leq(L+\mu)\langle q,d\rangle.
\]

Dividing by $L$ proves the requested inequality:

\[
\mu \|x-y\|^2+\frac{1}{L}\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|^2
\leq\left(1+\frac{\mu}{L}\right)
\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle.
\]

When $L=\mu$, the displayed co-coercivity premise has a zero denominator. The conclusion still follows directly from $\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)=\mu(x-y)$ (equivalently, $g$ has constant gradient), so this endpoint is not an exception to the final result.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 3}\label{sol:spr2025-opt-3}

\paragraph{Proximal one-step recurrence --- 6 points}

\paragraph{Exam-ready result.}
At an optimizer $x^*$, \cref{lem:prox-fixed} supplies the proximal fixed-point identity
\[
x^*=\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)\bigr).
\]
Compare it with the noisy update and use non-expansiveness of $\prox_{\gamma R}$.

\begin{lemma}[Proximal-gradient model and the fixed point $x^*$]\label[lemma]{lem:prox-fixed}
Let $f:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ be differentiable, let $R:\mathbb{R}^d\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ be proper, convex, and lower-semicontinuous, and let $\gamma>0$. For every $x\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
\[
\argmin_{z\in\mathbb{R}^d}
\Bigl(
f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),z-x\rangle+R(z)+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-x\|^2
\Bigr)
=\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x-\gamma\nabla f(x)\bigr).
\]
The left-hand side is the standard proximal-gradient model of $F=f+R$ at $x$: replace $f$ by its tangent, keep the same regularizer $R$, and add the same proximity quadratic that appears in the definition of $\prox_{\gamma R}$, now centered at the current point $x$. The two sides agree because
\[
\langle\nabla f(x),z-x\rangle+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-x\|^2
=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\bigl\|z-\bigl(x-\gamma\nabla f(x)\bigr)\bigr\|^2
-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2.
\]
In particular, a point $x^*$ minimizes $F$ if and only if it is a fixed point of this model,
\[
x^*=\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)\bigr),
\]
which is equivalent to the composite Fermat condition $0\in\nabla f(x^*)+\partial R(x^*)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The completing-the-square identity is elementary:
\begin{align*}
\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-x\|^2+\langle\nabla f(x),z-x\rangle
&=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\Bigl(\|z-x\|^2+2\langle\gamma\nabla f(x),z-x\rangle\Bigr)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\Bigl(\|z-x+\gamma\nabla f(x)\|^2-\gamma^2\|\nabla f(x)\|^2\Bigr)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\gamma}\bigl\|z-\bigl(x-\gamma\nabla f(x)\bigr)\bigr\|^2
-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2.
\end{align*}
The terms $f(x)$ and $-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x)\|^2$ do not depend on $z$. Therefore the proximal-gradient model differs from
\[
R(z)+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\bigl\|z-\bigl(x-\gamma\nabla f(x)\bigr)\bigr\|^2
\]
by a $z$-independent constant, and the two argmins coincide. The right-hand side is the original definition of $\prox_{\gamma R}(v)$ at the single input $v=x-\gamma\nabla f(x)$. The regularizer $R$ and the quadratic $\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2$ are exactly those of the proximal map; only the center $v$ has been chosen so that the linear piece of $f$ is absorbed into the quadratic.

It remains to identify the fixed points. By the first-order characterization of the proximal map,
\[
u=\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{v-u}{\gamma}\in\partial R(u).
\]
Set $u=x^*$ and $v=x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)$. The membership becomes
\[
\frac{(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*))-x^*}{\gamma}
=-\nabla f(x^*)
\in\partial R(x^*),
\]
that is, $0\in\nabla f(x^*)+\partial R(x^*)$. Since $f$ is differentiable, this is Fermat's rule for $F=f+R$, so it holds if and only if $x^*$ minimizes $F$.
\end{proof}

This is not a new formula for $\prox$. The map is still
\[
\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
=\argmin_z\Bigl(R(z)+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-v\|^2\Bigr),
\]
with the same regularizer $R$ and the same proximity quadratic. The only new choice is the input
\[
v=x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*).
\]
\Cref{lem:prox-fixed} shows why that input appears: linearizing $f$ at $x^*$ and completing the square converts the proximal-gradient model of $F=f+R$ into the original prox objective centered at $x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)$. A minimizer is precisely a point that is already optimal for its own model, hence a fixed point.

The same lemma also records the first-order route used in a timed answer. The optimality condition for the composite objective is

\[
0\in\nabla f(x^*)+\partial R(x^*).
\]

The characterization of a proximal point is

\[
u=\prox_{\gamma R}(v)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{v-u}{\gamma}\in\partial R(u).
\]

Apply it with $u=x^*$ and $v=x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)$. The optimality condition gives

\[
x^*=\prox_{\gamma R}\left(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)\right).
\]

The algorithm and this fixed-point identity can now be compared:

\begin{align*}
\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2
&=\Bigl\|
\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^k-\gamma(\nabla f(x^k)+\xi^k)\bigr)
-\prox_{\gamma R}\bigl(x^*-\gamma\nabla f(x^*)\bigr)
\Bigr\|^2\\
&\leq
    \left\|x^k-x^*-\gamma\bigl(\nabla f(x^k)-\nabla f(x^*)+\xi^k\bigr)\right\|^2.
\end{align*}

The inequality is non-expansiveness of the proximal map (\cref{lem:prox-nonexpansive}):

\[
\|\prox_{\gamma R}(u)-\prox_{\gamma R}(v)\|
\leq\|u-v\|.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Proximal map is nonexpansive]\label[lemma]{lem:prox-nonexpansive}
Let $R:\mathbb{R}^d\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ be proper, convex, and lower-semicontinuous, and let $\gamma>0$. The proximal map is firmly nonexpansive: for all $u,v\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
\[
\bigl\|\prox_{\gamma R}(u)-\prox_{\gamma R}(v)\bigr\|^2
\leq
\bigl\langle
\prox_{\gamma R}(u)-\prox_{\gamma R}(v),\,
u-v
\bigr\rangle.
\]
In particular it is nonexpansive,
\[
\|\prox_{\gamma R}(u)-\prox_{\gamma R}(v)\|
\leq\|u-v\|.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $z:=\prox_{\gamma R}(u)$ and $w:=\prox_{\gamma R}(v)$. The first-order characterization already used for the fixed point $x^*$ reads
\[
\frac{u-z}{\gamma}\in\partial R(z),
\qquad
\frac{v-w}{\gamma}\in\partial R(w).
\]
Convexity of $R$ makes $\partial R$ monotone, so
\[
\Bigl\langle
\frac{u-z}{\gamma}-\frac{v-w}{\gamma},\,
z-w
\Bigr\rangle
\geq 0.
\]
Multiplication by $\gamma>0$ rearranges to the firm inequality
\[
\|z-w\|^2
\leq\langle z-w,\,u-v\rangle.
\]
Cauchy--Schwarz on the right-hand side gives
\[
\|z-w\|^2
\leq\|z-w\|\,\|u-v\|.
\]
If $z=w$ the claim is immediate; otherwise divide by $\|z-w\|$.
\end{proof}

The same firm inequality can be read off the definition without naming $\partial R$. The prox objective
\[
\Phi_u(\zeta)=R(\zeta)+\frac1{2\gamma}\|\zeta-u\|^2
\]
is $\frac1\gamma$-strongly convex (the quadratic supplies the modulus; convexity of $R$ only keeps $\Phi_u$ convex). Strong convexity at the minimizer $z$ yields
\[
\Phi_u(w)\geq\Phi_u(z)+\frac1{2\gamma}\|w-z\|^2,
\]
and the symmetric inequality for $\Phi_v$ at $w$. Adding these two bounds cancels the $R$ terms and rearranges to $\langle z-w,\,u-v\rangle\geq\|z-w\|^2$. Comparing the two prox inequalities \emph{without} the strong-convexity surplus only produces monotonicity $\langle z-w,\,u-v\rangle\geq 0$, which does not imply a Lipschitz bound.

Firm nonexpansiveness is strictly stronger than nonexpansiveness: it is equivalent to
\[
\|z-w\|^2
+\bigl\|(u-z)-(v-w)\bigr\|^2
\leq\|u-v\|^2,
\]
so both $\prox_{\gamma R}$ and $I-\prox_{\gamma R}$ are nonexpansive. Projection onto a closed convex set is the special case $R=I_C$. The argument uses convexity of $R$; without it the prox need not be single-valued, and need not be $1$-Lipschitz.

This is the only place where the nonsmooth regularizer $R$ enters the convergence proof. Once the proximal map has reduced the update to the right-hand side, the rest of the argument is an ordinary squared-norm calculation.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 4}\label{sol:spr2025-opt-4}

\paragraph{Conditional variance decomposition --- 6 points}

\paragraph{Exam-ready result.}
Condition on the past, expand the square from \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3} by \cref{lem:sq-expand}, use $\E_k[\xi^k]=0$ to eliminate the cross term, and bound the conditional second moment of $\xi^k$ by $\sigma^2$.

The working identity is not the abstract bias--variance formula displayed in the readiness check. It is the elementary expansion of a squared Euclidean norm.

\begin{lemma}[Squared-norm expansion]\label[lemma]{lem:sq-expand}
For all $u,v\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
\[
\|u+v\|^2
=\|u\|^2+2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2,
\qquad
\|u-v\|^2
=\|u\|^2-2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2.
\]
In particular, for any three vectors $a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}^d$,
\[
\|a-b-c\|^2
=\|a-b\|^2-2\langle a-b,c\rangle+\|c\|^2.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By definition of the Euclidean norm,
\[
\|u+v\|^2
=\langle u+v,u+v\rangle
=\langle u,u\rangle+\langle u,v\rangle+\langle v,u\rangle+\langle v,v\rangle.
\]
The inner product is symmetric, so $\langle u,v\rangle+\langle v,u\rangle=2\langle u,v\rangle$, and the first identity follows. The second is the same expansion with $v$ replaced by $-v$. The three-term form is the second identity with $u=a-b$ and $v=c$.
\end{proof}

Let

\[
\Delta_k:=x^k-x^*,
\qquad
h_k:=\nabla f(x^k)-\nabla f(x^*).
\]

Both are $\mathcal{F}_k$-measurable. From \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3},

\[
\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2
\leq\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k-\gamma\xi^k\|^2.
\]

Apply \cref{lem:sq-expand} with $a=\Delta_k$, $b=\gamma h_k$, and $c=\gamma\xi^k$:

\[
\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k-\gamma\xi^k\|^2
=\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k\|^2
-2\gamma\langle\Delta_k-\gamma h_k,\xi^k\rangle
+\gamma^2\|\xi^k\|^2.
\]

Now take the conditional expectation $\E_k[\,\cdot\,]$. The first summand is $\mathcal{F}_k$-measurable, hence deterministic given the past. The cross term factors as $-2\gamma\langle\Delta_k-\gamma h_k,\E_k[\xi^k]\rangle$ and vanishes because $\E_k[\xi^k]=0$. The last summand is at most $\gamma^2\sigma^2$. Therefore

\begin{align*}
\E_k\left[\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2\right]
&\leq\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k\|^2
-2\gamma\left\langle\Delta_k-\gamma h_k,\E_k[\xi^k]\right\rangle
+\gamma^2\E_k\left[\|\xi^k\|^2\right]\\
&\leq\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k\|^2+\gamma^2\sigma^2.
\end{align*}

The readiness-check identity
\[
\E\bigl[\|Z-a\|^2\mid\mathcal{F}\bigr]
=\bigl\|\E[Z\mid\mathcal{F}]-a\bigr\|^2
+\E\bigl[\|Z-\E[Z\mid\mathcal{F}]\|^2\mid\mathcal{F}\bigr]
\]
is the same expansion after taking $\E[\,\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}]$: write $Z-a=(Z-\E[Z\mid\mathcal{F}])+(\E[Z\mid\mathcal{F}]-a)$ and use that the two summands are conditionally orthogonal. It is a packaging of \cref{lem:sq-expand}, not an extra ingredient. The calculation above never needs to name $Z$ and $a$; it expands the square first, then takes $\E_k$.

The resulting bound is exactly

\[
\E_k\bigl[\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2\bigr]
\leq\|x^k-x^* -\gamma(\nabla f(x^k)-\nabla f(x^*))\|^2+\gamma^2\sigma^2.
\]

The result does not say that stochastic gradients have no effect. It says their first-order effect averages to zero; their second-order effect remains as the additive term $\gamma^2\sigma^2$.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 5}\label{sol:spr2025-opt-5}

\paragraph{Choosing the contracting step size --- 5 points}

\paragraph{Exam-ready result.}
Use the \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-2}{Problem~2} inequality with $x=x^k$ and $y=x^*$ in the conditional recurrence from \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-4}{Problem~4}. With $\gamma=2/(\mu+L)$, the coefficient of $\|\nabla f(x^k)-\nabla f(x^*)\|^2$ is exactly zero, leaving the contraction $1-4\mu L/(\mu+L)^2$.

Expand the deterministic square in the \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-4}{Problem~4} bound, again by \cref{lem:sq-expand}:

\[
\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k\|^2
=\|\Delta_k\|^2-2\gamma\langle h_k,\Delta_k\rangle+\gamma^2\|h_k\|^2.
\]

\jump{prob:spr2025-opt-2}{Problem~2}, applied to $x^k$ and $x^*$, states

\[
\mu L\|\Delta_k\|^2+\|h_k\|^2
\leq(\mu+L)\langle h_k,\Delta_k\rangle.
\]

Set

\[
\gamma=\frac{2}{\mu+L}.
\]

Then

\begin{align*}
\|\Delta_k-\gamma h_k\|^2
&\leq \|\Delta_k\|^2
-\frac{4}{(\mu+L)^2}
\left(\mu L\|\Delta_k\|^2+\|h_k\|^2\right)
+\frac{4}{(\mu+L)^2}\|h_k\|^2\\
&=\left(1-\frac{4\mu L}{(\mu+L)^2}\right)\|\Delta_k\|^2.
\end{align*}

Define

\[
\rho:=\frac{4\mu L}{(\mu+L)^2}.
\]

Substitution into \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-4}{Problem~4} gives, conditionally on $\mathcal{F}_k$,

\[
\E_k\left[\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2\right]
\leq(1-\rho)\|x^k-x^*\|^2+\gamma^2\sigma^2.
\]

Finally apply the tower property $\E[\E_k[Z]]=\E[Z]$ to obtain

\[
\E\left[\|x^{k+1}-x^*\|^2\right]
\leq(1-\rho)\E\left[\|x^k-x^*\|^2\right]+\gamma^2\sigma^2.
\]

Equivalently,

\[
1-\rho=\left(\frac{L-\mu}{L+\mu}\right)^2.
\]

Thus a better conditioned problem ($L/\mu$ closer to one) contracts faster, while a poorly conditioned problem contracts slowly.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 6}\label{sol:spr2025-opt-6}

\paragraph{Iteration complexity and the stochastic error floor --- 5 points}

\paragraph{Exam-ready result.}
Choose $\gamma=2/(\mu+L)$ and unroll the \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-5}{Problem~5} recurrence. Bound $(1-\rho)^k$ by $e^{-\rho k}$, use $1/\rho\leq(L/\mu+3)/4$, and bound the geometric noise term by $\gamma\sigma^2/\mu$.

Let

\[
a_k:=\E\left[\|x^k-x^*\|^2\right].
\]

Repeatedly applying the \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-5}{Problem~5} recurrence gives

\begin{align*}
a_k
&\leq(1-\rho)^k a_0
+\gamma^2\sigma^2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(1-\rho)^j\\
&=(1-\rho)^k a_0
+\frac{\gamma^2\sigma^2}{\rho}\left(1-(1-\rho)^k\right)\\
&\leq e^{-\rho k}a_0+\frac{\gamma^2\sigma^2}{\rho}.
\end{align*}

For $\kappa:=L/\mu\geq1$,

\[
\frac{1}{\rho}
=\frac{(\mu+L)^2}{4\mu L}
=\frac{\kappa+2+1/\kappa}{4}
\leq\frac{\kappa+3}{4}
=\frac{L/\mu+3}{4}.
\]

For the meaningful precision range $0<\varepsilon<1$, the condition

\[
k\geq\frac{L/\mu+3}{4}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
\]

therefore implies $\rho k\geq\log(1/\varepsilon)$, hence

\[
e^{-\rho k}\leq\varepsilon.
\]

At the selected step size,

\[
\frac{\gamma^2}{\rho}
=\frac{1}{\mu L}
\leq\frac{\gamma}{\mu},
\]

where the final inequality uses $\gamma=2/(\mu+L)\geq1/L$, which follows from $\mu\leq L$. Consequently,

\[
\E\left[\|x^k-x^*\|^2\right]
\leq\varepsilon\|x^0-x^*\|^2+\frac{\gamma\sigma^2}{\mu}.
\]

For $\varepsilon\geq1$, the requested initialization term is already no smaller than $\|x^0-x^*\|^2$, so the interesting regime is $0<\varepsilon<1$. The complexity of shrinking the transient term is

\[
O\left(\frac{L}{\mu}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right).
\]

The additive term $\gamma\sigma^2/\mu$ is the stochastic error floor. With a constant step size and nonzero noise variance, this theorem does not claim arbitrarily accurate mean-square convergence. To reduce that floor, one needs a smaller or decaying step size, variance reduction, or a larger mini-batch; those changes are outside this particular proof.

\subsubsection{Common mistakes}

\begin{itemize}
  \item Writing strong convexity only as ``positive Hessian'' without stating the function-value or gradient inequality required by the question.
  \item Applying the co-coercivity inequality to $f$ directly. It is applied to the shifted convex, $(L-\mu)$-smooth function $g=f-\mu\|x\|^2/2$.
  \item Forgetting the proximal fixed-point identity for $x^*$. The comparison in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-3}{Problem~3} is between two proximal-map outputs, not between an update and an arbitrary vector.
  \item Conditioning on $\xi^k$ itself in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-4}{Problem~4}. Then the cross term is not eliminated by mean-zero noise; condition on the history before drawing $\xi^k$.
  \item Confusing an unconditional bound $\E\|\xi^k\|^2\leq\sigma^2$ with the conditional bound needed when $x^k$ depends on earlier randomness. Independence of fresh noise is a sufficient condition.
  \item Losing the cancellation in \jump{prob:spr2025-opt-5}{Problem~5} by choosing $\gamma$ incorrectly. The coefficient of $\|h_k\|^2$ is zero precisely at $\gamma=2/(\mu+L)$.
  \item Reading the final inequality as convergence to $x^*$ despite $\sigma^2>0$. The second term is a residual noise floor.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Exam-ready compression}

Under time pressure, retain this six-line proof skeleton:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item State the $L$-Lipschitz-gradient and $\mu$-strong-convexity definitions.
  \item Set $g=f-\mu\|\cdot\|^2/2$, use its $(L-\mu)$-smooth convex co-coercivity, and expand $\nabla g(x)-\nabla g(y)=\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)-\mu(x-y)$.
  \item Use $0\in\nabla f(x^*)+\partial R(x^*)$ to write the proximal fixed point for $x^*$, then invoke \cref{lem:prox-nonexpansive}.
  \item Condition on the past and expand the square; the zero-mean cross term disappears and leaves $\gamma^2\sigma^2$.
  \item With $\gamma=2/(\mu+L)$, apply the interpolation inequality so the gradient-norm term cancels and obtain $a_{k+1}\leq(1-\rho)a_k+\gamma^2\sigma^2$.
  \item Sum the geometric series, use $(1-\rho)^k\leq e^{-\rho k}$ and $1/\rho\leq(L/\mu+3)/4$.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{What this section does not cover}

This exam section proves a constant-step, strongly-convex proximal-SGD bound. It does not establish:

\begin{itemize}
  \item convergence for merely convex or nonconvex objectives, saddle-point escape, or global deep-network optimization;
  \item accelerated methods such as Nesterov acceleration, heavy-ball momentum, adaptive methods, or their different assumptions;
  \item diminishing-step stochastic approximation, iterate averaging, mini-batch variance scaling, or variance-reduced algorithms such as SVRG and SAGA;
  \item constrained optimization beyond what can be encoded by a convex proximal term, primal--dual methods, or augmented Lagrangians;
  \item high-probability bounds, heavy-tailed noise, biased gradients, or correlated data streams;
  \item machine-learning security and robust optimization. Adversarial or distributionally robust objectives need a separate treatment rather than being inferred from this convergence proof.
\end{itemize}

These are useful extension topics, but they are not prerequisites for reconstructing the six requested exam answers.

\input{opt-universal-review}

\section{D. Natural Language Processing}

\subsection{Syllabus map: six official items and their motivations}
\label{sec:spr2025-nlp-syllabus}

The official Qiuzhen AI qualifying-exam syllabus for natural language processing is not a list of classical tasks---tagging, parsing, translation---and it is not six unrelated slogans. It is one statistical story told in six compressions.

\paragraph{Why the subject is called natural language processing.}
The adjective \emph{natural} is a contrast with \emph{formal}. Computers were built to process formal languages: programs, logical formulae, mathematical notation. Those languages are designed by people for machines; their syntax is stipulated, and ambiguity is treated as a bug. English, Chinese, and the other human languages were not designed for machines. The same string can have several structures, the same intent can be phrased in many ways, and much of the meaning sits in unwritten context. \emph{Processing} means treating that human language as a computational object---to analyse, generate, or transform it---rather than only to describe it linguistically.

The statistical pressure behind the name is therefore this. Human language arrives as a discrete, high-dimensional, sparse, and ambiguous sequence. Inferring or generating it from a finite corpus requires compressing it into a probability object a machine can estimate. Formal languages rarely force one to estimate \(p(\text{next symbol}\mid\text{history})\); natural language does. A sentence, or any other tokenized string, is a finite sequence from a large discrete vocabulary, and the free joint on such sequences has far too many atoms to identify from any corpus.

The six official items are six layers of that compression, not six interchangeable solvers of one optimisation problem. First one chooses a factorisation that a finite sample can actually identify; then similar tokens share parameters through vectors; then long-range conditionals are computed without a left-to-right bottleneck; then the objective changes from ``imitate the web'' to ``match a human preference''; then one asks how test loss moves when data or parameters grow; and finally one stops asking the parameters to store every fact, by conditioning on retrieved text. Each layer relaxes a different constraint and pays a different cost. There is no globally best item: an improvement at one layer is not a substitute for the layer beneath it.

The six paragraphs below follow the official list. Each one starts from the modelling pressure, names the symbols before they appear in a display, records what the item buys and what it costs, and points to the Spring 2025 problem that uses the object. The map is study material. It does not change the eight official questions that follow.

\paragraph{1. Statistical modelling assumptions and methods.}
Write a sentence as a sequence of tokens\footnote{A \emph{token} is one atom of a finite alphabet \(\mathcal{V}\), produced by a tokenizer: a map from a raw string to a finite sequence in \(\mathcal{V}^*\). The sentence in the main text is only a convenient unit. The same discrete object may be a word, a subword piece (BPE, WordPiece, and similar schemes), a character, or a byte; the string being tokenized may be a sentence, a document, a dialogue, or a code file. What the probability model sees is only the resulting sequence \(w_1,\ldots,w_n\in\mathcal{V}\). The chain rule and the Markov assumption below therefore apply to any such sequence, not merely to an English sentence split on whitespace.} \(w_1,\ldots,w_n\), each taking values in a finite vocabulary \(\mathcal{V}\). The chain rule

\[
p(w_1,\ldots,w_n)
=\prod_{t=1}^{n} p(w_t\mid w_1,\ldots,w_{t-1})
\]

is an identity, not a model. The last factors still depend on a history whose number of possible values grows with \(t\), so they cannot be estimated by counting. The first modelling decision is therefore a Markov assumption of order \(k\): the next token depends only on the preceding \(k\) tokens \(w_{t-k:t-1}:=(w_{t-k},\ldots,w_{t-1})\). Then

\[
p(w_1,\ldots,w_n)
=\prod_{t=1}^{n} p(w_t\mid w_{t-k:t-1}).
\]

A \emph{trigram} model is the case \(k=2\). If \(c(\cdot)\) denotes corpus counts, maximum likelihood becomes the empirical conditional frequency

\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\frac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)}{c(w_1,w_2)},
\]

provided \(c(w_1,w_2)>0\). Most trigrams never occur. The estimate is then zero, or undefined when the whole context is unseen. Smoothing, interpolation, and backoff repair this by sharing mass with lower-order conditionals \(p(w_3\mid w_2)\) and \(p(w_3)\). The repair is an instance of the bias--variance trade-off in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-bias-variance}, not a cosmetic patch: one accepts a little bias in order to cut the sampling variance of rare events.

\begin{definition}[Bias--variance decomposition]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-bias-variance}
Let \(f^\star\) be a target object---a parameter, a conditional probability, or a predictor---and let \(\widehat f_S\) be an estimator computed from a random sample \(S\). Write \(\overline f:=\mathbb{E}_S[\widehat f_S]\) for the average estimator over samples. Whenever a squared error makes sense,
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[\|\widehat f_S-f^\star\|^2\bigr]
=\underbrace{\|\overline f-f^\star\|^2}_{\mathrm{bias}^2}
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[\|\widehat f_S-\overline f\|^2\bigr]}_{\mathrm{variance}}.
\]
The left-hand side is the mean squared error of \(\widehat f_S\). The first term on the right is the squared systematic error of the estimator's mean; the second is the scatter of \(\widehat f_S\) around that mean.

In statistical learning the same split is usually written for risk rather than for a single parameter. Let \(L\) be a population loss, let \(h^\star\) minimise \(L\) among all measurable predictors, let \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\) minimise \(L\) inside a model class \(\mathcal{H}\), and let \(\widehat h_S\in\mathcal{H}\) be the predictor fitted from \(S\). Then
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)\bigr]-L(h^\star)
=\underbrace{L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)}_{\text{approximation error (bias)}}
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]}_{\text{estimation error (variance)}}.
\]
The first gap is incurred by restricting attention to \(\mathcal{H}\). The second gap is incurred because a finite sample does not identify the best member of \(\mathcal{H}\). In AI theory this is the approximation--estimation decomposition: a more expressive class (or a less regularised estimator) shrinks the first term and typically inflates the second. The second half is easy to misread; \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-finer-class} records why the two terms need not fall together.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[A finer class need not shrink both terms]\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-finer-class}
The sentence in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-bias-variance} names three different objects, and only one of them is the truth. Keep the sample size \(n:=|S|\) fixed, and write \(\widehat h_S^{\mathcal{H}}\) for the predictor fitted in \(\mathcal{H}\). For that class the risks sit in the chain
\[
L(h^\star)
\;\leq\;
L(h_{\mathcal{H}})
\;\leq\;
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S^{\mathcal{H}})\bigr].
\]
Approximation error is the first gap, \(L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)\): how good the best member of \(\mathcal{H}\) is, with no sampling noise. Estimation error is the second gap, \(\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h_S^{\mathcal{H}})-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})]\): how far the fitted predictor is from that best member, not from \(h^\star\).

Now enlarge the class to \(\mathcal{H}'\supset\mathcal{H}\). The first gap can only fall, or stay: a minimum over a larger set cannot increase, so \(L(h_{\mathcal{H}'})\leq L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\). The second gap is measured against a new target \(h_{\mathcal{H}'}\). The same \(n\) samples now have to locate a point in a bigger set, so
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S^{\mathcal{H}'})-L(h_{\mathcal{H}'})\bigr]
\]
typically grows. The two terms can therefore move in opposite directions. Their sum---the excess risk---may still fall, if the drop in approximation outweighs the rise in estimation. That is a fact about the sum, not a claim that estimation itself got smaller. The word ``typically'' is not a monotonicity theorem; \cref{note:spr2025-nlp-est-not-mono} records what is monotone and what is not.
\end{remark}

\begin{note}[Estimation error is not monotone in \(\mathcal{H}\)]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-est-not-mono}
Keep \(\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{H}'\) and the sample size \(n\) fixed. Two comparisons are genuine inequalities. Approximation error cannot increase:
\[
L(h_{\mathcal{H}'})-L(h^\star)
\leq
L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star),
\]
because a minimum over a larger set cannot rise. The uniform deviation
\[
\Delta_n(\mathcal{H})
:=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\bigl\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\bigr\rvert
\]
is likewise nondecreasing: \(\Delta_n(\mathcal{H}')\ge\Delta_n(\mathcal{H})\). Rademacher and VC bounds control \(\mathbb{E}[\Delta_n(\mathcal{H})]\), so they inherit the same monotonicity. The textbook slogan ``a larger class needs more samples'' is a statement about this supremum, not about the estimation error itself.

Write
\[
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})
:=\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S^{\mathcal{H}})-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]
\]
for the estimation gap in \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-finer-class}. For ERM one has only a comparison of the form \(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\Delta_n(\mathcal{H})]\). An upper bound that grows does not force \(\mathcal{E}\) to grow. In particular \(\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{H}'\) does not imply \(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H}')\ge\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})\).

A one-point enlargement already gives a counterexample. Let \(\mathcal{H}\) consist of the two constant classifiers, and set \(\mathcal{H}'=\mathcal{H}\cup\{h^\star\}\) with \(h^\star\) the Bayes predictor. In \(\mathcal{H}\) the two empirical risks often tie, so the ERM is unstable and \(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})\) stays of constant order. In \(\mathcal{H}'\), if \(h^\star\) is separated on \(L_S\), the ERM selects it with high probability and \(\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H}')=0\). The class got larger; locating the in-class target got easier, because the added function is not one more interpolator of noise.

The unigram-to-trigram and histogram refinements below are the complementary, typical direction: the new functions are many and comparable, \(\Delta_n\) loosens, and \(\mathcal{E}\) usually rises. That is an observation about walking up one nested scale of models, not a claim about every inclusion.
\end{note}

\begin{example}[Unigram to trigram, small corpus]
A unigram cannot represent \(p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)\), so its approximation error is large, but every token updates a single distribution and the counts are of order \(n\). Passing to a trigram lets the best-in-class conditional depend on two words, so approximation error falls. The same \(n\) tokens are now split across order \(|\mathcal{V}|^2\) contexts. Most cells are empty, the count ratio is undefined or wildly unstable, and estimation error rises. The two terms do not fall together.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Histogram with \(k\) bins versus \(2k\) bins]
Estimate a non-constant conditional by a piecewise-constant function on \(k\) equal bins, using \(n\) samples. Doubling the number of bins improves the approximation of any Lipschitz conditional. Each bin then has about \(n/(2k)\) points instead of \(n/k\), so the variance of a bin frequency doubles, of order \(k/n\). Again approximation falls and estimation rises; only extra samples, not the refinement itself, would cut the second term.
\end{example}

\begin{note}[Why smoothing is called a bias--variance trade-off]
\noindent\textbf{Form in this paragraph.}
An interpolated trigram is a convex combination
\[
\widehat p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\lambda_3\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
+\lambda_2\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_2)
+\lambda_1\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3),
\]
with \(\lambda_i\geq 0\) and \(\lambda_1+\lambda_2+\lambda_3=1\). The subscript \(\mathrm{ML}\) means maximum likelihood: \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}\) is the raw count ratio displayed above, not the smoothed interpolant. Explicitly,
\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\frac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)}{c(w_1,w_2)},
\qquad
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_2)
=\frac{c(w_2,w_3)}{c(w_2)},
\qquad
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3)
=\frac{c(w_3)}{\sum_{w}c(w)},
\]
each defined only when the denominator is positive. Here \(w_1,w_2,w_3\in\mathcal{V}\) are three vocabulary items being queried, not the first three positions of one sentence. The symbol \(c(\cdot)\) is a corpus count: slide a window through the whole tokenized corpus and count how often that consecutive pattern occurs. Thus \(c(w_1,w_2,w_3)\) is the number of times the triple appears as three successive tokens; \(c(w_1,w_2)\) and \(c(w_2,w_3)\) are the corresponding pair counts; \(c(w_2)\) and \(c(w_3)\) are ordinary unigram counts (how often that single token appears); and \(\sum_{w}c(w)\) is the length of the corpus. The three ratios are therefore ``how often the next token was \(w_3\), among the windows whose history matched''. The hat marks an estimator computed from the corpus; the subscript distinguishes this unsmoothed frequency from the mixture \(\widehat p\) on the left-hand side. Backoff is the same idea with a hard switch: if \(c(w_1,w_2)\) is large one uses the trigram ratio; if not, one falls back to the bigram or the unigram. The target \(f^\star\) in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-bias-variance} is the true conditional \(p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)\); the sample \(S\) is a random corpus; \(\widehat f_S\) is either \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}\) or the smoothed \(\widehat p\).

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Why the first term is called bias.}
Bias is a property of the estimator's expectation, not of a single unlucky corpus. If \(\mathbb{E}_S[\widehat f_S]\neq f^\star\), the estimator is biased. Pure maximum likelihood, when the context count is positive, is unbiased for that conditional: its average over corpora recovers \(p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)\). Interpolation is biased because it systematically mixes in \(p(w_3\mid w_2)\) and \(p(w_3)\), which are different functions of the history. The estimator is being pulled toward a simpler model. In the learning-theoretic form, that pull is exactly the approximation error of a smaller class: one has replaced the trigram family by a mixture that cannot represent every trigram conditional.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Why the second term is called variance.}
Variance is fluctuation across samples. For a rare context, \(c(w_1,w_2)\) is small, so the ratio \(c(w_1,w_2,w_3)/c(w_1,w_2)\) jumps from one corpus to the next and is often zero. That is estimation error: the class can express the trigram, but \(S\) does not contain enough copies of the context to identify it. Lower-order counts \(c(w_2)\) and \(c(w_3)\) are larger, so \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_2)\) and \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3)\) move much less when \(S\) is redrawn. Mixing them in reduces \(\mathbb{E}_S[\|\widehat f_S-\overline f\|^2]\).

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Why the two terms trade off.}
The identity in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-bias-variance} is a sum of two nonnegative pieces. At finite sample size one cannot send both to zero unless the simpler model is already correct. Taking \(\lambda_3=1\) (pure maximum likelihood) sets the bias to zero on seen contexts and leaves the variance large---including the catastrophe that an unseen context makes the estimator undefined. Taking \(\lambda_3=0\) (pure unigram) makes the variance small and the bias large: the estimator ignores \(w_1,w_2\) even when those counts are abundant. Smoothing chooses an interior \(\lambda\), accepting a systematic pull toward lower-order conditionals in exchange for an estimator that exists and concentrates. The same trade-off reappears throughout the syllabus: a richer class or a less regularised fit (items~2--3, larger \(P\)) cuts approximation error and raises estimation error; more data (item~5) cuts estimation error without changing the class.
\end{note}

The same syllabus item also covers richer factorisations that appear on later papers. A topic model such as latent Dirichlet allocation introduces a latent topic for each word and a document-level mixture over topics. A Markov logic network is a log-linear model on a world \(x\): if formula \(i\) has weight \(\lambda_i\) and is true of \(n_i(x)\) groundings in \(x\), then

\[
P(X=x)
=\frac{1}{Z}\exp\Bigl(\sum_i \lambda_i n_i(x)\Bigr),
\]

where the partition function \(Z=\sum_{x'}\exp(\sum_i \lambda_i n_i(x'))\) restores a probability. The \(\lambda_i\) are formula weights, not the tokens \(w_t\) of the trigram model. In all of these cases the pattern is the same: name the random variables, write a factorisation, and say how the unknown factors are estimated.

What this item buys is an identifiable probability: the random variables are named, the factorisation is written, and the unknown factors can be estimated. What it costs is the assumption itself. A Markov truncation of order \(k\) cannot see past \(k\) tokens; raw types are atoms, so near-synonyms share no strength; in a Markov logic network the partition function \(Z\) is typically intractable. Later neural models do not abolish this layer. They still write a chain rule. They only change how \(p(w_t\mid\text{history})\) is parameterised.

\jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-1}{Problem~1} is the trigram-and-smoothing case. Once a conditional \(p(w_t\mid\text{history})\) is in hand, one still needs a rule that turns that distribution into a concrete string. That decoding step is \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-3}{Problem~3}; it does not retrain the model.

\paragraph{2. The mathematics of word2vec.}
A Markov model on raw word types treats ``bank'' next to ``finance'' and ``bank'' next to ``river'' as unrelated atoms. The number of free conditionals then grows with \(|\mathcal{V}|^k\), and synonyms cannot share statistical strength. The second item replaces each type by a vector, so that inner products can express ``used in similar contexts''.

Fix a centre word \(w\) and a context word \(c\) that appears near it. Skip-gram learns two embeddings \(v_w,u_c\in\mathbb{R}^d\) and models

\[
p(c\mid w)
=\frac{\exp(\langle v_w,u_c\rangle)}{\sum_{c'\in\mathcal{V}}\exp(\langle v_w,u_{c'}\rangle)}.
\]

The inner product is a log-odds: a large \(\langle v_w,u_c\rangle\) makes \(c\) a likely neighbour of \(w\). The denominator is a partition function over the whole vocabulary, hence unusable at typical \(|\mathcal{V}|\). Contrastive estimators replace that sum by a comparison between one true neighbour and a few random words. Write \(z\) for the current representation, \(z^+\) for the true neighbour, \(z_i^-\) for the \(i\)-th negative sample, and \(\tau>0\) for a temperature. The InfoNCE loss

\[
\mathcal{L}
=-\log\frac{\exp(\langle z,z^+\rangle/\tau)}{\exp(\langle z,z^+\rangle/\tau)+\sum_i\exp(\langle z,z_i^-\rangle/\tau)}
\]

is a softmax over that small set. Differentiating in \(z\) pulls \(z\) toward \(z^+\) and pushes it away from the \(z_i^-\). A \emph{false negative} is a random \(z_i^-\) that is in fact a reasonable neighbour; in high dimension the largest accidental inner product among many negatives can be large, which distorts the push. GloVe is the same co-occurrence signal written as a global regression on count tables rather than as a local predictive softmax.

What this item buys is parameter sharing: the same inner-product geometry lets ``finance'' and ``bank'' interact without a separate parameter for every type pair. What it costs is a static vector per type, which cannot represent polysemy, and a vocabulary-sized partition function, which forces negative sampling or InfoNCE and brings false negatives. Relative to item~1 this is a genuine improvement of the parameterisation, not a rival factorisation. Relative to item~3 the static embedding is later absorbed as the first layer of a contextual network; the map ``type \(\mapsto\) vector'' does not disappear.

\jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-2}{Problem~2} asks for this idea under the name representation learning: one learns a map from raw tokens to features, then predicts from those features.

\paragraph{3. Self-attention in the Transformer.}
A recurrent network updates a hidden state from left to right. If the sequence has length \(L\), information from position \(1\) reaches position \(L\) only after \(L-1\) transitions, and the training graph is sequential in \(L\). Self-attention is the response: each position is allowed to read any other position in one layer, with weights that depend on the content.

At position \(i\) let \(x_i\) be the incoming vector---a token embedding, or the hidden state of the previous layer. Three learned matrices send it to a query, a key, and a value in \(\mathbb{R}^d\):

\[
q_i=W_Q x_i,
\qquad
k_i=W_K x_i,
\qquad
v_i=W_V x_i.
\]

Repeat this at every position \(i=1,\ldots,L\), and stack the resulting vectors as rows. That produces three matrices \(Q,K,V\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\): the \(i\)-th row of \(Q\) is \(q_i^\top\), and likewise for \(K\) and \(V\).

\begin{center}

  \node[qkv=teal] (qi) at (1.35, 2.35) {\(q_i=W_Q x_i\)};

  \node[qkv=purple] (vi) at (6.65, 2.35) {\(v_i=W_V x_i\)};

  \draw[->, thick, teal!65!black] (xi.south) -- (qi.north);
  \draw[->, thick, orange!65!black] (xi.south) -- (ki.north);
  \draw[->, thick, purple!65!black] (xi.south) -- (vi.north);
  \node[font=\tiny, text=teal!50!black, left=1pt] at ($(xi)!0.52!(qi)$) {\(W_Q\)};
  \node[font=\tiny, text=orange!50!black, right=2pt] at ($(xi)!0.38!(ki)$) {\(W_K\)};
  \node[font=\tiny, text=purple!50!black, right=1pt] at ($(xi)!0.52!(vi)$) {\(W_V\)};

  % Q
  \begin{scope}[shift={(0.4,-0.15)}]
    \node[font=\scriptsize] at (0.95, 1.42) {\(Q\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\)};
    \node[row=teal] (Q1) at (0.95, 0.95) {\(q_1^\top\)};
    \node[row=teal, below=1.2pt of Q1] (Q2) {\(q_2^\top\)};
    \node[font=\scriptsize, text=black!40, below=0.6pt of Q2] (Qd) {\(\vdots\)};
    \node[rowhi=teal, below=0.6pt of Qd] (Qi) {\(q_i^\top\)};
    \node[font=\scriptsize, text=black!40, below=0.6pt of Qi] (Qd2) {\(\vdots\)};
    \node[row=teal, below=0.6pt of Qd2] (QL) {\(q_L^\top\)};
    \node[font=\tiny, text=teal!45!black, right=1pt] at (Qi.east) {row \(i\)};
    \draw[teal!45, rounded corners=2pt]
      ($(Q1.north west)+(-0.08,0.10)$) rectangle ($(QL.south east)+(0.08,-0.10)$);
  \end{scope}

  % K
  \begin{scope}[shift={(3.05,-0.15)}]
    \node[font=\scriptsize] at (0.95, 1.42) {\(K\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\)};
    \node[row=orange] (K1) at (0.95, 0.95) {\(k_1^\top\)};
    \node[row=orange, below=1.2pt of K1] (K2) {\(k_2^\top\)};
    \node[font=\scriptsize, text=black!40, below=0.6pt of K2] (Kd) {\(\vdots\)};
    \node[rowhi=orange, below=0.6pt of Kd] (Ki) {\(k_i^\top\)};
    \node[font=\scriptsize, text=black!40, below=0.6pt of Ki] (Kd2) {\(\vdots\)};
    \node[row=orange, below=0.6pt of Kd2] (KL) {\(k_L^\top\)};
    \node[font=\tiny, text=orange!45!black, right=1pt] at (Ki.east) {row \(i\)};
    \draw[orange!45, rounded corners=2pt]
      ($(K1.north west)+(-0.08,0.10)$) rectangle ($(KL.south east)+(0.08,-0.10)$);
  \end{scope}

  % V
  \begin{scope}[shift={(5.70,-0.15)}]
    \node[font=\scriptsize] at (0.95, 1.42) {\(V\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\)};
    \node[row=purple] (V1) at (0.95, 0.95) {\(v_1^\top\)};
    \node[row=purple, below=1.2pt of V1] (V2) {\(v_2^\top\)};
    \node[font=\scriptsize, text=black!40, below=0.6pt of V2] (Vd) {\(\vdots\)};
    \node[rowhi=purple, below=0.6pt of Vd] (Vi) {\(v_i^\top\)};
    \node[font=\scriptsize, text=black!40, below=0.6pt of Vi] (Vd2) {\(\vdots\)};
    \node[row=purple, below=0.6pt of Vd2] (VL) {\(v_L^\top\)};
    \node[font=\tiny, text=purple!45!black, right=1pt] at (Vi.east) {row \(i\)};
    \draw[purple!45, rounded corners=2pt]
      ($(V1.north west)+(-0.08,0.10)$) rectangle ($(VL.south east)+(0.08,-0.10)$);
  \end{scope}

  \draw[->, thick, teal!60!black] (qi) -- (Qi);
  \draw[->, thick, orange!60!black] (ki) -- (Ki);
  \draw[->, thick, purple!60!black] (vi) -- (Vi);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\end{center}

The other rows are the same construction at the remaining positions: row \(1\) of \(Q\) is \(q_1^\top= (W_Q x_1)^\top\), and so on. The layer then returns

\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V)
=\softmax\Bigl(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d}}\Bigr)V.
\]

The product \(QK^\top\) is an \(L\times L\) matrix of inner products \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\). Softmax applied to each row produces a discrete distribution \(p(\cdot\mid i)\) on positions, so the \(i\)-th output row is the conditional expectation \(\mathbb{E}_{j\sim p(\cdot\mid i)}[v_j]\). The factor \(\sqrt{d}\) keeps those inner products from having variance of order \(d\), which would saturate the softmax. Multi-head attention repeats the same construction on several linear projections, so the model can keep several different ``address books'' at once.

What this item buys is a short path between any two tokens and a training graph that is parallel across the \(L\) positions. What it costs, in the dense version, is \(O(L^2)\) time and memory. Autoregressive generation is still sequential in newly produced tokens: parallelism is a training fact, not a decoding fact. Relative to the trigram of item~1 this is an improvement of the conditional structure---the history is no longer truncated at \(k\) tokens. Relative to a recurrent net it is mainly an improvement of the computation graph, not a different philosophy of language. It uses the geometry of item~2; it does not replace it. The three remarks below unpack the sentence ``each position is allowed to read any other position in one layer'': the explicit sum that implements the read, the roles of \(Q,K,V\) and the row-softmax, and the distinction between one backward pass and a long Jacobian chain.

\begin{remark}[How the read is implemented]\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-attn-read}
The claim is not a metaphor. After the three linear maps, the output at position \(i\) is the single sum
\[
z_i
=\sum_{j=1}^{L}
\alpha_{ij}\,v_j,
\qquad
\alpha_{ij}
=\frac{\exp(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d})}
{\sum_{j'}\exp(\langle q_i,k_{j'}\rangle/\sqrt{d})},
\qquad
v_j=W_V x_j.
\]
Every index \(j\) appears explicitly. In particular, at \(i=L\) the term \(\alpha_{L1}v_1\) brings \(x_1\) into \(z_L\) without passing through \(z_2,\ldots,z_{L-1}\). That is what ``one layer, path length one'' means. An RNN instead uses \(h_t=f(h_{t-1},x_t)\), so \(x_1\) reaches \(h_L\) only after \(L-1\) applications of \(f\).

A causal language model masks \(j>i\) before the softmax, so position \(L\) may read \(\{1,\ldots,L\}\) and not the future. Bidirectional encoder attention omits that mask and may read every index, including \(j>i\). In either case the sum is still one layer; the mask only changes the support of \(\alpha_{i\cdot}\).

The architecture \emph{permits} the read. It does not force a large weight. If \(\langle q_L,k_1\rangle\) is tiny then \(\alpha_{L1}\approx 0\) and position \(L\) effectively ignores position \(1\). The RNN difficulty is harder than a small weight: even when \(h_L\) theoretically depends on \(x_1\), both the signal and the gradient are trapped on the length-\(L-1\) chain.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Roles of \(Q\), \(K\), \(V\), and the row-softmax]\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-qkv-softmax}
The three maps split one incoming vector into three jobs. The query \(q_i=W_Q x_i\) is what position \(i\) is looking for. The key \(k_j=W_K x_j\) is how position \(j\) advertises itself for retrieval. The value \(v_j=W_V x_j\) is the payload that is actually written into \(z_i\) if \(j\) is selected. Scoring uses only \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\); \(v_j\) does not enter the score. That is why one may retrieve a position whose address matches the query while copying a different set of features. Forcing \(q=k=v=x\) ties the scoring geometry to the payload.

The matrix \(S=QK^\top/\sqrt{d}\) stores every pair: \(S_{ij}=\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d}\). Softmax acts on \emph{each row}, not on the \(L^2\) entries as one pool. Row \(i\) becomes a probability \((\alpha_{i1},\ldots,\alpha_{iL})\) on positions, so \(z_i\) is a convex combination of the values. The softmax makes the weights positive and of unit sum, compares scores only relatively, and remains differentiable. Raw scores \(z_i=\sum_j S_{ij}v_j\) can be negative and grow with \(L\). The \(\sqrt{d}\) factor keeps \(\mathrm{Var}(\langle q,k\rangle)\) from scaling like \(d\), which would saturate the softmax into a near one-hot.

This \(U\)-side of attention is not the decoding rule of \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-3}{Problem~3}, and it is not a VAE decoder. Top-\(P\) turns an already computed next-token distribution into one token after training. The row-softmax here is inside the layer, during both training and the forward pass at inference. \Cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-wqkv-params} records which of these objects are trained parameters and which are computed activations.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Which pieces of attention are parameters]\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-wqkv-params}
Three objects sit in the same formulas and are easy to conflate: the matrices \(W_Q,W_K,W_V\), the vectors \(q_i,k_j,v_j\), and the softmax weights \(\alpha_{ij}\). Only the first are stored parameters. The other two are produced in the forward pass and discarded after the backward pass, except insofar as they affect \(\nabla_{W}L\).

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{%
  |>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.15cm}%
  |>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X%
  |>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X%
  |}
\hline
\textbf{Object} & \textbf{What it is} & \textbf{Who supplies it} \\
\hline
\(W_Q,W_K,W_V\) &
Three linear maps, typically of shape \(d_{\mathrm{in}}\times d\) (or \(d_{\mathrm{in}}\times d_h\) per head).
&
\textbf{Trainable parameters}, updated by \(\partial L/\partial W\) together with the embeddings, the feed-forward maps, and the unembedding \(W_U\). \\
\hline
\(q_i=W_Q x_i\), \(k_j=W_K x_j\), \(v_j=W_V x_j\) &
The query, key, and value at a position: activations of those maps on the current incoming vectors.
&
Computed in the forward pass. They change whenever the prefix or the \(W\)'s change. They are not a third lookup table. \\
\hline
\(\alpha_{ij}\) (row-softmax of \(QK^\top/\sqrt{d}\)) &
The weight with which position \(i\) reads position \(j\).
&
\textbf{Computed}, not stored. No one writes down an \(L\times L\) parameter matrix of ``who should read whom''. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

At initialisation the three \(W\)'s are ordinary random (or scaled) matrices. Nothing in the architecture specifies that position \(3\) should attend to position \(1\). After training, a large \(\langle q_3,k_1\rangle\) is a consequence of \(W_Q\) and \(W_K\) having moved, on the data, so that those two incoming vectors score highly. Inference on a frozen checkpoint keeps the \(W\)'s fixed; that is ``trained then frozen'', not ``given in advance by the designer''.

Two neighbouring objects are easy to drop into the same mental slot and should stay out of it. Positional encodings may be a fixed sinusoid or a learned table; they are added to \(x_i\) \emph{before} the three maps, and they are not \(W_Q\). The causal mask that sends \(S_{ij}\) to \(-\infty\) for \(j>i\) is a hard rule, not a learned matrix. Multi-head attention is several independent triples \((W_Q^{(h)},W_K^{(h)},W_V^{(h)})\), each a parameter block, so the layer can keep several different address books.

The exam sentence is therefore: \(W_Q,W_K,W_V\) are trainable linear layers; \(Q,K,V\) are their activations on the current \(x\); the softmax weights are a function of the current \(QK^\top\), not a third parameter table of size \(L\times L\).
\end{remark}

\begin{note}[One backward pass, a longer chain]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-attn-chain}
A training step is always: forward to a scalar loss, then \emph{one} backward traversal of the same graph, then a parameter update. Self-attention does not use fewer optimiser backward passes than an RNN. The recurrent defect is the \emph{length of the chain inside that single backward pass}.

For \(h_t=f(h_{t-1},x_t)\) and a loss that sees \(h_L\),
\[
\frac{\partial L}{\partial h_1}
=
\frac{\partial L}{\partial h_L}
\cdot
\frac{\partial h_L}{\partial h_{L-1}}
\cdots
\frac{\partial h_2}{\partial h_1}.
\]
The product contains \(L-1\) Jacobians of \(f\). If each has singular values below \(1\), the product vanishes; if above \(1\), it explodes. That is vanishing or exploding gradients along the time chain, not ``more backpropagation calls''. BPTT is this one reversed walk along the same path.

In one attention layer the analogous path from \(x_1\) to \(z_L\) is the single summand \(\alpha_{L1}v_1\), plus the local derivatives of the scores and the softmax. There is no product of \(L-1\) recurrent Jacobians. Stacking \(N\) layers lengthens the path to order \(N\) (attention plus feed-forward in each block). Typically \(N\ll L\), so the remaining depth tracks depth of the network, not the sentence length. The dense layer still pays \(\Theta(L^2)\) for writing every \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\) into \(QK^\top\).

The same distinction separates training from generation. While the whole prefix \(x_1,\ldots,x_L\) is already present---as it is in a training sequence---every \(q_i,k_j,v_j\) of the layer can be formed together, because none waits on another position's \emph{output of this layer}. Autoregressive generation is different: token \(t+1\) is not yet in the prefix, so \(p(w_{t+1}\mid w_{\le t})\) cannot be evaluated before \(w_t\) has been sampled. Attention removes the long mixing chain among tokens that are already written; it does not let the model emit the future before the present.
\end{note}

\jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-5}{Problem~5} asks for this comparison with recurrent models. \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-4}{Problem~4} treats the architecture as one ingredient among several, not as a complete explanation of modern systems.

\paragraph{4. Pretraining alignment: principle, limits, and repairs.}
Pretraining maximises next-token likelihood. The resulting policy \(\pi\) is a good model of the training distribution: its optimum is the web's conditional, not ``a useful answer to this instruction''. Before the target is rewritten as a preference, the same next-token loss is typically rerun on written demonstrations. That intermediate stage is supervised fine-tuning.

\begin{definition}[Supervised fine-tuning (SFT)]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-sft}
Supervised fine-tuning---called instruction tuning when the demonstrations are instructions---is next-token training of a pretrained language model on a finite set of prompt--completion pairs \((x,y_{\mathrm{demo}})\). Initialise \(\theta\leftarrow\theta_{\mathrm{PT}}\) and maximise

\[
\sum_{(x,y_{\mathrm{demo}})}\sum_t
\log\pi_\theta\bigl((y_{\mathrm{demo}})_t\mid x,\,(y_{\mathrm{demo}})_{<t}\bigr).
\]

The loss is the same cross-entropy as in pretraining; only the data change. The output \(\theta_{\mathrm{SFT}}\) is a policy that imitates the demonstrations. A frozen copy of this policy is the usual reference \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) in later alignment. SFT is not a preference loss: the human supplies one written answer, not an ordering \(y_w\succ y_l\).
\end{definition}

Alignment then changes the target. The same Transformer weights \(\theta\) are reused throughout: what is swapped is the datum and the objective, not the architecture. Three objects must be kept distinct---the generator \((\pi,\theta)\), the judge \((r,\phi)\), and a frozen copy \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) of the SFT policy in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-sft}. The time order is the diagram below.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/llm-training-pipeline/llm-pipeline-grok.png}
\end{center}

A typical data point is a prompt \(x\) together with two responses, a preferred one \(y_w\) and a dispreferred one \(y_l\). The Bradley--Terry model of \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-bradley-terry} turns that pairwise comparison into a scalar reward \(r_\phi(x,y)\) by

\[
\mathbb{P}(y_w\succ y_l\mid x)
=\sigma\bigl(r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l)\bigr),
\]

where \(\sigma(u)=(1+e^{-u})^{-1}\) and \(\phi\) are the reward parameters.

\begin{definition}[Bradley--Terry model]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-bradley-terry}
The classical model is a probability on pairwise comparisons. Let \(\mathcal{A}\) be a finite set of items, and assign each item \(a\in\mathcal{A}\) a real strength \(s_a\). A comparison of \(a\) against \(b\) is a Bernoulli trial
\[
\mathbb{P}(a\succ b)
=\sigma(s_a-s_b)
=\frac{e^{s_a}}{e^{s_a}+e^{s_b}}.
\]
The two writings are the same: \(\sigma(u)=(1+e^{-u})^{-1}\). The model is determined by differences of strengths, so adding one constant to every \(s_a\) does not change any probability. The logistic link is not an arbitrary sigmoid: it is the pairwise case of the Gumbel random-utility model in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-gumbel} and of Luce's choice axiom in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-luce}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Gumbel noise]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-gumbel}
A real random variable \(\varepsilon\) is \emph{standard Gumbel} if
\[
\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon\leq t)
=\exp(-e^{-t}),
\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.
\]
The density is \(e^{-t}\exp(-e^{-t})\). This is an extreme-value law: it is the limiting distribution of a suitably centred maximum of i.i.d.\ real random variables. In a random-utility model one does not observe \(\varepsilon\). One observes only which item won. The modelling hypothesis is that each item \(a\) has a latent utility \(U_a=s_a+\varepsilon_a\) with \(\varepsilon_a\) i.i.d.\ standard Gumbel, and the observed choice is \(\arg\max_a U_a\). A short computation with the displayed CDF then gives the closed form
\[
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}U_a=a^\star\bigr)
=\frac{e^{s_{a^\star}}}{\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}e^{s_a}}.
\]
Restricted to two items this is exactly Bradley--Terry: \(\mathbb{P}(U_a>U_b)=\sigma(s_a-s_b)\). The same identity with many items is the softmax that appears in classification and in attention. Gaussian noise yields a probit (Thurstone--Mosteller) model instead, which has no equally simple many-way formula. Gumbel is used because it is the additive noise that turns ``pick the largest noisy score'' into a softmax, not because language or human preference is literally Gumbel.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Luce's choice axiom]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-luce}
Let \(\mathcal{A}\) be a finite set of items, and for each nonempty menu \(C\subseteq\mathcal{A}\) let \(P(\,\cdot\mid C)\) be a probability on \(C\). Luce's axiom says that pairwise odds do not depend on the rest of the menu: whenever \(a,b\in C\),
\[
\frac{P(a\mid C)}{P(b\mid C)}
=\frac{P(a\mid\{a,b\})}{P(b\mid\{a,b\})}.
\]
The consequence is a representation by positive strengths \(v_a>0\):
\[
P(a\mid C)
=\frac{v_a}{\sum_{b\in C}v_b}.
\]
Writing \(v_a=e^{s_a}\) recovers softmax, and the two-item case is Bradley--Terry. The axiom is a consistency requirement on choice probabilities, not a claim about a physical noise source. The Gumbel model of \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-gumbel} is one generative mechanism that implies it.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[What \(U_y\) is]\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-latent-utility}
The symbol \(U_y\) is not an observed quantity and is not the reward. It is the \emph{latent utility} of a response \(y\) (given the prompt \(x\)) in a random-utility model: the judge is modelled as comparing these scalars and picking the larger one. Write
\[
U_y
= r_\phi(x,y)+\varepsilon_y,
\]
where \(r_\phi(x,y)\) is the deterministic score being fitted and \(\varepsilon_y\) is unobserved i.i.d.\ noise. The human reports only an ordering \(y_w\succ y_l\), which the model treats as
\[
y_w\succ y_l
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{y_w}>U_{y_l}.
\]
One never sees \(U_y\) or \(\varepsilon_y\), only who won. Classification uses the same story: the class logit is \(s_y\), the latent score is \(U_y=s_y+\varepsilon_y\), and softmax is \(\mathbb{P}(\arg\max U=y)\). The two-class case of that identity is \(\sigma\), i.e.\ Bradley--Terry. Gumbel versus Gaussian only chooses the law of \(\varepsilon\); it does not change what \(U_y\) stands for.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Gumbel noise is used here, by the way]
The object being fitted is Bradley--Terry, not a noise model. By the way, the same Gumbel noise already sitting behind softmax in classification and attention is what produces this pairwise formula: if \(U_y=r_\phi(x,y)+\varepsilon_y\) with \(\varepsilon_y\) i.i.d.\ standard Gumbel, then \(\mathbb{P}(U_{y_w}>U_{y_l}\mid x)=\sigma(r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l))\). Nothing new is being introduced for alignment.

The noise is chosen so that this probability \emph{is} the sigmoid, not so that the noise looks realistic. Write \(\Delta:=U_{y_w}-U_{y_l}=(r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l))+(\varepsilon_{y_w}-\varepsilon_{y_l})\). If the \(\varepsilon\) are i.i.d.\ standard Gumbel, the difference \(\varepsilon_{y_w}-\varepsilon_{y_l}\) is standard logistic, whose CDF is exactly
\[
\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon_{y_w}-\varepsilon_{y_l}\leq u)
=\sigma(u)
=(1+e^{-u})^{-1}
=\frac{e^{u}}{e^{u}+1}.
\]
The last writing is two-class softmax on the logits \((u,0)\). With more than two responses the same Gumbel calculation yields the full softmax \(p(a)\propto e^{s_a}\). So \(\sigma\) here is not an extra activation pasted on afterwards: it is the pairwise softmax, and it is the CDF of a Gumbel difference.

Normal noise does not give that link. If the \(\varepsilon\) are i.i.d.\ standard Gaussian, then \(\varepsilon_{y_w}-\varepsilon_{y_l}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2)\) and
\[
\mathbb{P}(U_{y_w}>U_{y_l}\mid x)
=\Phi\Bigl(\frac{r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l)}{\sqrt{2}}\Bigr),
\]
the probit (Thurstone--Mosteller) model. The Gaussian CDF \(\Phi\) is not \(\sigma\), and the many-way analogue has no closed form (it is an orthant probability of a multivariate Gaussian). That is why the syllabus uses Gumbel rather than normal: one noise law produces \(\sigma\), softmax, and Bradley--Terry as the same identity; Gaussian noise would replace \(\sigma\) by \(\Phi\) and break the match with the softmax already used in classification and attention.
\end{remark}

In alignment the items are responses to a fixed prompt \(x\), and the strength of a response \(y\) is a learned score \(s_y=r_\phi(x,y)\). The displayed formula above is that specialisation. A human comparison supplies only an ordering \(y_w\succ y_l\), not a numerical reward, so some such likelihood is needed before a scalar \(r_\phi\) can be estimated. The parameters \(\phi\) are fit by Bernoulli negative log-likelihood of the observed pairs:
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)
=-\mathbb{E}_{(x,y_w,y_l)}
\log\sigma\bigl(r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l)\bigr).
\]
The fitted \(r_\phi\) is therefore a ranking surrogate, not an absolute utility. The longer bookkeeping, including why the link is logistic rather than an arbitrary sigmoid, is in \cref{rem:rm-bt}.

\begin{note}[What \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\) is for]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-rm-loss}
Pretraining and SFT (\cref{def:spr2025-nlp-sft}) make a model speak like its corpus. They do not encode ``which of two answers a human would pick''. Humans also do not supply a calibrated scalar reward; they supply an ordering \(y_w\succ y_l\). The reward-model loss is the Bernoulli negative log-likelihood of those observed orderings under Bradley--Terry. Its content is a logistic regression on score differences: \(\phi\) is chosen so that \(r_\phi(x,y_w)\) systematically exceeds \(r_\phi(x,y_l)\). The language-model policy is not updated here.

The macroscopic purpose is to distill sparse, expensive pairwise judgments into a reusable scalar judge \(r_\phi(x,y)\). After this stage one has a cheap, automatic proxy for ``how preferred this completion is'', not yet a better generator.
\end{note}

After this reward model has been trained and frozen, one updates a policy \(\pi_\theta\) while staying near a fixed reference policy \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) (usually the pretrained or instruction-tuned model). With a prompt distribution over \(x\), a response \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\), and a coefficient \(\beta>0\), the KL-regularised objective is

\[
J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{x,\,y\sim\pi_\theta}
\Bigl[
r_\phi(x,y)
-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
\Bigr].
\]
The first term raises the probability of high-reward answers. The second term is \(\beta\) times a Kullback--Leibler penalty: it charges \(\pi_\theta\) for drifting from \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\), which limits \emph{reward hacking} (exploiting errors in \(r_\phi\)) and catastrophic drift.

\begin{note}[What \(J\) is for]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-rlhf-j}
The objective \(J\) is not another classification loss. It is a reinforcement-learning target for the generator, with \(\phi\) already frozen. The first term is the expected score of answers drawn from the current policy; maximising it raises the probability of completions the judge likes. The second term is a leash: \(\beta\,\mathrm{KL}(\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x))\) charges the policy for leaving the reference (usually the SFT model). A small \(\beta\) lets the policy exploit errors in \(r_\phi\); a large \(\beta\) freezes it.

The macroscopic purpose is to steer the same architecture from ``sounds like the training text'' toward ``wins the preference comparison'', without re-learning what ``better'' means and without drifting out of the region where the model still speaks coherently. Together, \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\) defines the target and \(J\) chases it. Without the reward model there is no scalar to optimise; without \(J\) the reward model remains only a judge.
\end{note}

Two parameter vectors run this item, and they must not be conflated. The pair \((\pi,\theta)\) is the \emph{generator}: \(\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\) is the language model viewed as a policy, and \(\theta\) are its weights, updated only when one maximises \(J\). The pair \((r,\phi)\) is the \emph{judge}: \(r_\phi(x,y)\) is a scalar score for a completion, and \(\phi\) are its weights, updated only when one minimises \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\) and then frozen. The reference \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) is a third, frozen copy of a generator (usually the SFT model); it is not trained in either stage.

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.35cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.15cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Role} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textit{Data}} \\
\hline
\(x\) & prompt & The instruction or context. Both the judge and the generator condition on it. \\
\hline
\(y\) & completion & A response to \(x\). In stage~2 it is sampled from \(\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\). \\
\hline
\(y_w,\,y_l\) & preference pair & Human (or proxy) ordering: \(y_w\) won, \(y_l\) lost. Stage~1 data; not a scalar reward. \\
\hline
\(\mathcal{A},\,C\) & menu & Finite set of items, or a subset offered as a choice set (Luce). In alignment the items are responses to a fixed \(x\). \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textit{Judge \(r_\phi\) (stage~1 trains \(\phi\), then freezes it)}} \\
\hline
\(r_\phi(x,y)\) & reward model & Scalar score of completion \(y\) given \(x\). A ranking surrogate, not an absolute utility. \\
\hline
\(\phi\) & RM parameters & Weights of the judge. Fit by \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\); held fixed while \(J\) is maximised. \\
\hline
\(s_a\), \(s_y\) & strength & Classical Bradley--Terry strength of an item. In alignment, \(s_y=r_\phi(x,y)\). \\
\hline
\(v_a=e^{s_a}\) & Luce weight & Positive strength in Luce's representation; softmax is \(v_a/\sum_b v_b\). \\
\hline
\(\sigma\) & logistic link & \(\sigma(u)=(1+e^{-u})^{-1}\). Pairwise softmax, and the CDF of a Gumbel difference. \\
\hline
\(\varepsilon_y\) & Gumbel noise & Unobserved i.i.d.\ additive noise. Chosen so that \(\arg\max U\) has a softmax law. \\
\hline
\(U_y\) & latent utility & \(U_y=r_\phi(x,y)+\varepsilon_y\). Not observed. The model treats \(y_w\succ y_l\) as \(U_{y_w}>U_{y_l}\). \\
\hline
\(\Phi\) & probit link & Standard Gaussian CDF. Appears only as the Gaussian-noise alternative, not the syllabus link. \\
\hline
\(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)\) & RM loss & Bernoulli NLL of observed pairs. Minimised. Produces the judge; does not update \(\pi_\theta\). \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textit{Generator \(\pi_\theta\) (stage~2 trains \(\theta\); \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) stays frozen)}} \\
\hline
\(\pi\) & pretrained policy & Next-token model of the training distribution, before the target is changed. \\
\hline
\(\theta_{\mathrm{SFT}}\), SFT & supervised fine-tune & Next-token CE on demos \((x,y_{\mathrm{demo}})\); see \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-sft}. Not a preference loss. \\
\hline
\(\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\) & current policy & The language model as an RL policy. This is the object being aligned. \\
\hline
\(\theta\) & policy parameters & Weights of the generator. Updated by \(\nabla_\theta J\); not touched by \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\). \\
\hline
\(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) & reference policy & Frozen copy, usually the SFT (or pretrained) model. The KL leash is measured against it. \\
\hline
\(\beta>0\) & KL coefficient & Strength of the leash. Small \(\beta\): reward hacking. Large \(\beta\): policy frozen at \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\). \\
\hline
\(J(\theta)\) & RLHF objective & \(\mathbb{E}[r_\phi-\beta\log(\pi_\theta/\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})]\). Maximised. Chases the judge under the leash. \\
\hline
\(b(x)\) & baseline & Prompt-only offset in \(\nabla_\theta J\). Cuts Monte Carlo variance; does not change \(\mathbb{E}[\nabla_\theta J]\). See \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-baseline}. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

Because the expectation is over \(y\sim\pi_\theta\), the gradient is the score-function identity \(\nabla_\theta\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[f]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}[f\,\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta]\).\footnote{In general, let \(p_\theta\) be a probability mass (or a density) on a space \(\mathcal{Y}\), depending smoothly on a parameter \(\theta\), and let \(f:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}\) be integrable and \emph{not} depend on \(\theta\). Write \(\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}[f]:=\sum_{y}p_\theta(y)\,f(y)\) in the discrete case (replace the sum by an integral in the continuous case). Differentiating under the sum, which is justified once a dominated integrable bound on \(\|\nabla_\theta p_\theta\|\,|f|\) is available,
\[
\nabla_\theta\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}[f]
=\sum_y\bigl(\nabla_\theta p_\theta(y)\bigr)\,f(y).
\]
The log-derivative identity \(\nabla_\theta p_\theta(y)=p_\theta(y)\,\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(y)\) holds at every \(y\) with \(p_\theta(y)>0\), because \(\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta=\nabla_\theta p_\theta/p_\theta\). Substituting it gives
\[
\nabla_\theta\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}[f]
=\sum_y p_\theta(y)\,f(y)\,\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta(y)
=\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}\bigl[f\,\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta\bigr].
\]
The same computation with \(f\equiv 1\) yields \(\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}[\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta]=\nabla_\theta 1=0\): the score has mean zero. That is why a baseline \(b\) that does not depend on the sample \(y\) (here, a function of the prompt \(x\) only) may be subtracted without changing the expectation, \(\mathbb{E}[b\,\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta]=0\), while typically cutting variance.

If the integrand itself depends on \(\theta\), write \(f_\theta\). The product rule adds one more term:
\[
\nabla_\theta\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}[f_\theta]
=\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}[\nabla_\theta f_\theta]
+\mathbb{E}_{p_\theta}\bigl[f_\theta\,\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta\bigr].
\]
In the displayed \(J\), the only explicit \(\theta\)-dependence inside the brackets is \(-\beta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\), so \(\nabla_\theta f_\theta=-\beta\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta\). The extra term is then \(-\beta\mathbb{E}[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta]=0\), and the \(\theta\)-independent formula still applies. The longer trajectory form of the same identity is the policy-gradient calculation around \jump{eq:policy-gradient}{the score-function theorem} in Unit~3.} Introducing a baseline \(b(x)\) that does not depend on \(y\) (its contribution has mean zero) to reduce variance, one obtains

\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}\Biggl[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)
\Biggl(
r_\phi(x,y)
-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
-b(x)
\Biggr)
\Biggr].
\]
\begin{remark}[What \(b(x)\) is for]\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-baseline}
