The baseline is not a second reward and is not \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\). It is a prompt-only offset subtracted from the per-sample return before the score \(\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta\) is multiplied. The score has mean zero,

\[
\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}\bigl[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\bigr]=0,
\]

so any \(b\) that does not depend on the sampled \(y\) satisfies

\[
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\,b(x)\bigr]=0.
\]

Subtracting it leaves \(\nabla_\theta J\) unchanged and typically cuts the variance of the Monte Carlo estimator. This is the language-model specialisation of the \jump{thm:baseline}{baseline identity} in Unit~3: the state is the prompt \(x\), the action is the whole completion \(y\).

The natural choice is the conditional mean of the same return,

\[
b^\star(x)
=\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta}
\Bigl[
r_\phi(x,y)-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
\Bigr],
\]

i.e.\ the value \(V^\pi(x)\) of this prompt. The leftover is then an advantage: whether this \(y\) is better or worse than the typical completion of \(x\). In practice \(b(x)\) is a learned critic or a batch mean.

The motivation is that absolute scores are not comparable across prompts. An easy question returns a high \(r_\phi\) for almost every fluent \(y\); a hard question returns lower scores even for good answers. Without \(b(x)\), a routine high score on an easy prompt looks like a large win, and a slightly-above-average answer on a hard prompt looks like a loss. The baseline converts the signal into ``relative to this prompt's usual return''.

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.15cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{1.85cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Completion} & \(r_\phi\) & \textbf{After \(b(x)=8\)} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textit{Prompt \(x=\texttt{``2+2=?''}\); ignore the KL term for one line}} \\
\hline
\(y=\texttt{``4''}\) & \(9\) & \(+1\): reinforce this completion. \\
\hline
\(y=\texttt{``5''}\) & \(2\) & \(-6\): suppress this completion. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

A classroom reading of the same table: do not decide whether today's teaching worked from the absolute mark \(82\); ask whether the essay beat the class average \emph{for this prompt}. That average is \(b(x)\). It may depend on the question, but not on the wording of the particular essay being graded---otherwise the mean-zero identity fails.

Three contrasts that must not be collapsed:

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.35cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Object} & \textbf{Role} \\
\hline
\(b(x)\) & Offset inside \(\widehat{\nabla}J\) only. Cuts variance; does not appear in the definition of \(J\). \\
\hline
\(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) & Frozen SFT copy. Enters \(J\) as the KL leash. \\
\hline
\(r_\phi\) & Frozen judge. Enters \(J\) as the scalar being maximised. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

PPO's critic is one estimator of \(b^\star\). Problem~7 only needs the identity: write \(-b(x)\) in the gradient and say that it does not change the expectation.
\end{remark}

The limitations are built into the objects. The reward \(r_\phi\) is only a proxy for preference, so a small \(\beta\) lets \(\pi_\theta\) exploit the proxy; a large \(\beta\) freezes the policy. The Monte Carlo gradient is high-variance because it is on-policy.\footnote{The displayed \(\nabla_\theta J\) is an expectation over prompts \(x\) and completions \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\). The sum (or integral) over all \(y\) is intractable, so one replaces the expectation by an unbiased sample average. Draw a mini-batch of prompts \(x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(B)}\), and for each prompt sample a fresh completion \(y^{(i)}\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x^{(i)})\) from the \emph{current} policy. Write
\[
\widehat A^{(i)}
:=
r_\phi\bigl(x^{(i)},y^{(i)}\bigr)
-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta\bigl(y^{(i)}\mid x^{(i)}\bigr)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\bigl(y^{(i)}\mid x^{(i)}\bigr)}
-b\bigl(x^{(i)}\bigr)
\]
for the scalar already sitting in the gradient display. The Monte Carlo estimator is then
\[
\widehat{\nabla_\theta J}
=\frac1B\sum_{i=1}^B
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta\bigl(y^{(i)}\mid x^{(i)}\bigr)\,
\widehat A^{(i)}.
\]
Each term is computable: \(\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta\) is ordinary backpropagation through the token likelihood of the sampled \(y^{(i)}\), and \(r_\phi\) is a forward pass of the frozen judge. The estimator is unbiased for \(\nabla_\theta J\) at the \(\theta\) that generated the samples. It is \emph{on-policy} because those samples must come from the current \(\pi_\theta\): after \(\theta\) moves, yesterday's completions are draws from the wrong distribution, so one rolls out again. Variance is high because each \(y^{(i)}\) is a long discrete sequence and \(B\) is small relative to that space; the baseline \(b(x)\) cuts variance without changing the expectation, but does not remove the need for fresh on-policy samples. By contrast, \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\) is ordinary supervised logistic regression on a frozen pair dataset and needs no rollouts.} Direct preference optimisation is one repair of that on-policy loop, not a better Monte Carlo estimator of the same \(\nabla_\theta J\).\footnote{DPO does not estimate \(\nabla_\theta J\) at all. It drops both the explicit judge \(r_\phi\) and the on-policy rollouts, and instead minimises a supervised Bernoulli likelihood on the same frozen pairs \((x,y_w,y_l)\). The algebraic reason it can do so is a change of variables, recorded in \cref{rem:dpo}: the unconstrained maximizer of \(J\) has Gibbs form \(\pi_r(y\mid x)\propto\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)\,e^{r(x,y)/\beta}\), which inverts to \(r(x,y)=\beta\log(\pi_r/\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})+\beta\log Z(x)\). The partition function cancels in a pairwise difference, so Bradley--Terry becomes a function of policy log-ratios only, and \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}\) turns into
\[
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DPO}}(\theta)
=-\mathbb{E}_{(x,y_w,y_l)}
\log\sigma\Biggl(
\beta\Biggl[
\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y_w\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_w\mid x)}
-\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y_l\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y_l\mid x)}
\Biggr]
\Biggr).
\]
Under that model the two routes have the same target. Operationally they do not: DPO never samples \(y\sim\pi_\theta\) and never backpropagates a score-function times a reward. The cost is the missing on-policy exploration against a scalar scorer. So ``preferred optimisation'' here means a different computational path with the same KL-regularised Bradley--Terry goal, not a variance-reduced Monte Carlo for \(J\).}

What this item buys is a change of target: the same architecture can be steered toward preferred answers rather than toward web text. What it costs is the proxy. Alignment is not ``more reasonable'' than item~3; without a policy from items~2--3 there is nothing to align. It is an objective correction on top of that policy, not a competing architecture.

\jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-7}{Problem~7} is exactly the second stage: the reward model is already in hand, and the task is to write the data flow, the objective \(J\), and this gradient.

\paragraph{5. Scaling laws.}
N-gram modelling, word2vec, self-attention, and pretraining alignment say how to specify a model class and an objective. They do not say how test loss moves when one spends more data or more parameters. A scaling law is an empirical answer to that question: over a wide but finite regime, the test loss \(\mathcal{L}\) falls as a power of the resource being increased, until it hits a floor. The two questions sit on different axes: the four topics choose \(\mathcal{H}\) and the fitted scalar; the power law diagnoses which gap in expected test loss falls when \(N\) or \(P\) grows.

\begin{center}
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/llm-training-pipeline/scaling-laws-grok.png}
\end{center}

\begin{note}[How the earlier syllabus topics specify a class and an objective]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-class-objective}
``Specify a model class'' means: name the family of predictors one is willing to search (the hypothesis class, or the architecture). ``Specify an objective'' means: name the scalar that is being fitted inside that family. The four topics below do those two jobs; they do not produce a curve of test loss against data size \(N\) or parameter count \(P\).

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Statistical modelling / n-grams.} The class is the family of order-\(k\) Markov conditionals \(p(w_t\mid w_{t-k:t-1})\), estimated by counts and then smoothed. The objective is next-token likelihood on the corpus (maximum likelihood, or a smoothed surrogate of it).
  \item \textbf{Word2vec.} The predictive objective is kept, but the parameterisation changes: each type is a vector, and \(p(c\mid w)\) is a softmax of inner products (or a contrastive stand-in such as InfoNCE). The class is now embedding maps, not a free table of \(k\)-grams.
  \item \textbf{Self-attention.} Next-token likelihood is kept again, but the class is enlarged to contextual maps computed by the Transformer. The architecture can represent long-range conditionals; the training target is still ``imitate the next token''.
  \item \textbf{Pretraining alignment.} No new architecture is invented. The objective is changed on a policy already supplied by word2vec and self-attention: first \(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RM}}(\phi)\) fits a judge from pairwise preferences, then \(J(\theta)\) steers \(\pi_\theta\) toward that judge under a KL leash.
\end{itemize}

So n-grams, word2vec, and self-attention mainly choose \emph{what family} one fits, under a likelihood-type target; pretraining alignment mainly chooses \emph{which scalar} that family is asked to optimise. None of them answers: if one multiplies \(N\) or \(P\), by what factor does test loss fall, and which error term is responsible. That is the separate question of scaling laws.
\end{note}

Let \(N\) be an effective training-set size and \(P\) a parameter count. A typical fit is

\[
\mathcal{L}(N,P)
\approx\mathcal{L}_\infty+A N^{-\alpha}+B P^{-\gamma},
\]

where \(\mathcal{L}_\infty\geq 0\) is an irreducible floor, and \(A,\alpha,B,\gamma>0\) are empirical constants that depend on the architecture and the data. (The literature often writes \(\beta\) for the parameter exponent \(\gamma\); we use \(\gamma\) here so that it is not confused with the KL coefficient of the previous paragraph.) A compute budget \(C\) can be written in the same way. This is a local regression, not a theorem with a universal exponent.

The statistical content is an error decomposition, and \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-6}{Problem~6} asks for that decomposition without assuming that the data are Gaussian. Reuse the objects of \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-bias-variance}. Let \(L(h)=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\ell(h;x,y)]\) be the population risk of a predictor \(h\), let \(h^\star\) minimise \(L\) among all measurable predictors, let \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\) minimise \(L\) inside the chosen class \(\mathcal{H}\), let \(\widehat h_S\in\mathcal{H}\) be the exact in-class fit from a sample \(S\) of size \(N\) (empirical risk minimiser, or the same training objective run to completion), and let \(\widehat h\in\mathcal{H}\) be the iterate actually returned after a finite optimisation. The same objects, lined up:

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.55cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.55cm}|
}
\hline
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Needs / remark} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textit{Risks and predictors}} \\
\hline
\(L(h)\) & \(\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\ell(h;x,y)]\). Population / test risk of a fixed predictor. & Not the training NLL. A fresh draw from \(\mathcal{D}\). \\
\hline
\(L_S(h)\) & \(\frac1N\sum_{(x,y)\in S}\ell(h;x,y)\). Empirical risk on the training sample. & Random through \(S\). The optimiser sees this, not \(L\). \\
\hline
\(h^\star\) & \(\arg\min L(h)\) over all measurable predictors. Bayes / oracle predictor. & Requires knowing \(\mathcal{D}\). Need not lie in \(\mathcal{H}\). \\
\hline
\(h_{\mathcal{H}}\) & \(\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}L(h)\). Best-in-class predictor. & Infinite data, exact opt, but still trapped in \(\mathcal{H}\). \\
\hline
\(\widehat h_S\) & \(\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}L_S(h)\). Exact in-class fit from \(S\). & Finite \(N\); \(L_S\neq L\), so this is not \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\). \\
\hline
\(\widehat h\) & The iterate actually returned after a finite optimisation. & In \(\mathcal{H}\), but need not minimise \(L_S\). \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textit{The four terms in the display below}} \\
\hline
\(L(h^\star)\) & Bayes / irreducible error. & Floor: noise, or \(x\) does not determine \(y\). \\
\hline
\(L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)\) & Approximation error. & Class too small. Falls if \(\mathcal{H}\) is enlarged, not if \(N\) grows. \\
\hline
\(\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})]\) & Estimation error. & \(L_S\) is a noisy \(L\). Falls if \(N\) grows; typically rises if \(\mathcal{H}\) is enlarged at fixed \(N\). \\
\hline
\(\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h)-L(\widehat h_S)]\) & Optimisation error. & Did not finish minimising \(L_S\). About the optimiser, not about \(\mathcal{D}\). \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

Then

\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h)\bigr]
=
L(h^\star)
+\underbrace{L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)}_{\text{approximation}}
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]}_{\text{estimation}}
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h)-L(\widehat h_S)\bigr]}_{\text{optimisation}}.
\]

The left-hand side is expected \emph{test} loss: the risk of the shipped predictor on a fresh draw from \(\mathcal{D}\), averaged over the random training sample. It is not the training NLL watched during SGD. The first term on the right is the Bayes / irreducible risk. The remaining three gaps are the usual approximation--estimation split plus the leftover from not finishing the optimisation. The exponents \(\alpha\) and \(\gamma\) are therefore architecture-dependent, and the fit can fail under duplicated or low-quality data, a distribution shift, an underpowered class, or an optimiser that does not keep up.

\begin{note}[What the four terms are, in familiar settings]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-error-decomp}
\textbf{Test loss.} Fix the trained predictor and evaluate \(\ell\) on new pairs \((x,y)\) drawn from the same \(\mathcal{D}\), not on the training set. For a language model this is typically next-token cross-entropy on held-out text. For a classifier it is \(0\)--\(1\) error (or logistic loss) on a held-out test set. Training loss can be driven toward zero while test loss stays large; the display is about the latter.

\textbf{Bayes / irreducible error, \(L(h^\star)\).} Even an oracle that knows \(\mathcal{D}\) cannot go below this. The label is noisy, or the features do not determine the target.
\begin{itemize}
  \item A coin with \(\mathbb{P}(Y=1\mid X)=0.7\): the best \(0\)--\(1\) predictor still errs \(30\%\) of the time.
  \item After the six tokens ``I deposited money at the'', the next word is not a deterministic function of that short history; several fluent continuations have positive probability. Next-token cross-entropy therefore has a positive floor.
  \item Five percent of training images are mislabelled at random. No classifier can beat a \(5\%\) error floor on the same noisy test law.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Approximation error, \(L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)\).} The class is too small, even with infinite data and exact optimisation.
\begin{itemize}
  \item A unigram cannot represent ``New York''; the best unigram still predicts poorly on that bigram.
  \item Linear classification of XOR: the best hyperplane has error \(1/4\).
  \item A histogram with too few bins, or a tiny Transformer that cannot carry subject--verb agreement across a long clause.
\end{itemize}
This term falls when the class is enlarged (trigram instead of unigram; self-attention instead of a short Markov window; larger \(P\)) or when the architecture matches the data better. It does not fall merely by collecting more samples of the same class.

\textbf{Estimation error, \(\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})]\).} The class can express a good predictor, but \(N\) samples do not identify it.
\begin{itemize}
  \item A trigram on a small corpus: most contexts are unseen, so the count ratio is undefined or jumps from sample to sample, even though some trigram is best-in-class.
  \item A degree-\(20\) polynomial fitted on \(20\) points: the class contains a smooth target, but the interpolant wiggles.
  \item A rare fact or a rare sense of ``bank'' appears twice in the corpus; the fitted conditional is dominated by those two draws.
\end{itemize}
This term falls when \(N\) grows (rare contexts are covered; empirical averages track population conditionals). Enlarging the class at fixed \(N\) typically raises it; see \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-finer-class}.

\textbf{Optimisation error, \(\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h)-L(\widehat h_S)]\).} The training procedure did not reach the in-class fit.
\begin{itemize}
  \item SGD is stopped while the training loss is still decreasing.
  \item The step size is so large that the iterate oscillates and never settles near a minimiser.
  \item A deep net is trained for too few steps, or is stuck at a poor critical point, so the returned weights are not the empirical-risk minimiser of \(\mathcal{H}\).
\end{itemize}
This term is about the optimiser, not about \(\mathcal{D}\) or \(N\). In the scaling-law fit it is absorbed into the floor or into a failure of the power law when the optimiser does not keep up with \(P\).
\end{note}

What this item buys is a quantitative learning curve: ``add data'' and ``add parameters'' become statements about which error term should fall. What it costs is that the power law is a local fit, not a theorem, and that it can fail under duplicated or shifted data, or when the floor or the optimiser dominates. It is not a modelling method at all, and it cannot be ranked against word2vec or attention. It is a diagnosis of a model class already specified by n-gram modelling, word2vec, and self-attention.

Together with \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-4}{Problem~4} and \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-5}{Problem~5}, this is why ``scale plus Transformers'' is a mechanism: Transformers change the approximation term and the feasible \(N\), while extra data change the estimation term.

\paragraph{6. Embedding-based knowledge-base reasoning.}
Even a well-aligned, well-scaled model stores facts only as compressed parameters. Those parameters go stale, and next-token sampling can invent fluent falsehoods. A document collection is an uncompressed memory. The sixth item is the decision to retrieve from that collection and then generate, rather than to ask \(\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\) to recall every domain fact.

Split the corpus into chunks \(d_1,\ldots,d_M\). Let \(e(\cdot)\) be an embedding map of the kind trained in item~2, and let \(\operatorname{sim}\) be a similarity on the embedding space, typically a cosine or an inner product. Offline one indexes the vectors \(e(d_i)\). Online, given a question \(q\), one retrieves the \(k\) nearest chunks

\[
i^\star\in\operatorname*{arg\,top\text{-}k}_{i}\operatorname{sim}\bigl(e(q),e(d_i)\bigr)
\]

and replaces the bare conditional by \(p(y\mid q,d_{i^\star})\). The generator is instructed to answer from that evidence, to cite it, and to abstain when the evidence is insufficient. This pipeline is retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The two stages are separate clocks: the index is built once from the corpus; each question only embeds \(q\), looks up neighbours, and then samples \(y\).

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  >=Stealth,
  font=\small,
  node distance=7.2mm and 6.2mm,
  box/.style={
    draw=#1!62!black, fill=#1!10, rounded corners=2.5pt,
    align=center, font=\scriptsize, inner sep=4.2pt,
    text width=2.28cm, minimum height=1.12cm
  },
  lane/.style={
    draw=#1!35!black, fill=#1!4, rounded corners=4pt,
    inner sep=5pt
  },
  arr/.style={->, thick, draw=black!55}
]
  \node[box=black] (corp) {corpus\\[-1pt]{\tiny uncompressed documents}};
  \node[box=teal, right=of corp] (chunks) {chunks \(d_1,\ldots,d_M\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny split, not compressed}};
  \node[box=orange, right=of chunks] (embd) {vectors \(e(d_i)\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny same \(e\) as word2vec}};
  \node[box=olive, right=of embd] (idx) {index\\[-1pt]{\tiny store \(e(d_i)\) offline}};
  \node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, text=olive!50!black, above=2.4mm of chunks]
    {offline: build the memory once};
  \begin{scope}[on background layer]
    \node[lane=olive, fit=(corp)(chunks)(embd)(idx), inner sep=7pt] {};
  \end{scope}
  \draw[arr] (corp)--(chunks);
  \draw[arr] (chunks)--(embd);
  \draw[arr] (embd)--(idx);

  \node[box=blue, below=16mm of corp] (q) {question \(q\)};
  \node[box=orange, below=16mm of chunks] (embq) {query vector \(e(q)\)};
  \node[box=purple, below=16mm of embd] (topk) {top-\(k\) by \(\operatorname{sim}\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny retrieve \(d_{i^\star}\)}};
  \node[box=red, below=16mm of idx] (gen) {sample \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid q,d_{i^\star})\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny cite or abstain}};
  \node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, text=blue!50!black, below=2.2mm of topk]
    {online: each question retrieves, then generates};
  \begin{scope}[on background layer]
    \node[lane=blue, fit=(q)(embq)(topk)(gen), inner sep=7pt] {};
  \end{scope}
  \draw[arr] (q)--(embq);
  \draw[arr] (embq)--(topk);
  \draw[arr] (topk)--(gen);
  \draw[arr, dashed, draw=olive!60!black] (idx)--(topk)
    node[midway, right, font=\scriptsize, text=olive!45!black, xshift=1pt] {read the index};
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\end{center}

A knowledge-graph embedding such as TransE is the same geometry with a relational bias. A triple \((s,r,o)\) of subject, relation, and object is scored by how nearly \(e_s+e_r\) matches \(e_o\). In both cases the failure mode is probabilistic rather than merely engineering: if the retrieved \(d_{i^\star}\) is the wrong condition, the generator is being asked the wrong question; a nearest neighbour under \(\operatorname{sim}\) is not an entailment. Hybrid lexical-plus-dense retrieval, a reranker, citation constraints, and abstention are the standard repairs.

What this item buys is an uncompressed, updatable memory: facts need not be packed into \(\theta\), and a domain corpus can be used even when no question--answer pairs have been labelled. What it costs is the retrieved condition. If \(d_{i^\star}\) is wrong, the generator is asked the wrong question; a nearest neighbour under \(\operatorname{sim}\) is not an entailment. Relative to ``store every fact in the parameters'' this is complementary, and for domain question answering it is usually the more reasonable first design. Relative to item~2 it \emph{uses} the embedding; it does not replace it.

\jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-8}{Problem~8} is the system-design form of this item, under the extra constraint that no labelled question--answer pairs are available. The domain corpus itself has to serve as the knowledge base.

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  >=Stealth,
  node distance=7mm and 8mm,
  box/.style={
    draw=#1!60!black, fill=#1!8, rounded corners=3pt,
    align=center, font=\scriptsize, inner sep=5pt,
    text width=3.15cm, minimum height=1.15cm
  },
  arr/.style={->, thick, draw=black!45}
]
  \node[box=teal] (s1) {\textbf{1.\ Statistical assumptions}\\[-1pt]{\tiny factorisation and smoothing}};
  \node[box=orange, right=of s1] (s2) {\textbf{2.\ Embeddings}\\[-1pt]{\tiny inner product, InfoNCE}};
  \node[box=purple, right=of s2] (s3) {\textbf{3.\ Self-attention}\\[-1pt]{\tiny content-weighted average}};
  \node[box=blue, below=of s3] (s5) {\textbf{5.\ Scaling laws}\\[-1pt]{\tiny approximation vs.\ estimation}};
  \node[box=red, below=of s2] (s4) {\textbf{4.\ Alignment}\\[-1pt]{\tiny reward, KL, policy gradient}};
  \node[box=olive, below=of s1] (s6) {\textbf{6.\ RAG / knowledge base}\\[-1pt]{\tiny generate given retrieved text}};
  \draw[arr] (s1)--(s2);
  \draw[arr] (s2)--(s3);
  \draw[arr] (s3)--(s5);
  \draw[arr] (s3)--(s4);
  \draw[arr] (s2)--(s6);
  \draw[arr] (s4)--(s6);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\end{center}

Read the graph left to right, then down. The six items are nested constraints on one object,

\[
p(\text{output}\mid\text{input}),
\]

estimated from a finite corpus. They are not six rival algorithms for the same optimisation problem, and they should not be ranked as if one of them were globally ``more reasonable''.

Item~1 is the base: without a factorisable \(p(\text{tokens})\) there is no probability problem. Item~2 improves the parameterisation of item~1, replacing atomic types by vectors. Item~3 improves the conditional structure of item~1---and the sequential computation graph of a recurrent net---while using the geometry of item~2. From there the syllabus splits rather than continues a single replacement chain. Item~5 is a diagnosis of that model class: it asks which error term falls when \(N\) or \(P\) grows. Item~4 is an objective correction on a policy already built from items~2--3: likelihood is replaced by preference plus a KL penalty. Item~6 is a change of conditioning, not of architecture: it takes the embedding from item~2 and the generator from items~3--4, and replaces \(p(y\mid x)\) by \(p(y\mid x,d_{i^\star})\).

The inheritance that is actually an improvement of a previous layer is therefore

\[
1\;\longrightarrow\;2\;\longrightarrow\;3.
\]

Items~4 and~6 then change the target and the condition of that stack; item~5 evaluates the stack. An exam answer that treats the six items as six parallel fill-in-the-blank methods misses this: the grader is asking which constraint you are relaxing, and what cost that relaxation incurs.

On this paper the eight problems sit on that graph as follows. \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-1}{Problem~1} is item~1; \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-2}{Problem~2} is item~2; \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-5}{Problem~5} is item~3; \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-3}{Problem~3} is the decoding layer on top of item~1. \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-6}{Problem~6} is item~5, and \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-7}{Problem~7} is item~4. \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-8}{Problem~8} is item~6. \jump{prob:spr2025-nlp-4}{Problem~4} is the synthesis question: which of these mechanisms, taken together, make current systems work.

\noindent\textbf{Short Answer Questions on Concepts}

\subsection{Problem 1. [3 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-1}
In a trigram language model, how is $p(w_3 \mid w_1, w_2)$ learned from a training corpus? In real-world applications, we may encounter situations where $(w_1, w_2)$, or even $w_1$ or $w_2$, do not appear in the training corpus. How can we estimate $p(w_3 \mid w_1, w_2)$ in such cases?

\subsection{Problem 2. [3 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-2}
What is the central idea behind representation learning? Which algorithms implement this concept?

\subsection{Problem 3. [3 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-3}
Why do current large language models (LLMs) use top-$P$ sampling during inference instead of greedy or beam search? Additionally, why is top-$P$ sampling considered superior to other methods, such as top-$K$ sampling?

\subsection{Problem 4. [3 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-4}
List several key points that contribute to the success of current LLM based approach of Artificial General Intelligence and explain their importance.

\subsection{Problem 5. [3 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-5}
Identify at least two advantages of the Transformer architecture compared to recurrent-based models (e.g., LSTM, GRU) for sequence-to-sequence tasks.

\noindent\textbf{Questions on Algorithm Analysis}

\subsection{Problem 6. [5 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-6}
Explain why the data scaling law, which describes the relationship between performance and sample size, holds true from the perspective of statistical learning. Please provide a detailed analysis process, avoiding overly simplistic assumptions (such as assuming the data follows a Gaussian distribution).

\subsection{Problem 7. [5 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2026-rlhf}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-7}
We are implementing the Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF) procedure to train a Large Language Model (LLM). Assuming that a reward model has already been trained, explain how reinforcement learning is used to align the model with human preferences. The explanation should include details about data usage, the reinforcement learning loss function, and the gradient of the loss.

\noindent\textbf{Questions on Applied System Design}

\subsection{Problem 8. [8 pts.]}\label{prob:spr2025-nlp-8}
We have a general-purpose Large Language Model (LLM), but it performs poorly on question-answering tasks in a specific problem domain due to a lack of domain-specific knowledge. Although we have collected a large corpus of documents in this domain, we do not have labeled question-answering pairs. Design a system that can accurately answer questions within this domain. You may incorporate additional components, such as embedding models, into the system.

\subsection{Learning map for Section D}

The official six-item syllabus and the motivations for each item are in \jump{sec:spr2025-nlp-syllabus}{the syllabus map above}. Section D is not one long proof chain. It contains three different answer types:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Problems 1--5 are concept questions: define the object, give the mechanism, and name the relevant trade-off.
    \item Problems 6--7 require an argument with mathematical structure: separate the objective, assumptions, and derivation from the practical interpretation.
    \item Problem 8 is a system-design question: specify data flow, interfaces, evaluation, and failure handling rather than merely listing model names.
\end{itemize}

For every problem, first reconstruct the short answer without looking, then use the longer explanation only to repair a missing concept or justification. The verb ``learn'' in Problems~1--2, 6--7 is disambiguated in \jump{sec:spr2025-learn}{What learn means}.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 1: trigram estimation and sparse contexts}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
Write how \(p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)\) is estimated from counts, then say what to do when the pair \((w_1,w_2)\)---or even \(w_1\) or \(w_2\)---never occurs, and when a test type lies outside \(\mathcal{V}\).

\paragraph{Learning goal.}
Understand the distinction between a maximum-likelihood count estimate and a usable language-model probability when the observed context is rare or absent.

\paragraph{Core objects.}
For a corpus count function $c(\cdot)$, the maximum-likelihood trigram estimate is

\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
= \frac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)}{c(w_1,w_2)},
\]

whenever $c(w_1,w_2)>0$. It is the empirical conditional frequency: among all occurrences of the context $(w_1,w_2)$, it records the fraction followed by $w_3$.

\paragraph{Solution.}
The estimate above is learned by counting trigrams in the training corpus and normalizing by the corresponding bigram-context count. It fails for an unseen trigram by assigning probability zero, and it is undefined when the whole context $(w_1,w_2)$ is unseen.

The standard repair is smoothing together with backoff or interpolation. For example, an interpolated model takes the form

\[
\widehat p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
= \lambda_3\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
+ \lambda_2\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_2)
+ \lambda_1\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3),
\]

where \(\lambda_i\geq 0\) and \(\lambda_1+\lambda_2+\lambda_3=1\). The three maximum-likelihood pieces are the count ratios already written above, each defined only when its denominator is positive. If a denominator vanishes, drop that term and renormalize the remaining weights. The mixture is then always a probability on \(\mathcal{V}\).

Backoff is the same idea with a hard switch rather than a convex combination:

\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)}{c(w_1,w_2)}
  & \text{if }c(w_1,w_2)>0,\\[6pt]
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3\mid w_2)
  & \text{if }c(w_1,w_2)=0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3\mid w_2)
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{c(w_2,w_3)}{c(w_2)}
  & \text{if }c(w_2)>0,\\[6pt]
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3)
  & \text{if }c(w_2)=0,
\end{cases}
\]

and \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3)=c(w_3)/\sum_w c(w)\) after the unigram itself has been smoothed so that every \(w\in\mathcal{V}\) has positive mass. Katz backoff additionally discounts the seen counts and spends the leftover mass on the lower-order branch; the cases above are the exam skeleton.

A different hole is an unseen \emph{type}, not an unseen context. The model is a distribution on a finite set \(\mathcal{V}\). A string that is not an element of \(\mathcal{V}\) is not an atom of that distribution, so neither \(c(\texttt{dog})\) nor \(p(\texttt{dog}\mid w_1,w_2)\) is defined.

\begin{definition}[Unknown token]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-unk}
Fix a finite vocabulary \(\mathcal{V}\) that contains one distinguished symbol \(\mathrm{UNK}\).\footnote{The problem is that a trigram model is a probability on the finite set \(\mathcal{V}\), while language is open: a test type may be absent from \(\mathcal{V}\). Then \(w\notin\mathcal{V}\) is not an atom, so \(c(w)\) is undefined and the chain-rule factor \(p(w\mid\text{history})\) cannot be evaluated; any sentence containing that type would be assigned probability zero even after backoff. Replacing every such type by one reserved symbol makes every string a sequence in \(\mathcal{V}^*\), so the count ratios, interpolation, and backoff above apply. The benefit is a closed, estimable support rather than a new parameter per word: if rare training types are also mapped to \(\mathrm{UNK}\), then \(c(\mathrm{UNK})>0\) and the model spends a positive mass on ``some type not listed in \(\mathcal{V}\)''. All out-of-vocabulary types become indistinguishable; that is the price of sharing one atom. \(\mathrm{UNK}\) does not repair an unseen context \((w_1,w_2)\).} A tokenizer map \(\tau\) sends every raw token to \(\mathcal{V}\) by
\[
\tau(w)
=
\begin{cases}
w & \text{if }w\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{\mathrm{UNK}\},\\
\mathrm{UNK} & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
Training counts and test queries are computed after \(\tau\). All out-of-vocabulary types therefore share the single atom \(\mathrm{UNK}\), and
\[
\widehat p(\mathrm{UNK})
=\frac{c(\mathrm{UNK})}{\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}}c(w)}
\]
is the probability assigned to ``some type not listed in \(\mathcal{V}\)''.
\end{definition}

UNK does not invent a separate parameter for each new word. It collapses every unseen type onto one already-counted symbol, so a query such as \(p(\texttt{sat}\mid\texttt{the},\texttt{dog})\) is rewritten as \(p(\texttt{sat}\mid\texttt{the},\mathrm{UNK})\) and then estimated by the interpolation or backoff formulae above. If that rewritten context is itself unseen, one still backs off; UNK only makes the query an element of \(\mathcal{V}^3\).

\begin{example}[Two holes, one tiny corpus]
Take \(\mathcal{V}=\{\texttt{the},\texttt{cat},\texttt{sat},\mathrm{UNK}\}\) and the two training sentences \(\texttt{the cat sat}\) and \(\texttt{the cat sat}\). Then \(c(\texttt{the},\texttt{cat})=2\), \(c(\texttt{the},\texttt{cat},\texttt{sat})=2\), so
\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(\texttt{sat}\mid\texttt{the},\texttt{cat})=1.
\]
The test sentence \(\texttt{the dog sat}\) is mapped by \(\tau\) to \(\texttt{the UNK sat}\). The type \(\texttt{dog}\) has been replaced; the remaining problem is that \(c(\texttt{the},\mathrm{UNK})=0\), so the trigram ratio is undefined. Interpolation drops the first term and uses \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(\texttt{sat}\mid\mathrm{UNK})\) and \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(\texttt{sat})\); backoff does the same by the cases above. Laplace smoothing
\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{add\text{-}1}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\frac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)+1}{c(w_1,w_2)+|\mathcal{V}|}
\]
is another explicit repair: the denominator is at least \(|\mathcal{V}|=4\), so the estimate exists even when \(c(\texttt{the},\mathrm{UNK})=0\), and equals \(1/4\) for \(w_3=\texttt{sat}\).
\end{example}

Modified Kneser--Ney is a stronger named answer: it discounts observed counts and spends the removed mass on lower-order continuations. The exam point is the mechanism, not the exact discount: share strength with a well-defined lower-order distribution whenever a count is zero.

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
\textit{Short form.}
Write the count ratio, assuming the three tokens already lie in a finite vocabulary \(\mathcal{V}\):
\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\frac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)}{c(w_1,w_2)}
\qquad\text{only if }c(w_1,w_2)>0.
\]
Name two \emph{different} in-vocabulary failures of this ratio, not one vague ``unseen'' case. If \(c(w_1,w_2)>0\) but \(c(w_1,w_2,w_3)=0\), the ratio equals \(0\) and the sentence is killed; repair by smoothing (Laplace, interpolation, or Kneser--Ney). If \(c(w_1,w_2)=0\), the ratio is undefined; repair by interpolation or backoff to \(\widehat p(w_3\mid w_2)\) and then \(\widehat p(w_3)\). A type outside \(\mathcal{V}\) is a third hole: map it to a shared \(\mathrm{UNK}\in\mathcal{V}\) so the query lies in \(\mathcal{V}^3\), then apply the same formulae to the rewritten triple. Smoothing/backoff and \(\mathrm{UNK}\) are not two methods for the same failure; they are used in that order.

\textit{Expanded form.}
The MLE is an estimator of a conditional distribution on \(\mathcal{V}\), not a claim that the model ``learns the trigram'' as a single event. Work in three steps.

\textit{Step 1 (in-vocabulary query).}
Assume \(w_1,w_2,w_3\in\mathcal{V}\). The object being estimated is the whole table \(\widehat p(\,\cdot\mid\cdot,\cdot\,)\). A single cell of that table is just one coordinate of the estimator.

\textit{Step 2 (two count failures).}
Do not write ``if the trigram is missing, condition on \(w_2\).'' Split on which count vanished.

(i) Unseen continuation: \(c(w_1,w_2)>0\) and \(c(w_1,w_2,w_3)=0\). The ratio is defined and equals \(0\). The context was observed; only this continuation was not. Give the unseen continuation positive mass, e.g.\ add-one
\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{add\text{-}1}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\frac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)+1}{c(w_1,w_2)+|\mathcal{V}|},
\]
or mix in lower-order estimates (the interpolation displayed next). Backoff is not forced here.

(ii) Unseen context: \(c(w_1,w_2)=0\). Division is illegal, so the order must drop. Interpolation is the convex combination
\[
\widehat p(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=\lambda_3\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
+\lambda_2\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3\mid w_2)
+\lambda_1\widehat p_{\mathrm{ML}}(w_3),
\]
with \(\lambda_i\ge 0\) and \(\lambda_1+\lambda_2+\lambda_3=1\). Any term whose denominator is \(0\) is dropped and the remaining weights are renormalized. Backoff is the same idea with a hard switch:
\[
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3\mid w_1,w_2)
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{c(w_1,w_2,w_3)}{c(w_1,w_2)}
  & \text{if }c(w_1,w_2)>0,\\[6pt]
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3\mid w_2)
  & \text{if }c(w_1,w_2)=0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3\mid w_2)
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{c(w_2,w_3)}{c(w_2)}
  & \text{if }c(w_2)>0,\\[6pt]
\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3)
  & \text{if }c(w_2)=0,
\end{cases}
\]
and \(\widehat p_{\mathrm{bo}}(w_3)\) is a smoothed unigram, so every \(w\in\mathcal{V}\) has positive mass. Katz / Kneser--Ney additionally discount the seen counts and spend the leftover mass on the lower-order branch; the exam skeleton is the case split, not the exact discount.

\textit{Step 3 (unseen type, then reuse Step 2).}
If a raw token \(w\notin\mathcal{V}\), then \(w\) is not an atom of the model: \(c(w)\) is undefined, and neither smoothing nor backoff can be evaluated, because the query is not in \(\mathcal{V}^3\). Reserve one symbol \(\mathrm{UNK}\in\mathcal{V}\) from the start (it is part of the specification of \(\mathcal{V}\), not a word the model later invents) and apply
\[
\tau(w)
=
\begin{cases}
w & \text{if }w\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{\mathrm{UNK}\},\\
\mathrm{UNK} & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
Training counts and test queries are computed after \(\tau\). All out-of-vocabulary types share one atom, so
\[
\widehat p(\mathrm{UNK})
=\frac{c(\mathrm{UNK})}{\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}}c(w)}
\]
is the mass on ``some type not listed in \(\mathcal{V}\)''. The price is that those types become indistinguishable. A query such as \(p(\texttt{sat}\mid\texttt{the},\texttt{dog})\) is rewritten as \(p(\texttt{sat}\mid\texttt{the},\mathrm{UNK})\). If \(c(\texttt{the},\mathrm{UNK})=0\), that is failure (ii) of Step~2, not a new UNK mechanism: interpolate or back off. \(\mathrm{UNK}\) only closes the support.

\textit{Pipeline to write.}
\(\tau\) first, so the query is an element of \(\mathcal{V}^3\); then the count-ratio cases of Step~2 on the rewritten triple. The two repairs answer two different questions: ``is this symbol even in the model?'' versus ``does this in-vocabulary cell have a usable count?''

\paragraph{Common mistake.}
Do not say that a model can ``learn'' an unseen trigram directly from its count. It cannot; it must share statistical strength with lower-order contexts or another representation. The verb is defined in \jump{sec:spr2025-learn}{What learn means}: \(A\) returns a table on \(\mathcal{V}\), not a local fact about a zero cell.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 2: representation learning}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
Name the idea---learn an intermediate feature map instead of writing features by hand---and name algorithms that implement it. The answer is a definition plus examples, not a training recipe for one model.

\paragraph{Objects.}
Let \(x\) be a raw input (a word type, a sentence, an image). A \emph{representation} is a map
\[
z=e_\theta(x)\in\mathbb{R}^d
\]
with trainable parameters \(\theta\). A downstream predictor then uses \(z\), for example \(\widehat y=g_\phi(z)\). The pair \((e_\theta,g_\phi)\) is trained from a loss on \(\widehat y\), or \(e_\theta\) is trained first from an auxiliary loss and \(g_\phi\) is fitted later. The point of the split is that \(e_\theta\) is reused: the same \(z\) can serve several predictors.

Without a learned \(e_\theta\), one would specify coordinates of \(x\) by hand (indicator features, POS tags, hand-built lexicons). Those coordinates do not adapt when the loss says they are useless.

\begin{definition}[Representation learning]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-repr}
Representation learning is the estimation of \(e_\theta\) so that \(z=e_\theta(x)\) is a useful input to a later predictor. ``Useful'' is defined by an objective: reconstruction, contrastive agreement, next-token likelihood, or a supervised label. The algorithms below differ in that objective; they share the split \(x\mapsto z\mapsto\widehat y\).
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Algorithms, by objective.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Classical linear maps.} PCA sends \(x\) to its top principal coordinates, i.e.\ the subspace of largest variance. ICA and matrix factorizations are the same pattern with a different criterion. A topic model such as LDA sends a document to a vector of topic proportions.
    \item \textbf{Predictive embeddings.} Word2vec skip-gram replaces a centre type \(w\) and a context type \(c\) by vectors \(v_w,u_c\in\mathbb{R}^d\) and models
    \[
    p(c\mid w)
    =\frac{\exp(\langle v_w,u_c\rangle)}{\sum_{c'\in\mathcal{V}}\exp(\langle v_w,u_{c'}\rangle)}.
    \]
    Types that occur in similar windows receive nearby \(v_w\). GloVe regresses the same co-occurrence table globally. InfoNCE replaces the vocabulary-sized denominator by one positive neighbour and a few negatives.
    \item \textbf{Neural hidden states.} An autoencoder trains \(e_\theta\) to reconstruct \(x\) from \(z\). A supervised network treats a hidden layer as \(z\). A Transformer contextualizes: the vector at a token depends on the whole sequence, so the two senses of ``bank'' need not share one point.
    \item \textbf{Modern self-supervision.} Masked-token prediction, contrastive image--text training, and next-token prediction are all losses that produce a reusable \(z\) without a labeled example for every downstream task.
\end{itemize}

\begin{example}[One-hot versus a \(2\)-d embedding]
Take \(\mathcal{V}=\{\texttt{cat},\texttt{dog},\texttt{bank},\texttt{river},\texttt{finance}\}\). A one-hot encoding of \(\texttt{bank}\) is the standard basis vector \(e_3\in\mathbb{R}^5\). Then \(\langle e_3,e_4\rangle=0=\langle e_3,e_5\rangle\): \(\texttt{river}\) and \(\texttt{finance}\) are orthogonal to \(\texttt{bank}\) and to each other, so the encoding cannot express that one sense of bank co-occurs with rivers and the other with finance. A learned map \(e_\theta:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^2\) can place the finance-sense neighbours in one direction and the river-sense neighbours in another. Skip-gram does this by making \(\langle v_{\texttt{bank}},u_{\texttt{finance}}\rangle\) large when those types co-occur. A single static vector still cannot be in two places at once; a contextual map \(e_\theta(w,\text{sentence})\) can.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
The central idea is to learn \(z=e_\theta(x)\) rather than to write the coordinates of \(x\) by hand, then to predict from \(z\). Classical implementations include PCA, ICA, matrix factorization, and topic models. Neural implementations include autoencoders, word2vec / GloVe / InfoNCE embeddings, supervised hidden layers, and contextual Transformer states. Self-supervised losses (masked tokens, contrastive pairs, next-token prediction) train \(e_\theta\) without a labeled example for every task. In NLP the distinction that matters on the exam is static versus contextual: one vector per type versus a vector that depends on the surrounding tokens. The latter is why a single embedding of ``bank'' is not enough for polysemy.

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
Representation learning estimates a map \(z=e_\theta(x)\) from a raw input to a feature vector, then predicts from \(z\). The features are not specified by hand; they are the parameters \(\theta\) of \(e_\theta\), trained from a reconstruction, contrastive, likelihood, or supervised loss. Classical algorithms: PCA (keep the high-variance subspace), ICA, matrix factorization, LDA. Neural algorithms: autoencoders; word2vec skip-gram, which models \(p(c\mid w)\propto\exp(\langle v_w,u_c\rangle)\); GloVe; InfoNCE; supervised hidden layers; Transformer contextual states. In NLP, a word embedding is a static vector per type; a contextual representation depends on the sentence, which is required for polysemy (``bank'' next to ``river'' versus ``finance''). The same idea covers masked-token and next-token pretraining: those losses produce a reusable \(z\) for later predictors.

\paragraph{Boundary.}
A representation is not automatically interpretable, invariant, or transferable. Those properties come from the data and the objective, not from the phrase ``representation learning.''

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 3: top-$P$ sampling}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
The model has already been trained. At each generation step it outputs a distribution \(p(\cdot\mid x,y_{<t})\) on the vocabulary \(\mathcal{V}\). A \emph{decoding rule} turns that distribution into one token. The question asks why open-ended LLM inference typically uses nucleus (top-\(P\)) sampling rather than greedy search or beam search, and why that rule is preferred to a fixed top-\(K\) cutoff.

\paragraph{Objects.}
Write \(p_w\) for the model's probability of token \(w\) at the current step, and sort the vocabulary so that
\[
p_{(1)}\ge p_{(2)}\ge\cdots\ge p_{(|\mathcal{V}|)},
\qquad
\sum_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|}p_{(j)}=1.
\]
Four rules act on this sorted list.

\begin{definition}[Four decoding rules]\label{def:spr2025-nlp-decoding}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Greedy:} emit \(\arg\max_w p_w\), i.e.\ the token of rank \(1\). The rule is deterministic.
    \item \textbf{Beam search of width \(B\):} keep the \(B\) prefixes with the largest joint score \(\sum_{s\le t}\log p(y_s\mid x,y_{<s})\). At the end return the highest-scoring complete string. This approximates \(\arg\max_y \pi_\theta(y\mid x)\).
    \item \textbf{Top-\(K\):} keep only the \(K\) largest atoms, renormalize
    \[
    \widetilde p_{(j)}=\frac{p_{(j)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{K}p_{(i)}}
    \quad (j=1,\ldots,K),
    \]
    and sample from that \(K\)-point distribution. The integer \(K\) is fixed in advance.
    \item \textbf{Top-\(P\) (nucleus):} keep the \emph{smallest} initial segment whose mass reaches a threshold \(P\in(0,1)\),
    \[
    m=\min\Bigl\{m':\sum_{j=1}^{m'}p_{(j)}\ge P\Bigr\},
    \qquad
    V_P=\bigl\{(1),\ldots,(m)\bigr\},
    \]
    renormalize on \(V_P\), and sample. The integer \(m\) is not chosen in advance: it is a function of the current list \((p_{(j)})\).
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

Renormalization means: discard the tokens outside the candidate set, then divide the surviving probabilities by their sum so that they again add to one. Sampling then draws one token from that restricted distribution.

\paragraph{Why not greedy or beam.}
Greedy never samples. Every time the same prefix occurs, the same next token is emitted, so open-ended text repeats high-probability local choices (loops, stock phrases). It is also myopic: a slightly less likely token now may open a much better continuation, but greedy cannot take that token.

Beam search does look at whole-string probability, but it is still an \(\arg\max\). For open-ended generation the highest-probability strings are typically short, generic, and high-frequency (``I don't know'', ``As an AI\ldots''). Diversity is not a parameter of the rule: unless one randomizes the beam, the output is essentially determined by \(\pi_\theta\) and \(B\).

Top-\(P\) keeps randomness on a set that already carries almost all of the mass, and throws away the long tail of tokens with probabilities \(10^{-5}\) and smaller, which is where fluent nonsense usually lives.

\paragraph{Why not a fixed top-\(K\).}
The same integer \(K\) cannot be right for both a peaked distribution and a flat one. Top-\(P\) changes \(m\) automatically; top-\(K\) does not.

\begin{example}[One sharp step and one flat step]\label{ex:spr2025-nlp-nucleus}
Truncate the vocabulary to six tokens for the arithmetic. Take \(P=0.90\) and, for the comparison, \(K=3\).

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Sharp step} (the model is nearly sure):
\[
(p_{(j)})=(0.80,\,0.10,\,0.04,\,0.03,\,0.02,\,0.01).
\]
Greedy emits rank \(1\). Top-\(K=3\) keeps ranks \(1,2,3\) and renormalizes to
\[
\bigl(0.80,0.10,0.04\bigr)/0.94
\approx(0.851,\,0.106,\,0.043).
\]
Top-\(P=0.90\) stops at \(m=2\) because \(0.80+0.10=0.90\), and renormalizes to \((0.80,0.10)/0.90\approx(0.889,\,0.111)\). The third token, with mass only \(0.04\), is excluded. A fixed \(K=3\) would have kept it.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Flat step} (several continuations are reasonable):
\[
(p_{(j)})=(0.22,\,0.20,\,0.18,\,0.16,\,0.14,\,0.10).
\]
Greedy still emits rank \(1\), discarding four almost-equally-likely alternatives. Top-\(K=3\) keeps only mass \(0.22+0.20+0.18=0.60\) and throws away ranks \(4\) and \(5\), whose probabilities are comparable to rank \(3\). Top-\(P=0.90\) needs
\[
0.22+0.20+0.18+0.16+0.14=0.90,
\]
so \(m=5\). The candidate set grew because the distribution flattened. That is the only difference between the two rows: the rule is the same, the integer \(m\) is not.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
Decoding is not training. After pretraining one has, at each step, a distribution \(p(\cdot\mid\text{prefix})\) on \(\mathcal{V}\). Greedy takes the mode and is therefore deterministic and locally repetitive. Beam search approximates the maximum-probability string; for open-ended generation that maximum is typically generic, and the rule still does not sample. Nucleus sampling keeps the smallest prefix of the sorted list whose mass is at least \(P\), renormalizes, and samples. Randomness among plausible tokens is retained; the long low-probability tail is discarded. Relative to top-\(K\), the size of the candidate set is a function of the current entropy: peaked steps give a small \(m\), flat steps give a large \(m\). A single fixed \(K\) cannot do both. The threshold \(P\) (often around \(0.9\)) is a hyperparameter; temperature, task constraints, and calibration can still change which rule is appropriate, so nucleus sampling is the default for open-ended LLM decoding, not a theorem.

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
At each step the trained model outputs \(p(w\mid\text{prefix})\) on a finite vocabulary. Decoding is the rule that turns this distribution into one token; it does not retrain the model. Greedy always emits \(\arg\max p_w\). It is deterministic, so repeated prefixes produce repeated tokens, and it cannot choose a slightly less likely token that would improve the rest of the string. Beam search of width \(B\) keeps the \(B\) highest-scoring prefixes and returns an approximate \(\arg\max_y\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\). For open-ended generation the highest-probability strings are short and generic, and the rule still does not control diversity by sampling. Top-\(P\) (nucleus) sorts \(p_{(1)}\ge p_{(2)}\ge\cdots\), takes the smallest \(m\) with \(\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{(j)}\ge P\), renormalizes on that set, and samples. This keeps randomness on a set that already carries mass \(P\) and drops the long tail. Top-\(K\) instead keeps a fixed number of tokens. When the distribution is peaked, a large \(K\) admits implausible tokens; when it is flat, a small \(K\) drops reasonable ones. Top-\(P\) changes \(m\) with the shape of \(p\); top-\(K\) cannot. Hence current open-ended LLM inference uses top-\(P\) rather than greedy, beam, or a fixed top-\(K\).

\paragraph{Boundary.}
Top-\(P\) is not universally superior. Translation and other tasks with a nearly unique target still use beam search. Temperature, safety filters, and a badly calibrated \(p\) all change the right rule. Nucleus sampling does not by itself make the model factual.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 4: why current LLM systems are effective}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
List several mechanisms that jointly explain why present LLM systems work, and attach a reason to each. ``More parameters'' is not a mechanism. The official wording mentions AGI; the exam answer should explain capability, then refuse the leap from capability to AGI.

\paragraph{What ``mechanism'' means here.}
Each item below names (i)~an object that was changed relative to earlier NLP systems and (ii)~the term in the modelling stack that this change relaxes. The stack is: a factorisation of \(p(w_1,\ldots,w_n)\), a parameterisation of each factor, an objective, a scaling regime, and an inference-time condition.

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Self-supervised pretraining.} The objective is next-token likelihood
    \[
    \max_\theta\sum_t\log\pi_\theta(w_t\mid w_{<t})
    \]
    on a large unlabelled corpus. Every token is a training event; one does not need a labeled pair for each downstream task. The resulting \(\pi_\theta\) is a model of the training distribution and can be adapted in context by changing the prefix rather than the parameters.
    \item \textbf{Transformer parameterisation.} Self-attention replaces a left-to-right recurrent state by a content-weighted average of all positions in one layer. Training is parallel across the \(L\) tokens of a sequence; any two tokens have a path of length one inside a layer. That is why long-range conditionals and large-batch training became jointly feasible. The cost is \(O(L^2)\) attention.
    \item \textbf{Scaling of data \(N\), parameters \(P\), and compute.} Empirically, test loss often follows a local power law in \(N\) or \(P\) until a floor. In the four-term decomposition, extra data cut estimation error and extra capacity cuts approximation error, provided optimization keeps up. Scaling is a statement about those terms, not a third architecture.
    \item \textbf{Post-training / alignment.} Pretraining imitates the web. Instruction tuning and RLHF change the target from \(\pi_{\mathrm{web}}(y\mid x)\) to a preference-weighted policy, typically by a reward model and a KL leash toward a reference policy (Problem~7). Without this step the system continues text; with it, it answers instructions.
    \item \textbf{Inference-time systems.} Decoding (Problem~3), retrieval-augmented generation (Problem~8), tools, and verification change the condition or the sampling rule without retraining \(\theta\). They supply facts that were never packed into the parameters and control how a distribution becomes a string.
\end{itemize}

These ingredients multiply. A Transformer without data does not estimate its conditionals; a pretrained model without alignment is not an assistant; alignment without retrieval still cannot update stale facts.

\begin{example}[One capability, five missing pieces]
A 2015 LSTM language model already maximised next-token likelihood, but: the computation graph was sequential in \(L\); \(N\) and \(P\) were orders of magnitude smaller; there was no preference stage; decoding was often greedy; and there was no retrieved corpus. Changing only one of those---for example adding RLHF to a tiny LSTM---does not reproduce current systems. The exam list is a product, not a menu from which one item suffices.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
Current LLM systems work because several constraints were relaxed together, not because of a single trick. (1)~Self-supervised next-token pretraining turns every token of a large corpus into a training signal, so the model estimates \(p(w_t\mid w_{<t})\) without a labeled example for each task and can be steered by the prompt. (2)~The Transformer parameterises that conditional by self-attention: each position reads all others in one layer, training is parallel in sequence length, and long-range dependence no longer travels through \(L\) recurrent steps. (3)~Scaling data, parameters, and compute reduces estimation and approximation error in the usual risk decomposition, which is why test loss has fallen as a local power law in \(N\) and \(P\). (4)~Alignment (instruction tuning, RLHF) replaces the web-imitation objective by a preference objective, so the policy answers instructions rather than continuing internet text. (5)~Inference-time mechanisms---nucleus sampling, retrieval, tools---change how a string is chosen or which text the generator is conditioned on, without storing every fact in \(\theta\). These pieces are complementary. They explain large capability gains; they do not by themselves show that a system is AGI, which is not a well-posed claim on this paper.

\paragraph{Boundary.}
Do not write ``scale is enough'' or ``Transformers are enough.'' Do not treat the official word AGI as a theorem to be proved.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 5: Transformer advantages over recurrent models}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
Compare the computation graphs of a Transformer and an RNN (LSTM/GRU) on a sequence-to-sequence task. Names are not enough: say what is parallel, how far a signal must travel, and what the attention weights depend on. Give at least two advantages and the quadratic-cost caveat.

\paragraph{Objects.}
Let the input be vectors \(x_1,\ldots,x_L\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\). The symbols used in this solution---including the two dimensions \(d_{\mathrm{in}}\) and \(d\)---are collected first. Two indices are kept distinct: \(i\) is the position that is reading, and \(j\) is the position being read. In particular \(v_j=W_V x_j\) is the value vector at the source position \(j\).

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.2pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.05cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\hline
\(L\) & Sequence length. Positions are \(1,\ldots,L\). \\
\hline
\(i\) & The position that is \emph{reading} (query / output index). \\
\hline
\(j\) & The position being \emph{read} (source index). Dummy index in \(\sum_j\). \\
\hline
\(t\) & RNN time index, running through the same positions \(1,\ldots,L\). \\
\hline
\(d_{\mathrm{in}}\) & Incoming dimension. Each \(x_t\) lives in \(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\): a token embedding, or the hidden state of the previous layer. In the numerical example, \(d_{\mathrm{in}}=2\). \\
\hline
\(d\) & Attention (matching) dimension. Queries and keys live in \(\mathbb{R}^d\), so that the inner product \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\) is defined. The scale in the attention formula is \(\sqrt{d}\). In the numerical example, \(d=2\). If keys and values are allowed different widths one writes \(d_k\) and \(d_v\); the standard abbreviation is \(d=d_k=d_v\). \\
\hline
\(d_k\), \(d_v\) & Optional split of \(d\): \(q_i,k_j\in\mathbb{R}^{d_k}\) and \(v_j\in\mathbb{R}^{d_v}\). Then \(QK^\top\) is still \(L\times L\), while \(V\) has width \(d_v\). \\
\hline
\(x_t\), \(x_i\), \(x_j\) & Incoming vector at that position, in \(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\). \\
\hline
\(X\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}\) & Data matrix whose \(t\)-th row is \(x_t^\top\). \\
\hline
\(h_t\), \(f\), \(h_0\) & RNN hidden state after step \(t\); the update map \(h_t=f(h_{t-1},x_t)\); the initial state. \\
\hline
\(W_Q,W_K,W_V\) & Learned matrices. \(W_Q,W_K\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}\) (or \(d_k\times d_{\mathrm{in}}\)) and \(W_V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}\) (or \(d_v\times d_{\mathrm{in}}\)). They send an incoming \(x\) to query, key, and value. \\
\hline
\(q_i=W_Q x_i\) & Query at \(i\), in \(\mathbb{R}^d\): what position \(i\) is looking for. \\
\hline
\(k_j=W_K x_j\) & Key at \(j\), in \(\mathbb{R}^d\): the address of the content at \(j\). \\
\hline
\(v_j=W_V x_j\) & Value at \(j\), in \(\mathbb{R}^d\) (or \(\mathbb{R}^{d_v}\)): the content stored at the source position. \\
\hline
\(Q,K,V\) & Stacked matrices. \(Q,K\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\) and \(V\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\) (or \(L\times d_v\)), whose \(i\)-th rows are \(q_i^\top\), \(k_i^\top\), \(v_i^\top\). \\
\hline
\(S=QK^\top/\sqrt{d}\) & Score matrix in \(\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}\). Entry \((i,j)\) is \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d}\). The factor \(\sqrt{d}\) keeps typical scores from having variance of order \(d\). \\
\hline
\(\softmax\) & Row-wise map from scores to a distribution; see \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-softmax}. On a vector, \((\softmax(s))_j=e^{s_j}/\sum_\ell e^{s_\ell}\). \\
\hline
\(P=\softmax(S)\) & Attention-weight matrix in \(\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}\). Row \(i\) is the distribution \(p(\cdot\mid i)\). \\
\hline
\(p(j\mid i)\) & Attention weight of source \(j\) at query \(i\); see \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-attn-weights}. Equals \(P_{ij}\). \\
\hline
\(\Delta^{L-1}\) & Probability simplex in \(\mathbb{R}^L\): nonnegative coordinates summing to one. Each row of \(P\) lies in \(\Delta^{L-1}\). \\
\hline
\(\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V)\) & The layer map \(\softmax(QK^\top/\sqrt{d})\,V=PV\). \\
\hline
\(z_i=\sum_j p(j\mid i)\,v_j\) & Layer output at \(i\), in \(\mathbb{R}^d\). A convex combination of the values; equivalently row \(i\) of \(PV\). \\
\hline
\(Z=PV\) & Stacked outputs, in \(\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\). The \(i\)-th row is \(z_i^\top\). This is a context matrix, not a next-token distribution. \\
\hline
\(R=X+Z\) & Residual stream (LayerNorm omitted). Positionwise sum; \(R\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\) only when \(d_{\mathrm{in}}=d\), as in the numerical example. A feed-forward map then acts on each row separately. \\
\hline
\(W_U\), \(u\) & Unembedding and logits at the language-model head. After the last layer one reads a single residual row \(r_i\) and sets \(u=W_U r_i\); a second softmax, now over types, gives \(p(\text{next token})\). \\
\hline
\(W_Q^{(h)},W_K^{(h)},W_V^{(h)}\) & The same three maps for attention head \(h\). Multi-head attention repeats \(\mathrm{Attn}\) on several such projections. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

An RNN updates a hidden state by
\[
h_t=f(h_{t-1},x_t),\qquad t=1,\ldots,L,
\]
with \(h_0\) fixed. The map \(x_1\mapsto h_L\) therefore composes \(L\) copies of \(f\). Training must wait for \(h_{t-1}\) before \(h_t\). A signal from position \(1\) to position \(L\) traverses \(L-1\) Jacobians \(\partial f/\partial h\), which is the vanishing/exploding-gradient path.

A Transformer layer does not recycle a single state. At each position \(i\) one forms
\[
q_i=W_Q x_i,\qquad k_i=W_K x_i,\qquad v_i=W_V x_i,
\]
stacks them as rows of \(Q,K,V\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\), and returns
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V)
=\softmax\Bigl(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d}}\Bigr)V.
\]
The three maps are already the two slots of this display; see \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-qkv-from-formula}. Row \(i\) of the softmax is the distribution \(p(\cdot\mid i)\) of \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-attn-weights}. The output at \(i\) is \(\mathbb{E}_{j\sim p(\cdot\mid i)}[v_j]\). Every pair \((i,j)\) interacts inside one matrix product. The \(L\) rows can be computed together; there is no \(h_{t-1}\) to wait for. Multi-head attention repeats the same block on several projections \((W_Q^{(h)},W_K^{(h)},W_V^{(h)})\).

\begin{definition}[Softmax]
\label{def:spr2025-nlp-softmax}
For a score vector \(s=(s_1,\ldots,s_L)\in\mathbb{R}^L\),
\[
\bigl(\softmax(s)\bigr)_j
=\frac{e^{s_j}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}e^{s_\ell}},
\qquad j=1,\ldots,L.
\]
The image lies in the simplex \(\Delta^{L-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^L\),
\[
\Delta^{L-1}
=\Bigl\{ p\in\mathbb{R}^L :
p_j\ge 0\text{ for all }j=1,\ldots,L,\quad
\sum_{j=1}^{L}p_j=1
\Bigr\}.
\]
Softmax in fact lands in the interior: every coordinate is strictly positive. Adding the same constant to every entry of \(s\) does not change the value, because \(e^{s_j+c}=e^c\,e^{s_j}\) cancels in the ratio. A large gap \(s_j-s_\ell\) is exponentiated, so the mass concentrates on the largest scores; if all scores are equal, the output is uniform. Applied to a matrix, \(\softmax\) acts independently on each row, not on each column.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Attention weights]
\label{def:spr2025-nlp-attn-weights}
Fix queries \(q_1,\ldots,q_L\) and keys \(k_1,\ldots,k_L\), and write
\[
S_{ij}
=\frac{\langle q_i,k_j\rangle}{\sqrt{d}},
\qquad
s^{(i)}
=(S_{i1},\ldots,S_{iL})\in\mathbb{R}^{L}.
\]
The attention weight of source position \(j\) at query position \(i\) is the \(j\)-th coordinate of the vector softmax of that row:
\[
p(j\mid i)
=\bigl(\softmax(s^{(i)})\bigr)_j
=\frac{e^{S_{ij}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}e^{S_{i\ell}}}
=\frac{\exp\bigl(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d}\bigr)}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\exp\bigl(\langle q_i,k_\ell\rangle/\sqrt{d}\bigr)}.
\]
For each fixed \(i\), the map \(j\mapsto p(j\mid i)\) is a distribution on \(\{1,\ldots,L\}\), written \(p(\cdot\mid i)\in\Delta^{L-1}\). The matrix \(P=\softmax(S)\) is exactly this family of distributions:
\[
P_{ij}=p(j\mid i).
\]
The layer output at \(i\) is the corresponding mixture of values,
\[
z_i
=\sum_{j=1}^{L}p(j\mid i)\,v_j
=\mathbb{E}_{j\sim p(\cdot\mid i)}[v_j].
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The object \(p(\cdot\mid i)\) is a distribution on source positions, not on vocabulary types. The language-model head uses a second softmax, on a different simplex.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[A \(3\times 2\) layer, computed through \(\mathrm{Attn}\) and the next-token head]
The maps are linear, so the spaces are just Euclidean spaces of the stated dimensions. Take \(L=3\), \(d_{\mathrm{in}}=d=2\), and the tokens \texttt{not}, \texttt{very}, \texttt{good}. Write incoming vectors\footnote{The tokens themselves are discrete types. An incoming vector \(x_t\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\) is the real vector that represents the token at this layer: the embedding of that type, or the hidden state coming out of the previous layer. The two-dimensional numbers below are a toy embedding chosen so that polarity and intensity are visible, not a trained word2vec vector.} as columns
\[
x_1=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
x_2=\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
x_3=\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}=\mathbb{R}^2,
\]
and stack their transposes as the data matrix
\[
X=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}=\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}.
\]
The first coordinate is a polarity axis (\texttt{not} vs.\ quality); the second is intensity (\texttt{very}). The three matrices \(W_Q,W_K,W_V\) below are stipulated by hand for this toy, to match that story of \(x_1,x_2,x_3\). They are not a natural object that comes with the tokens, and they are not the output of training.\footnote{In a real layer the same three maps are learned. Here one picks \(W_Q\) so that \(q_3\parallel x_1=k_1\) and \(q_3\perp x_2=k_2\), hence \texttt{good} looks for \texttt{not} rather than \texttt{very}; \(W_K=W_V=I_2\) is the same kind of stipulation, so that keys and values remain the incoming vectors and the arithmetic stays readable.}
\[
W_Q=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
W_K=I_2,\qquad
W_V=I_2
\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}=\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}.
\]
The queries, keys, and values are obtained from these maps by matrix--vector products, not by writing the vectors down independently. For the queries,
\begin{align*}
q_1
&=W_Q x_1
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix},
\\
q_2
&=W_Q x_2
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}0\\0\end{pmatrix},
\\
q_3
&=W_Q x_3
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
So \(W_Q\) keeps only the polarity coordinate. Keys and values use the same rule with \(W_K=W_V=I_2\):
\begin{align*}
k_1
&=W_K x_1
=I_2\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
\quad\text{and}\quad
v_1
=W_V x_1
=I_2\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix},
\\
k_2
&=W_K x_2
=I_2\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}
\quad\text{and}\quad
v_2
=W_V x_2
=I_2\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix},
\\
k_3
&=W_K x_3
=I_2\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
\quad\text{and}\quad
v_3
=W_V x_3
=I_2\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
Stack the transposes as rows:
\[
Q
=\begin{pmatrix}q_1^\top\\q_2^\top\\q_3^\top\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
K
=\begin{pmatrix}k_1^\top\\k_2^\top\\k_3^\top\end{pmatrix}
=X,\qquad
V
=\begin{pmatrix}v_1^\top\\v_2^\top\\v_3^\top\end{pmatrix}
=X
\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}=\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}.
\]
The Gram matrix of queries against keys is
\[
QK^\top
=\begin{pmatrix}
1&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
1&0&1
\end{pmatrix}
\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}=\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}.
\]
Scale by \(\sqrt{d}=\sqrt{2}\) to obtain the score matrix
\[
S=\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d}}
=\begin{pmatrix}
1/\sqrt{2}&0&1/\sqrt{2}\\
0&0&0\\
1/\sqrt{2}&0&1/\sqrt{2}
\end{pmatrix}
\approx
\begin{pmatrix}
0.707&0&0.707\\
0&0&0\\
0.707&0&0.707
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The factor \(\sqrt{d}\) is not optional decoration: without it the typical inner product has variance of order \(d\), the exponentials saturate, and \(\softmax\) collapses to a one-hot. Here \(d=2\) is small, so the effect is mild.

The map in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-softmax} is
\[
\softmax:\mathbb{R}^{L}\to\Delta^{L-1}.
\]
It takes one score vector. It does not take a matrix. The written composition \(P=\softmax(S)\) is therefore an abbreviation, not a new map \(\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}\to\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}\). Extract each row of \(S\) as a vector in \(\mathbb{R}^{L}\), apply the vector formula, and write the resulting distribution back as that same row. Columns are left alone: they are not normalized, and two entries in one column need not sum to one.

Write \(s^{(i)}=(S_{i1},S_{i2},S_{i3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\) for row \(i\), and set \(p^{(i)}=\softmax(s^{(i)})\in\Delta^{2}\). By \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-attn-weights},
\[
p(j\mid i)=p^{(i)}_{j},\qquad
P
=\begin{pmatrix}
(p^{(1)})^\top\\
(p^{(2)})^\top\\
(p^{(3)})^\top
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
P_{ij}=p(j\mid i).
\]

Row \(i=1\). The score vector is
\[
s^{(1)}
=\bigl(S_{11},S_{12},S_{13}\bigr)
=\bigl(1/\sqrt{2},\,0,\,1/\sqrt{2}\bigr).
\]
Coordinatewise exponentials:
\[
e^{S_{11}}=e^{1/\sqrt{2}}\approx 2.028,\qquad
e^{S_{12}}=e^{0}=1,\qquad
e^{S_{13}}=e^{1/\sqrt{2}}\approx 2.028.
\]
The denominator in the definition is their sum,
\[
\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}e^{S_{1\ell}}
=2e^{1/\sqrt{2}}+1
\approx 5.056.
\]
Hence
\begin{align*}
p^{(1)}_{1}
&=\frac{e^{S_{11}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}e^{S_{1\ell}}}
=\frac{e^{1/\sqrt{2}}}{2e^{1/\sqrt{2}}+1}
\approx 0.401,\\
p^{(1)}_{2}
&=\frac{e^{S_{12}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}e^{S_{1\ell}}}
=\frac{1}{2e^{1/\sqrt{2}}+1}
\approx 0.198,\\
p^{(1)}_{3}
&=\frac{e^{S_{13}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}e^{S_{1\ell}}}
=\frac{e^{1/\sqrt{2}}}{2e^{1/\sqrt{2}}+1}
\approx 0.401.
\end{align*}
So \(p^{(1)}\approx(0.401,0.198,0.401)\), which lies in \(\Delta^{2}\).

Row \(i=2\). The score vector is
\[
s^{(2)}=(S_{21},S_{22},S_{23})=(0,0,0).
\]
Every exponential equals \(1\), the denominator is \(3\), and
\begin{align*}
p^{(2)}_{1}
&=\frac{e^{0}}{e^{0}+e^{0}+e^{0}}
=\frac{1}{3},\\
p^{(2)}_{2}
&=\frac{1}{3},\\
p^{(2)}_{3}
&=\frac{1}{3}.
\end{align*}
A zero query has no preferred key, so the vector softmax returns the uniform distribution \(p^{(2)}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)\).

Row \(i=3\). The third row of \(S\) is the vector
\[
s^{(3)}
=\bigl(S_{31},S_{32},S_{33}\bigr)
=\bigl(1/\sqrt{2},\,0,\,1/\sqrt{2}\bigr)
=s^{(1)}.
\]
The same three divisions therefore give \(p^{(3)}=\softmax(s^{(3)})=p^{(1)}\).

Stack the three output vectors as rows:
\[
P=\softmax(S)
\approx
\begin{pmatrix}
0.401&0.198&0.401\\
0.333&0.333&0.333\\
0.401&0.198&0.401
\end{pmatrix}
\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}.
\]
Each row sums to one. The first column sums to \(0.401+0.333+0.401\neq 1\): that is the distinction between applying \(\softmax\) to rows and applying it to columns.

This \(P\) is not yet the layer output. The definition is the matrix product
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V)=PV.
\]
With \(V=X\),
\[
PV
\approx
\begin{pmatrix}
0.401&0.198&0.401\\
0.333&0.333&0.333\\
0.401&0.198&0.401
\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}
1&0\\
0&1\\
1&1
\end{pmatrix}
=
\begin{pmatrix}
0.802&0.599\\
0.667&0.667\\
0.802&0.599
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
By \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-attn-weights}, row \(i\) of \(PV\) is the context vector
\[
z_i=\sum_{j=1}^{3}p(j\mid i)\,v_j\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
\]
written at position \(i\). It is a mixture of the three values, in the same Euclidean space as the \(v_j\). It is not a token, and it is not a distribution on tokens.

Position \(i=3\) is \texttt{good}. The weights are \(p(\cdot\mid 3)\approx(0.401,0.198,0.401)\), so
\begin{align*}
z_3
&=p(1\mid 3)\,v_1+p(2\mid 3)\,v_2+p(3\mid 3)\,v_3
\\
&\approx 0.401\,v_1+0.198\,v_2+0.401\,v_3
\\
&=0.401\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
+0.198\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}
+0.401\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}0.802\\0.599\end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
This is the third row of \(PV\). The summand \(v_1\) is the value at \texttt{not}, \(v_2\) at \texttt{very}, and \(v_3\) at \texttt{good}. The first coordinate \(0.802\) is polarity copied from \texttt{not} and \texttt{good}; the second coordinate \(0.599\) is leftover intensity from \texttt{very} and \texttt{good}. The weights are not equal: \(q_3\parallel k_1\) and \(q_3\perp k_2\), so \texttt{good} reads \texttt{not} and itself more than it reads \texttt{very}. In the phrase \texttt{not very good}, the query at \texttt{good} was stipulated to look along the polarity axis. The negation is therefore retrieved in one matrix product, and the intensifier is down-weighted. That is the content-addressed conclusion: who is read is decided by \(\langle q_3,k_j\rangle\), not by distance along the string.

Position \(i=2\) is \texttt{very}. The query is \(q_2=0\), the weights are uniform, and
\begin{align*}
z_2
&=\tfrac13 v_1+\tfrac13 v_2+\tfrac13 v_3
=\frac{x_1+x_2+x_3}{3}
=\begin{pmatrix}2/3\\2/3\end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
This is the second row of \(PV\). A query that asks for nothing matches every key equally, so the layer writes the mean of the values at that position.

Position \(i=1\) is \texttt{not}. The score row equals that of \(i=3\), hence \(z_1=z_3\). The first and third rows of \(PV\) therefore coincide.

Write \(Z=PV\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}\) for the stacked context vectors. The three rows are new representations at \texttt{not}, \texttt{very}, and \texttt{good}. They do not replace the incoming vectors. The residual (LayerNorm omitted) is the positionwise sum \(R=X+Z\): at each position one adds the context back onto the token that asked for it,
\[
r_i=x_i+z_i\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
\]
In particular, at \texttt{good},
\begin{align*}
r_3
&=x_3+z_3
\approx
\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}
+\begin{pmatrix}0.802\\0.599\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1.802\\1.599\end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
This is the third row of
\[
R=X+Z
\approx
\begin{pmatrix}
1.802&0.599\\
0.667&1.667\\
1.802&1.599
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The first two rows are \(r_1=x_1+z_1\) and \(r_2=x_2+z_2\), by the same rule. The vector \(r_3\) is still a point in \(\mathbb{R}^{2}\): the original embedding of \texttt{good}, plus the mixture it just read. It is not a token and not a next-word distribution.

A feed-forward network then acts on each row of \(R\) separately: it does not mix positions, and it does not recompute \(Q,K,V\). The next layer takes that result as its new incoming matrix and repeats the same attention block. After the last layer the language-model head reads only the current position. Here that position is \(3\), so the head sees \(r_3\), not \(X\), not \(Z\), and not the whole of \(R\).

The string \(\texttt{not very good}\) is the input prefix: three positions already read. It is not a label, and \(\{\texttt{bad},\texttt{ok},\texttt{great}\}\) is not a set of paraphrases of that string. Those three types are a toy vocabulary for the \emph{next} token after the prefix. The head therefore computes
\[
p(w\mid\texttt{not very good})
=p\bigl(w_4=w\mid w_1=\texttt{not},\,w_2=\texttt{very},\,w_3=\texttt{good}\bigr),
\qquad
w\in\{\texttt{bad},\texttt{ok},\texttt{great}\}.
\]
This is next-token prediction, the same object as a language model. It is not the sentiment of the phrase, and it is not the statement ``\(\texttt{not very good}\) means \(\texttt{bad}\)'' or ``means \(\texttt{great}\)''. Take the unembedding
\[
W_U
=\begin{pmatrix}
-1&0\\
0&1\\
1&0
\end{pmatrix}
\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}.
\]
The next-token logits are
\[
u=W_U r_3
\approx
\begin{pmatrix}-1.802\\1.599\\1.802\end{pmatrix},
\]
i.e.\ \(\langle w_{\texttt{bad}},r_3\rangle\), \(\langle w_{\texttt{ok}},r_3\rangle\), \(\langle w_{\texttt{great}},r_3\rangle\). This is a second, unrelated softmax: the first was over positions \(\{1,2,3\}\), the second is over types. Exponentiate,
\[
e^{-1.802}\approx 0.165,
\qquad
e^{1.599}\approx 4.948,
\qquad
e^{1.802}\approx 6.062,
\]
sum \(Z_{\mathrm{voc}}\approx 11.175\), and obtain
\[
p(\cdot\mid\texttt{not very good})
\approx
\bigl(0.015,\,0.443,\,0.542\bigr)
\]
on \((\texttt{bad},\texttt{ok},\texttt{great})\).
