In words: after reading the prefix, the head puts mass \(0.542\) on the next type being \(\texttt{great}\), \(0.443\) on \(\texttt{ok}\), and \(0.015\) on \(\texttt{bad}\). The first softmax, \(p(j\mid 3)\), was a distribution on the three input positions. This second softmax is a distribution on three candidate continuations. They are not the same simplex.

English would suggest that \(\texttt{not very good}\) is closer to a mild negative than to \(\texttt{great}\). The numbers go the other way. That is not a discovery about the phrase, and it is not evidence that a trained model ``failed to understand'' the phrase. No model was trained. The maps \(W_Q,W_V,W_U\) were stipulated by hand, and \(W_U\) reads the first coordinate of \(r_3\) as a logit for \(\texttt{great}\) versus \(\texttt{bad}\). That coordinate is \(+1.802\), so \(\texttt{great}\) wins by arithmetic.

The token \(\texttt{not}\) in the prefix is just position \(1\), the incoming vector \(x_1=(1,0)^\top\). Attention did retrieve it: \(p(1\mid 3)\approx 0.401\). Retrieving the type \(\texttt{not}\) is not the same as applying English negation to the meaning of \(\texttt{good}\). In this toy \(W_V=I_2\), so the payload of \(\texttt{not}\) is still \(v_1=(1,0)^\top\), on the same polarity axis as \(\texttt{good}\). Adding it makes the polarity of \(r_3\) larger, not smaller, and the head therefore prefers \(\texttt{great}\) rather than \(\texttt{bad}\). Matching is a statement about \(\langle q_3,k_1\rangle\); changing the predicted continuation would be a statement about \(v_1\). Those are different maps, which is why value is not key. A trained \(W_V\) could send \(x_1\) to a different direction and then the same prefix could put mass on \(\texttt{bad}\). The later linguistic examples prescribe a weight vector in order to isolate a syntactic claim; they are not this numerical layer.

\begin{center}
\resizebox{0.98\linewidth}{!}{%

  font=\small,

    draw=#1!62!black, fill=#1!10, rounded corners=2pt,
    align=center, font=\scriptsize, inner sep=3.2pt,
    minimum height=0.78cm, text width=1.72cm
  },
  arr/.style={->, thick, draw=black!50}
]
  \node[box=orange] (s) {\(S=QK^\top/\sqrt{d}\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny scores}};
  \node[box=teal, right=5.2mm of s] (p) {\(P=\softmax(S)\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny over positions}};
  \node[box=purple, right=5.2mm of p] (z) {\(Z=PV\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny context}};
  \node[box=black, right=5.2mm of z] (r) {\(R=X+Z\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny residual}};
  \node[box=blue, right=5.2mm of r] (u) {\(u=W_U r_3\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny logits}};
  \node[box=olive, right=5.2mm of u] (y) {\(\softmax(u)\)\\[-1pt]{\tiny over types}};
  \draw[arr] (s)--(p);
  \draw[arr] (p)--(z);
  \draw[arr] (z)--(r);
  \draw[arr] (r)--(u);
  \draw[arr] (u)--(y);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Reading query, key, and value from the formula]
\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-qkv-from-formula}
The roles of \(W_Q,W_K,W_V\) are not an extra story. They are the two slots of
\[
\mathrm{Attn}(Q,K,V)
=\softmax\Bigl(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d}}\Bigr)V.
\]
At one position this is
\[
z_i
=\sum_{j=1}^{L}
\mathrm{softmax}_j\Bigl(\frac{\langle q_i,k_j\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\Bigr)\,v_j.
\]
Substitute the three definitions \(q_i=W_Q x_i\), \(k_j=W_K x_j\), \(v_j=W_V x_j\):
\[
z_i
=\sum_{j=1}^{L}
\mathrm{softmax}_j\Bigl(\frac{\langle W_Q x_i,W_K x_j\rangle}{\sqrt{d}}\Bigr)\,(W_V x_j).
\]
Read the occurrences.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=0.25em]
  \item \(W_Q\) and \(W_K\) appear only inside the inner product, hence only inside the softmax. They affect the weights \(p(j\mid i)\). They do not appear as the vector being added. That is match: who is read.
  \item \(W_V\) appears only outside the softmax, as the vector \(W_V x_j\) in the sum. It does not appear in the score. That is mix: what is copied.
  \item The score \(x_i^\top W_Q^\top W_K x_j\) is not, in general, equal to the swapped score \(x_j^\top W_Q^\top W_K x_i\). The same formula is directed: \(i\) reading \(j\) is a different number from \(j\) reading \(i\).
\end{enumerate}
If one set \(W_Q=W_K=W_V=I\), the display would collapse to \(\softmax(XX^\top/\sqrt{d})\,X\). Then the coordinates that decide \(p(j\mid i)\) would be forced to be the coordinates written into \(z_i\). The three matrices are in the formula precisely so that those two appearances of \(x\) need not be the same linear image.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why three maps: query, key, value]
\label{rem:spr2025-nlp-qkv}
The split in \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-qkv-from-formula} is the reason one incoming vector is given three jobs. As a reader it must say what it is looking for; as a source it must say how it can be found; as a payload it must say what should be copied if it is selected. Those three statements are the three linear maps already sitting in the formula:
\[
q_t=W_Q x_t,\qquad
k_t=W_K x_t,\qquad
v_t=W_V x_t.
\]
Self-attention means that one sequence supplies all three. It does not mean \(q_t=k_t=v_t\).

The layer is two steps, not one map.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=0.25em]
  \item \textbf{Match} (who to read). Compare the query at \(i\) to every key, and turn the scores into a distribution on positions, as in \cref{def:spr2025-nlp-attn-weights}:
  \[
  p(j\mid i)
  =\mathrm{softmax}_j\bigl(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d}\bigr).
  \]
  Only the inner products \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\) enter this step.
  \item \textbf{Mix} (what to copy). Write the corresponding mixture of values,
  \[
  z_i=\sum_j p(j\mid i)\,v_j.
  \]
  Only the values \(v_j\) enter this step.
\end{enumerate}
Queries and keys must live in the same Euclidean space, otherwise \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\) is undefined. Values need not: \(d_k\) and \(d_v\) are independent.

\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Two collapses fail. If match and mix share one vector, the coordinates that make a token easy to find are forced to be the coordinates written into \(z_i\). In the numerical layer, \(W_Q\) kept polarity so \(\texttt{good}\) found \(\texttt{not}\), while \(W_V=I_2\) copied the raw vectors, so the payload of \(\texttt{not}\) did not flip the meaning. A negation cue and a negation operator need not live on the same axis. If one uses a symmetric score \(x_i^\top A x_j\), then ``\(i\) reads \(j\)'' and ``\(j\) reads \(i\)'' are tied. Language is directed: \(\texttt{good}\) may look for \(\texttt{not}\) without \(\texttt{not}\) looking for \(\texttt{good}\) with the same strength. The form
\[
\langle W_Q x_i,W_K x_j\rangle
=x_i^\top W_Q^\top W_K x_j
\]
is directed. Three matrices are the standard parameterisation of that directed match together with a separate payload \(W_V\).

The next-token head never sees \(Q,K,V\). It sees \(z_i\) after residual and feed-forward maps. Query, key, and value are an internal assembly of context, not three extra vocabularies.

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.2pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.14}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.15cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
 & \textbf{Query \(q_i\)} & \textbf{Key \(k_j\)} & \textbf{Value \(v_j\)} \\
\hline
Role & what \(i\) asks for & how \(j\) is addressed & what \(j\) contributes \\
\hline
Space & matching \(\mathbb{R}^{d_k}\) & matching \(\mathbb{R}^{d_k}\) & content \(\mathbb{R}^{d_v}\) \\
\hline
Enters as & left factor of \(\langle q,k\rangle\) & right factor of \(\langle q,k\rangle\) & atom of \(\sum_j p(j\mid i)\,v_j\) \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}
\end{remark}

\paragraph{The two computation graphs.}
The principle is a change of wiring, not a change of the object being modelled. Both architectures estimate a next-token conditional. An RNN forces the whole history through one state that is updated from the left: \(h_t\) cannot be formed until \(h_{t-1}\) exists, and a token at position \(1\) reaches position \(L\) only after \(L-1\) applications of \(f\). A Transformer layer keeps a vector at every position. Position \(i\) writes a query \(q_i\), compares it to every key \(k_j\), and reads \(\sum_j p(j\mid i)\,v_j\). The next-token head is still a softmax on a linear map of that vector; only the path that assembled the vector has changed. The list and the two graphs below are the same comparison.

{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.35cm}|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X|
}
\hline
 & \textbf{RNN / LSTM / GRU} & \textbf{Transformer layer} \\
\hline
Update & \(h_t=f(h_{t-1},x_t)\); one state, left to right. & \(q_i,k_i,v_i\) at every \(i\); no recycled \(h_{t-1}\). \\
\hline
Training graph & A chain of length \(L\). Position \(t\) waits for \(t-1\). & One \(L\times L\) product \(QK^\top\). All rows together. \\
\hline
Path \(1\to L\) & \(L-1\) maps; vanishing / exploding \(\partial f/\partial h\). & One inner product \(\langle q_L,k_1\rangle\) inside the layer. \\
\hline
Mixing weights & Implicit in \(f\); not a distribution on positions. & \(p(j\mid i)=\mathrm{softmax}_j(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle/\sqrt{d})\), content-dependent; several heads. \\
\hline
Cost & \(O(L)\) sequential steps. & \(O(L^2)\) time and memory. Decoding of new tokens is still serial. \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
}

\end{center}

The left graph is sequential: the thick path from \(x_1\) to \(h_3\) is \(x_1\to h_1\to h_2\to h_3\). The right graph is one layer of self-attention: every \(x_j\) can contribute to every \(z_i\), and the thick edge is the direct read of position \(1\) by position \(3\). Autoregressive decoding of newly sampled tokens remains serial in both pictures; the contrast is the training graph, and the path length inside one layer. Dense attention costs \(O(L^2)\).

The examples below are not extra architecture. They instantiate two rows of the table already written: \textbf{Path \(1\to L\)} and \textbf{Mixing weights}. The first shows why a short path matters; the second shows that the same wiring can carry a long-range agreement; the third shows that \(p(\cdot\mid i)\) depends on the query \(q_i\), not on a fixed window.

\begin{example}[Negation: path length, \(L=3\)]
\label{ex:spr2025-nlp-not-good}
Tokens: \(x_1=\texttt{not}\), \(x_2=\texttt{very}\), \(x_3=\texttt{good}\). The polarity of the phrase is decided at position \(3\), but the decisive evidence sits at position \(1\).

An RNN must form
\[
h_3=f\bigl(f\bigl(f(h_0,x_1),x_2\bigr),x_3\bigr).
\]
The map \(x_1\mapsto h_3\) is a composition of two copies of \(f\) after \(h_1\). If those Jacobians \(\partial f/\partial h\) shrink, the bit ``negation'' is gone before it reaches \(\texttt{good}\). That is the table row Path \(1\to L\).

In one self-attention layer the third softmax row is a distribution on \(\{1,2,3\}\). A typical negation-reading row is
\[
p(\cdot\mid 3)=(0.70,\,0.10,\,0.20),
\qquad
z_3=0.70\,v_1+0.10\,v_2+0.20\,v_3,
\]
with \(v_j=W_V x_j\). The coefficient \(0.70\) is large because \(\langle q_3,k_1\rangle\) is large: position \(3\) asked for a polarity cue and position \(1\) advertised one. No recurrent state is traversed.

\begin{example}[Agreement across a relative clause]
\label{ex:spr2025-nlp-keys-are}
Tokens: \(\texttt{The keys that the man holds are}\). The verb \(\texttt{are}\) at position \(7\) must agree with \(\texttt{keys}\) at position \(2\), not with the nearer noun \(\texttt{man}\). An RNN path from position \(2\) to position \(7\) has length \(5\). A Transformer layer still has a single score \(\langle q_7,k_2\rangle\). If that score dominates, \(p(2\mid 7)\) is large and \(z_7\) mixes in \(v_2\) (plural) rather than \(v_5\) (singular). This is again Path \(1\to L\), only the distance is larger; it is also Mixing weights, because the query at \(\texttt{are}\) is allowed to ignore the nearest noun.

\begin{example}[Two queries, two different reads: mixing weights]
\label{ex:spr2025-nlp-two-queries}
Take \(\texttt{The cat sat on the mat}\). The wiring is the same all-to-all graph as in the figure above. The distributions are not. At \(i=\) \(\texttt{sat}\) the query asks for a subject, so mass sits on \(\texttt{cat}\). At \(i=\) \(\texttt{on}\) the query asks for a landmark, so mass sits on \(\texttt{mat}\). That is the table row Mixing weights: \(p(j\mid i)\) is a function of \(q_i\), not a convolution window that is the same for every position.

    node[midway, left, font=\tiny, xshift=-1pt] {\(0.65\)};
  \draw[->, draw=teal!40] (c3)--(qs);
  \draw[->, very thick, draw=orange!70!black] (c6)--(qo)
    node[midway, right, font=\tiny, xshift=1pt] {\(0.60\)};
  \draw[->, draw=orange!40] (c4)--(qo);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\end{center}

An RNN has no such explicit \(p(\cdot\mid i)\). Whatever \(\texttt{cat}\) contributed has already been folded into \(h_t\) and then overwritten by later updates. The Transformer keeps \(v_{\texttt{cat}}\) and \(v_{\texttt{mat}}\) as separate summands and lets each query pick.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
For sequence-to-sequence training, a Transformer has two structural advantages over an LSTM/GRU. First, self-attention computes all token representations from \(Q,K,V\) in one parallel step, whereas an RNN must form \(h_t\) from \(h_{t-1}\). Second, any two positions interact inside a layer, so long-range information does not pass through \(L-1\) recurrent Jacobians and is less exposed to vanishing or exploding gradients. A third, related, advantage is that the mixing weights are input-dependent and can be replicated across heads. The trade-off is quadratic cost in \(L\), and generation remains sequential in new tokens.

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
Compare computation graphs, not names. An RNN updates \(h_t=f(h_{t-1},x_t)\). Training is sequential in \(L\). A signal from position \(1\) to \(L\) crosses \(L-1\) maps, which causes vanishing or exploding gradients and makes long-range dependence hard. A Transformer layer maps each \(x_i\) to \(q_i,k_i,v_i\), forms \(Q,K,V\), and computes \(\mathrm{Attn}=\mathrm{softmax}(QK^\top/\sqrt{d})\,V\). All \(L\) positions are computed together, so training is parallel in \(L\). Any two positions have a direct inner product \(\langle q_i,k_j\rangle\), so path length inside a layer is one. The softmax weights depend on the current query, and multi-head attention stores several such relations. Two exam advantages: parallel training, and short paths for long-range dependence (content-dependent retrieval is a valid third). Caveat: dense attention costs \(O(L^2)\), and autoregressive decoding is still sequential in newly generated tokens. The advantage is not ``parallel everywhere.''

\begin{note}[Do not count backward passes]\label{note:spr2025-nlp-p5-bwd}
The vanishing/exploding sentence is about the product of \(L-1\) recurrent Jacobians inside \emph{one} backward traversal, not about running backpropagation more times than a Transformer. Both models take one backward pass per training step. \Cref{note:spr2025-nlp-attn-chain} records the chain, the \(N\)-layer comparison, and why generation remains serial after the layer has already mixed the known prefix. \Cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-attn-read,rem:spr2025-nlp-qkv-softmax,rem:spr2025-nlp-wqkv-params} record the sum that implements the read, the three roles of \(Q,K,V\), and which of those objects are trained parameters.
\end{note}

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 6: a statistical-learning view of data scaling laws}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
Why test performance often improves as a regular function of sample size \(N\), from statistical learning, without assuming that the data are Gaussian and without treating a single exponent as a theorem.

\paragraph{Objects.}
Let \(\ell(h;x,y)\) be a pointwise loss and let \(\mathcal{D}\) be any distribution on \((x,y)\) with finite risk (no Gaussianity). Write
\[
L(h)=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\ell(h;x,y)]
\]
for the population risk. Let \(\mathcal{H}\) be the model class. Four predictors sit on this risk:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(h^\star\in\arg\min L\), the Bayes predictor (all measurable functions);
    \item \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\in\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}L\), the best member of the class;
    \item \(\widehat h_S\in\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}L_S\), the exact empirical-risk minimiser on a sample \(S\) of size \(N\);
    \item \(\widehat h\in\mathcal{H}\), the iterate actually returned after a finite optimisation.
\end{itemize}
The expected test risk then splits as
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h)\bigr]
=
L(h^\star)
+\bigl(L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)\bigr)
+\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]
+\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h)-L(\widehat h_S)\bigr].
\]
The four terms are irreducible error, approximation error, estimation error, and optimisation error. Only the third term is forced downward by larger \(N\): \(L_S\) concentrates around \(L\), so the in-class fit \(\widehat h_S\) moves toward \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\). The first term is a property of \(\mathcal{D}\) and \(\ell\). The second is a property of \(\mathcal{H}\). The fourth is a property of the optimiser and the number of steps.

An empirical data scaling law is a local fit
\[
\mathcal{L}(N)\approx\mathcal{L}_\infty+aN^{-\alpha}
\]
to the left-hand side (or to a related test loss) on a finite window of \(N\). The floor \(\mathcal{L}_\infty\) absorbs whatever the first, second, and fourth terms refuse to give up. The exponent \(\alpha>0\) is not universal.

\paragraph{Why the third term falls without Gaussians.}
Estimation error is a concentration statement. For a fixed \(h\), the empirical risk \(L_S(h)\) is the sample mean of the i.i.d.\ losses \(\ell(h;X_i,Y_i)\). The two inequalities below control \(\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\). Neither assumes that \((X,Y)\) is Gaussian.

\begin{lemma}[Chebyshev]
\label{lem:spr2025-nlp-chebyshev}
Let \(Z\) be a real random variable with finite variance. Then, for every \(\varepsilon>0\),
\[
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
\le
\frac{\mathrm{Var}(Z)}{\varepsilon^2}.
\]
In particular, if \(Z_1,\ldots,Z_N\) are i.i.d.\ copies of \(Z\) and \(\overline{Z}_N=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}Z_i\), then
\[
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert\overline{Z}_N-\mathbb{E}[Z]\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
\le
\frac{\mathrm{Var}(Z)}{N\varepsilon^2}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Markov's inequality on the nonnegative random variable \((Z-\mathbb{E}[Z])^2\) gives
\[
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
=\mathbb{P}\bigl((Z-\mathbb{E}[Z])^2\ge\varepsilon^2\bigr)
\le
\frac{\mathbb{E}[(Z-\mathbb{E}[Z])^2]}{\varepsilon^2}
=\frac{\mathrm{Var}(Z)}{\varepsilon^2}.
\]
For the sample mean, \(\mathbb{E}[\overline{Z}_N]=\mathbb{E}[Z]\) and independence yields
\[
\mathrm{Var}(\overline{Z}_N)
=\frac{1}{N^2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{Var}(Z_i)
=\frac{\mathrm{Var}(Z)}{N}.
\]
The first display with \(Z\) replaced by \(\overline{Z}_N\) is the second claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Hoeffding]
\label{lem:spr2025-nlp-hoeffding}
Let \(Z_1,\ldots,Z_N\) be independent and \(Z_i\in[a,b]\) almost surely. Then, for every \(\varepsilon>0\),
\[
\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\Biggl\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}Z_i-\mathbb{E}[Z_1]\Biggr\rvert\ge\varepsilon\Biggr)
\le
2\exp\Biggl(-\frac{2N\varepsilon^2}{(b-a)^2}\Biggr).
\]
If \(Z_i\in[0,1]\), this is \(2e^{-2N\varepsilon^2}\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It is enough to treat the centred variables \(W_i=Z_i-\mathbb{E}[Z_i]\), which still lie in an interval of length \(b-a\). The function \(w\mapsto e^{\lambda w}\) is convex, so on an interval \([c,d]\) of length \(b-a\) one has
\[
e^{\lambda w}
\le
\frac{d-w}{d-c}\,e^{\lambda c}+\frac{w-c}{d-c}\,e^{\lambda d}.
\]
Write \(\ell=d-c=b-a\) and \(p=-c/\ell\), so \(1-p=d/\ell\) and \(pc+(1-p)d=0\). Taking expectations and using \(\mathbb{E}[W_i]=0\) yields
\[
\mathbb{E}\bigl[e^{\lambda W_i}\bigr]
\le
(1-p)\,e^{\lambda c}+p\,e^{\lambda d}
=\exp\bigl(\psi(\lambda)\bigr),
\]
where \(\psi(\lambda)=\log\bigl((1-p)e^{\lambda c}+p\,e^{\lambda d}\bigr)\). Then \(\psi(0)=\psi'(0)=0\), and \(\psi''\) is the variance of a two-point law on \(\{c,d\}\), hence \(\psi''\le(\ell/2)^2=(b-a)^2/4\). Taylor expansion gives \(\psi(\lambda)\le\lambda^2(b-a)^2/8\), so
\[
\mathbb{E}\bigl[e^{\lambda W_i}\bigr]
\le
\exp\bigl(\lambda^2(b-a)^2/8\bigr).
\]
Write \(S_N=\sum_{i=1}^{N}W_i\). Independence and Chernoff give, for every \(\lambda>0\),
\[
\mathbb{P}(S_N\ge N\varepsilon)
\le
e^{-\lambda N\varepsilon}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[e^{\lambda W_i}\bigr]
\le
\exp\bigl(-\lambda N\varepsilon+N\lambda^2(b-a)^2/8\bigr).
\]
The exponent is minimised at \(\lambda=4\varepsilon/(b-a)^2\), and the minimum value is \(-2N\varepsilon^2/(b-a)^2\). The lower tail \(\mathbb{P}(S_N\le -N\varepsilon)\) is the same bound applied to \(-W_i\). A union of the two tails is the claim. The case \([a,b]=[0,1]\) is the specialisation \(b-a=1\).
\end{proof}

Fix \(h\in\mathcal{H}\) and set \(Z_i=\ell(h;X_i,Y_i)\). Then \(L_S(h)=\overline{Z}_N\) and \(L(h)=\mathbb{E}[Z_1]\). If \(\mathrm{Var}(\ell(h;X,Y))\le\sigma^2\) uniformly in \(h\), \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-chebyshev} gives
\[
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
\le
\frac{\sigma^2}{N\varepsilon^2}.
\]
If \(\ell(h;x,y)\in[0,1]\) (rescale a bounded loss by its range), \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-hoeffding} gives
\[
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
\le
2e^{-2N\varepsilon^2}.
\]
Neither bound uses a Gaussian law of \((X,Y)\). A single fixed \(h\) is not enough: the empirical minimiser \(\widehat h_S\) is a function of \(S\), so it is not an instance of the previous displays. The correct event is a uniform deviation. Write
\[
\Delta_N
:=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert.
\]

\begin{lemma}[ERM is controlled by \(\Delta_N\)]
\label{lem:spr2025-nlp-erm-unif}
Let \(\widehat h_S\in\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}L_S(h)\) and \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\in\arg\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}L(h)\). Then
\[
0
\le
L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})
\le
2\Delta_N.
\]
In particular
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]
\le
2\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N].
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Nonnegativity is the definition of \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\). Split
\begin{align*}
L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})
&=
\bigl(L(\widehat h_S)-L_S(\widehat h_S)\bigr)
+\bigl(L_S(\widehat h_S)-L_S(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr)
+\bigl(L_S(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr).
\end{align*}
The middle difference is \(\le 0\) because \(\widehat h_S\) minimises \(L_S\) on \(\mathcal{H}\). Each of the remaining two terms is at most \(\Delta_N\), so the sum is at most \(2\Delta_N\). Take expectations.
\end{proof}

Thus it is enough to send \(\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]\) to zero. If the loss takes values in \([0,1]\), then \(\Delta_N\in[0,1]\) and
\[
\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]
=\int_0^1\mathbb{P}(\Delta_N>t)\,dt.
\]

\paragraph{Finite class.}
Let \(\lvert\mathcal{H}\rvert=M<\infty\). The event \(\{\Delta_N>\varepsilon\}\) is the union over \(h\in\mathcal{H}\) of the pointwise events. The union bound and \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-hoeffding} give
\[
\mathbb{P}(\Delta_N\ge\varepsilon)
\le
\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
\le
2M\,e^{-2N\varepsilon^2}.
\]
Hence, for every \(\varepsilon\in(0,1]\),
\[
\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]
\le
\varepsilon+\int_{\varepsilon}^1 2M\,e^{-2Nt^2}\,dt
\le
\varepsilon+2M\,e^{-2N\varepsilon^2}.
\]
Choose \(\varepsilon_N=\sqrt{(\log(2MN))/(2N)}\). Then \(\varepsilon_N\to 0\) and
\[
2M\,e^{-2N\varepsilon_N^2}
=2M\cdot e^{-\log(2MN)}
=\frac{1}{N}\to 0,
\]
so \(\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]\to 0\). \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-erm-unif} yields
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]\to 0.
\]
The same argument with \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-chebyshev} replaces the tail by \(M\sigma^2/(N t^2)\). Then
\[
\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]
\le
\varepsilon+\int_{\varepsilon}^1\frac{M\sigma^2}{Nt^2}\,dt
=\varepsilon+\frac{M\sigma^2}{N}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}-1\Bigr)
\le
\varepsilon+\frac{M\sigma^2}{N\varepsilon}.
\]
The choice \(\varepsilon_N=\sqrt{M\sigma^2/N}\) gives \(\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]\le 2\sqrt{M\sigma^2/N}\to 0\).

\paragraph{VC-controlled class.}
Now \(\mathcal{H}\) may be infinite. Take first the \(0\)--\(1\) loss, so that restrictions of \(\mathcal{H}\) are dichotomies, and assume \(\VCdim(\mathcal{H})=d<\infty\). (If \(\ell\) is only \([0,1]\)-valued, replace \(\mathcal{H}\) by the subgraph class of the loss; finite VC dimension of that class is the same polynomial growth.) Let \(\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n)\) be the growth function: the maximal number of dichotomies \(\mathcal{H}\) realises on \(n\) points. Sauer--Shelah gives \(\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(n)\le(en/d)^d\) for \(n\ge d\). A single union bound over \(\mathcal{H}\) is impossible. Pass to a ghost sample \(S'=(X_i',Y_i')_{i=1}^N\), independent of \(S\) and with the same law, and write \(L_{S'}\) for its empirical risk.

On the event \(\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon\), \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-hoeffding} on \(S'\) (loss in \([0,1]\), deviation \(\varepsilon/2\)) yields
\[
\mathbb{P}_{S'}\bigl(\lvert L_{S'}(h)-L(h)\rvert<\varepsilon/2\bigr)
\ge
1-2e^{-N\varepsilon^2/2}.
\]
For \(N\ge(2/\varepsilon^2)\log 4\) this probability is at least \(1/2\). Therefore
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{P}_S(\Delta_N\ge\varepsilon)
&=\mathbb{P}_S\bigl(\exists h\in\mathcal{H}:\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon\bigr)
\\
&\le
2\,\mathbb{P}_{S,S'}\bigl(\exists h:\lvert L_S(h)-L(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon,\ \lvert L_{S'}(h)-L(h)\rvert<\varepsilon/2\bigr)
\\
&\le
2\,\mathbb{P}_{S,S'}\bigl(\exists h:\lvert L_S(h)-L_{S'}(h)\rvert\ge\varepsilon/2\bigr).
\end{align*}
Conditionally on the pooled \(2N\) points, independently swap each pair \(\bigl((X_i,Y_i),(X_i',Y_i')\bigr)\) with probability \(1/2\). Equivalently, if \(\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_N\) are i.i.d.\ Rademacher signs independent of the pool and \(Z_i(h)=\ell(h;X_i,Y_i)\),
\[
L_S(h)-L_{S'}(h)
=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_i\bigl(Z_i(h)-Z_i'(h)\bigr).
\]
The number of distinct restrictions of \(\mathcal{H}\) on the pool is at most \(\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(2N)\). For each fixed restriction the summands \(\sigma_i(Z_i-Z_i')\) are independent, mean-zero, and valued in \([-1,1]\). \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-hoeffding} with range length \(2\) and deviation \(\varepsilon/2\) gives the conditional tail
\[
2\exp\Biggl(-\frac{2N(\varepsilon/2)^2}{4}\Biggr)
=2e^{-N\varepsilon^2/8}.
\]
The union bound over at most \(\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(2N)\) dichotomies, then Sauer--Shelah, produces
\[
\mathbb{P}(\Delta_N\ge\varepsilon)
\le
4\,\Pi_{\mathcal{H}}(2N)\,e^{-N\varepsilon^2/8}
\le
4\Bigl(\frac{2eN}{d}\Bigr)^d e^{-N\varepsilon^2/8},
\]
for all \(N\) large enough that the ghost-sample comparison applies (in particular \(2N\ge d\)). The same integral as in the finite case gives, for every \(\varepsilon\in(0,1]\),
\[
\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]
\le
\varepsilon+4\Bigl(\frac{2eN}{d}\Bigr)^d e^{-N\varepsilon^2/8}.
\]
Choose \(\varepsilon_N=\sqrt{(16d\log N)/N}\). Then \(\varepsilon_N\to 0\) and \(N\varepsilon_N^2/8=2d\log N\), so
\[
\Bigl(\frac{2eN}{d}\Bigr)^d e^{-N\varepsilon_N^2/8}
=\Bigl(\frac{2e}{d}\Bigr)^d N^d\cdot N^{-2d}
=\Bigl(\frac{2e}{d}\Bigr)^d N^{-d}\to 0.
\]
Hence \(\mathbb{E}_S[\Delta_N]\to 0\), and \cref{lem:spr2025-nlp-erm-unif} again yields
\[
\mathbb{E}_S\bigl[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})\bigr]\to 0.
\]
Bounded loss on a discrete vocabulary is the language-model case (rescale if the range is \([0,M]\)). No coordinate of \((X,Y)\) need be Gaussian. What extra data do, concretely, is fill rare contexts: a trigram cell with count \(1\) has variance of order \(1\); the same cell with count \(N_c\) has variance of order \(1/N_c\).

\begin{example}[Trigram cell, not a Gaussian]
Fix a context \(u=(\texttt{New},\texttt{York})\) and a next token \(w\). The ML estimate \(\widehat p(w\mid u)=c(u,w)/c(u)\) is a multinomial frequency. Its variance is \(p(1-p)/c(u)\), which is a function of a discrete distribution. Doubling the corpus, if it doubles \(c(u)\), halves that variance. The population target \(p(w\mid u)\) need not come from a normal; the tokens are atoms in \(\mathcal{V}\). The same \(N\) does nothing to the approximation error of a unigram, which cannot represent \(p(w\mid u)\) at all, and nothing to an optimiser that is stopped far from \(\widehat h_S\).
\end{example}

\paragraph{When the curve is not a power law.}
The fit \(\mathcal{L}_\infty+aN^{-\alpha}\) fails or changes slope when: the new data are duplicates or from a shifted \(\mathcal{D}\); \(\mathcal{H}\) is too small, so the second term dominates and extra \(N\) cannot help; optimisation error dominates (early stopping, bad stepsize); or one has already reached the floor \(\mathcal{L}_\infty\). The official warning against a Gaussian assumption is the same point: the mechanism is concentration of \(L_S\), not a normal likelihood.

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
Write the four-term decomposition. Identify data scaling with a drop in estimation error: more samples make \(L_S\) a better proxy for \(L\), so \(\widehat h_S\) approaches \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\). State that this uses only bounded or finite-variance losses and a complexity control on \(\mathcal{H}\), not Gaussian data. Quote the empirical power law as a local description of that drop plus a floor. Name at least two ways the curve can break.

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
Do not assume that \((X,Y)\) is Gaussian. Let \(L(h)=\mathbb{E}[\ell(h;X,Y)]\). Split
\[
\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h)]
=L(h^\star)+\underbrace{L(h_{\mathcal{H}})-L(h^\star)}_{\text{approx.}}
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h_S)-L(h_{\mathcal{H}})]}_{\text{estimation}}
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_S[L(\widehat h)-L(\widehat h_S)]}_{\text{opt.}}.
\]
A data scaling law is the observation that, over a finite range, the left-hand side behaves as \(\mathcal{L}_\infty+aN^{-\alpha}\). The statistical reason is the third term. For a bounded loss (language-model NLL on a finite \(\mathcal{V}\) is enough) and a class of controlled complexity, \(L_S\) concentrates around \(L\), so the empirical minimiser \(\widehat h_S\) approaches the best-in-class \(h_{\mathcal{H}}\). Rare conditionals get more counts; their frequencies have variance of order \(1/c(\text{context})\). Extra data do not shrink approximation error (that is \(\mathcal{H}\)) or irreducible error (that is \(\mathcal{D}\)). The power \(\alpha\) is empirical. The curve fails if the new data are duplicated or shifted, if \(\mathcal{H}\) is too small, if optimisation never reaches \(\widehat h_S\), or if one is already at the floor. Gaussianity is unnecessary because concentration does not require it.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 7: RLHF after reward-model training}
\label{sol:spr2026-rlhf}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
The reward model \(r_\phi\) is already trained and frozen. Explain how reinforcement learning then aligns the language model: what data are used at this stage, what scalar is maximised, and what the gradient of that scalar is. Pairwise preference labels are \emph{not} the training data of this stage; they were used earlier to fit \(\phi\).

\paragraph{Objects.}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(x\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{prompt}}\): a prompt. This is the only human-facing input at the RL stage.
    \item \(\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\): the generator, an autoregressive policy with weights \(\theta\). Completions are \(y=(y_1,\ldots,y_T)\) and
    \[
    \log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)
    =\sum_{t=1}^{T}\log\pi_\theta(y_t\mid x,y_{<t}).
    \]
    \item \(r_\phi(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\): the frozen reward model. It returns a scalar score, not a pair \((y_w,y_l)\).
    \item \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\): a frozen reference policy, usually the supervised-fine-tuned model. It is not updated.
    \item \(\beta>0\): the KL coefficient, a leash strength.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Data flow, in order.}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Draw a prompt \(x\).
    \item Sample a completion \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\) from the \emph{current} generator (on-policy).
    \item Score it by the frozen judge: \(r\leftarrow r_\phi(x,y)\).
    \item Compute the KL penalty \(\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)-\log\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)\).
    \item Form a scalar advantage and take a policy-gradient step in \(\theta\).
    \item Repeat. Never open \(\phi\) or \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Objective and gradient.}
The KL-regularised return is
\[
J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{prompt}},\,y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}
\Bigl[
r_\phi(x,y)
-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
\Bigr].
\]
The first term raises the probability of completions the judge likes. The second term is \(\beta\,\mathrm{KL}(\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot\mid x))\) written as an on-policy expectation; it charges the generator for leaving the reference, which limits reward hacking (exploiting errors in \(r_\phi\)) and collapse of fluency.

The score-function identity \(\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta(y\mid x)=\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\,\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\) gives
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\Bigl[
\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)
\bigl(
r_\phi(x,y)
-\beta\log\tfrac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}
-b(x)
\bigr)
\Bigr].
\]
The baseline \(b(x)\) (a value estimate, or a batch mean) does not change the expectation; it cuts the variance of the Monte Carlo estimator. The motivation and a two-completion reading are in \cref{rem:spr2025-nlp-baseline}. In practice one estimates the expectation by a batch of on-policy rollouts and applies PPO or a related trust-region update so that \(\theta\) does not jump out of the region where \(r_\phi\) and \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) still make sense.

\begin{example}[One prompt, two completions]
Let \(x=\) ``Translate into French: dog''. Suppose the current policy samples \(y^{(1)}=\texttt{chien}\) with \(\log\pi_\theta=-0.4\) and \(y^{(2)}=\texttt{dog dog}\) with \(\log\pi_\theta=-1.2\). The frozen judge returns \(r_\phi(x,y^{(1)})=1.8\) and \(r_\phi(x,y^{(2)})=-0.6\). If \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) assigns \(\log\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}=-0.5\) and \(-2.0\) respectively, and \(\beta=0.1\), the unnormalised returns are
\[
1.8-0.1\bigl((-0.4)-(-0.5)\bigr)=1.79,
\qquad
-0.6-0.1\bigl((-1.2)-(-2.0)\bigr)=-0.68.
\]
A policy-gradient step multiplies \(\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y^{(i)}\mid x)\) by these (baseline-centred) returns: probability mass moves toward \(y^{(1)}\) and away from \(y^{(2)}\). No preference pair is read at this step; the judge has already compressed preferences into \(r_\phi\).
\end{example}

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
After \(\phi\) is frozen, alignment is on-policy RL on prompts. Sample \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\), score it by \(r_\phi\), and maximise expected reward minus a KL leash to \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\). The gradient is the score function times a centred return. PPO is the usual stable implementation. The pairwise labels are not reused here.

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
Assume \(r_\phi\) is trained and frozen. The RL stage uses prompts \(x\), not the original pairs \((y_w,y_l)\). For each \(x\), sample \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\) from the current policy, evaluate \(r_\phi(x,y)\), and maximise
\[
J(\theta)
=\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\Bigl[r_\phi(x,y)-\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(y\mid x)}\Bigr].
\]
The reward term steers toward completions the judge likes. The KL term, with frozen reference \(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\) (usually the SFT model), prevents reward hacking and loss of fluency. The gradient is
\[
\nabla_\theta J
=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\,(r_\phi-\beta\log(\pi_\theta/\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})-b(x))\bigr],
\]
by the score-function identity; \(b(x)\) is a baseline for variance reduction. For an LLM, \(\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)=\sum_t\log\pi_\theta(y_t\mid x,y_{<t})\). In practice one estimates this by on-policy batches and updates with PPO. Do not say that RLHF at this stage trains on pairwise labels; those labels trained \(r_\phi\).

\paragraph{Common mistake.}
Do not reopen \(\phi\), do not treat \(r_\phi\) as a human in the loop, and do not replace this stage by DPO unless the question asks for an alternative that drops the reward model.

\subsubsection{Solution to Problem 8: domain question answering without labeled QA pairs}

\paragraph{What the question is asking.}
A general-purpose LLM lacks domain facts. A large domain corpus is available, but \emph{no} labeled question--answer pairs. Design a system that answers domain questions. The constraint forbids supervised QA fine-tuning as the first move: the corpus must be used as an uncompressed memory.

\paragraph{Objects.}
Let \(\mathcal{C}\) be the domain corpus. Split it into chunks \(d_1,\ldots,d_M\). Let \(e(\cdot)\) be an embedding map (the representation of Problem~2). Let \(\operatorname{sim}\) be cosine or an inner product. Let \(q\) be a user question and let \(\pi_\theta\) be the given LLM, used only as a generator, not as a store of domain facts.

\paragraph{Offline (once).}
Parse and clean \(\mathcal{C}\), keep source identifiers, split into chunks, compute \(e(d_i)\), and index the vectors. A lexical index (BM25) may sit beside the dense index for hybrid retrieval. No QA pair is required: the objects stored are raw passages.

\paragraph{Online (each question).}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Embed the question: \(e(q)\).
    \item Retrieve
    \[
    i^\star\in\operatorname*{arg\,top\text{-}k}_{i}\operatorname{sim}\bigl(e(q),e(d_i)\bigr),
    \]
    optionally fuse with BM25 and rerank with a cross-encoder, then pack a token-budgeted evidence set \(d_{i^\star}\) plus metadata.
    \item Generate from the \emph{conditioned} distribution \(\pi_\theta(y\mid q,d_{i^\star})\), with instructions to answer only from that evidence, to cite chunk identifiers, and to abstain if the evidence is insufficient.
\end{enumerate}
This is retrieval-augmented generation. Fine-tuning \(\theta\) on the raw corpus is the wrong first design: next-token training on documents does not produce question--answer behaviour, and it still compresses facts into \(\theta\), where they go stale.

\begin{example}[Three chunks, one question]
The corpus yields \(d_1=\) ``Drug A is contraindicated in pregnancy.'', \(d_2=\) ``Drug A is taken once daily with food.'', \(d_3=\) ``Drug B treats hypertension.'' The question is \(q=\) ``Can a pregnant patient take Drug A?'' A usable \(e\) places \(e(q)\) nearest \(e(d_1)\), then \(e(d_2)\). The generator sees \((q,d_1,d_2)\) and is required to cite \(d_1\). It should not answer from \(d_3\), and it should abstain if the top chunks had been only \(d_3\). No labeled pair \((q,\text{``No''})\) was needed to build the index.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Evaluation and failures.}
Score retrieval and generation separately: recall@\(k\) and reranker quality for \(d_{i^\star}\); correctness, groundedness (does the cited chunk entail the answer?), citation support, abstention, and latency for \(y\). A small expert-reviewed set of questions is enough to start; synthetic questions must not be the only test. Failures: wrong neighbours under \(\operatorname{sim}\) (similarity is not entailment), stale or conflicting documents, context-window overflow, prompt injection inside retrieved text, and leakage of restricted documents. Repairs: hybrid retrieval plus reranking, access control before lookup, provenance, abstention, and logs of empty or contradictory retrievals.

\paragraph{Complete solution.}
Build an offline chunk index with embeddings; at query time retrieve top-\(k\), rerank, and generate \(y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid q,d_{i^\star})\) under a cite-or-abstain instruction. The unlabeled corpus is the knowledge base. Do not start by fine-tuning facts into \(\theta\).

\paragraph{Exam-ready answer.}
There are no labeled QA pairs, so do not fine-tune a QA head and do not try to pack the corpus into \(\theta\). Split the domain documents into chunks \(d_i\), embed them with \(e(\cdot)\), and index \(e(d_i)\) offline; keep source ids and, if possible, a BM25 index. Online, embed the question \(q\), retrieve \(i^\star=\arg\mathrm{top}\text{-}k_i\operatorname{sim}(e(q),e(d_i))\), optionally rerank, and generate from \(\pi_\theta(y\mid q,d_{i^\star})\) with instructions to use only that evidence, cite it, and abstain if it is insufficient. This is RAG: the corpus is uncompressed memory; the LLM is a generator, not a knowledge base. Evaluate retrieval (recall@\(k\)) separately from answer groundedness. Main failures: a nearest neighbour need not be an answer, documents go stale, and retrieved text can inject prompts---hence hybrid retrieval, reranking, access control, and abstention. Fine-tuning on raw documents is a later, optional patch for style, not the way to supply missing facts.
